**Author:** Joseph D. Kirchner

**Date:** Pre-publishing Edition, September 2026

# Preface

This book grew out of two articles written in the summer of 2026 on a single question: where the immunity of officials comes from, and whether the source bears the weight placed on it. The first read the founding case of the doctrine from its surviving file in The National Archives; the second traced the American doctrine built on that case from the bottom up, from what the opinions themselves cite. The two arguments proved to be one, and this book is that argument, with the record restored and the comparison added.

Part of what launched me into the research was K. J. Kesselring's study of conspiracy in the Court of Star Chamber, published in 2025, which set *Floyd v. Barker* back among the court's own cases and named the archives in which the case survives: Coke's printed report, a second account in a Harvard manuscript, and the interrogatories and pleadings that remain in The National Archives. Reading those archives entire is what followed, and her work is cited wherever the book rests on it.

I do not read Latin, Greek or Hebrew. Where the book quotes those languages it quotes what is printed on the page, sets the translations of the period beside it, and states as undecided any reading that remains in doubt; those readings await professional review, and the book says so where it happens. Where no translation of the period exists and the note names no published translation, as for the Greek and Latin of Part 5 and the Latin and Hebrew of chapter 1.4, the English beside the original was prepared for this book from the edition the note cites and stands unverified against any published translation; those renderings await the same review. The transcription of the cause file was verified by Christopher Whittick, who is the author of the final texts the book quotes. The errors that remain are mine.

Joseph D. Kirchner
September 2026

# Introduction

In 1600 a man drowned in Anglesey, with his horse. Years later the death was recast as murder. Two men of one family were executed, one of them for that death, on the examinations of Welsh-speaking witnesses "written in English contrary to their sayings in welsh," and the judge who tried the cause would not let the jury hear the examinations of the men who had found the body. When a surviving kinsman carried a sworn complaint against that judge to the Star Chamber at Westminster, under a vow made to a dying man, the court did not answer it. In the Easter term of 1607, by the report's own dating, it ordered the bill "taken off the file and cancelled, and utterly defaced," fined the complainant, sent him to the pillory and to prison, and resolved that a judge "shall not be drawn in question before any other Judge, for any surmise of corruption, except before the King himself." The case is *Floyd v. Barker*, and it is named for the man it crushed: Floyd is the complainant, not the judge.

The question this book follows is older than any clause of the American Constitution. In every legal order this book has read before 1607, from Babylon to the England of the dooms, the judge who wronged a subject answered for it: each order met the wrong with a remedy and stated the judge's duty, and none met it with a shield. Among the orders read, the rule of 1607 is the exception, and English and American jurisprudence, the two orders that carried it forward, stand apart from that record: their doctrine extinguishes the subject's remedy against the judge and, unlike even the codes that share that denial, the sovereign's remedy against him as well. The forum the rule reserved supplies the question's second part. A plea transferred to a King who never answered it was not adjudged, only refused, and a complaint rejected unheard is not, on any page of the record this book has read, justice done. The clauses and statutes of the American chapters, the oath, the duty to take care, the law of records, are the instruments by which the receiving order is measured against its own text; they are not the question.

Every American whom official immunity turns away from a federal courthouse is turned away by the decree of 1607. The doctrine entered United States law in 1869, when the Supreme Court in *Randall v. Brigham* received the rule of 1607 by citation, "as old as the law," from Coke's report and without the record. It was read into a statute whose text makes "every person" liable; it hardened into the rule that an official who violates the Constitution answers only if the right was "clearly established"; it reached the presidency in 1982; and in 2024 it reached the criminal law itself. Each decision in that chain is a node. Common law, in this book, is the body of judicial decisions that interpret de jure law, the text as written, according to the principle of stare decisis. A node is one such decision, whatever the court and whatever the law. The received usage keeps the name common law for the English inheritance and calls American decisions precedent, as if the two were different things. No such distinction exists; this book finds none. At no node of that chain did a court produce a constitutional provision, and at no node did a court read the case. What the receiving courts had was the report. What none of them had was the file, which the report's own decree had mutilated and which this book reads entire. Even the report went unread. Its root sentence, as printed from 1656 to this day, forbids proof against the presumption that a judge sworn to do justice "will do injustice", the reverse of the rule four centuries of courts have taken it to state; the sentence the right way round lay in Coke's notebook, unprinted until 2025, and no decision in the chain appears to have noticed that the page in front of it said the opposite. The Court that received the case dated it a year late and repeated the misdate a century on; a court that had read the report would have taken its term from the report.

Three bars faced the subject wronged by a sworn officer in 1607. No proof could be admitted against the official's act, because records "import verity in themselves" and the law would admit nothing against the presumption of official rectitude: the first bar, verity by status. The grievance could be heard nowhere but "before the King himself," because to question the officer was to question the Crown: the second bar, the reserved forum. And the King "in his own person cannot adjudge any case," and "the party cannot have remedy against the King": the third bar, the remedy foreclosed. No proof, only the sovereign, and the sovereign cannot act and owes nothing. Chapter 1.3 § A sets the three out from the reports of 1607 in which Coke fixed them; chapter 2.1 shows the Founding cutting all three; chapter 3.1 shows the modern doctrine rebuilding them, bar for bar.

The rule was not English law at its own leaf. For every proposition about the record and about conspiracy the report cites English authority; for the rule it cites none. It rests the rule on the King's person, on a line of a Roman tragedian taken through Cicero, and on a gloss of what it is to answer to God, and the one English judge it names, Thorpe, was a Chief Justice condemned to death for the corruption the rule declares unquestionable, under the very oath the report invokes. The court's own practice in the decade before ran the other way: it recited Thorpe as living law, heard a subject's bill against a justice of the peace for a judicial act and decided it on the merits, and a contemporary hand that sat through the Easter term of 1607 recorded four sittings and no cause between Floyd and Barker. Chapter 1.4 reads the leaf and each source it names.

The book's case against the doctrine rests on two grounds, stated in chapters 2.2 and 3.3 and applied in the chapters around them. The first is the transfer thesis. The rule of 1607 did not extinguish the subject's plea against a judge; it transferred the plea to the King, the one person who on the rule's own theory of delegation could hear it. The King never answered such a plea. Where a subject sought that forum the petition itself was prosecuted; the one hearing on record was a peer's; and a rule whose only operation was the King's answer had been refused by its own apex a generation before the court that made it was abolished in 1641.

American jurisprudence then received the rule with no King to receive the plea. Two arguments have been made against it since, and this book's case begins where they stop. The first is irony. In 1989 Donald and Joan Hunziker, hog farmers of Stephenson County, Illinois, whose livestock, grain and equipment a sheriff had seized at their farmstead on a writ issued without notice, asked the Seventh Circuit, without counsel, how the courts of a sovereign people can rest on a rule "derived from a system assuming the power to govern by divine right." The writ had been served on a bond that was not secured, though the statute required a secured bond before service. The court of appeals held the writ proper all the same, extended the judge's immunity to the sheriff who served it, and answered the argument from 1607 in a footnote of an order it left unpublished and, by the circuit's own rule, not to be cited as precedent. The order of 1607 took the bill off the file and defaced it; the order of 1990 kept its answer out of the record of the law. Thirty-five years later the dissent of 2024 found a "twisted irony" in the duty to take care read as a license. The second is a weighing, the official's fear of suit against the citizen's fear of a lawless official, from Justice Douglas in 1967 to that same dissent. But irony alone cannot move a court, and a fear set against a fear leaves the court free to credit whichever it prefers.

The restored record and the receiving order's judgment on the act of 1607 show repugnancy; they do not say where the constitutional incompatibility lies. This book says where. A plea that can be heard by none but the King presupposes a King able to answer it. In American jurisprudence the King is the Constitution. The Constitution cannot be sat before, take an account of a judge or rehear what an Article III judge left unheard; it delegates the judicial power to courts, so that the answer is given in court. A rule whose whole operation is the sovereign's answer cannot operate as common law under a sovereign that cannot answer, and the founding generation adopted of the common law only what was "applicable to their situation." That is not an irony that lurks in the reader's eye. It is a logic that lurks in the record, and a question that has lurked there unconsidered is a question no court has decided.

The second ground is the founding act itself. The law that received the rule treats the destruction of a court-filed complaint as a felony that forfeits office, presumes destroyed proof adverse to its destroyer, names official concealment that extinguishes a claim a denial of due process and of access to the courts, and withdraws from every tribunal the power to deface a thing whose existence is the offence. Measured by the receiving order's own law (an order, as this book uses the word, is a sovereign's law taken whole, not a single decree), the act that made the rule is the wrong the rule exists to foreclose.

One rationale holds the chain together at every node, and the book follows it from its birth. The doctrine rests on a prediction, that the official exposed to suit will not act. The prediction entered the cause of 1607 as the accused sheriff's pleading, that the bill was contrived to "stryke a feare into the h[a]rts" of the county's jurors, and left it as the report's rule; the report did not find the fear, it adopted it. The Founding met the same rationale in Parliament's own voice, in the Act of 1774 that removed the trials of officials lest they be "discouraged from the proper Discharge of their Duty", and, in the Declaration, enumerated the protecting of officials "by a mock Trial, from punishment" among the acts that define a tyrant. At every node since, the prediction has been credited for the official and never tested against the citizen, and the doctrine's critics have answered it with predictions of their own. This book answers it with the record. A record is not weighed against a fear. It displaces the need for one.

Put to other sovereigns as their law stands today, the question the rule reserved, what a subject may do against a judge, returns a finding of the same shape as the older record's. Asked who pays for the wrong done to the subject, England's law as written gives the answer of the codes of Moscow, Beijing, Pyongyang and Riyadh, not the answer of the continental kingdoms, which moved that cost onto the State. Asked whether the judge is exposed for his decision, those codes keep him punishable and removable by a political or supervisory organ, so that the exposure of the judge which Part 1 records in Coke's England survives in them as an instrument of control, and England's judge is the most immune of any order read. The Anglo-American rule thus shares with the codes the denial of the subject's remedy and, alone among the orders read, denies the sovereign's as well; the kingdoms differ from both, for the State pays and the judge answers to no sovereign's displeasure. Chapter 4.1 states the degree to which each order is examined; the finding is a classification of texts, and the book claims nothing more for it.

The title names the plea and the book's two labours. The plea is Floyd's, defaced unheard, and the plea of every subject after him whom the rule sent to a King who did not answer. The history is restorative because it restores the record the report displaced: the cause file in The National Archives, transcribed entire and conformed to a commissioned professional transcription; the second contemporaneous account of the case in a Harvard manuscript; the letters and registers of the county where the cause arose; the reporter's own notebook, in print only since 2025; and the early printed Bibles, the Roman and canon texts and Hobbes, each read at the page for what the 1607 court made of it. It is comparative because it puts the question the rule reserved, what a subject may do against a judge, to other sovereigns and to the older record: to England as it stands today and as it stood in 1701, to the kingdoms whose laws answer the subject and the codes that answer the sovereign against the judge, and to Babylon and Athens, Rome, Byzantium, Francia and the England before the rule. And it treats sovereign, absolute and qualified immunity as three names for one exemption, descended from one decree, because that is what the genealogy shows them to be. The decree itself had no name for what it conferred: the report's phrase is the account to God and the King, the century's theorist of sovereignty called the thing impunity and counted it a vice, and "immunity" as a right of the officer is the doctrine's own word, not its sources'. The title says history because the case is made from the record it restores: Part 3 argues it.

A word on what this book does not argue. It does not contend that the distinction between public and private wrongs is illegitimate, or that crimes may be privately prosecuted, or that the executive's enforcement discretion is void. It does not ask any court to overrule *Harlow v. Fitzgerald* or *Trump v. United States*. Its ambition is narrower and more unsettling: to show that a court faithful to those precedents can decline the apparatus on the doctrine's own terms, because the doctrine read as written reaches less than its defenders suppose, because the structural questions the lineage never reached remain open under the settled principle that what an opinion never considered it never decided, and because several of the critiques hold even if the doctrine is assumed sound in every respect its defenders claim. The argument is available to a bound district court, not only to a Supreme Court inclined to reconsider, because the principle that keeps those questions open is the Supreme Court's own: questions "which merely lurk in the record, neither brought to the attention of the court nor ruled upon, are not to be considered as having been so decided as to constitute precedents", the Court said in *Webster v. Fall* in 1925, and chapter 3.5 § B applies the principle to the lineage.

The book proceeds in five Parts, and the order needs a word. Part 1 (1591–1769) reads the English origin and repudiation: the country, the cause file, the report, the sources the report named, Blackstone's statement of the rule, and the dismantling of the court and its premise between 1641 and 1701. Part 2 (1761–2024) follows the reception in America from the Revolution to the apex: what stood ready for reception in 1791 was not a rule awaiting adoption but a rule whose court, record and receiver were gone, and it crossed anyway, by citation. The two Parts overlap by eight years because the two histories do: the American quarrel with the prerogative began in 1761, before Blackstone had finished stating the English rule in 1769, and Blackstone's statement is the one the reception took. Part 3 makes the constitutional case: the three bars rebuilt, the two Articles the doctrine names and neither of which supplies what it needs, the founding act measured by the receiving order's law, the guarantees the rebuilt bar on proof displaces stated clause by clause, the doctrine failing its own canons, and what a court may do without overruling anything; a Conclusion to Parts 2 and 3 closes the American argument before the comparison begins. Part 4 puts the same question to other sovereigns, in a vocabulary of four terms that chapter 4.1 defines. Part 5 then reaches back before 1607 and reads the older record in the vocabulary Part 4 has fixed. The book runs forward from 1607 to 2024 and then back, because the older record is read for what each order did with the judge's answer, and that is the question Part 4 has taught the reader to ask. A Conclusion closes the five Parts.

# Method

A doctrine that names no constitutional provision cannot be measured against one. It can be measured against itself, against the words it uses, the sources it cites and the canons its issuing court applies to everyone else; and its foundation can be measured against the record that foundation was made by destroying. This book's method has three parts, and the chapters cite them by letter. Section A states the hierarchy of interpretive discipline the doctrine inverts. Section B states the reading discipline: the record before the report, every source at its page, the genealogy built from the bottom up, and the vocabulary in which the book's comparative claims are made. Section C states the source-text audit, the verb test and the strategic paraphrase. Nothing in the method depends on a contested theory of interpretation. It depends on two propositions: that a court is bound by the linguistic rules it imposes on the litigants before it, and that a reader is bound to read what is on the page before crediting what is said of it.

### A. The Hierarchy of Interpretive Discipline: The More Democratic the Text, the Stricter Its Reading

Every interpretive method recognised in American law presupposes that words have determinate meaning recoverable by analysis. The presupposition is the condition of adjudication itself: a court's authority to say what a legal text means rests on the premise that the text means something independent of the court's preference, and a tribunal that announced that legal words need not bear their ordinary senses, or that an incoherent doctrine still binds, would in the same breath dissolve the ground of its own authority to declare anything. When a doctrine's defence depends on reading a word against its settled sense, treating a singular as a plural, a qualified term as an absolute, or a verb of acknowledgment as a verb of creation, the defence cannot be sustained from the bench without forfeiting the authority the statement claims to exercise. The linguistic objection is one a court cannot answer adversely while preserving its power to answer anything.

Legal texts are not equal in democratic provenance, and the strictness with which they are read should track that provenance. The Constitution issues from the people in their ratifying capacity; a statute from the elected legislature through presentment; a regulation from delegated authority under notice and comment; a common-law rule from centuries of decision under legislative tolerance; a modern judge-made doctrine from judicial preference alone. The ordering is one of legitimacy, and it yields a rule that should be uncontroversial: the more democratically grounded the text, the stronger its claim to be read exactly as written, and the weaker a text's democratic warrant, the less latitude its author may claim to have it read loosely. Federal courts honour the first half of that rule scrupulously, holding Congress to the indefinite article, the choice of preposition and the grammar of a clause. They do not honour the second half. Judge-made immunity is read with a generosity no statute receives, and the result is an inversion: the statute that makes "every person" liable is construed narrowly, the court's own creation expansively. Strict construction is reserved for the enactments of the people's representatives; loose construction is lavished on the judiciary's own product. The same rule, applied evenhandedly, must run at least as strictly against the doctrine as against the statute, and run evenhandedly it is fatal to the doctrine, as chapter 3.5 shows.

### B. The Record Before the Report: Every Source Read at Its Own Page

The method is archival before it is doctrinal. The foundation of the doctrine is a case, and the case is read here from its own record against the report that transmitted it: the cause file in The National Archives, ten membranes in English secretary hand, comprising the examinations of the counsel who drew the destroyed bill and of the complainant who carried it, the court's single instrument of interrogatories in sixteen articles, and the sworn answer of the defendant sheriff. A partisan report read against its own cause file is not a symmetrical comparison, and the asymmetry is the finding: everything the report states as rule, the file shows first as one side's advocacy. The transcription is layered and the layers are disclosed rather than merged. The author transcribed the ten membranes entire from the Archives' record copy; a professional verification transcription was then commissioned, and every quotation from the file has been conformed to its final texts, proper names above all, with the readings the verification displaced recorded site by site rather than silently overwritten. Chapter 1.2 § C states the discipline in full. No reading in this book rests on the author's eye alone, and where a reading remains undecided the book says so.

The same discipline governs every other source. The report's own transmission is tested against the reporter's notebook, in print only since 2025, and the received print fails that test at the rule's root sentence (chapter 1.3 § B). The Bibles, the Roman and canon texts, Hobbes, the statutes and the reports of Parts 1 to 5 are read at the page or at the image of the page, never from a later edition's summary of it; early prints are quoted as printed, with the long s normalised; manuscripts are cited by leaf; translations are set beside the originals they translate. Where a source is read at length, the footnote at the foot of the page keeps the citation, the pin, the words the sentence rests on and the statement of what was not examined, and the text itself, original and rendering together with the account of how it was read, stands in the Notes at the back of the book, marked in the text by a letter beside the footnote's number. That is the reverse of the usual arrangement, in which the substance stands at the foot and the citations at the back; it is chosen so that the reader has the source at the line and the record where it can be read entire. Quotations of forty words or more that complete the sentence introducing them are set off from the text; shorter quotations, and those through which the sentence continues, are run in. The Introduction's quotations are documented where the chapters read them, and the Introduction carries no apparatus of its own. The genealogy of Part 2 is built from the bottom up, from what the opinions themselves cite. A doctrine grounded in text invites testing from the top down, by naming the constitutional provision and reading it; a doctrine that names no provision can be tested only from its root, by tracing the chain of authority backward and asking at each node whether the opinion produces the constitutional source the opinion citing it presupposes. The chain is built only from the opinions' own stated antecedents, and at each node the book records the source the opinion claimed and the source it produced; the gap between the two is the finding.

Comparative claims carry a discipline of their own. Every statement this book makes about another legal order classifies that order's de jure law, the text as written, and names what it does not measure: the order's common law, the congruency between the two, and the order's adherence to stare decisis. For the American order the book measures all four; for England, in part; for the others, the text alone, and it says so at each place. Two of the four terms, common law and stare decisis, are defined at chapter 2.2 § B, and all four for the comparison at chapter 4.1; the degree of examination is stated wherever a comparative finding is drawn.

### C. The Source-Text Audit, the Verb Test, and the Strategic Paraphrase: Three Checks on a Court's Use of Its Own Authorities

If interpretive discipline binds the court at least as strictly against its own doctrine as against enacted text, the court's use of its own precedents is auditable by the same standard, and the audit is mechanical. For each citation on which a doctrine's leading opinion rests, the citing opinion's characterisation is placed beside the cited source's own words and compared element by element. Does the cited case hold what it is cited for? Does a quotation reproduce its source, or silently insert a word the source does not contain? Does a parenthetical that omits internal quotation marks conceal a substantive alteration? Does the opinion attribute to a founding source a sense the source's own text forecloses? The audit tests a specific hypothesis: that the citation chain supporting a sourceless doctrine is not merely thin but at points inverted, held up by authorities which, read in their own words, cut the other way. Chapter 2.5 applies it to the apex decision and finds seven such instances. Each is established by quotation, and the method's virtue is that anyone with the reporter volumes can check it.

The verb test is the cleanest instance of the discipline turned on the court's own prose. To "recognize" a right, a doctrine or an immunity is to acknowledge something that already exists, prior to and independent of the act of acknowledgment; the verb presupposes an antecedent that can be produced. It is not a synonym for "establish," "construct" or "hold to be implicit," each of which presupposes less. When an opinion describes an immunity as one the Court has "recognized" and cannot produce the antecedent, when the chain of recognition runs back not to a constitutional provision but to a prior opinion that itself recognised without producing, the verb has been misused, and the activity it names is not recognition but invention. Under the Constitution, legal authority is not invented but ratified: Article V prescribes the only route by which its text gains a word, proposal by two thirds of Congress or by a convention the States call for and ratification by three fourths of the States, and no court stands anywhere on that route. A court that rests a holding on an indefinite article cannot exempt its own verbs from the same scrutiny.

The strategic paraphrase is the audit's third object. It is paraphrase that strips a source's hedging: a limiting clause moved out of the quotation, a qualified statement restated without its qualification, a source's own disclaimer omitted so that the source appears to say without condition what it said with one. The 1607 report practised it on its Roman authority (chapter 1.4 § A); the apex decision practises it on its American authorities (chapter 2.5 § B). The book names each instance where it occurs, sets the paraphrase beside the source, and lets the reader read both.

# Part 1 — English Origin and Repudiation, 1591–1769

*Part 1 restores the record that the report displaced. Chapter 1.1 reads the Anglesey of 1591 to 1600 from the Privy Council's registers and the Exchequer's commissions, a county in which the persons with documented reason for fear were the men who had called the local officers to account. Chapter 1.2 reads the cause of 1607 from its surviving file, the ten membranes of STAC 8/203/38 transcribed entire, and finds that every article of the court's instrument interrogated the accuser and none the accused, that the fear the report made law entered the cause as the accused sheriff's pleading, that the bench's own presents were custom while a pauper's ten shillings were conspiracy, that the order was the only one in the reports surveyed destroying a subject's pleading unheard, and that the judge headed the same bench within three years while the acquitted accuser stood indicted again. Chapter 1.3 reads the report that carried the rule out of the court, fixes the three bars it set, shows its root sentence standing inverted in the received print, finds the reporter's own hand stopping the reading of an exculpation on a trial record fifteen months before, and tests the forum it reserved from above, against the reporter himself in 1616, and from below, against a petitioning subject in 1618; the court's own registers being lost, the report is the only text there is. Chapter 1.4 reads the leaf and the sources the report named at their own pages, and finds that the leaf cites no English case for its rule and that the one English judge it names was condemned to death for the corruption the rule declares unquestionable, that a contemporary hand which sat through the term recorded no such cause, and that the Roman, scriptural and canon texts the 1607 court invoked, and the court's own practice in the decade before, each say the opposite of what the report made them say; the century's theorist of absolute sovereignty, reasoning from the same sources, refused the judge's exemption at every point, and the founders who read him refused even the sovereign's. Chapter 1.5 finds Blackstone stating the rule for the sovereign on one premise, while the practice of his own century kept the sovereign's servants answerable. Chapter 1.6 follows England dismantling the court, its methods and its premise between 1641 and 1701, so that what stood ready for reception was the repudiation.*



---

## 1.1 Anglesey before the Case, 1591–1600

The case did not begin at Westminster. It began on Anglesey, and the island's decade before the drowning is on the record — in the Crown's own books.

The Crown's council registers for 1591–92 — fifteen years before the case, in the same county, among some of the same houses — document where fear in Anglesey in fact resided. A subject, Owen Wood, sued the island's leading magnate, Sir Richard Bulkeley, in the Star Chamber and won a censure against him; within nine days the Council released Bulkeley to command the island's defence amid that season's Spanish-invasion scare, instructed the county's justices that "the censure he receaved in that Court should be no occasion to diminish his credit and reputacion," attributed the adjudicated conduct to "the greate provokement of the said Owen Wood" — and, the same day, confined the prevailing complainant to his house.[^vr19a] Within the year Wood was put out of the commission of the peace on the informations of Bulkeley and his fellow Deputy Lieutenants; Bulkeley recovered eight hundred pounds against him in a slander action Wood protested had been enforced "contrary to an order sett downe by theire Lordships"; the Council could "see no cause to deale with the said Sir Richard to myttygate that somme which by due course of lawe he hath obtayned"; and the whole of the relief afforded the man who had won the censure was twelve days free of execution, "in which tyme he maie repaire into his contry."[^vr19b]

In the same registers, a private man, Foulk Thomas, performed an apprehension at Holyhead that the county's officers would not — "a Seminary preist and other suspected persons" — and the county's officers undid the act at every level: the prisoners "were taken from him by the Under-sheriffe of Anglesey" and escape followed; the bailiff and constable of Llifon "did not onely refuse . . . to give their assistance to the said Foulk, but used divers undutifull and lewd speeches to discorage others, whereby som escaped"; and on his return home he was "abused and offered violences . . . for doing her Majesty's service."[^vr19c] The Council's remedy was to deputize the Bishop of Bangor over the lay apparatus entire, and to instruct the local gentry that the man "may not in any kinde of sort be injured or molested, having deserved to be favored in respect of his dutifull endevour to apprehend these lewd persons" — desert the center could declare but not protect. The register's editor gave that entry its lasting marginal title: "Sympathy with offenders in Wales."[^vr19d]

So when the sheriff's answer told the Star Chamber, fifteen years later, that a bill against the county's officials would "stryke a feare into the h[a]rts" of the county's jurors, the county's registered experience, in the Crown's own council books, was that the persons with documented reason for fear were the complainant who had prevailed and the citizen who had enforced. The conjecture the doctrine adopted assigns the fear to the office; the register of the doctrine's birthplace assigns it to the men who called the office to account.[^vr19][^vr19_text] The exhibit is offered for the structure, for who absorbed the cost of enforcement, not for any causal identity with the case that followed: the 1591 release had a non-pretextual ground in the invasion scare and Bulkeley's Deputy-Lieutenancy, Wood's own answers confessed "very indyscreet speeches", and the Thomas matter carried a confessional charge the murder case did not.

And the compulsion ran the other way: two years before the sheriff's answer was drawn, the Crown's escheat commission for the island — issued out of the Exchequer in 1605, teste the same chief baron whom the notebook report places among the *Floyd* resolvers — had put the county's free tenants, comote by comote, on juries presenting their neighbors' holdings as the King's escheated and concealed lands, and had bound any empanelled man who withdrew to appear before the Barons of the Exchequer to show cause; the November 1605 return survives, sworn by a named Anglesey jury that closed one finding by confessing it did not know the parcel's bounds. So when the report reasons that indictors cannot be impeached because they are "returned by the sheriff by process of law" and "compellable to serve the law and the court," the compulsion it invokes was no hypothetical of the county's life; it was the Exchequer's regular practice, and the jurors it conscripted were the same freeholders the answer professed to find so easily frightened.[^ve178][^ve178_text]

A man drowned by accident, with his horse, in 1600. That is the account recoverable now, from a second contemporaneous report and a modern reconstruction of the suppressed pleadings. Years later the death was recast as murder, and the prosecution was assembled on examinations taken from Welsh-speaking witnesses who were "not capable of the English Tongue" but whose statements were "written in English contrary to their sayings in welsh."[^vr2] On those examinations a first man was indicted; when he denied the charge, "a large summe of money was offered" to him to accuse another in his stead, and the prosecution shifted to the man eventually convicted. The indicting grand jury was largely kin to the prosecutor, and one juror was afterward "fyned by the Councell of the Marches for a false verdict."[^vr3a] At trial an alibi placed the accused miles from the death; the judge knew that some of the trial jurors carried consideration "in their pocketts" and yet "did take noe order in it"; and when the jury "desired to heare the examinacons of those whoe did first fynde the Murdred man and his horse", the drowning evidence that distinguished accident from murder, the judge "would not permit it."[^vr3b] The witnesses recorded in English "denyed their examinacons sett downe in Engl[ish] and affirmed they made noe such deposicons."[^vr3c] The jury convicted and "asked forgivenes of [the accused] for the verdict"; he "notwithstanding was condemned and executed."[^vr3] What the reports preserve are the complainant's allegations; no tribunal ever adjudicated them, because the court ordered the bill destroyed rather than examined.

The record that convicted carried two defects, each fatal. It was never authenticated: an instrument offered as a witness's sworn examination, but set down in a language the witness did not speak, is not an authentic record of what the witness said — it is a different statement attributed to him. And it was impeached past survival: its purported authors, brought to the bar, disavowed it. A conviction was entered, and a man put to death, on a record that was neither what it purported to be nor believed by the persons whose words it claimed to contain.

---

## 1.2 The Cause of 1607: *Floyd v. Barker* Read from Its File

### A. The Order of 1607: The Complaint Destroyed Without an Answer

The three bars did not enter the law as abstractions. They were fixed in the course of deciding an actual case, and that case repays examination — because the record the receiving courts never saw, the record that had been destroyed, shows the mechanism performing, at its very origin, the closure it would later be invoked to license. The point is not that the doctrine was once misapplied; it is that the doctrine's foundational case was itself an instance of the wrong the bars foreclose, and was put beyond answer by the destruction of the proof.[^vr1]

When a living complainant carried that record to the Star Chamber on the dead man's behalf, the court did not examine it. The same report that fixed the first bar — declaring that records "import verity in themselves, and none shall be received to aver any thing against the record itself" (chapter 1.3) — in the same proceeding, and "without any answer" from the accused judge, ordered the complaining bill "taken off the file and cancelled, and utterly defaced"; the second contemporaneous account of the case records the sentence the printed report omits, the complainant fined, pilloried and committed to prison until he should "confess[] his abettors."[^vr4] The presumption of regularity was not inferred from an unrebutted record; it was manufactured by destroying the rebuttal. A presumption that must erase the contrary proof to sustain itself is not a finding of regularity but the suppression of one — and the report discloses the character of that verity in its own logic, holding a record conclusive even against demonstrable impossibility, so that a litigant could never aver "that the party who levied the fine was dead before the fine was levied."[^vr5] Verity of that kind attaches to status, not to fact: the record is true because of who made it, not because it corresponds to what happened.

### B. The Only Destroyed Pleading in the Reports, and the Second Report That Preserved It

The suppression failed, and its failure is what turns the point from inference into documentation. The order to deface the bill did not erase the bill's contents: a second, independent report preserved them, recording that the bill had been "read in open Court . . . to this effect"; the public archive still holds the cause's own file, its bill alone catalogued as "wanting"; and a modern peer-reviewed study has reconstructed the account from the surviving fragments.[^vr6] Because the suppressed side survives, the erasure is visible for what it was — not a neutral rule of evidence operating upon an absent record, but the destruction of a present one. Nor was the reporter through whom the doctrine descends, Sir Edward Coke, a disinterested chronicler. He was a principal in the early-seventeenth-century contest between the common-law courts and the prerogative courts, and he used the decision to fix the proposition that common-law judges could not be called to account elsewhere for their judicial acts, an instrument in a jurisdictional struggle, not a considered rule of citizen redress. On the historian's finding, it was the reporter himself who secured the destruction of the bill.[^vr4a] The decision "created precedents cited even today in defense of judicial immunity"; the courts that later received it took the partisan report for settled law and never inspected the record behind it, because the record was gone.[^vr7]

Nor is the gravity of that erasure a judgment imported from a later age. The report supplies the standard itself: the stated reason a judge may not be drawn in question is that such questioning "extends to the annihilating of a record" — a rule of record-sanctity announced in the very order that annihilated the accusation record, unanswered.[^ep9a] The age's own standard, then, condemns the act: a court that had in the same breath declared records sacred destroyed one.

The surrounding law measures how far the court had stepped outside its own practice. A survey made for this book of the record-destruction vocabulary in the reported English cases, reading at its site every reported order that a record or pleading be expunged, taken off the file, rased, cancelled, or defaced, across 150 of the 178 volumes of the English Reports from the Year Books to 1865, finds no other order destroying a subject's pleading without an answer to it.[^ep9][^ep9_text] Every other destruction the reporters record rides a stated ground and a hearing.

The Star Chamber's own practice book shows the court's ordinary course ten years before this cause: in 1597 a bill charging riot and felony, found "scandalous and slanderous," was dismissed and "withdrawn from the file and cancelled" only after "[d]ivers witnesses were examined and proved nothing at all," and the plaintiff was fined and imprisoned.[^vr27] The Chancery would amend a record only where amendment did not "blot and deface" it; where it would, the Record "should be taken off the file; and a new Record put upon it." Even a forged deed, the Crown's own law officer reminded the Chancery in 1682, "cannot be torn or defaced by law, but must be kept so that the king may proceed upon it against the criminal."[^vr27a] And in 1658, when an acquitted defendant prayed the Upper Bench to rase his name from its record so that it should not remain a disparagement to his posterity, the court resolved that it would do so only if a precedent could be shown. Rasure of a record by which a judgment was avoided had been felony by statute since 1429, and the reporter's own Institutes hold that a record once removed into the King's Bench "cannot (being as it were in his centre) be remaunded back, unlesse it be by act of parliament."[^vr27b]

The order's second verb had a home of its own. In the statute book of the order's generation, *cancel* is the word for instruments and their enrolments, and *deface* is the word for forbidden objects to be marred past use: images and books, which the statute of 1549–50 ordered "openlye brent or otherwayes defaced and destroyed"; engines, which the statute of 1597–98 ordered the justices to "utterlye deface . . . in suche sorte as they cannot be ymployed agayne"; and, in the commission an ecclesiastical court received in 1620, "massing Stuff Reliques or other like superstitious things," to be defaced "in such manner as from thenceforth they may not serve for any supersticious Use, without destroyeing the Substance thereof except otherwise it cannot be defaced."[^vr25] When a subject did the same to a court's paper it was a crime: the Crown's instructions to the Council in the Marches of Wales in 1617 listed "Raceing or Defacing of any Bills, Pleadings, Orders, Rules, Proceedings or Records of the same Court" among the offences that Council was to try.[^vr26] The reporter's own Eighth Part treats a record left "imperfect or erroneous" by a clerk's "voluntary defacing" as a misprision to be amended from the rest of the record, and recites in the same resolution the statute that made a clerk's stealing or avoiding a record felony.[^vr28] *Floyd* added the object-verb, the adverb, and the absence of any examination. The complaint was not answered; it was marred past use, as the statute book marred the objects it forbade.

Against that uniform practice, the ground named, the process run, the roll preserved even at the price of a new one, the corpus holds a single order destroying a subject's pleading unheard, and it is the order in this cause. Its stated ground, that questioning judges "extends to the annihilating of a record," makes the preservation of records the reason for the one destruction of a record the reporters preserve; and the same page of the report adds that "though the party is acquitted, yet the accusing stands with the record": the accusation against a subject outlives his acquittal on the roll, while the accusation against the judge was defaced without answer.[^ep9b] How the legal order that ultimately received the rule measures the same act — as felony, as constitutional violation, as ground to presume the destroyed accusation true — is the subject of Parts 2 and 3.

### C. The Surviving File: Sixteen Articles Against the Accuser

The record, it turns out, is not entirely gone. The National Archives holds the cause's surviving file, STAC 8/203/38 — catalogued not under the judge's name but under a defendant sheriff's, as *Lloyde v. Lewys*, its inventory noting "Bill wanting," though the file's own Latin caption still styles the cause against Barker and others: the 1607 defacement order operates in the archive to this day, the bill absent from the file, the judge's name absent from the index. The modern study that reconstructed the case from the report and the Harvard manuscript discloses the file's survival in a footnote without using its contents, so that both of this book's manuscript witnesses stand in that study's apparatus, the one read there as a second account, the other named and left unread. For this book the record copy was obtained and its ten membranes transcribed entire, apparently for the first time; the quotations that follow are from that transcription, made and disclosed on the author's authority, and since conformed — readings, proper names above all — to the professional transcription commissioned from the Archives, whose final texts of all three surviving documents returned in August 2026.[^vst1] What survives is everything around the destroyed bill: the examination of the counsel who drew it; the examination of the complainant, taken while he lay a prisoner in the Fleet, "attached" in the course of the cause he had brought; the Court's single instrument of sixteen interrogatories, administered selectively to each man; and the answer of John Lewis, the sheriff who had returned the juries.

The Court's instrument shows what the tribunal thought wanted proof. All sixteen articles interrogate the accusation, not the accused: who procured the bill and who advised it; who drew it; who paid, or promised payment yet to come ("did they or any of them give you any fees for the same, or promise you hereafter"); who carried money to the prisoner; who passed words to the Warden for his appearance; whether the counsel is the alleged maintainer's kinsman ("is not the said Towneley of kynred w[i]th the said S[i]r R[i]chard").[^vst2b] Not one article asks whether what the bill said of the judge was true. The single article that so much as recites the bill's charges — among them that Barker "used to staie matters in Chancerie after Judgment and execuc[i]on" — asks only who gave "Instructions to insert" them; and the instrument's own noun for its quarry is maintenance ("what maynetenaunce or money have you receaved . . . since your imprisonment in the ffleete").[^vst2][^vst2_text] The complainant's own counsel had foreseen the charge, deposing that he had warned, before any bill was filed, that the matters "would be . . . in the nature of a conspiracie." The premise the printed report announces as law — that a complaint against a judge is presumptively a conspiracy against him — was the working premise of the Court's questionnaire first.

The sheriff's answer completes the picture, because it contains, as advocacy, the two moves the printed report would make law within the year. Its theory of the bill's purpose is fear of the fact-finders: the bill was contrived, it pleads, "of purpose not onlie to vex and trouble this def[endan]t w[i]th longe and tedious suits in lawe," "butt alsoe to stryke a feare into the h[a]rts of all such freeholders of the said Countie of Anglesey [as] . . . shalbe retorned or impannelled for or upon the try[a]ll" of the men then standing indicted.[^vst3][^vst3_text]

Coke's report resolves the case on the same argument, generalized into a rule: to admit such complaints "will be a means *ad deterrendos et detrahendos juratores a servitio Regis*" — to deter and draw away jurors from the King's service. And the answer's denial runs in the report's very vocabulary: there was no plot, it pleads, "touchinge the callinge in question of anie of the said p[er]sons in the said bill of compl[ain]t named"; the sentence that follows carries the companion phrase, Barker's upright conduct "in all the said . . . p[ro]ceedinges in or upon the said Inditem[en]ts"; and the report converts the pair into the shield — "none of the parties to the proceedings are to be drawn in question," the judge least of all, "except before the King himself."[^vst4][^vst4_text] The answer even supplies the maintenance theory the interrogatories hunt: the complainant, "not worthe x s in all the worlde" (his poverty interlined into the engrossed answer), "is countenaunced and mainteyned herein by some privie Adversary that will not him self (as yt seemeth) be seene."

A pleading is not a source of law; it is a party's characterization, and here it is the characterization of the accused side. The file thus shows the immunity rationale at the moment of its birth: the fear entered the case as the defendants' framing of their accuser's bill and left it as doctrine — the report did not find the fear, it adopted it. The answering advocate's client, the sheriff, sits inside the report's protected class; the pleading and the rule share their operative phrase and their operative fear. That is a party's pleading mistaken for a source of law at the first node of the chain.

### D. Two Accounts: The Bench's Presents Were Custom, the Pauper's Ten Shillings Were Conspiracy

The instrument's economics deserve a section of their own, because the era's own archives price what the Court was pricing. The sixteen articles hunted the money behind a pauper's bill — the ten shillings the answer swears the complainant was not worth, the fees "give[n] . . . for the same, or promise[d] . . . hereafter," the "maynetenaunce or money . . . receaved . . . since your imprisonment in the ffleete."[^vst2a]

In the same years, inside the same judicial establishment, the private archive of a Council-in-the-Marches member — the Wynn of Gwydir Papers at the National Library of Wales, cited here solely by the Library's own published catalogue descriptions[^ew1] — records the bench's own money moving the other way, openly and over the judges' signatures. The catalogue describes the trial judge of the underlying cause himself, in his first months on the circuit: "Richard Barker, second justice of north Wales, at Beaumaris," writing in October 1602 to thank the Gwydir magnate "for the Gwydir wine, to his thinking more excellent than the grape of France or the wines of Greece or Spain."[^ew1a] It describes the senior common-law judge of the Welsh circuits, the Chief Justice of Chester, thanking the same magnate in December 1604 "for his gift, the most valuable of the many received by the Justice since his coming into these parts" — adding that had Wynn sent "a pair of gloves, or any thing of less worth, it might have been requited by another, while now (to his shame) he must remain Wynn's debtor."[^ew1b] It describes the Lord President of the Council in the Marches — the supervisory organ over the very county apparatus this file concerns — declining that magnate's present in October 1603 "for fear that (there being three Caernarfonshire murderers up for trial) he should be expected to show partiality in the matter": a refusal that names, in the supervision's own correspondence, exactly what the presents were understood to buy, and marks refusal as the exception that pending capital trials occasioned.[^ew1c] It describes the practice institutionalized — a sheriff of Denbighshire in 1616 delivering "£ 20 a week, through his steward," to the judges "for their joint provision, as was done by other sheriffs", and advised to write the second justice "enclosing two or three jacobs in the letter" — and it describes the practice enforced from the bench's side, in Wynn's own relation of a chief justice's displeasure "because he gave him no presents".[^ew1d]

Eight months after the resolution this Part examines, the same catalogue has the Chief Justice of Chester thanking Wynn again "for a present of fowls and other Christmas fare".[^ew2]

The asymmetry between those two accounts is the finding. Ten shillings of alleged maintenance behind a poor Welshman's bill was conspiracy, hunted through sixteen articles while the complainant lay attached in the Fleet; the priced favor of the English-appointed bench was custom, accounted like revenue and acknowledged in the judges' own thanks. The inequity ran along the same line the mistranslated examinations ran: money moving up from Welsh subjects to the bench was service, and money alleged to move between Welshmen to question the bench was crime — a distribution the country itself would name within the generation, petitioning the Prince of Wales in 1624 against a revenue scheme advanced "under pretence that the royal revenues out of Wales are not paid owing to the dishonesty of the King's officers, which is against common justice".[^ew3]

### E. The Star Chamber Bill Was the Ordinary Remedy Against an Officer

Nor was the bill that was defaced an exotic instrument. The Star Chamber bill was the era's ordinary remedy against every other officer of the same county (the catalogue shows the same magnate, over a gaol abuse, "ready to prosecute both Sheriff and gaoler in the Star Chamber, or Court of the Marches, or before Lewkenor at the Assizes"), and the channel was in living use against the bench itself on the eve of the resolution: in January 1606/7, three months before the cause this Part examines was resolved, the catalogue records a Star Chamber proceeding in which a Ruthin man, "charged by his neighbour . . . with having brought false information against him, at the last Sessions, to Justice Lewkenor," begs his patron to "certify the Lord Chancellor in his behalf" — the description noting that Justice Lewkenor "is also cited for proceeding unjustly against Parry."[^ew4] A subject's proceeding against a sitting justice was pending in fact, not in imagination, in the months the resolution was prepared. What the resolution protected, on the archive's own showing, was not the purity of the bench; it was the purchasability of it — and the office it lifted out of the remedy's reach was the one office whose favor the archive shows priced. Nor was the Star Chamber the only door. A century before, in the King's Bench rolls of 1485 to 1495, subjects were suing constables and a sheriff's constable by the appeal of felony, the oldest private action the common law had, and the officer's answer was his own indictment, removed into the court, which usually ended the appeal before a declaration; it is from those indictments that the officer's side of the incident survives.[^wh4]

### F. The Sequel: The Judge Promoted, the Accuser Tried Again

The sequel stands in the modern edition of the reporter's own notebook, in its biographical note on this very case: Richard Barker, the trial judge whose thanks for the Gwydir wine open this section, "became C.J. Anglesey in 1610," chief justice of the same great sessions on which he had sat when the defaced bill charged him, three years after the resolutions put that bench beyond the subject's remedy.[^ew5]

[^ew1b]: [*Calendar of Wynn (of Gwydir) Papers* no. 316 (Sir Richard Lewkenor, chief justice of Chester, to John Wynn, 5 Dec. 1604), in the Library's item description (the catalogue note in this section).](cite:ew1b/1)

The sequel completes the pattern, and the receiving system has a name for it. The complainant had stood trial in the underlying matter himself — co-indicted in the cause of the wife's murder, he "hardly escaped and was found not guiltie," as the defendant sheriff's own sworn answer concedes: a common-law acquittal recast by the adversary's pen as an escape from justice.[^vr21] And the acquittal did not end it: the follow-on file shows him prosecuted a second time upon the same transaction — acquitted as accessory before the fact "by good & lawfull verdict," then standing "indicted as accessarie after the saide murther."[^vr22][^vr22_text] The foundation case of official immunity thus carries, in its own files, what the doctrine never discloses: finality for the official — not to be "drawn in question" — and no finality for the acquitted accuser.

### G. The Doctrine Bears the Complainant's Name, Not the Judge's

What those courts received as venerable authority was, moreover, a case named for the man it crushed. The caption is the complainant's: *Rice ap Evan ap Floyd v. Richard Barker* — "Floyd," the living complainant who carried the bill, against Barker, the judge who had tried the cause. The printed report gives the executed man as another, "William Price"; the second account's defense recital speaks of "the pl[aintif]ts Brother . . . lately executed for Murder"; and the surviving file resolves the pair into something worse than either source alone discloses: two men of the complainant's family had been executed — his elder brother, John ap Jevan Lloyd, convicted of murdering his own wife, in a cause in which the complainant himself stood co-indicted and "hardly escaped and was found not guiltie"; and his kinsman, William ap Rees ap William, the print's "Price," "executed w[i]th yo[ur] said brother" upon the drowned man's murder — and it was the kinsman, at his death, who handed the complainant the written "direction . . . against Mr Barker" with the plea to "make [it] knowne to the state after I am dead, as you are my kinsman," which the complainant "vowed" to perform.[^vr9][^vr9_text] The bill the court defaced was the performance of that vow. The executed could bring nothing, the dead leaving no record, so the suit descends under the name of the survivor — who was fined, imprisoned, and ordered to the pillory for bringing it.

The doctrine of judicial immunity thus comes down under the name of its first victim, and the American line that built on it never registered the fact, because it never read the case: *Randall* cited *Floyd and Barker's case* alone, in a single sentence of the opinion, and took from it only the rule, and a century later grand-juror immunity was still traced to the same case as "an almost equally venerable common-law tenet."[^vr10][^vr10_text] *Randall* dated the case to 1608, a year late, and *Imbler* repeated the misdate a century on, a small but exact measure of how the source travelled: a court that had read the report would have taken its term from the report. No node of the receiving chain noted that the eponym was not a disappointed litigant vexing the judge who had ruled against him — the immunity rule's paradigm target — but a man twice bereaved by the record he was forbidden to question, once co-indicted himself, imprisoned as he carried a dead man's sworn charge, his bill "utterly defaced." The name on the doctrine is the name of the wrong it was made to conceal.

A professional transcription was commissioned from The National Archives and made by Christopher Whittick, who is to be identified as the author of the transcripts he supplied; its final texts of all three surviving documents returned in August 2026, and every quotation from the file in this book has been read against them and conformed. Readings — proper names above all — follow the professional final text wherever the two layers diverged; the author's diplomatic orthography is retained where the professional text, which lightly modernizes spelling, confirms the word; and tokens the professional adjudicated letter-exact are printed in his forms. A record of the readings displaced by that comparison is available from the author.

An earlier state of this book read, in the art.-13 zone of membrane 009, an apparently struck line whose recoverable words concerned a reward "tendred or promised" respecting Mr Barker himself](cite:vst2_text/5); only the name-token was ever firm, and the professional transcription does not record such a line — the reading is accordingly withdrawn from the text and noted here as an open question of the leaf only. The instrument is unsigned, and was administered selectively: the imprisoned complainant was "not appointed to be examined" upon the drawer-directed articles 13, 15, and 16 (his examination so states article by article), which the counsel answered — between them the two examinations left no article unanswered, the counsel also deposing to arts. 3–6 and objecting, on the scope of the court's own order, to the parts of 13 and 16 that reached beyond it. Counsel's warning that the matters "would be . . . in the nature of a conspiracie" (the final text: "would be specially in the nature of a conspiracy") is deposed at membrane 001 (Towneley's examination, in his answer to the fourth interrogatory). All readings per the transcription note at chapter 1.2 § C.

The print names the executed man "William Price" (77 Eng. Rep. at 1306: "the grand jury who indicted one William Price for the murder of Hugh ap William")](cite:vr9_text/2); [the second account's defense recital, given while reciting the destroyed bill in open court, describes "the pl\[aintif\]ts Brother . . . lately executed for Murder" (MS 149, f. 82v). The recital alone would equate the two men](cite:vr9_text/3); [the surviving file shows that they were two. The Court's interrogatories name the brother, "John ap Jevan Lloyd your brother" (in the professional transcription's form), in the cause of his wife's death, and ask the complainant "whether you were justlie or wrongfullie indicted for that fact" (STAC 8/203/38, membrane 008, art. 2)](cite:vr9_text/4); they name the kinsman in the full patronymic form, "William ap Rees ap William" (arts. 2–3), "executed w[i]th yo[ur] said brother" (art. 2), and the complainant's own examination swears the degree — the two men "were the sons of cousin germans removed" (his answer to the second interrogatory); and the sheriff's answer supplies the outcomes — "upon their tryall the said Elder brother was thereof found guiltie and the said Compl[ainan]t . . . hardly escaped and was found not guiltie" (membrane 010). The dying charge is deposed at membrane 004: the condemned kinsman's written "direction for you against Mr Barker & others of their dealinges, w[hi]ch I praie you make knowne to the state after I am dead, as you are my kinsman," which the deponent "vowed" to perform. The plaintiff's name varies across the records (Floyd, Lloyd, Lloyde, Fludd; *see* the name-forms note at chapter 1.2 § B); his autograph in the professional final reads "Rees ap Jevan Lloid." Readings are conformed to the professional final texts (the transcription note at chapter 1.2 § C); the name-forms above follow them.

Barker"; the same note grounds judicial immunity in "roots extending to the earliest days of the common law," citing *Floyd*, and repeats the 1608 date). Neither opinion — nor *Bradley v. Fisher* nor *Spalding v. Vilas* — recounts the underlying execution or the destruction of the bill. *See* Part 2, chapters 2.3–2.4 (reception by citation, not adjudication).](cite:vr10/2)
[^vr10_text]: [*Randall v. Brigham*, 74 U.S. (7 Wall.) 523, 536 (1869), quoting only the immunity rule and reciting none of the case's facts: "it was said as long ago as 1608, as reported by Lord Coke in *Floyd and Barker's case*, that insomuch as the judges of the realm have the administration of justice, under the king, to all his subjects, they ought not to be called in question for any judicial proceedings by them, except before the king himself." Three features of the invocation are worth recording. It stands alone in the Court's own sentence — not inside a string of authorities (the string of five cases at 535, four of them English and the fifth *Yates v. Lansing*, sits in a footnote to the *argument of counsel*, and the taproot is not among them; the long chronological strings that do carry *Floyd*, at 532 and 534, are counsel's too). Its citation is the report's short form, "12 Coke, 25," pinning a page in the middle of the report rather than its first. And the receiving court dates the case to 1608, a year late — the resolutions were of Easter term 1607. The misdating then propagates for a century: *Imbler*'s footnote, below, cites "*Floyd v. Barker*, 12 Coke 23, 77 Eng. Rep. 1305 (1608)," so the modern prosecutorial-immunity node repeats the origin decision's error. *Imbler v. Pachtman*, 424 U.S. 409, 423 n.20 (1976) (grand-juror immunity "an almost equally venerable common-law tenet, see Floyd v. Barker"; the same note grounds judicial immunity in "roots extending to the earliest days of the common law," citing *Floyd*, and traces its American reception through *Yates v. Lansing* and *Bradley*). Neither opinion — nor *Bradley v. Fisher* nor *Spalding v. Vilas* — recounts the underlying execution or the destruction of the bill. *See* Part 2, chapters 2.3–2.4 (reception by citation, not adjudication).](cite:vr10_text/1)

The quotation begins at "hardly" (the final text reads "very hardly escaped"); readings are conformed to the professional final text (the transcription note at chapter 1.2 § C).
