## 1.3 Coke's Report: Three Bars to the Subject's Plea, Their Author, and the King's Forum It Reserved

### A. The Three Bars: No Proof Against the Record, No Forum but the King, No Remedy from the King

The prerogative's procedural form was fixed in two companion decisions of 1607, and on their face they do not announce unaccountability. They announce a remedy — the official remains answerable "to God and the King," "except before the King himself" — and the modern doctrine leans on exactly that, urging that immunity leaves other channels intact. But the promised accountability closes on itself, and the three bars are in the text.

*The first bar excludes proof.* Records, the report holds, "import verity in themselves, and none shall be received to aver any thing against the record itself."[^v1] The bar is a rule of evidence, not of venue: the fact of wrongdoing cannot be established because evidence of it is inadmissible by rule, and the verity attaches to the maker's sworn *status* — "the law will not suppose any [partiality], when he is sworn to serve the King," so that the law will "not . . . admit proof against this presumption."[^v2] *The second bar routes the grievance to the sovereign alone.* To draw the officer into question "trench[es] to the scandal of the King himself, except it be before the King himself; for they are only to make an account to God and the King."[^v3] To impugn the officer is to impugn the Crown, and an offense against the sovereign can be answered only by the sovereign. *The third bar closes that forum.* The companion decision holds that "the King in his own person cannot adjudge any case," and — decisively for the wronged subject — "the party cannot have remedy against the King."[^v4] So no proof may be heard; only the sovereign may hear it; and the sovereign cannot adjudicate and owes the party nothing. The exception "before the King himself" has nothing in it. The promised remedy leads nowhere.

The common law of the century before knew a presumption in these cases, and it ran the other way and yielded to process: every appeal of felony was presumed malicious, and the presumption fell as soon as an indictment stood beside it.[^wh5] The rule of 1607 presumed for the judge and let nothing stand beside the record.

### B. The Rule's Founding Sentence, Printed Backwards Since 1656, and Its Two Limits

The receiving tradition's failure to read runs past the caption, to the letters of the rule itself. The foundational sentence of the rule, as it stands in the received print — the English Reports text that is all any citing court has ever had — negates itself: "the law will not admit any proof against this vehement and voilent [sic] presumption of law, that a justice sworn to do justice *will do injustice*."[^vr18][^vr18_text] As printed, the sentence forbids proof against a presumption that the sworn justice will do *wrong* — an irrebuttable presumption of official misconduct — the exact inverse of the rule four centuries of courts have taken it to state. The reading is a transmission error, and a datable one: Coke's own notebook report of the case, in print only since 2025, gives the clause sense-correct — *que justice jure a faire justice voet faire justice*, "that a justice sworn to do justice will do justice" — and its editor's apparatus shows the sentence unstable in every direction the copying tradition allows: dropped whole by eye-skip in two manuscript witnesses and rescued in their margins, and inverted to "will do injustice" in a third — the reading the posthumous print received and the English Reports perpetuate. The first edition of 1656 already prints the inversion while spelling "violent" correctly, so the error is native to the first print and no later reprint's. The correction is welcome; the diagnosis is the point. The sense-correct text was never before any court that built the doctrine — it lay unprinted in notebook manuscripts for the whole of the doctrine's career — while the text that was before every one of them stated the presumption backwards, and no decision in the receiving chain appears ever to have noticed. A lineage that rested its rule on this sentence without registering that the sentence, as printed, says the opposite was not reading its foundation even at the level this book's method treats as minimal: what the letters on the page in fact say (the Method). The reception's failure to read the record is not a characterization; at the doctrine's root sentence, it is a typographical fact.

The words "vehement" and "violent" are not rhetoric but the top rank of the era's technical proof taxonomy, stated in the doctrine's own author's commentary on Littleton: presumptions come in "three sorts, *viz.* violent, probable, and light or temerary. *Violenta praesumptio* is manie times *plena probatio*," and Blackstone's chapter on trial carries the grade forward. The same treatise supplies the rule that even the top grade is weight, not preclusion, "*Quod stabitur praesumptioni donec probetur in contrarium*; but, as you see, it hath many exceptions," and bounds the class the law will hear no proof against to named positive-law categories with stated rationales: the sealed acquittance, which is an estoppel; legitimacy within the four seas; flight-forfeiture. Measured by that rulebook, the report's "the law will not admit any proof against this vehement and voilent presumption" is a category violation rather than a strong metaphor: a violent presumption promoted to an irrebuttability the taxonomy nowhere licenses, with no named category, no stated rationale that survives its own facts, and the presumption's substrate standing decreed "utterly defaced" on the report's next page.[^ih15e] Story's prize court administered the identical grade the rulebook's way, as rebuttable weight with explanation the escape, against the destroyer of records.[^ih15a]

Two features of the root, finally, bound the rule its own text stated. First, the 1607 rule reached only judicial acts: a judge could not be charged "for that which he did openly in Court as Judge," but conspiracy "before out of Court" was "extrajudicial," and "subornation of witnesses, and false and malicious prosecutions, out of Court" remained actionable.[^vr8a] Second, the rule was not an exemption from accountability but a channeling of it — the officer answered "to God and the King," "except before the King himself" — and the report itself records that forum operating, naming judges whose "causes were determined in Parliament, where some were banished, and some were fined and imprisoned."[^vr8] The rule presupposed a live enforcement authority standing above the immunized official. What the transplant made of those two limits is the subject of Part 2; here it is enough that the rule as its own age wrote it secured accountability's forum before it barred the parties' suit.

### C. Coke, the Reporter, as the Court's Prosecutor, 1605–07

The reporter through whom the rule descends confirms the point from his own career: the Coke who spoke in *Floyd* for the prerogative court was the Coke who would turn against it — in *Dr. Bonham's Case*, in the Petition of Right — and become the oracle of common-law liberty the colonists invoked. *Floyd* is what remains of the side its own author abandoned.[^vr16]

Nor is the "participant" reading a modern retrospection: the charge was contemporary, printed, and addressed to the reporter by name. In January 1605/6 — fifteen months before the resolutions — the same Attorney General had carried the Crown's case against the eight surviving Gunpowder plotters, upon an indictment naming Henry Garnet, "Superior of the Jesuits within the Realm of England," among the movers of the treason.[^ep1] Within the year, Robert Persons answered the Fifth Part of the *Reports* in a printed rejoinder whose title names "Syr Edvvard Cooke Knight, the Kinges Attorney generall," recounting Coke's conduct of Garnet's own trial and quoting the prosecutor's flourish back at him: "And you added in the heat of your eloquence, that if you proued [n]ot this, then let the vvorld saie: That Garnet is an honest man."[^ep2][^ep2_text] An adversary's polemic proves no charge; what it fixes is a date. The reporter's neutrality was contested in print, by a contemporary, in the months between the trials he prosecuted and the resolutions he reported: the participant of modern scholarship's caution was described as one in his own season. And the reporter answered — by refusing to. The preface to the Sixth Part of the *Reports*, in the resolutions' own year, acknowledges the answer "as lately hath beene published" — "full of maledictions and calumniations," it holds, "nothing pertinent to the state of the question" — and declines it in terms: "Expect not from me (good Reader) any reply at all, for I will not answere vnto his inuectiues, and I cannot make any replie at all to any part of his discourse" — the stated ground being that a reporter has nothing to defend: "The cause that I cannot replie is, for that I haue onely reported the Text, and as it were the verie voice of the auntient Lawes of this Realme."[^ep2a] The answer to the partisanship charge was a claim of pure transmission — the claim this book's method tests, and the one the root sentence, four centuries unread, fails.

What the prosecutor carried into that season, moreover, was not only a side but a premise. Expounding the treason statute of 25 Edw. III to the January commission, Coke stated as the trial's own law that to kill "any Justice of the one Bench or other; Justices of Assize, or any other Judge mentioned in the Statute of 25 Edw. III. sitting in their Judicial Places, and exercising their Offices" is treason, "for that every Judge so sitting by the King's Authority, representeth the Majesty and Person of the King; and therefore it is Crimen læsæ Majestatis, to kill him, the King being always in Judgment of Law present in Court."[^ep3][^ep3_text] That is the second bar's equation — the judge is the King's person, so that to reach the judge is to reach the Crown — in the reporter's own words, as prosecutor, fifteen months before the resolutions generalized it; what *Floyd* performs is thereby visible as an extension of a premise its author already carried, from killing the judge to complaining of him. And the proceeding in which he stated it convicted on the record the state had itself prepared — *reos confitentes*, "the Traitors own voluntary Confessions at the Bar," their subscribed examinations "openly and distinctly read" to the court[^ep4] — before a bench the resolutions would partly reconvene: Popham C.J. and the Chief Baron sat on the January commission and among the judges the notebook report names at the resolutions, where the Attorney General of the trial now sat as Chief Justice of the Common Pleas — and reported.[^ep5]

The same hand survives on the trial's own record. The examination of Guy Fawkes taken 9 November 1605 "was read at the trial, with the exception of the part exculpating Gerard" — the Jesuit whom the indictment named among the movers: in the original, the omitted passage "is scored with red ink," and before it, in the margin, "in his own hand," stand the words *huc usque* — thus far — the Attorney General, as the nineteenth-century editors who inspected the original put it, "unwilling to publish to the world a passage which might serve for the justification of one whom he meant to accuse."[^ep6][^ep6_text] A reading-stop in the prosecutor's own hand, drawn on the record to keep the exculpation from the courtroom, some fifteen months before the same hand reported the resolutions holding that records "import verity in themselves" and none shall aver against them. The two acts are one method, select the record and then declare it beyond question, and the three bars gave the method its rule.

### D. The King's Forum Used Once, From Above: Coke Removed, 1616

Coke's abandonment of the prerogative side was answered in kind, and by the forum *Floyd* had reserved. In October 1616 the King set down for his council the grounds on which Coke was to be dealt with, and among them that he might "very justly have proceeded then, not only to have put him from his place of Lord Chief Justice, but to have brought him in question in the Star Chamber, which would have been his utter overthrow."[^v5ea] The court whose suggestion no judge was to answer, on the report Coke had written nine years before, was the court the King now held over Coke; the grace of not putting him to answer there was recorded as a grace. The same paper charged his "perpetual turbulent carriage . . . towards all the settled jurisdictions of all his other courts, the High Commission, the Star Chamber, the Chancery," and required his answer to five passages of the Reports, "being all such as were expatiations of his own, and no judgments" — *Bonham* among them.[^v5eb] He was removed the next month, by a writ that stated no cause.[^v5e] The rule that judges answer only "before the King himself" was, for its author, exactly true.

The forum had already sat that summer. In June 1616, over a royal command to stay proceedings that the twelve judges had written they held "to be contrary to law, and such as we could not yield to by our oaths," the judges were summoned before the King in council, where "they all kneeled, confessed themselves in error, and solicited pardon" — Coke alone holding, in the King's presence, that the command "was a delay of justice, against the law, and contrary to their oaths."[^v6ea] The question then put to them was not whether any subject had been wronged but whether, "in a case were [sic] the King believed his prerogative or interest concerned," they "ought not to stay proceedings till his Majesty had consulted them."[^v6eb] All but Coke answered yes; Coke answered that "when the case happened, he should do that which should be fit for a judge to do."[^v6e]

When his successor was sworn in November, the Lord Chancellor told the new Chief Justice what the vacancy meant. He was called to

> a place vacant, not by death or cession, but by amotion and deposing of him that held the place before you, and that not as Sir William Thorpe in the time of the absence of King Ed. 3 . . . nor as Sir Robert Tresilian in the disordered and unruly time of King R. 2. But in the peaceable and happy Reign of Great King James . . . He putteth down one, and setteth up another; a Lesson to be learned of all, and to be remembred and feared of all that sit in Judicial places.

\noindent The fault was named in the same speech: the new judge's grandfather had

> challenged not power for the Judges of this Court . . . to judge Statutes and Acts of Parliament to be void, if they conceived them to be against common right and reason; but left the King and the Parliament to judge what was common right and reason.

\noindent And the tenure was named: "sithe the Kings Majesty hath enabled you, who shall or can disable you."[^v7e] Thorpe is the precedent *Floyd* itself recites for a judge called to account; the crown added Tresilian. Nine years after the report, it recited the two as the measure of what it had done to the report's author, and called it a lesson to be feared.

### E. The Forum Closed From Below: The Court Judged Judges Itself and Punished Those Who Petitioned the King

The court that made the rule did not observe it. On 11 February 1596/7, ten years before *Floyd*, the Star Chamber "by the whole Court" sentenced a Justice of the Peace "to be deposed from the Commission of the Peace" for misdemeanours "in him as a Justice of the Peace," while holding the underlying riot "not triable in this Court, but by an action at common law."[^v8e] In 1622 the same court sentenced the Judge of the Prerogative Court of Canterbury, "for bribery and other misdemeanors in his office of Judge," to a fine of twenty thousand pounds, imprisonment, and incapacity for "any office of judicature"; the whole bench of England, assembled at Serjeants' Inn, later held the King's pardon to have discharged everything but the fine.[^v9e] Half a century on, Vaughan, Chief Justice, stated the course as history: judges who "have given corrupt and dishonest judgments . . . have in all ages been complained of to the King in the Starr-Chamber, or to the Parliament," and he listed the instances, from the judges Alfred punished to "the judgment of the ship-mony in the time of Charles the First, question'd, and the particular Judges impeacht."[^v10e] On the same page he stated the forum's own theory of tenure: a judge's "fitness for his place" is not to be "censured, defamed, or vilified by any other person," the King alone being the one "to whom only it belongs to judge of the fitness of his ministers." The rule the receiving courts took as a settled exemption was, in the court that made it, a proposition contradicted by its own docket.

Nor did the forum the rule reserved ever open to the subject who sought it. In 1618 a Chancery suitor, John Wraynham, put into the King's hands a book against the Lord Chancellor's decree and asked in its epistle for "a public hearing before your majesty."[^v11ea] The King's recorded answer was that though he receives petitions from his subjects against his highest justices, "he will have his judges know, that they are subject to his account only, and to none else upon earth," and he delivered the book to his Attorney General.[^v11eb] The Star Chamber, sentencing, resolved that a subject may petition the King "in an humble and modest manner" for a re-hearing, that such a review is "a Commission of Grace, not of Right," and that "the justice of the decree was not to be questioned"; the petitioner was sentenced, Coke speaking first and the rest agreeing, to imprisonment, a fine of a thousand pounds, riding with his face to the horse's tail, and the loss of his ears.[^v11e][^v11e_text] Three years later the sentencer carried the same kind of complaint himself. When the Commons in March 1621 weighed two suitors' charges of corruption against the same Lord Chancellor and were told the proof rested on single, interested witnesses, Coke answered that "he that accuseth himself by accusing another, is more than three witnesses," and was named to draw the complaint up for the Lords.[^ep10] In 1629 the rule protected its author. A libel "directed to the King" against Coke, "late Chief Justice," calling him "traitor, perjured Judge" for a judgment of his court, sent the libeller to the pillory at Westminster and Cheapside, to prison until he had made "his submission in every Court," and to a fine of a thousand pounds.[^v12e] Before and after, the petitioners who charged a Lord Chancellor or a Lord Keeper to the King lost their ears on the pillory and were fined, and a subject who accused a judge of treason in open court paid five thousand pounds to the King and ten thousand to the judge.[^v13e] The one complaint against judges the King is recorded hearing came from a peer, the President of the Council of the North; the judge sent for to answer it before the King, in person, was Coke.[^v14e]

The Lord Chancellor whom Wraynham accused had set the theory of the office down in print in 1612, six years before, and enlarged it in the *Essayes* of 1625, the text quoted here. *Of Iudicature* arranges what a judge owes by four references — "Vnto the *Parties that sue*; Vnto the *Aduocates that Plead*; Vnto the *Clerkes* and *Ministers of Iustice* vnderneath them; And to the *Soueraigne* or *State* aboue them" — names the faults toward the first three, among them the party left to say his counsel or proofs were not heard (quoted at chapter 1.4 § C), knows their price, "One Foule Sentence, doth more Hurt, then many Foule Examples," and names a remedy for none of them; the fourth reference alone runs upward, and it is consultation, ending with the judges as "Lions vnder the Throne."[^v15]

### F. The Court's Own Registers Are Lost: The Report Cannot Be Checked Against Them

Nor could the report ever be checked against the court's own registers, because the registers themselves did not survive. Hudson, writing his treatise on the court under James I, already complained that its records "are negligently kept, and many times lost," and that "the very sentences by which severe punishment hath been executed upon offenders have, by mere neglect, been wholly left unentered; so that there is no record to justify the inflicting of that punishment"; the order books passed to Sir Francis Bacon on his becoming clerk, delivered by the Lord Chancellor on 19 August 1608 as volumes of the "Kallender of Orders"; and in 1732 the keeper of records in the Exchequer reported of the court that "the decrees of this court are not at present anywhere to be found."[^ep7][^ep7_text] No decree in *Floyd v. Barker* survives in the court's own archive. That the receiving tradition rests on the report alone is therefore not a preference the receiving courts exercised; it is a fact of the archive — the report is the only text there is, and the record behind the report was destroyed by the very order the report memorializes.

The era itself knew what that practice was worth, and said so through the officer best placed to say it. William Prynne — Bencher of Lincoln's Inn and, as his title page styles him, "Keeper of His Majesties Records in the TOWER of LONDON" — re-ran the record citations of Coke's chapter on "the Honourable Court of Star-chamber," the *Floyd* forum, against the original rolls in his custody, and published the results in 1669 with the transcripts "out of the Originals . . . at large inserted": the chapter "abound[s] with several mistaken numbers of membranaes, misquotations, and some grosser mistakes"; cited records "are not to be found in those Membranaes or Rolls, upon my diligentest perusal of them"; and the review states, of the precedents re-run there, the standing verdict — "how unsafe it is to take Records upon trust, from the reports of learned Judges, who never read nor perused their originals."[^ep8][^ep8_text] The warning issued under royal dedication, from the keeper of the originals, while originals could still be checked. What the immunity lineage would do is the condemned practice exactly — the report taken on trust, the original never perused — in the one case where perusal had been made impossible by the report's own decree.

Seventy years on the Attorney General cited "the Case of *Wrenham* in my *Lord Hobart*" against the Seven Bishops' petition: *The Proceedings and Tryal of the Seven Bishops* 382–84 (London 1739).](cite:v11e/3)
[^v11e_text]: [*Wrenham's Case*, Hob. 220, 80 Eng. Rep. 367 (Star Ch. 1618) ("it was lawful for any subject to petition to the King for redress in an humble and modest manner . . . but on the other side, it is not permitted under colour of a petition and refuge to the King, to rail upon the Judge or his sentence, and to make himself Judge in his own cause, by prejudging it before the re-hearing (for which his suit to the King should be)"; "the justice of the decree was not to be questioned in this case"; "So he was censured a thousand pounds fine")](cite:v11e_text/1); [*Proceedings against Mr. Wraynham*, 2 How. St. Tr. 1059, 1065–66 (the epistle: "appoint a day of hearing . . . a public hearing before your majesty"; the King's saying), 1071 ("Had I thought his majesty would not have heard my cause, I would have sat down in silence"), 1073 (Sir Edward Coke: a review of a decree "is very rare: and that is a Commission of Grace, not of Right"), 1078 (Montague, C.J.: "shall we then undergo the censure of every suitor? No state, at no time, ever suffered this"). The sentence lines at col. 1080 are lost in the print consulted](cite:v11e_text/2); [the terms and the date are reported, from Chamberlain's letter to Carleton of 5 May 1618, in 6 James Spedding, *The Letters and the Life of Francis Bacon* 311 (London 1872) (13 *The Works of Francis Bacon*): Wraynham "was sentenced by Coke (who as the last appointed councillor spoke first—and with whom all the rest agreed) to be punished according to a precedent made while he was himself Attorney General . . . to be imprisoned, to pay a fine of £1000, to ride with his face to the horse's tail, and to lose his ears," Chamberlain adding that "there is hope it was rather *in terrorem* than to be put in execution." Seventy years on the case was still the Crown's authority: answering the Seven Bishops' petition, the Attorney General allowed that "access to the King by *Petition* is open to every Body, the most inferior *Person* is allowed to *petition* the King, but because he may do so, may he therefore suggest what he pleases in his *Petition*," and cited "the Case of *Wrenham* in my *Lord Hobart*" for the rule that "the Justice of the Decree is not to be questioned in the Case." *The Proceedings and Tryal of the Seven Bishops* 382–84 (London 1739) (K.B. Trin. 1688; the speaker is labelled "Mr. *Att. Gen.*" and not named in the print).](cite:v11e_text/3)
[^v12e]: [*Jeffes' Case*, Cro. Car. 175, 79 Eng. Rep. 753 (K.B. 1629).](cite:v12e/1)
[^v13e]: [John Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata* 176–77 (William Paley Baildon ed., London 1894) (Fourde, 1604: "perpetual imprisonment, to lose his ears upon the pillory, having papers on his head, 'to ride w\[i\]th his face to the horse taile, & to be fyned 1000li.'"; the court: "Let all men hereby take heede how they complayne in wordes againste any magistrate, for they are gods")](cite:v13e/1); [John Rushworth, *Historical Collections*, App. 29–30 (Norton, 1630: the petitioner "fined 3000 l. to acknowledge at the Chancery-Bar, with a Paper on his head declaring his offence, that the Decree . . . was just"; his agents "set on the Pillory with one Ear nailed to it, while the Courts sit . . . there to remain during Life")](cite:v13e/2); [*Harrison's Case*, Cro. Car. 503, 79 Eng. Rep. 1034 (K.B. 1638) ("I accuse Mr. Justice Hutton of high treason"; a fine of five thousand pounds, imprisonment during the King's pleasure, and "his submission in every Court in Westminster-Hall"; the judge's own action for defamation recovered "10,000l. damages")](cite:v13e/3); [*Hutton v. Harison*, Hut. 131, 123 Eng. Rep. 1151–52 (1638) (the judge's own memorandum of the fine and the damages).](cite:v13e/4)
[^v14e]: [*Prohibitions* (the President of York's complaint), 13 Co. Rep. 30–31, 77 Eng. Rep. 1440 ("And the King sent for me to answer to that complaint: and I only, all the rest of the justices being absent, waited upon the King in the chamber near the gallery").](cite:v14e/1)
[^v15]: [Francis Bacon, *Of Iudicature*, in *The Essayes or Counsels, Civill and Morall* 316, 317, 318, 321, 323–24 (London, Iohn Haviland for Hanna Barret, 1625) (Essay LVI; quoted as printed, long s normalised): at 317, the four references, and the passage the text quotes](cite:v15/1); [at 318, "that that which was meant for Terrour, be not turned into Rigour"](cite:v15/2); [at 321, the passage the text quotes](cite:v15/3); [at 323, "when *Iudges* doe often Consult with the *King* and *State*"](cite:v15/4); [at 324, "Let them be Lions, but yet Lions vnder the Throne; Being circumspect, that they doe not checke, or oppose any Points of *Soueraigntie*." The essay first appeared in *The Essaies of Sr Francis Bacon Knight* (London 1612), where *Ius dicere* and not *Ius dare*, the foul sentence that corrupts the fountain, the four references and the patience and gravity of hearing already stand (read in the Internet Archive's copies of the 1612 printing and its 1613 reprint)](cite:v15/5); the text quoted here is that of this, the third and final authorial edition, which enlarged the essay; [the 1629 reprint (London, Haviland for Allot) resets it page for page, misnumbering 324 as 243, and is word-identical in the passages quoted but for *Soueraingtie* at 324](cite:v15/7); spellings vary elsewhere on the same leaves (at 323 *Happy* for *Happie*, *Soueraignty* for *Soueraigntie*, and *Rex* for *Lex* in the Twelve Tables' *Salus Populi Suprema Lex*); [the modernised text is in *The Essays of Francis Bacon* 256–62 (George Edward Woodberry intro., Century Classics 1900).](cite:v15/9)
[^vr8]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1306–08 (the office/function limit — no charge "for that which he did openly in Court as Judge," but conspiracy "before out of Court" is "extrajudicial," and "subornation of witnesses, and false and malicious prosecutions, out of Court" remain actionable; the reserved forum — answerable "to God and the King," "except before the King himself"; the forum operating — Weyland, Hengham, "and the other justices . . . determined in Parliament, where some were banished, and some were fined and imprisoned"). On the non-reception into American law of a monarchical immunity premised on answerability to the Crown, see *Van Ness v. Pacard*, 27 U.S. (2 Pet.) 137, 144 (1829)](cite:vr8/1); [Part 2, chapter 2.2. *Webster v. Fall*, 266 U.S. 507, 511 (1925)](cite:vr8/2); see Part 3, chapter 3.5 § B.
[^vr8a]: [The limb survived, for officers other than judges, into the American common law of 1871, and the judges' branch of that corpus removed it: Scott A. Keller, *Qualified and Absolute Immunity at Common Law*, 73 Stan. L. Rev. 1337, 1358–59 (2021)](cite:vr8a/1); see chapter 2.2.
[^vr18]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1306 (the sentence is quoted as the English Reports reprint prints it, including the reprint's misspelling "voilent" for "violent"; the 1656 first edition and the 1677 edition both spell "violent" correctly). The notebook text and apparatus: *Reports from the Notebooks of Edward Coke*, vol. VII: 1604–1608, at 1656 (J.H. Baker ed., 142 Selden Soc. 2025) (entry "3. Lloyd v. Barker," law-French text "que justice jure a faire justice voet faire justice"; apparatus n. 8: "Om. by haplography in Q and S, but in both MSS. supplied in margin. voet faire injustice I."). The 1656 first edition already prints the inversion ("that a Justice sworn to do Justice will do injustice"): the inversion is first-print-native. This book quotes the reprint because the reprint is the text the receiving courts cite](cite:vr18/1); the print is quoted as printed, never silently corrected.
[^vr18_text]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1306 (the sentence is quoted as the English Reports reprint prints it — including the reprint's misspelling "voilent" for "violent," a second letter-level decay mark in the same sentence; both readings were checked in two independent copies of the Full Reprint, which agree, the same volume elsewhere spelling "violent" normally; the 1656 first edition and the 1677 edition both spell "violent" correctly, so the misspelling is reprint-chain decay entering after 1677). The notebook text and apparatus: *Reports from the Notebooks of Edward Coke*, vol. VII: 1604–1608, at 1656 (J.H. Baker ed., 142 Selden Soc. 2025) (entry "3. Lloyd v. Barker," base MS "L," law-French text "que justice jure a faire justice voet faire justice" with the edition's facing translation "will do justice"; apparatus n. 8: "Om. by haplography in Q and S, but in both MSS. supplied in margin. voet faire injustice I."). The edition — the notebook report's first appearance in print — issued in 2025](cite:vr18_text/1); the readings here are from the printed edition. Three disclosures. *First*, the transmission is now witnessed at every printed stage: the 1656 first edition of 12 Co. Rep. — Edward Coke, *The Twelfth Part of the Reports* (London 1656) (posthumous; *Floyd* at pp. 23–26) — has been collated against the notebook and the reprint. The first edition already prints the inversion ("that a Justice sworn to do Justice will do injustice") while spelling "violent" correctly: the inversion is first-print-native, standing in the doctrine's received text from its first appearance in print, and the notebook-vs-print divergence accordingly sits between the manuscript tradition and the posthumous first print — consistent with descent from an exemplar of MS I's family (descent and independent corruption remain formally indistinguishable, but the first edition forecloses the possibility that the inversion is a later reprint's error). This book quotes the reprint because the reprint is the text the receiving courts cite. *Second*, the sense of the rule is not in doubt — the report's surrounding text and the notebook alike state an irrebuttable presumption of official *rectitude*; the point is not that the doctrine secretly presumes misconduct, but that its foundational sentence has stood self-inverting in the standard text, apparently unremarked, for the doctrine's entire citing career — none of the 149 decisions in the genealogy record in Part 2 engages the sentence's wording, and the author has found no decision that remarks the inversion. *Third*, this book's own practice: the print is quoted as printed, never silently corrected.
[^ih15e]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1305–06 (the root sentence as printed)](cite:ih15e/1); above in this section.
[^ih15a]: Co. Litt. 6b ("three sorts, *viz.* violent, probable, and light or temerary. *Violenta praesumptio* is manie times *plena probatio*"); [3 Bl. Comm. \*371, whose first-edition note *u* cites Co. Litt. 373](cite:ih15a/2); [Co. Litt. 373a–b ("*Quod stabitur praesumptioni donec probetur in contrarium*; but, as you see, it hath many exceptions," the margin sourcing the maxim to Bracton, lib. 1, cap. 9; "the law will admit no proofe against that which the law presumeth"; Butler's note 326 on the sealed acquittance as "an estoppel"). *Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1306–07](cite:ih15a/3); [*The Pizarro*, 15 U.S. (2 Wheat.) 227, 241 (1817) (Story, J.), the note at chapter 3.3 § A.](cite:ih15a/4)
[^vr16]: [On Coke's role in *Floyd* as a partisan of the prerogative courts, see the note at chapter 1.2 § B. His later turn against the prerogative — in *Dr. Bonham's Case*, 8 Co. Rep. 113b (1610), and as draftsman of the Petition of Right, 3 Car. 1, c. 1 (1628) — made him the common-law authority the Revolutionary generation invoked, Otis among them. *Dr. Bonham's Case*, 8 Co. Rep. 113b, 77 Eng. Rep. 646, the operative holdings at 118a and 121a](cite:vr16/1); on the chain's descent from *Floyd* by its own citations, chapter 2.4.
[^ep1]: [The proceedings of 27 January 1605/6 against the eight surviving conspirators — the indictment and the Attorney General's prosecution entire — are preserved in *A Complete Collection of State-Trials*, vol. I, item XIX (Francis Hargrave ed., 4th ed., London 1776). The indictment's naming clause is quoted in the text](cite:ep1/1); [the compilation names "Sir Edward Coke Attorney-General" speaking for the Crown, and the body of the prosecution is his. Every passage this book quotes from the item was read in the 1776 print ("the fourth edition . . . a new preface, by Francis Hargrave, Esquire," London, T. Wright, sold by G. Kearsly, 1776): the indictment's naming clause and the commissioner roll at cols. 231–32, the Attorney General's answer-attribution at col. 244. Long s is normalized to s in quotation](cite:ep1/2); the compilation remains a text at a remove from the record, cited as what it is.
[^ep2a]: Edward Coke, *La Size Part des Reports* (London 1607), the English preface "To the Reader" (unpaginated; the three passages quoted in the text); see the note on Persons's *Ansvvere* in this section.
[^ep2]: Robert Persons, *An Ansvvere to the Fifth Part of Reportes Lately Set Forth by Syr Edvvard Cooke Knight, the Kinges Attorney Generall* ([Saint-Omer] 1606) (issued "By a Catholicke deuyne"). Read in the 1606 print at leaves 23–24 (British Museum copy); the account of the reading, with its two residues, is in the Notes. The Sixth-Part preface quoted in the text: Edward Coke, *La Size Part des Reports* (London 1607), the English preface "To the Reader", read at the first-edition leaves (British Library microfilm; long s normalized). The modern historian's caution: the note at chapter 1.2 § B.
[^ep2_text]: Robert Persons, *An Ansvvere to the Fifth Part of Reportes Lately Set Forth by Syr Edvvard Cooke Knight, the Kinges Attorney Generall* ([Saint-Omer] 1606) (issued "By a Catholicke deuyne"; the dedicatory epistle is addressed to Coke directly). The Garnet-trial passage: Coke, it recounts, "protested to proue" the statute against the coming-in of priests "to be the most myldest law, the sweetest law, the law most full of mercy and pitty, that euer was enacted by any Prince so iniuriously prouoked," adding the flourish quoted in the text — which, Persons presses, "vvas a vvarrāt to al the hearers" that "in all their hearings you canonized his honesty." Quoted from the Text Creation Partnership keyboarding of the 1606 print (EEBO-TCP A09061, CC0 1.0), spelling and printed contractions as keyboarded; the bracketed letter in the text supplies a character the keyboarding marks illegible; TCP note-markers and a page-boundary marker (falling inside the quoted flourish, after "added") omitted. Every passage quoted here was read in the 1606 print (British Museum copy; the quoted matter at its leaves 23–24 as filed, the flourish standing in italic with the printer's own marginal note beside it — "M. Garnet an honest man by M. Attorneyes warrant." — and the page boundary falling after "And you adde[d]" as disclosed above), with two residues: the bracketed letter remains supplied, this copy's inner gutter being damaged at that glyph (the trace is consistent with "n" but not letterform-provable), and the title quotation continues to rest on the TCP keyboarding, the filed copy's title leaf wanting. The Sixth-Part preface quoted in the text: Edward Coke, *La Size Part des Reports* (London 1607), the English preface "To the Reader" — every quoted passage read at the first-edition leaves (British Library microfilm; long s normalized), including the print's own spelling shift "reply"/"replie" within one sentence. The acknowledgment names the answer to "that Case" (Caudrey's, of the Fifth Part) "as lately hath beene published," and its author only as "him that vsurpeth the sublime and broad spreading name of The Catholike Diuine" — the *Answere*'s stated authorship. The same leaf glosses the reporter's office by Moses (the margin prints "Moyses." beside "a president of a Iudge, (who also was the first Reporter of Law)"). The modern historian's caution: the note at chapter 1.2 § B.
[^ep3]: [*State-Trials*, vol. I, item XIX (Hargrave 4th ed. 1776), at col. 236, in the Attorney General's address, expounding the treasons of 25 Edw. III (source per the State Trials note above in this section)](cite:ep3/1); long s and the ligature normalised. On the office/function line the passage itself draws, the note at chapter 1.3 § B.
[^ep3_text]: [*State-Trials*, vol. I, item XIX (Hargrave 4th ed. 1776), at col. 236. The print reads long-s "ſitting" in both clauses — "ſitting in their Judicial Places" and "every Judge ſo ſitting"](cite:ep3_text/1); a prior transcription had misread the long s as "fitting," the reading itself being the print's own. The ligature "læſæ" is the print's, normalized in the text as "læsæ." The premise's own limit is part of the record: the statute's protection, as expounded, attaches to the judge "sitting in their Judicial Places, and exercising their Offices" — the office/function line the *Floyd* resolutions would also carry.
[^ep4]: [*Id.* at col. 243 ("especially seeing we have Reos confitentes, the Traitors own voluntary Confessions at the Bar"; the "voluntary and free Confessions of all the said several Traitors in writing subscribed with their own proper Hands, and acknowledged at the Bar, by themselves to be true, were openly and distinctly read"). Source per the State Trials note above in this section.](cite:ep4/1)
[^ep5]: [The January commission's roll, as the compilation prints it at col. 232, includes "The Lord Chief Justice of England, Sir John Popham" and "The Lord Chief Baron of the Exchequer, Thomas Fleming" (source per the State Trials note above in this section). The resolutions' bench: the notebook report's caption — "it was resolved by Popham and Coke C.JJ., \[Fleming\] C.B., Egerton L.C., and all the court of Star Chamber" (Baker ed., 142 Selden Soc. at 1655; the chief baron's name is the edition's bracketed identification; the printed report likewise names Popham, Coke, "the Chief Baron," and Egerton, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1305). Coke's own seat: he passed from Attorney General to Chief Justice of the Common Pleas in June 1606, between the trial and the resolutions. The overlap stated in the text is confined to what these two cited rolls show — Popham, the Chief Baron, and Coke himself](cite:ep5/1); no claim is made for the commission's other members.
[^ep6]: *A True Account of the Gunpowder Plot, Extracted from Dr. Lingard's History of England and Dodd's Church History, Including the Notes and Documents Appended to the Latter by the Rev. M. A. Tierney . . . with Notes and Prefatory Remarks by Vindicator* (London, C. Dolman 1851), at p. 8 n.† and p. 44 (Tierney's annotation: "the passage is scored with red ink, and that before it, in the margin, are written, in his own hand, the words '*huc usque*,'—*thus far*."). Both quotations are from the 1851 print; the description of the manuscript is the 1851 editors' report from their inspection of the State Paper Office original, and this book asserts nothing about the manuscript beyond their published statements. The exculpated man's presence in the indictment: the State Trials note above in this section.
[^ep6_text]: *A True Account of the Gunpowder Plot, Extracted from Dr. Lingard's History of England and Dodd's Church History, Including the Notes and Documents Appended to the Latter by the Rev. M. A. Tierney . . . with Notes and Prefatory Remarks by Vindicator* (London, C. Dolman 1851). The volume records the fact twice over, in substantially converging terms: the note to its Lingard narrative at p. 8 n.† ("See the fifth examination of Faukes, taken November 9th, and subscribed by him November 10th, in the State Paper Office. It was read at the trial, with the exception of the part exculpating Gerard. Before that in the original is drawn a line, with the words *huc usque*, in the handwriting of Sir Edward Coke, who was unwilling to publish to the world a passage which might serve for the justification of one whom he meant to accuse."), and Tierney's annotation to Dodd's account at p. 44 ("This examination Coke professed to read at the trial of the conspirators: but he thought it advisable to omit the part which I have printed in italics, and, accordingly, we find, in the original, that the passage is scored with red ink, and that before it, in the margin, are written, in his own hand, the words '*huc usque*,'—*thus far*."). Both quotations are from the 1851 print. The description of the manuscript — the red-ink scoring, the marginal words, the hand — is the 1851 editors' report from their inspection of the State Paper Office original; this book asserts nothing about the manuscript beyond their published statements. The exculpated man's presence in the indictment: the State Trials note at chapter 1.3 § C.
[^ep7]: Cora L. Scofield, *A Study of the Court of Star Chamber* (Chicago 1900), at iii–iv; [William Hudson, *A Treatise of the Court of Star Chamber*, pt. I, in 2 *Collectanea Juridica* 1, 6, 41 (Francis Hargrave ed., London 1792) (at 6, the sentence the text quotes). The 1732 report: Daniel Gosling, *The Records of the Court of Star Chamber at The National Archives and Elsewhere*, in *Star Chamber Matters: An Early Modern Court and Its Records* 19, 21 & n.13 (K.J. Kesselring & N. Mears eds., Univ. of London Press 2021) (open access), quoting John Lawton, keeper of the records in the Exchequer, in the words the text quotes, citing TNA SP 46/140. Whether BL Harley MS 2143, published as K. J. Kesselring (ed.), *Star Chamber Reports: BL Harley MS 2143* (List and Index Society, Special Series 57, 2018), carries a *Floyd* abstract is an open question, disclosed as such.](cite:ep7/2)
[^ep7_text]: Cora L. Scofield, *A Study of the Court of Star Chamber* (Chicago 1900), at iii: "the most valuable records of the Court are no longer to be found"; [Hudson's complaint, quoted there in part and now read in the treatise itself: William Hudson, *A Treatise of the Court of Star Chamber*, pt. I, in 2 *Collectanea Juridica* 1, 41 (Francis Hargrave ed., London 1792) ("the records are negligently kept, and many times lost," said of the clerk's unsworn under-officers), and *id.* at 6 ("this negligence hath crept into the court of very late time, either in mr. Mills's age, or since the office hath been executed by many deputies"; on the same page, in Hudson's own words, "the very sentences by which severe punishment hath been executed upon offenders have, by mere neglect, been wholly left unentered; so that there is no record to justify the inflicting of that punishment")](cite:ep7_text/2); [Scofield's next clause qualifies the point: Hudson's complaint "will not account for the complete disappearance of the decrees and orders of the Court," *id.*, and the House of Lords committee of 1719 found that the last notice of the decree and order books "that could be got was that they were in a house in St. Bartholomew's Close, London," *id.* at iv](cite:ep7_text/3); the 19 August 1608 delivery to "Sir Francis Bacon, then become clerk of the Star Chamber," of the "Kallender of Orders" volumes is set out *id.*, itemized book by book. The 1732 report: Daniel Gosling, *The Records of the Court of Star Chamber at The National Archives and Elsewhere*, in *Star Chamber Matters: An Early Modern Court and Its Records* 19, 21 & n.13 (K.J. Kesselring & N. Mears eds., Univ. of London Press 2021) (open access), quoting John Lawton, keeper of the records in the Exchequer ("the decrees of this court are not at present anywhere to be found"), citing TNA SP 46/140; the same chapter traces the 1719 deputy-chamberlains' report of the records lying "many years in a very great heap, undigested." The court's surviving proceedings class (STAC) holds pleadings and examinations — the class this book's cause file inhabits — not the decree and order books. A manuscript of "rough summaries taken from the order and decree books," touching just over a thousand cases, "mostly from the reigns of Elizabeth and James" (BL Harley MS 2143), is published as K. J. Kesselring (ed.), *Star Chamber Reports: BL Harley MS 2143* (List and Index Society, Special Series 57, 2018), Introduction at vii; whether it carries a *Floyd* abstract is an open question, disclosed as such.
[^ep8]: [William Prynne, *Brief Animadversions on, Amendments of, & Additional Explanatory Records to, the Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England . . . Compiled by the Late Famous Lawyer Sir Edward Cooke Knight* (London 1669), the Epilogue's review of 4 Inst. cap. 5 at pp. 417–22](cite:ep8/1); [the chapter verdict and the diligentest-perusal statement at p. 417](cite:ep8/2); [the records-upon-trust verdict at p. 421. Quotations are from the 1669 print, long s normalized. Prynne's own Star Chamber history is disclosed lest the office be mistaken for disinterest: that court sentenced him, and took his ears. The verdict quoted is nonetheless a records-office finding, stated of named rolls, checkable then and now.](cite:ep8/3)
[^ep8_text]: [William Prynne, *Brief Animadversions on, Amendments of, & Additional Explanatory Records to, the Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England . . . Compiled by the Late Famous Lawyer Sir Edward Cooke Knight* (London 1669), the Epilogue's review of 4 Inst. cap. 5 at pp. 417–22](cite:ep8_text/1); [the chapter verdict and the diligentest-perusal statement at p. 417](cite:ep8_text/2); [the records-upon-trust verdict at p. 421, stated of "these two Reverend Chief Justices Reports" of the Abbot of Westminster's case, Dyer's and Coke's (4 Inst. 61; Prynne at 419: "misreported both by the Lord Dier . . . and by the reverend Author of the Institutes"), its own sentence submitting the comparison "to the indifferent Readers" upon that precedent "and that which next follows." Title-page matter as printed: the author styled "Keeper of His Majesties Records in the TOWER of LONDON"](cite:ep8_text/3); the promise that "The transcripts of which Records out of the Originals, are at large inserted"; the epigraph "Tutius est petere fontes, quàm sectari rivulos." Quotations are from the 1669 print, long s normalized; [the fuller record of each medieval precedent Prynne re-ran at the rolls is available from the author. Prynne's own Star Chamber history is disclosed lest the office be mistaken for disinterest: that court sentenced him, and took his ears — a 1638 continental eyewitness pamphlet of the 14 June 1637 sentencing survives: *Een Cort ende Bondich Verhael, van seeckere bysondere ende voornaemste Redenen en Propoosten ghehouden in den Hoogen Raedt binnen Londen in Enghelandt . . . op den 14. Junij 1637* (1638), a Dutch account of the sentences on Bastwick, Burton and Prynne set down, its title page says, by one who was present. The verdict quoted is nonetheless a records-office finding, stated of named rolls, checkable then and now.](cite:ep8_text/6)
[^ep9]: The survey ran over the 150 volumes of *The English Reports* (Full Reprint, 178 vols., London 1900–32) available to the author; its method is stated in the Notes. The cell reported in the text is court-ordered destruction of a subject's pleading without an answer to it: one, this cause. In order quoted: *Maye v. Ebden* (Star Chamber, Mich. 1597), in John Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata, 1593 to 1609*, at 81 (William Paley Baildon ed., London 1894); [*Boyd v. Mills*, 13 Ves. Jun. 85, 87, 33 Eng. Rep. 226, 227 (Ch. 1806)](cite:ep9/3); [*Frankland v. Hampden*, 1 Vern. 66, 23 Eng. Rep. 315 (Ch. 1682)](cite:ep9/4); [*Dudly's Case*, 2 Sid. 71, 72, 82 Eng. Rep. 1263 (Upper Bench, Pasch. 1658)](cite:ep9/5); [*Anonymous*, Jenk. 162, 145 Eng. Rep. 104 (digesting 8 Hen. 6, c. 12)](cite:ep9/6); [Edward Coke, *The Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England* 73 (London 1797 ed.)](cite:ep9/7); [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1307 (12 Co. Rep. 25). Where the reprint is the only witness read, the pin is to the reprint.](cite:ep9/8)

[^ep9b]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1307 (12 Co. Rep. 25): both clauses stand on the one page, the second in the report's statement of the conspiracy resolutions that precede the order.](cite:ep9b/1)

The cell reported in the text is court-ordered destruction of a subject's pleading without an answer to it: one, this cause. Every other destruction order in the corpus names its ground (impertinence, scandal, irregularity, fraud, satisfaction) and rides motion, reference, or show-cause process; and the courts treat the irreversibility of expunction as itself a reason for restraint. ---

## 1.4 What the Report Cited for the Rule, Read at Its Own Page

The *Floyd* report cites English authority for every proposition it states about the record and about conspiracy, and for the rule itself it cites none. It rests the rule on the King's person, on a sentence it attributes to "the orator," and on a gloss of what it is to answer to God, and the one English judge it names was condemned at the roll for the very corruption the rule declares unquestionable. The reception question therefore cannot be asked of the rule as an English rule. It has to be asked of the sources the leaf itself names, and this chapter reads them at their own pages. Section A reads the leaf and the Thorpe roll, and the court's own practice in the decade before the resolution. Section B reads each source the leaf names, the King, the Roman law, and Scripture as the canon law and the English Bibles of 1607 read it, and states what the 1607 court and what Hobbes made of each; what the receiving order made of each is stated at chapter 3.3 § B, and the question the sources raise, whose decisions count, is answered at chapter 2.2 § B. Section C states why Hobbes is the link between the three eras this chapter joins, and records that the founders named him only to reject him.

### A. For the Rule Itself, the Report Cites No English Case

The *Floyd* report, at 12 Co. Rep. 24–25, cites English authority for every proposition it states about the record and about conspiracy: the Year Books of Edward III, Edward IV and Henry VI, the Assize book, Dyer, and the resolutions of Catlyn and Dyer, some fifteen pins in a page. For the rule itself it cites none. The judges

> ought not to be drawn into question for any supposed corruption … except it be before the King himself; for they are only to make an account to God and the King, and not to answer to any suggestion in the Star-Chamber.

\noindent The reasons given are that the King "is *de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects," that the judges "have the custody and guard of the King's oath," that the matter "concerns the honour and conscience of the King," and that "the orator said well, *invigilandum est semper, multæ insidiæ sunt bonis*."[^sf4b] One English instance follows, and it cuts the other way: "Thorp's judgment, who was drawn in question for corruption before commissioners, was held against the law, and upon that he was pardoned," to which the report's editor adds that "the whole set of Judges were then so corrupt that the King was forced to try him by commission."[^sf4][^sf4_text]

The rolls say otherwise. William de Thorpe, Chief Justice of the King's Bench, confessed in 1350 to taking gifts in his office against his oath, was adjudged to be drawn and hanged and his goods forfeited, and was pardoned by the King on 10 March 1351 on the grounds the enrolled pardon states: mercy, his former services, and compassion for his desolation, the roll reciting that "in justice execution of the judgement should have been demanded." The Parliament of 1351 had the record and process read and found them rightly and duly made and the judgment upon them reasonable. Nothing in the roll or the Parliament says the judgment was against the law. The roll's own reason for the judgment is that Thorpe had broken, as far as in him lay, the King's oath to his people, which he had in his keeping, and it confines the judgment to those "qui praedictum sacramentum fecerunt & fregerunt, & habent leges regales Angliae ad custodiendas," the judges alone.[^sf4a][^sf4a_text] The sentence of death came on the King's second writ, and the historian of the English bench found no law then in force that made the offence capital.[^sf36][^sf36_text] The report's own ground for the rule, that the judges "have the custody and guard of the King's oath," is therefore the language of the Thorpe roll; and in the roll it is the reason a Chief Justice was sentenced to death, not the reason he could not be questioned. At its source the one English case the leaf names is authority for the judge's answerability under the very oath the report invokes, not for his exemption. The name the crown paired with Thorpe in 1616 (chapter 1.3 § D) is a record of the same kind: Tresilian, who drew the answers given to the King at Nottingham in 1387, was appealed of treason and hanged at Tyburn on 19 February 1388, and the judges who had set their seals to the answers were convicted, sentenced to death, and spared into banishment.[^sf47][^sf47_text]

The court that issued the rule had itself recited the roll as living law twice within the decade before, in 1598 and again in 1602, the second time in sentencing a justice of the peace and Councillor of the Marches who had taken money to acquit an accessory to murder, with the rule that a judge by patent, though never sworn, "shall be punished for extortion or corruption as a Judge." In June 1598, sentencing a servant who had slandered the Chief Justice over a trial the court first examined and pronounced just, the Lord Keeper said that had the Chief Justice been found guilty of the wrong charged, "this would have been his punishment, as the precedent is 24 Edward III.," and recited Thorpe's commission, his confession, and his condemnation "to death, and to forfeit all his goods and land for ever."[^sf34][^sf34_text] Six years after that, in Michaelmas term 1604, the same court heard Coke himself, as Attorney General, prosecute a recusant for a petition to the King against two judges of the Northern Circuit, and the ground of the charge was the judges' accountability: the petitioner

> scandalized Justice, the Judges whoe are sworne to do Justice & represente the kinge & haue his charge, & are to gyue to him an accounte, & for there ill Cariage are to receaue a heauye iudgemente, as [in] the Case of Justice Thorpe.[^sf34a][^sf34a_text]

\noindent And in the year of the report a charge to the Norwich assizes, printed under Coke's name, told the county that the King "is constrayned by his Ministers, Deputies, Iustices, and Iudges, to administer Iustice vnto all his people," that "into their hands, is (as it were) deliuered the Kinges owne Oath," that "if any (with a Kings Oath trusted) shall bee so vilde, as to falsifie their trust, such offence is more then Capitall," and that "to judge in a point of difference, hearing but one partie speake, is assuredly to be vnjust."[^sf35][^sf35_text] And in November 1606, with Coke four months on the Common Pleas bench, the same court heard a subject's bill that charged a justice of the peace with a judicial act, his refusal to grant restitution after a riot, and decided it on the depositions: Coke "Commended Dolman, the Justice of peace, & he did very discreatelye, for he had done iniustice yf he had graunted restitucyon … & so he freede him wth Commendacion *a poena et culpa*," adding that a judge "showlde not, neyther Can he, iudge accordinge to his owne pryuate knowledge, mes *secundum allegata et probata*."[^sf35a][^sf35a_text] And in January 1607, in the term before the resolution, the same court heard a man acquitted of murder charge his accusers with conspiring to indict him. Coke argued from the bench that a man lawfully acquitted could and should prosecute the confederates at common law, whose remedies against the false accuser were old, the exception *de odio et atia* from the late twelfth century, a writ of conspiracy from 1293, and the statute of 1305 that first defined the crime as an agreement to maintain false accusations; the Lord Chancellor answered that "this court will proceed for the king also," and the court, finding reasonable grounds for the prosecution, sided with the accusers.[^sf35b] In the court's own precedent and in its reporter's own charge, the custody of the King's oath was the ground of the judge's answerability, and the hearing of both parties the condition of a just sentence; and the subject's complaint against a judicial officer's judicial act was heard on the record and decided on its merits. Hawarde sat in the court through the Easter term of 1607, the term to which the report assigns the resolution, and his notes record four sittings, on 24 and 29 April and 1 and 6 May, with the councillors and judges counted at each: a sheriff's blank warrant, a forged bond, a forged will, and a riot, and no cause between Floyd and Barker. The Trinity and Michaelmas terms that followed carry none either, and Baildon's index of the cases does not list it. A contemporary hand that sat through the term did not record the resolution the report prints as the court's.[^sf34b][^sf34b_text]

"The orator" is Cicero, and the sentence is not Cicero's own. It is a line of Accius's tragedy *Atreus*, which Cicero quotes in the speech for Plancius, in the form *vigilandum est semper: multae insidiae sunt bonis*, as the advice of a king sprung from Jupiter to his sons, and quotes again, in part, in the speech for Sestius, where the same passage of Accius also supplies *oderint dum metuant*.[^sf5] The 1607 court's *invigilandum* is its own reading. The rule's Roman source, at the point where the court named it, is a tragic poet's king, cited through an orator who was pleading for a defendant.

At its own leaf, then, the rule is not offered as English common law. It rests on the King's person, on a Roman sentence, and on a gloss of what it is to answer to God. It also fails the test its reporter set for his own reports. In the preface to the Ninth Part, printed in his lifetime, Coke wrote that it is "one amongst others of the great Honours of the Common Laws, that Cases of great Difficulty are never adjudged or resolved *in tenebris* or *sub silentio suppressis rationibus*; but in open Court, and there upon solemn and elaborate Arguments," the judges "declaring at large the Authorities, Reasons and Causes of their Judgments and Resolutions in every such particular Case," and that he printed only "such … as (in my Opinion) should hereafter be leading Cases for the publick Quiet."[^sf49a] The *Floyd* leaf records no argument at the bar, names no English authority for its rule, and was printed by other hands after his death.[^sf49] The reception question cannot be asked of it as an English rule, because at its source it is not one. It has to be asked of the three sources the rule names, and Section B asks it.

The record as recited by counsel: Oliver St John's speech to the Lords at the conference of both Houses on the Commons' votes of 7 December 1640 (Cobbett dates the conference only "16 Car. 1640"), 3 *Cobbett's Complete Collection of State Trials* 1262, 1273–74 (London 1809). The enrolment itself (TNA C 66) is cited here from the Record Commission's print.](cite:sf4a/2)
[^sf4a_text]: [Rymer, *Foedera, Conventiones, Litterae et Acta Publica*, III, pt. i, at 208–10 (Record Comm'n 1825) ("Processus factus contra Willielmum de Thorpe, capitalem justiciarium placitorum," from Pat. 24 Edw. III, pt. 3, mm. 2, 3d): the commission of 3 November 1350 "ad deliberationem de Willielmo de Thorpe chivaler, capto & detento in prisona nostra, pro quibusdam delictis & maleficiis, per ipsum contra nos, ad dampnum fidelium nostrorum, ac contra formam sacramenti sui … factis"](cite:sf4a_text/1); his confession of five gifts, £100 in all, taken from men indicted before him at Lincoln to stay the process against them; the King's writ reciting the ordinance that the justices of both benches and the barons of the Exchequer take fee of no one but the King and swear the oath, with its penalty "quod ipse erit ad voluntatem nostram, de corpore, terris & catallis," the King imposing by word of mouth ("tunc oretenus talem imposuimus poenam") the penalty of drawing and hanging and forfeiture; the judgment, "consideratum est per praedictos justiciarios … quod, quia praedictus Willielmus de Thorpe, qui, sacramentum domini Regis, quod erga populum suum habuit custodiendum, fregit maliciose, false, & rebelliter, in quantum in ipso fuit, & ex causis supradictis, per ipsum Willielmum, ut praedictum est, expresse recognitis, suspendatur, & quod omnia terrae & tenementa, bona & catalla sua, domino Regi remaneant forisfacta"; the King's writ of 19 November under the privy seal, in French, granting his life "de notre especiale grace & auctoritate roiale … mes volons que son corps soit remys en prison attendant notre grace"; the close, "Et non est intentio domini Regis, quod hujusmodi judicium in consimili casu versus quemcumque alium, ex quacumque causa, se teneat vel extendat, set solummodo versus illos, qui praedictum sacramentum fecerunt & fregerunt, & habent leges regales Angliae ad custodiendas"; [and the judges' oath in French at 209 ("Vous jurrez que bien & loialment servirez a notre seignur le Roi & seon poeple en l'office de justice … & que vous ferrez owel lei & execution de droit a touz ses sugitz, riches & povres, sanz avoir regarde a quecumque persone, & que vous ne prendrez par vous ne par autre, en prive nen apert, doun, regarde, d'or ne d'argent … & en cas que vous soiez trove en defaut desore en nuls des pointz avantditz, vous serrez en la volente le Roi de corps, terres, & davoir de faire ent ce que lui plerra"). The record as recited by counsel: Oliver St John's speech to the Lords at the conference of both Houses, delivering the Commons' votes of 7 December 1640 against the ship-money judgment, 3 *Cobbett's Complete Collection of State Trials* 1262, 1273–74 (London 1809) ("Sir William Thorpe, chief justice of the King's-Bench, in Edw. 3's time, having of five persons received five several bribes, which in all amounted to 100*l.* was for this alone adjudged to be hanged, and all his lands and goods forfeited. The reason of this Judgment is entered in the roll, in these words; 'Quia prædict' Willielmus Thorpe, qui sacramentum domini regis erga populum suum habuit ad custodiendum, fregit malitiose, false et rebelliter, quantum in ipso fuit;' … There is this notable declaration in that judgment; that is, that this judgment was not to be drawn into example against any other officers who should break their oaths, but only against those 'qui prædictum sacramentum fecerunt et fregerunt, et habent leges Angliæ ad custodiendum;' that is, only to the judges oaths, who have the laws entrusted to them. This judgment was given 24 Ed. 3. The next year in the parliament 25 Ed. 3, it was debated in parliament, whether this judgment was legal; *et nullo contradicente*, it was declared to be just and according to the law; and that the same judgment may be given in time to come upon the like occasion. This case is in point, that it is death for any judge wittingly to break his oath, or any part of it. This oath of Thorpe is entered in the roll, and is the same *verbatim* with the judges oath in 18 Ed. 3, and the same which the judges now take."). St John's account of the trust is on the same column: the King "at his coronation, is bound by his oath to execute justice to his people, according to the laws … His majesty again, as he trusts the judges with the performance of this part of the oath, so doth he likewise exact another oath of them, for their due execution of justice to the people, according to the laws." St John's Latin differs from the enrolment as printed by the Record Commission in four small readings ("erga populum suum habuit ad custodiendum" for "quod erga populum suum habuit custodiendum"; "malitiose" for "maliciose"; "quantum" for "in quantum"; "leges Angliae" for "leges regales Angliae")](cite:sf4a_text/7); the sense is the same. The enrolment itself (TNA C 66) is cited here from the Record Commission's print.

The court's maxim that rank aggravates the fault: *Plowden v. Braughton* (9 June 1602), at 146](cite:sf34/1); [the entry of 18 May 1603, at 162](cite:sf34/2); [*Throgmorten v. Stafforde* (11 October 1605), at 234–35.](cite:sf34/3)
[^sf34_text]: [John Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata, 1593 to 1609*, at 99–101 (William Paley Baildon ed., London 1894) (30 June 1598, Trin. 40 Eliz., eleven councillors and judges present: the information against Benjamin Bulle, servant to Lady Paget, "on confession by the party of slanderous words against the Lord Chief Justice of the Bench \[Sir John Popham\]," for telling a witness after a capital trial that the prisoner "coulde not be guyltye" and that "the matter shall be hearde againe before the Councelle"; "adjudged by the whole Court to be a heinous offence against the Queen and her dignity, against the common justice of the land, and a slander of the same, and a great defamation of the Lord Chief Justice of England; and he \[the Lord Chief Justice\] was acquitted of this, and discharged"; the trial reported by Anderson C.J., the Recorder and others "the most just that could be; inasmuch as the prisoner had a copy of the indictment … and of the examinations of the witnesses on both sides"; the Lord Keeper: "if the Chief Justice had been found guilty of this offence, in the first place, he would draw the vengeance of God on all the land for innocent blood, he had abused the Queen and her justice … And this would have been his punishment, as the precedent is 24 Edward III.: Sir William Thorpe, Chief Justice of England, was accused of taking bribes; whereupon the King directed a grave Commission of the most honourable lords of the land, who examined the matter, and found him guilty of taking of one £10, of another £20, and in all not £60, and condemned him to death, and to forfeit all his goods and land for ever; and the cause of his judgment was 'for that he had falselye, iniustlye, & trayterouslye, & rebelliouselye, as muche as in him laye, offended the kinge's mastie, his lawes, his Crowne & dygnitye'"; Bulle sentenced to imprisonment, £200, and the pillory at Westminster, Newgate and Cheapside). The court examined the judge's conduct before it punished the slander](cite:sf34_text/1); [Baildon's note refers the Thorpe recital to Foss, *Judges*, iii. 527. Nine years before the *Floyd* resolution, in the same court, the rule of jurisdiction was stated the other way, at 98 (26 May 1598; shoulder-note "Jurisdiction of the Star Chamber, which is great"): "Ruled by the Court, that if any Judge, Justice, officer or subject of the realm shall go beyond his bounds and limits, and misdemean himself in any manner, this Court has power and authority to examine and punish this; and this is clear law without any doubt or question." The court recited Thorpe again on 7 May 1602, at 134–39 (*Winston v. Throckmorton*, eleven councillors and judges present), sentencing a justice of the peace and member of the Council of the Marches who had taken a statute for £1600, of which £800 was paid, to acquit and discharge an accessory to murder: the Attorney on the slandered peers and Chief Justice, "if theise Fountaines be thus scandalized wth Corruption, what shall wee poore brookes look for"](cite:sf34_text/2); [the recital at 136, "the punishment of Sir William Thorpe, Chief Justice of England, for adjourning the trial of a felon to the next Assizes following, for which offence he was indicted, arraigned, and executed for high treason; and this was confirmed by Act of Parliament, so heinous, grievous and odious is the offence of corruption and extortion in a Judge," a telling the roll contradicts on the offence and the execution, as Baildon's note from Foss records](cite:sf34_text/3); the rule against the plea that the defendant was never sworn, "if a man be made a Judge by patent and be not sworn, still he is a Judge, and shall be punished for extortion or corruption as a Judge: and that he is not sworn is his own fault"; [the judges' resolution at 137 that the offence was extortion and not within the pardons of 35 and 39 Elizabeth](cite:sf34_text/5); [and the sentence at 139, "for the first offence only, the extortion, fined 2000 marks, with imprisonment and public confession at the next Assizes." The court's own maxim in those years was that the officer's rank aggravated his fault. *Plowden v. Braughton* (9 June 1602), at 146: a justice of the peace and councillor of the Marches, "in a place of justice and dignity for his virtue and erudition, *et quanto maior est dignitas tanto magis fuerit plus reum* \[sic\]," fined £200 and removed from the commission. The entry of 18 May 1603, at 162, reporting the King's "expresse Charge" that "the vulgar shall not leape into his throne & reforme any contempte herein, but shall Complaine to the maiestrate," closed by the reporter with the tags "*Culpabilis neclectus damnabilis contemptus. Dignitas personae auget culpam*"](cite:sf34_text/6); [on the same page a recusant informed against *ore tenus* on his confession was refused a copy of it and counsel, and delivered "a noate declaringe that by the lawes of this Realme no man ys to be Condemned but vpon bille & aunsweare or originall proces serued vpon him." *Throgmorten v. Stafforde* (11 October 1605), at 234–35: a justice of the peace fined £20 for a blow with a riding rod, "for it was a great offence because he was a Justice of the Peace and ought to keep the peace," the court holding the cause "fitter for another place than this Court" and adding that "although malice be the accuser, justice should be the punisher. … *Dignitas delinquentis auget peccatum*."](cite:sf34_text/7)
[^sf34a]: [Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 182–84 (29 Oct. 1604, Mich. 2 Jac.; Mr Pounde prosecuted *ore tenus* by Coke, the King's Attorney, for a petition to the King against two Judges of the Northern Circuit; the charge as reported at 183; the sentence at 184). The Crown's roll of fines taxed in the court, which Baildon prints as Appendix VII, enters Pounde for Michaelmas 2 Jac. under "Sclaunderinge the Justices of assize" at "Mli" and taxes the fine at "Cli": a thousand pounds was the sentence, a hundred the sum assessed (at 411). The Thorpe sentence is Hawarde's narrative of the Attorney's charge, the speaker named "Mr Attornie" at 183 and 184. The court had stated the rule two years before, in *Plowden v. Braughton*, at 145–46 (9 June 1602): "if he do evil and offend, this Court will punish him with great and heavy punishment".](cite:sf34a/1)
[^sf34a_text]: [Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 182–84 (29 Oct. 1604, Mich. 2 Jac.; "Mr Pounde, a great and ancient recusant, was prosecuted *ore tenus* by Coke, the King's Attorney, for a petition delivered by him to the King, against Baron Sauuelle and Sir Edwarde Phillips, the King's Serjeant, Judges of the Northern Circuit, for executing one Baillie"; the charge as reported at 183; "he Called them Hamans & Susanna's Judges"; the sentence at 184, "Fyned 1000li, imprisonmente duringe ye kinge's pleasure in ye tower, & to stande one the pillorie here at Westminster, & in Lancashire, & to haue his eares nailled, but not cutte of"). The Thorpe sentence is Hawarde's narrative of the Attorney's charge, the speaker named "Mr Attornie" at 183 and 184. The court had stated the rule two years before, in *Plowden v. Braughton*, at 145–46 (9 June 1602): a justice of the peace accused of procuring an informer before himself was acquitted for want of proof, "For a Judge, in his just and honest courses, will be protected by this Court with grace and favour, and the proof to accuse him must be plain and apparent, and such evidence is only *ad informandam conscientiam*; but if he do evil and offend, this Court will punish him with great and heavy punishment"](cite:sf34a_text/1); [and on the same day, at 146, a wife who had petitioned the Queen and the Lord Keeper for a common-law trial after a Star Chamber sentence was ordered "that if she did this again, she should be lashed."](cite:sf34a_text/2)

C2r–C3v (London: Nathaniel Butter, 1607), an unauthorized print of the charge at the Norwich assizes, attributed to Coke, quoted as printed ("Qui Iudicat causam parte inaudita altera, Æquum licet, Statuat, Iudex iniquus est"). [^sf35_text]: *The Lord Coke his Speech and Charge. With a Discoverie of the Abuses and Corruption of Officers*, sigs. C2r–C3v (London: Nathaniel Butter, 1607), an unauthorized print of the charge at the Norwich assizes, attributed to Coke, quoted as printed ("the Kings Maiestie at his Coronation is sworne to doe Iustice vnto all his Subiects, which in his owne Person it is impossible to performe. And therfore his Highnesse is constrayned by his Ministers, Deputies, Iustices, and Iudges, to administer Iustice vnto all his people … for vnto them, and into their hands, is (as it were) deliuered the Kinges owne Oath; because, what he is sworne vnto, must be by them in his behalfe performed … Thus by the Kings Commission the Kings owne Oath is put into our hands"; the rule of judging "not according to our owne Will, Conceit, or Opinion, but Secundum Legem & Consuetudinem Maneriae Anglicanae"; "Partialitie in a Iudge, is a Turpitude … A Iudge that will bee Partiall, will receiue a Bribe"; "A Iudge must vprightly, with an equall and indifferent eare and minde fully heare and vnderstand each cause before hee judgeth … Qui Iudicat causam parte inaudita altera, Æquum licet, Statuat, Iudex iniquus est"). The same charge, at sig. C4r, turns the psalm on the judges themselves: "To Kings, Rulers, Iudges, and Magistrates, this sentence is proper, *Vos Dij estis*; you are Gods on earth: when by your execution of Iustice and Iudgement, the God of heauen is by your actions represented: but if by vs, that so are called Gods, Iustice and Iudgement be peruerted; it will be heauy for our soules, when we shall dye like men"; and at sig. C1v the protection the law gives a judge is of his person, by the King's presence: a blow struck before him costs the hand "because the offence was done as in the presence of the Prince," and "if a Iustice of Assise shall happen by any in his Circuit to be slaine, the Law adiudgeth it to bee *Lese Crimen Maiestatis*."

The editor's note identifies the justice as Thomas Dolman. The suit reached the court after the petition route had been tried and had failed. On 24 September 1603 the Earl of Worcester wrote to the Earl of Shrewsbury that the Lord Admiral had taken possession of Donnington "vppon my Lady Russell, and kepethe the Castell and her lady\[-ship\] owt of dores; who complayned to the King, but found littell redress, and so is turned to the Lawe" (Nichols, *Progresses of James I*, i. 274, as printed in Baildon's Appendix XVII, at 434).](cite:sf35a_text/1)
