The first eleven Parts appeared in Coke's lifetime, between 1600 and 1615, the Twelfth in 1656: Ian King, *The Legal Writing of Sir Edward Coke, the Anglo-Saxons, and Lex Terrae*, 39 The Seventeenth Century 329, 332 (2024); for the 1656 print and the notebook report of *Floyd*, the notebook-edition note at chapter 1.3 § B.

### B. The King, the Roman Law, Scripture and the Canon Law, Each at Its Own Page

What the receiving order made of each source is stated at chapter 3.3 § B, and the conclusions drawn from the sources together at chapter 2.2 § B. This section states what each source the leaf names says at its own page, what the 1607 court made of it, and what Hobbes made of it, and it adds the fourth body of law through which Scripture reached an English court, the canon law. The table gives the summary; the paragraphs and notes carry the texts.

| Source | What the source says at its leaf | What the 1607 court made of it | What Hobbes made of it (1651) |
|---|---|---|---|
| The King | The judge is questioned only "before the King himself"; the King "is *de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects." No subject's complaint against a judge is recorded in that forum (chapters 1.3 § D, 3.5 § C). | A receiver who adjudicates, named and never opened to a subject; the petition prosecuted. | The sovereign alone exempt, because the subject is author of his acts (ch. 18); the judge a subject, judged "by men agreed on by consent of both" (ch. 23); the subject may sue the sovereign on a former law (ch. 21). |
| Roman law | The definitions of chapter 2.2 § B; no one condemned absent or unheard (D. 48.17.1, 48.19.5); the failed accuser not thereby a calumniator, and the angry word no accusation (D. 48.16.1); the anonymous libel punished and the signed accuser rewarded (CTh 9.34.1; C. 9.36.2); the law's fear aimed at the reckless litigant (Inst. 4.16 pr., chapter 2.2 § B). | The vocabulary kept and the law reversed: "*de jure*" in the idiom of the *lex regia*; "the orator" for the maxim; the deterrent turned from the litigant onto the subject's accusation; a signed, sworn bill destroyed as a libel. | Justinian the catalogue of a law's sources; Cicero maxim; Rome's rules stated as laws of nature without citation. |
| Scripture | Deut. 1:16–17 (hear both, fear no face, "for the iudgment is Gods"); Deut. 17:8–12 (the contemner of the determination dies); Ps. 82 ("Ye are gods … But ye shall die like men"); Exod. 22:28 ("Gods," margin "Or, Iudges"); Acts 25:16 (the accused present, the accusers face to face, licence to answer); the Elizabethan glosses, below. | "The judgment is God's" read as "the judge answers to no man." | Scripture as history of lawful practice, never as authority for a rule. |
| Canon law | No one condemned absent or unheard (C. 3 q. 9 cc. 1–6, c. 5 drawn from Acts 25:16); the failed accuser and the angry word (C. 2 q. 3, Gratian importing D. 48.16.1); the good judge judges as he hears (C. 3 q. 7 c. 4); no sentence binds but from one's own judge (C. 3 q. 8 c. 1); the record kept against the unjust judge (Lateran IV c. 38). | The pattern the Tudor injunctions and the High Commission inherited: one class denied a hearing, one instrument destroyed with its author. | Words made a crime must be reduced to a formula the accused can know before he is cited (*Appendix*, 1668). |

*The King.* The rule's operative clause names a receiver who adjudicates: the judge is not to be questioned "except it be before the King himself."[^sf_king1] Chapter 3.5 § C finds no instance of a subject's complaint against a judge adjudicated in that forum. Hobbes concedes the sovereign everything and the judge nothing. The one person a subject cannot accuse is the sovereign acting by his power, and the reason is the subject's own authorship: "every Subject is Author of the actions of his Soveraigne; he punisheth another, for the actions committed by himselfe."[^sf_king2] The judge is a subject, and

> there may arise a controversie between the party Judged, and the Judge; which because they be both Subjects to the Soveraign, ought in Equity to be Judged by men agreed on by consent of both; for no man can be Judge in his own cause.

\noindent Even against the sovereign the subject may sue wherever the sovereign "demandeth by force of a former Law, and not by vertue of his Power."[^sf11] Coke's own *Institutes*, written after the report, leave the receiver the leaf names no seat. The King

> hath committed all his power judiciall, some in one court, and some in another, so as if any would render himselfe to the judgement of the king in such case where the king hath committed all his power judiciall to others, such a render should be to no effect.

\noindent "[T]he king doth judge by his judges," and the King's Bench exists "regularly to examine and correct all and all manner of errors in fait, and in law, of all the judges and justices of the realm,"[^sf37a] and other misdemeanours

> tending to the breach of the peace, or oppression of the subjects … so that no wrong or injury, either publick or private, can be done, but that this shall be reformed or punished in one court or other by due course of law.[^sf37][^sf37_text]

\noindent Behind the *Institutes* stands the charter they cite: the King's promise to appoint as justices "only such as know the law of the realm and mean to observe it well," and to sell, deny, or delay right or justice to no one.[^sf38]

*The petition to the King, after the rule.* Eighty-one years after the resolution the question the leaf decided went to a jury. Seven bishops had presented a petition to James II stating why they could not read his Declaration in their churches; the information, as counsel opened it to the jury, charged it as "a false, scandalous, malicious and seditious Libel, under Pretence of a Petition."[^sf39a] The Attorney General opened with the rule in its widest form:

> No Man is allowed to accuse even the most inferior Magistrate of any Misbehaviour in his Office, unless it be in a legal Course, though the Fact is true … no Man may come to a Judge, either by Word or Petition, You have given an unjust, or an ill Judgment.

\noindent The Chief Justice told the jury he took the paper to be a libel; two of the three puisne judges did not, Holloway because "it's the Birth-right of the Subject to Petition" and "to deliver a Petition cannot be a Fault," Powell because "to make it a Libel it must be False, it must be Malicious, and it must tend to Sedition"; and the jury returned Not Guilty.[^sf39][^sf39_text] When the question the leaf decided against the subject reached a jury of the same order, the answer went the other way.

*Roman law.* Chapter 2.2 § B gives the Roman definitions, and Part 5, chapter 5.3, gives the Roman remedies against the judge; what belongs here is only what the leaf borrowed or inverted. The unheard plea, in words: "neque enim inaudita causa quemquam damnari aequitatis ratio patitur," the rescript of Severus and Antoninus as Marcian reports it, and Trajan's, that no one is condemned on suspicion, "satius enim esse impunitum relinqui facinus nocentis quam innocentem damnari."[^sf12] The failed accuser: the accused is acquitted first, and only then is the accuser's mind inquired into, honest error acquitting him, "Sed non utique qui non probat quod intendit protinus calumniari uidetur."[^sf13][^sf13_text] The libel: the paper Constantine's law pursued was the anonymous one, the persons it named to suffer no charge from it, its author to be sought and made to prove his words and punished even if he proved something, "qui infamare maluit quam accusare," because he preferred to defame rather than to accuse; the law of 365 that Justinian's Code received rewards the man who "nomen suum profiteatur" and proves his charge, "laudem maximam ac praemium," and ends "huiusmodi autem libellus alterius opinionem non laedat."[^sf14] Rome's one shield for a judge closed one route only: a wrong judgment was not treason, while the party's action against the judge and the public accusation of corruption stayed open. The 1607 court took Rome's vocabulary and reversed Rome's law. The King "*de jure*" delivers justice, in the idiom of the *lex regia*; "the orator" supplies the maxim; the deterrent is kept and turned from the litigant onto the subject's accusation (chapter 2.2 § B); and the paper it punished as a libel was a signed bill filed in court, which is the paper Rome's law protected. Of Rome's two actions, the litigant's against the unjust judge and the defamed man's against the writer, the Star Chamber kept the second, in the *Case de Libellis Famosis* of 1605, and destroyed the first. Hobbes took Rome's law and left its citations. Justinian is his catalogue of a law's sources, paired with English counterparts; Cicero is maxim; and the rules that no sentence binds a successor, that the judge who condemns unheard is unjust, and that fear is the partial judge's effect on litigants, he states as laws of nature without a Roman name attached.[^sf15]

*Scripture.* The court's gloss reads "the judgment is God's" as "the judge answers to no man." The gloss was in use in the same court three years earlier. In Michaelmas term 1604 a suitor who had petitioned the King against the Lord Chancellor's injunction was sentenced, for the petition, to perpetual imprisonment, the loss of his ears on the pillory, and a fine of a thousand pounds, and the court

> condemned all petitions, especially where a Judge of the Court, a great man, or a magistrate is charged. "Let all men hereby take heede how they complayne in wordes againste any magistrate, for they are gods; & he must haue verye good matter that will goe aboute to Conuynce them, for feare he ouerthrowe not himselfe."[^sf40][^sf40_text]

\noindent The verses it leans on read otherwise at their own pages. In Deuteronomy the clause is printed as the reason for two commands to the judge, to hear both parties and to fear no face:

> Heare the causes betweene your brethren, and iudge righteously betweene euery man and his brother … Ye shall not respect persons in iudgement, but you shall heare the small aswell as the great: you shall not bee afraid of the face of man, for the iudgment is Gods.[^sf16][^sf16_text]

\noindent The verse that puts the contemner of the priest's or judge's determination to death is, in the 1611 editors' own chapter argument, "The contemner of that Determination must die," contempt of a determination and not complaint of a wrong, and on the same leaf the king is to read the law all his days "that his heart bee not lifted vp aboue his brethren."[^sf17][^sf17_text] The psalm that calls the judges gods is headed by the same editors "Iudges are gods" and cross-referenced to Deuteronomy 1:17, and it runs from the divinity to the reproof and the sentence in seven verses: "I haue said, Ye are gods … But ye shall die like men."[^sf18][^sf18_text] The verse Coke's generation used for the reviling of judges, "Thou shalt not reuile the Gods," carries the judges only in the margin, "Or, Iudges," and the next chapter on the same leaf is the law of the judge: "neither shalt thou speake in a cause, to decline after many, to wrest iudgement … And thou shalt take no gift: for the gift blindeth the wise."[^sf19][^sf19_text] The folio Bibles of that generation, the Bishops' of 1568 and 1572, had glossed the same verse as a licence and not a shield: "A priuate man may reprehende the magistrate, so it be done with modestie: but without rebukes, railing, and euyl speakyng," the note reads, "considering whose person he representeth." The quarto of 1569 dropped the note, and the 1602 and the 1611 print the verse without it. And the Roman rule the canon law received stands in Acts in the mouth of a Roman governor: "It is not the maner of the Romanes to deliuer any man to die, before that he which is accused, haue the accusers face to face, and haue licence to answere for himselfe concerning the crime laid against him."[^sf20][^sf20_text] Erasmus, annotating the chapter in 1535, read the favour the priests asked of Festus as the judge's favour, and their request as one for a sentence under a feigned show of hearing, "ficta specie cognitionis," since "Quis enim postulat damnationem à iudice indicta causa?", who asks a judge for condemnation with the cause unheard. Hobbes uses Scripture as history of lawful practice and never as authority for a rule; his one text on accusation is the town clerk's at Ephesus, who sends the accusers to a lawful assembly.[^sf21]

The 1611 Bible postdates the resolution by four years. The Bibles in use in 1607 were the Geneva of 1560 and the Bishops' of 1568, and both gloss the verses the leaf leans on. At Deuteronomy 17:12 the Geneva glosses the contemner's death with a proviso on the judge, "So long as he is the true minister of God, and pronoūceth according to his worde," and the Bishops' with the same, "As long as he is the true minister of god and pronounceth agreeable to his worde"; at 1:17 both gloss "the iudgement is Gods" as the judge's subordination, the Geneva "And you are his lieutenants," the Bishops' "And man is but Gods minister."[^sf22][^sf22_text] The verse the court read as the judge's exemption was thus printed, in the two English Bibles of the court's own day, with a condition on the judge and a name for his office that answers to a superior. And the psalm that calls the judges gods was, in the same year and in the reporter's own charge to the Norwich jury quoted in Section A, the verse of the judge's death: "Vos Dij estis; you are Gods on earth … but if by vs, that so are called Gods, Iustice and Iudgement be peruerted; it will be heauy for our soules, when we shall dye like men." The glosses are the Genevan annotators' of 1560, taken into the Bishops' of 1568; the two English Bibles before Geneva, Coverdale's of 1535 and the Great Bible of 1540, print the verses without any note, as the 1611 does after them. The 1611's silence at 17:12 was not inherited from a bare page. The Bishops' folio of 1602, the base text its translators annotated, had dropped the note at 1:17, as the printing of 1577 already had while the folio of 1578 still carried it, but still printed the proviso at 17:12, and the hand that rewrote the verse around it left the note standing.[^sf22a][^sf22a_text] Two leaves on, at 19:17, where both parties to a cause "shall stand before the LORD, before the Priests and the Iudges," the same hand rewrote the verse around the Bishops' note "God is present, where his true ministers are," and the 1611 printed nothing there either. Which Bible the 1607 court read is not known; its leaf does not say, and Coke's own Bible is not identified. The reading survived into the receiving century. In 1644 Rutherford's *Lex, Rex*, arguing from the same verses against the royalist reading, held that "Not only kings but all inferior judges are gods," that "the judge is but God by office," that because "the judgment is the Lord's" the king "cannot limit the conscience of the inferior judge," and that inferior judges are "admonished" and "condemned in God's word for unjust judgment."[^sf41][^sf41_text]

*Canon law.* The church's law received the Roman rule and the verse together. At Gratian's page the canons that no one is condemned absent or unheard are, in Friedberg's notes, Pseudo-Isidore's: the first from the *interpretatio* to a Theodosian law, the third from another, the fourth from Chalcedon, and the fifth "petitum ex Act. ap. c. 25. v. 16," Festus's answer put into the mouth of a third-century pope by a ninth-century forger and received as law by Gratian: "Non oportet quemquam iudicari uel dampnari prius, quam legitimos habeat presentes accusatores, locumque defendendi accipiat ad abluenda crimina."[^sf23][^sf23_text] The three sources are one source at this leaf. The Latin the canonists read at Jehoshaphat's charge to the judges turns the judgment back upon the judge: "non enim hominis exercetis iudicium, sed Domini: & quodcumque iudicaueritis, in vos redundabit," whatever you shall judge will fall back upon you; the 1611 renders the same clause as God's presence with the judge, "who is with you in the iudgement," the reading of the Hebraist Latin of Tremellius and Junius, and puts the Hebrew in the margin, "in the matter of iudgement."[^sf23a][^sf23a_text] The 1611's vocabulary at the judge verses is otherwise the Latin's: "Gods" for *Diis* at Exodus 22:28, "the wise" for *prudentes* and "gift" for *munera* at 23:8, "respect persons" for *accipere personam* at Deuteronomy 1:17 and 16:19, "sentence" for *sententiam* at 17:11, "Answere not" for *ne respondeas* at Ecclesiasticus 11:8, and at Luke 18:6 "vniust iudge," which is Beza's "iudex ille iniustus" rather than the Vulgate's *judex iniquitatis*; and Festus's answer, which Gratian quotes in the Vulgate's words, is the sentence the canon received.[^sf23b][^sf23b_text] The same *causa* carries Ambrose on the good judge, addressed to the judges of the world, "Discite iudices seculi": he "nichil ex arbitrio suo facit … sed sicut audit, ita iudicat," and judges as he hears;[^sf24][^sf24_text] and the rule that "Nullum … sententia a suo iudice non dicta constringat," no sentence binds but from one's own judge, "quia et leges seculi idipsum fieri precipiunt," the Roman Correctors referring the secular law to Justinian's Code.[^sf25][^sf25_text] On the failed accuser Gratian copies the Digest by name. The calumniator who fails "talionem recipiat," but "Aliud est accusationem non inplere, et aliud conuicium non probare," and Fabian's canon rules that "Conuicium non est pro accusatione habendum," the angry word no accusation and carrying no penalty unless its speaker chooses to write it; then Marcian's text follows, "sicut in Digest. l. XLVIII. legitur," and the Code on pardon, "Indulgentia, patres conscripti, quos liberat notat."[^sf26][^sf26_text] And the Fourth Lateran Council of 1215 ordered every judge to keep a written record of the whole proceeding by a public person or two fit men, "Quoniam contra falsam assertionem iniqui iudicis innocens litigator quandoque non potest veram negationem probare," so that "si super processu iudicis fuerit suborta contentio, per hoc possit veritas declarari": the record is kept for the litigant against the judge, and the originals stay with the scribes.[^sf27][^sf27_text] The one class the canon law denied a hearing was the relapsed heretic, left to the secular judge "sine ulla penitus audientia," and the one instrument it destroyed with its author was the condemned notary's, "cum auctore damnato damnentur."[^sf28] That is the pattern the Tudor injunctions and the High Commission inherited, and Hobbes's Latin *Appendix* gives the burning regime's span as the life of that commission, "Ab anno primo Elizabethae ad annum decimum septimum Caroli primi," naming the *Injunctiones* of 1559.[^sf29] Hobbes's own rule for the crime of words is a legality standard: "quoties Verba fiunt crimen," the words must be reduced to a formula, so that anyone may know without syllogisms which words are criminal and which are not.[^sf30] Part 5 carries the Basilika's statement of the rule that a man judges unjustly in his own cause, the Greek form of the canon at C. 3 q. 7.[^sf31] The one immunity the canon law did hold was the Roman see's, and its record in the *Liber Pontificalis* is a trial. In 501 Symmachus, accused before King Theodoric by senators who "suborned false witnesses," stood before a synod of a hundred and fifteen bishops and "was acquitted of the false accusation"; the editor of the English text records that he appeared "waiving the claim of his see not to be judged by inferiors," and that the bishops "left the accusations against him to the judgment of God."[^sf42][^sf42_text]

III c. 5)](cite:sf37/2); [*id.* at 60–66 (cap. 5, the Star Chamber).](cite:sf37/3)
[^sf37a]: [Coke, *The Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England* 71 (London 1797) (cap. 7: "the king doth judge by his judges," and the second head of the King's Bench's jurisdiction, as printed).](cite:sf37a/1)
[^sf37_text]: [Coke, *The Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England* 70–71 (London 1797) (cap. 7, the Court of King's Bench: the ordinance of 20 Edw. III c. 1 quoted "speaking in the kings person," that the justices "shall from henceforth do even law and execution of right to all our subjects, rich and poor, without having regard to any person, without letting to do right for any letters or commandement which may come to them from us, or from any other"; "8 H. 4. the king hath committed all his power judiciall …"; "8 H. 6. the king doth judge by his judges (the king having distributed his power judiciall to severall courts) and the king hath wholly left matters of judicature according to his lawes to his judges"; "the king himselfe cannot be judge in propria causa"; the King's Bench's second and third heads of jurisdiction as quoted). *Id.* at 67–69 (cap. 6, the court for redress of delays of judgments under 14 Edw. III c. 5: a prelate, two earls and two barons "to heare by petition delivered unto them the complaints of those that will complaine them of such delayes and grievances" by the justices; "In divers cases the party grieved shall have an action for unjust delay"; Shard J., "We cannot nor will delay any man in respect of our oath"). *Id.* at 60–66 (cap. 5, the Star Chamber: its standing judges, its jurisdiction "for oppression, and other exorbitant offences of great men, (whom inferiour judges and jurors (though they should not) would in respect of their greatnesse be affraid to offend) bribery, extortion … scandalous libelling," the paragraph before it having set the court's end, "Ut poena ad paucos, metus ad omnes perveniat, without respect of persons"; "Quod arbitrio judicis relinquitur, non facile trahit ad effusionem sanguinis"; the court "cannot sit for hearing of causes under the number of eight at the least"; the Procter case of 12 Jac., "paribus sententiis reus absolvitur").](cite:sf37_text/1)
[^sf38]: [Magna Carta (1215) cc. 40, 45, in William Sharp McKechnie, *Magna Carta: A Commentary on the Great Charter of King John* 395–98, 431–33 (2d ed., Glasgow 1914) ("Nulli vendemus, nulli negabimus aut differemus rectum aut justiciam"; "Nos non faciemus justiciarios, constabularios, vicecomites vel ballivos, nisi de talibus qui sciant legem regni et eam bene velint observare," McKechnie's rendering "We will appoint as justices, constables, sheriffs, or bailiffs only such as know the law of the realm and mean to observe it well," the clause "embracing all royal ministers and agents, both of the central and of the local government, from the chief justiciar down to the humblest serjeant"). Coke cites the 1225 form, c. 29, at 4 Inst. 67 ("Nulli vendemus, nulli negabimus, aut differemus justitiam vel rectum"; the 1644 pagination's 68, bracketed in the 1797 margin).](cite:sf38/1)
[^sf39]: [*The Proceedings and Tryal in the Case of the Seven Bishops, in the Court of King's-Bench, Trinity Term 1688*, at 75–76 (the petition, and the information in Latin), 139 (the information read in English), 142–43 (the opening of it to the jury, the words the text quotes, and the Attorney General's opening), 407, 413 (Holloway J.), 420–23 (the directions), 425–28 (Allybone J.), 430–31 (the verdict of 30 June 1688) (London 1739). The court was Wright C.J. and Holloway, Powell and Allybone JJ.](cite:sf39/1); the judges divided two and two on the question of law.
[^sf39a]: [*The Proceedings and Tryal in the Case of the Seven Bishops* 142–43 (London 1739) (the information as opened to the jury).](cite:sf39a/1)
[^sf39_text]: [*The Proceedings and Tryal in the Case of the Seven Bishops, in the Court of King's-Bench, Trinity Term 1688*, at 75–76 (the petition and the information, "in quo quidem falso, ficto, malitioso, pernitioso & seditioso libello continetur"), 143 (the Attorney General's opening), 407 (Wright C.J.: "the Bishops might petition the King, but this is not the right way of bringing it"; Serjeant Baldock: "the Subject has a Right to petition; but I shall affirm it also, he has a Duty to obey"), 413 (Holloway J.: "don't compare the writing of a Book to the making of a Petition; for it's the Birth-right of the Subject to Petition"), 420–23 (the directions: Wright C.J., "any thing that shall disturb the Government, or make Mischief and a Stir among the People, is certainly within the Case of *Libellus Famosis*; and I must in short give you my Opinion, I do take it to be a Libel"; Holloway J., "to deliver a Petition cannot be a Fault, it being the Right of every Subject to Petition … I cannot think it is a Libel"; Powell J., "If there be no such Dispensing Power in the King, then that can be no Libel which they presented to the King"), 425–28 (Allybone J.: "whether it be in the form of a Supplication, or an Address, or a Petition, if it be what it ought not to be, let us call it by its true Name … It is a Libel"; "there may be every Tittle of a Libel true, and yet it may be a Libel still"; his recollection of a resolution of the judges in James I's time "That to frame a Petition to the King to put the Penal Laws in Execution, is next door to Treason," which Powell J. corrected as a petition carrying a threat), 430–31 (the verdict of 30 June 1688) (London 1739). The court was Wright C.J. and Holloway, Powell and Allybone JJ.](cite:sf39_text/1); the judges divided two and two on the question of law.
[^sf40]: [Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 176–77 (Fourde's case, Michaelmas term 1604; Baildon's note: docquet of 22 May 1605, "pardon to William Foorth of fine and corporal punishments for slanderous petitions against the Lord Chancellor"). The roll of fines taxed in the court, Baildon's Appendix VII, at 411, enters Fourde under "Sclaunder against his maties magistrates & Justice" at "Mli" and taxes the fine at "Cli": the thousand pounds was the sentence, a hundred the sum assessed, and the pardon followed. The petition went to the one receiver the rule names, and the court punished it as a libel](cite:sf40/1); the pardon came by the judge's own intercession.
[^sf40_text]: [Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 176–77 (Fourde's case, Michaelmas term 1604: "He having a cause pending in the Chancery, and foreseeing that the Lord Chancellor would grant an Injunction against him, preferred a petition to the King that he would require the Lord Chancellor to stay the granting of the Injunction, for it would undo him, his wife and his children"; the King's messenger; the Chancellor "notwithstanding, granted the Injunction, and Fourde preferred another petition to the King informing him of this, and concluded that the question now was whether the commandment of the King or the order of the Chancellor should take effect"; "for sowing sedition between the King and his Peers, Fourde was sentenced to have perpetual imprisonment, to lose his ears upon the pillory, having papers on his head, 'to ride w\[i\]th his face to the horse taile, & to be fyned 1000li.'"; the Lord Chancellor "left the Court, and the Lord Treasurer gave sentence"; the next Star Chamber day the Chancellor "was with the Kinge, vpon his knee, for Fourde's pardon, & had obtayned the same"; Baildon's note: docquet of 22 May 1605, "pardon to William Foorth of fine and corporal punishments for slanderous petitions against the Lord Chancellor").](cite:sf40_text/1)
[^sf41]: [Samuel Rutherford, *Lex, Rex: The Law and the Prince* (London 1644), Questions XIX–XX, in the Edinburgh edition of 1843, at 87–90 (the passages the text quotes, entire in the Notes; the gods of Psalm lxxxii are "not a congregation of kings"). The royalist answered is Maxwell's *Sacro-sancta Regum Majestas* (1644), Rutherford's "Prelate."](cite:sf41/1)
[^sf41_text]: [Samuel Rutherford, *Lex, Rex: The Law and the Prince* (London 1644), Questions XIX–XX, in the Edinburgh edition of 1843, at 87–90 (the first two passages quoted here close Question XIX, at 87, answering the Prelate's "Kings are *Dii Elohim*"; Question XX, "Whether or no inferior judges be univocally and essentially judges, and the immediate vicars of God, no less than the king, or if they be only the deputies and vicars of the king," begins at 88) ("Not only kings but all inferior judges are gods. Psal. lxxxii., God standeth in the congregation of the gods, that is not a congregation of kings. So (Exod. xxii. 8,) the master of the house shall be brought … to the gods, or to the judges"; "The judge is but God by office, and representation, and conservation of the people"; on 2 Chron. xix. 6–7, "if the inferior judges, in the act of judging, were the vicars and deputies of king Jehoshaphat, he would have said, judge righteous judgment. Why? For the judgment is mine … But the king saith, They judge not for man, but the Lord"; "the king cannot limit the conscience of the inferior judge, because the judgment is not the king's, but the Lord's"; on Deut. i. 17, "Moses appointed judges, but not as his deputies to judge and give sentence, as subordinate to him; for the judgment (saith he) is the Lord's, not mine"; "then could neither inferior judges be admonished nor condemned in God's word for unjust judgment," with Deut. xvii. 11, i. 17 and Exod. xxiii. 2 among the texts cited; "I judge it blasphemy to say, that a sentence of an inferior judge must be no sentence, though never so legal nor just, if it be contrary to the king's will, as Grotius saith"). The royalist answered is Maxwell's *Sacro-sancta Regum Majestas* (1644), Rutherford's "Prelate."](cite:sf41_text/1)
[^sf42]: [*The Book of the Popes (Liber Pontificalis)* I, 116–19 (Louise Ropes Loomis trans., Records of Civilization, New York 1916) (the life of Symmachus; Loomis's note on the synod of 501). The maxim itself, *prima sedes a nemine iudicatur*, is not printed in the *Liber Pontificalis* at these pages. Its canonical place is C. 9 q. 3 c. 13, Friedberg I, col. 610 (rubric "Prima sedes nullius iudicio subiaceat"), with the rule's one exception at D. 40 c. 6, col. 146, the pope "a nemine est iudicandus, nisi deprehendatur a fide deuius." All from the 1879 edition.](cite:sf42/1)
[^sf42_text]: [*The Book of the Popes (Liber Pontificalis)* I, 116–19 (Louise Ropes Loomis trans., Records of Civilization, New York 1916) (the life of Symmachus: the double election of 498 and Theodoric's "righteous judgment"; "some of the clergy and some of the senate, in particular Festus and Probinus, full of zeal and craft, brought charges against Symmachus and suborned false witnesses whom they sent to King Theodoric, the heretic, at Ravenna to accuse the blessed Symmachus"; the king's "visitor" Peter of Altinum, "although the canons forbade it"; "Then the blessed Symmachus assembled 115 bishops and in the synod was acquitted of the false accusation and Peter of Altinum, the intruder upon the apostolic see, and Laurentius of Nuceria were condemned"; Loomis's note on the synod of 501: "At this session Symmachus agreed to appear, waiving the claim of his see not to be judged by inferiors … Nevertheless in October the assembled bishops declared that they could find no reason why he should not continue in full enjoyment of his office and left the accusations against him to the judgment of God"). The maxim itself, *prima sedes a nemine iudicatur*, is not printed in the *Liber Pontificalis* at these pages. Its canonical place is C. 9 q. 3 c. 13, Friedberg I, col. 610 (rubric "Prima sedes nullius iudicio subiaceat"; "Nemo iudicabit primam sedem, iustitiam temperare desiderantem. Neque enim ab augusto, neque ab omni clero, neque a regibus, neque a populo iudex iudicabitur"), with Gratian's dictum after c. 9 ("Sola enim Romana ecclesia sua auctoritate ualet de omnibus iudicare; de ea uero nulli iudicare permittitur"), c. 10 (rubric "Apostolicae sedis iudicium a nemine est retractandum") and c. 14, Symmachus's own synod ("sedis istius presulem suo sine questione reseruauit arbitrio"). On the same leaf the Roman Correctors of 1582 give the source: on c. 13, "In omnibus vetustis Gratiani exemplaribus citatur ex Innocentio. Sed fere eadem habentur in concilio Romano sub Silvestro c. 20"](cite:sf42_text/1); on c. 10, "neque in Bonifacii, neque in Innocentii epistolis inventum est quod hic affertur"; [and Friedberg's note refers c. 13 to "c. 20. apocryphi constituti Sylvestri ap. Mansi II, 631." The rule's one exception is stated at D. 40 c. 6, col. 146, the pope "a nemine est iudicandus, nisi deprehendatur a fide deuius." The same Silvester texts supply the clerical forum at C. 11 q. 1 c. 9, col. 628 ("Testimonium clerici aduersus laicum nemo recipiat, nemo clericum quemlibet in publico examinare presumat nisi in ecclesia"), beside the imperial law at c. 5 ("Habent illi suos iudices, nec quicquam his publicis est commune cum legibus"): a rule of forum for clerics, not an exemption of any judge from judgment. All from the 1879 edition.](cite:sf42_text/3)
[^sf12]: [D. 48.17.1 pr. (Marcian) ("Diui Seueri et Antonini Magni rescriptum est, ne quis absens puniatur: et hoc iure utimur, ne absentes damnentur")](cite:sf12/1); [D. 48.19.5 pr.–1 (Ulpian) ("Absentem in criminibus damnari non debere diuus Traianus Iulio Frontoni rescripsit. sed nec de suspicionibus debere aliquem damnari diuus Traianus Adsidio Seuero rescripsit"; the contumacious absent may be sentenced only in money or reputation, "uerum si quid grauius irrogandum fuisset, puta in metallum uel capitis poenam, non esse absentibus irrogandam"), in Theodor Mommsen & Paul Krueger (eds.), *Digesta Iustiniani Augusti* II, at 839, 846 (Berlin 1870). On the same page as the Trajan rescript, D. 48.19.6.1 punishes a governor's slaves "ut exemplo deterriti minus delinquant": the one *deterrere* on these leaves is aimed at the wrongdoer.](cite:sf12/2)
[^sf13]: [D. 48.16.1 pr.–3 (Marcian), in Mommsen & Krueger, *Digesta* II, at 835 (the passage entire in the Notes). The only name Rome strikes from its own register is the accused's, for his protection: D. 48.2.3.1 (Paul), *id.* at 797 (in the Notes).](cite:sf13/1)
[^sf13_text]: [D. 48.16.1 pr.–3 (Marcian), in Mommsen & Krueger, *Digesta* II, at 835 ("Accusatorum temeritas tribus modis detegitur et tribus poenis subicitur: aut enim calumniantur aut praeuaricantur aut tergiuersantur. … Sed non utique qui non probat quod intendit protinus calumniari uidetur: nam eius rei inquisitio arbitrio cognoscentis committitur, qui reo absoluto de accusatoris incipit consilio quaerere, qua mente ductus ad accusationem processit, et si quidem iustum eius errorem reppererit, absoluit eum, si uero in euidenti calumnia eum deprehenderit, legitimam poenam ei irrogat"). The only name Rome strikes from its own register is the accused's, for his protection: D. 48.2.3.1 (Paul), *id.* at 797 ("Quod si libelli inscriptionum legitime ordinati non fuerint, rei nomen aboletur et ex integro repetendi reum potestas fiet").](cite:sf13_text/1)
[^sf14]: [CTh 9.34.1 (Constantine, a.d. 319), in Theodor Mommsen & Paul M. Meyer (eds.), *Theodosiani Libri XVI cum Constitutionibus Sirmondianis* I.2, at 486 (Berlin 1905) (those the *libellus famosus* names "nullas exinde calumnias patiantur"; the author to be sought and to prove his charge, "nec tamen supplicio, etiamsi aliquid ostenderit, subtrahatur"; the *Interpretatio*: "fustigetur," and the clause the text quotes). Constantine's law was not received into Justinian's Code](cite:sf14/1); [the Code's title *de famosis libellis* carries the law of 365, C. 9.36.2, in Krueger (ed.), *Codex Iustinianus* 856 (Berlin 1877) (the finder who publishes an anonymous libel punished; the named accuser who proves his charge rewarded; the closing clause on the libel's effect, all in the words the text quotes). The shield: C. 9.8.1 (Alexander Severus, a.d. 223), *id.* at 819 ("nedum etiam admittam te paratum accusare iudicem propterea crimine maiestatis, quod contra constitutionem eum dicis pronuntiasse")](cite:sf14/2); [the party's action and the public accusation, Part 5, chapter 5.3. *Case de Libellis Famosis* (Star Ch. 1605), 5 Co. Rep. 125a, 77 Eng. Rep. 250.](cite:sf14/3)
[^sf15]: [Hobbes, *Leviathan* ch. 26, at 137–47 (London 1651) (the seven sorts of civil law "in the Institutions of Justinian," "the Edicts, Constitutions, and Epistles of the Prince," "Senatus consulta," "the Edicts of Prætors," "Responsa Prudentum," each paired with its English counterpart)](cite:sf15/1); [ch. 47, at 381 (*cui bono* as Cicero's maxim). The rules as laws of nature: ch. 26, at 144–45 (the sentence no law to a successor; the judge who condemns unheard unjust)](cite:sf15/2); [ch. 15, at 77 (the partial judge deters men "from the use of Judges"). On the channels by which the Roman texts reached Coke's England, Paul Vinogradoff, *Roman Law in Mediaeval Europe*, Lecture IV, at 84–105 (London 1909)](cite:sf15/3); [on the Roman law's entry into North American law by way of Quebec, William Bennett Munro, *The Genesis of Roman Law in America*, 22 Harv. L. Rev. 579, 585–89 (1909).](cite:sf15/4)
[^sf16]: [Deut. 1:16–17, in *The Holy Bible* (London: Robert Barker, 1611) (margin at v. 17 "* Leuit. 19. 15. chap. 16. 19. 1. sam. 16. 7. pro. 24. 23." and "† Hebr. acknowledge faces."), consulted in *The Holy Bible: An Exact Reprint in Roman Type, Page for Page, of the Authorized Version Published in the Year 1611* (A. W. Pollard ed., Oxford 1911) and in a first-folio copy. The 1611 readings throughout this section are given as printed, with the long *s* normalized. Collations of the 1611 against the Aleppo and Leningrad codices at the judge loci found no textual variant, and the printed text of Kittel's *Biblia Hebraica* (2d ed., Stuttgart 1925) reads the same at Ps. 82 and 2 Chron. 19:6–7, its apparatus recording no manuscript variant at those verses](cite:sf16/1); the commentary printed beside the verse in the first printed Hagiographa (*Ketuvim* with commentary, Naples 1487, at the leaf carrying 2 Chron. 19) reads it as a rule of the judge's liability, quoted in the Notes; the one word אלהים is "the Iudges" at Exod. 22:8–9, "Gods" with the margin "Or, Iudges" at 22:28, and "Gods" in "the iudgment is Gods" at Deut. 1:17.
[^sf16_text]: [Deut. 1:16–17, in *The Holy Bible* (London: Robert Barker, 1611) (running head "A repetition of · Deuteronomie. · former things."; margin at v. 17 "* Leuit. 19. 15. chap. 16. 19. 1. sam. 16. 7. pro. 24. 23." and "† Hebr. acknowledge faces."), consulted in *The Holy Bible: An Exact Reprint in Roman Type, Page for Page, of the Authorized Version Published in the Year 1611* (A. W. Pollard ed., Oxford 1911) and in a first-folio copy. The 1611 readings throughout chapter 1.4 § B are given as printed, with the long *s* normalized. A collation of the 1611 against the Aleppo Codex, made for this work at the seven judge loci the codex still carries (its Torah leaves are lost before Deut. 28:16, so Exod. 22:28 and the Deuteronomy verses cannot be read there: Ps. 82; 2 Chron. 19:6–7; Jer. 36:23–25; 1 Sam. 8:1–11; 1 Kings 3:9, 11; Prov. 18:5, 13, 17; Prov. 24:23–25), found no textual variant at any locus](cite:sf16_text/1); [the 1611 follows the qere ובא over the codex's ketiv יבא at Prov. 18:17](cite:sf16_text/2); [the 1611's own margins record the Hebrew at six of these points ("Hebr. hearing" at 1 Kings 3:9, "Hebr. to heare" at 3:11, "Heb. iudge" at Ps. 82:3, "Hebr. in the matter of iudgement" at 2 Chron. 19:6, "Hebr. returneth a word" at Prov. 18:13, "Hebr. acknowledge faces" at Deut. 1:17)](cite:sf16_text/3); and at 1 Kings 3:9 the hearing heart, לב שמע, is rendered "vnderstanding." In every Hebrew locus the fear is the judge's toward God, and the litigant's face is what he must not regard. A second collation, against the Leningrad Codex at the seven Pentateuch loci (Exod. 22:8–9, 28; 23:1–9; Deut. 1:16–17; 16:18–20; 17:8–13, 18–20; 19:15–21), likewise found no textual variant. A third reading, of the printed text in Rudolf Kittel (ed.), *Biblia Hebraica*, editio altera emendatior, Pars II (Stuttgart 1925), at 973–74 (Ps. 82) and 1293 (2 Chron. 19:6–7), found the same text at every judge word; the apparatus at Ps. 82 records the Greek and Syriac transposing words at v. 3 and the editors' own conjectures at vv. 6–8 ("frt dl," "prps"), and at 2 Chron. 19 carries no entry at vv. 6–7. The commentary the first printed Hagiographa sets beside the verse, transmitted under Rashi's name (*Ketuvim* with commentary, Naples 1487, at the leaf carrying 2 Chron. 19, pencilled p. 60 in the copy read), takes "for yee iudge not for man" as a rule of liability, "אם הטית את הדין חייב … לשלם מה שגזלת," if you have perverted the judgment you are liable to repay what you have robbed, and "who is with you in the iudgement" as the end of the judge's excuse, He sits with you in judgment "שלא יאמ׳ הדיין מה לי," so that the judge should not say, what have I to do with this trouble, adding the maxim "אין לו לדיין אלא מה שעיניו רואות," a judge has nothing but what his eyes see. The renderings are this book's; the print's abbreviations are expanded, its line-end catch-letters dropped, and one word between "liable" and "to repay" left unread, and the noun after "what have I to do with" rendered as this trouble from its sense, the type of the word not being read with confidence. It shows the translators' choices on the word the 1607 court read divinely: one word, אלהים, is "the Iudges" three times in the 1611's own text at Exod. 22:8–9 (the reading of the English text since the Great Bible of 1540; Coverdale's of 1535 has "the Goddes"), "Gods" with the margin "Or, Iudges" at 22:28, and "Gods" in "the iudgment is Gods" at Deut. 1:17; שחד is "gift" in every statement of the judge's rule (Exod. 23:8; Deut. 16:19; 2 Chron. 19:7) and "bribes" only of Samuel's sons (1 Sam. 8:3); the four "sentence" of Deut. 17:9–11 render "word" three times and "mouth" twice, the "sentence" of v. 10 standing for both; the hearing verb is kept three times in Moses' charge at Deut. 1:16–17, and the contemner of 17:12 is one who "will not hearken"; and seven further margins at these loci record the Hebrew the text smoothed. The Septuagint at the same verses reads "Διακούετε ἀνὰ μέσον τῶν ἀδελφῶν ὑμῶν, καὶ κρίνατε δικαίως … οὐκ ἐπιγνώσῃ πρόσωπον ἐν κρίσει· κατὰ τὸν μικρὸν καὶ κατὰ τὸν μέγαν κρινεῖς, οὐ μὴ ὑποστείλῃ πρόσωπον ἀνθρώπου, ὅτι ἡ κρίσις τοῦ θεοῦ ἐστίν," keeping the Hebrew's acknowledging of faces that the 1611 margin records and adding that the judge shall not shrink from a man's face: H. B. Swete (ed.), *The Old Testament in Greek according to the Septuagint* I, at 340 (3d ed., Cambridge 1901). At Exod. 22:8–9 (its 22:7–8) the Septuagint reads the deposit law's threefold אלהים as the one God, "ἐνώπιον τοῦ θεοῦ" twice and "διὰ τοῦ θεοῦ," where the 1611 has "the Iudges" and the Vulgate "deos," and at 22:28 (its 22:27) it keeps the plural, "θεοὺς οὐ κακολογήσεις," with the Vulgate's "Diis" (Swete I, at 147–48). The 1611 reads the same word "the Iudges" once more at Exod. 21:6, "Then his master shall bring him vnto the Iudges," with no note in the margin at the verse; [a lawyer's defence of that rendering against the critics' "God" is H. M. Wiener, *Studies in Biblical Law* 25–26 (London 1904) ("only an admittedly doubtful rendering of אֱלֹהִים, a word which is by some translated 'judges'"; "Now let us try substituting 'judges' for 'God.' Master and slave go to the judges. Where do the judges meet? Not at the notional door of a hypothetical 'sanctuary,' but at the gate"). The Hebraist Latin printed in 1599 had already put the judges in the text and the gods in the margin: the new Latin column of the Commelin polyglot, Pagnini's version, reads at Exod. 22:8–9 "applicabitur dominus domus ad Iudices" and "vsque ad Iudices veniet causa vtriusque: is quem cõdemnauerint Iudices," each "Iudices" lettered to the margin "H. Deos," while the Vulgate column beside it reads "ad eos" at v. 8 and "ad deos" at v. 9, and the Greek "ἐνώπιον τοῦ θεοῦ": *Sacra Biblia, Hebraice, Graece, et Latine, cum annotationibus Francisci Vatabli* (ex officina Commeliniana, 1599), at 169.](cite:sf16_text/11)
[^sf17]: [Deut. 17:8–13, 18–20 (1611) (chapter argument "8 Hard controuersies are to bee determined by the Priests and Iudges. 12 The contemner of that Determination must die."; v. 12 margin "† Hebr. not to hearken."; vv. 18–20, the king's copy of the law). The chapter before, Deut. 16:18–19](cite:sf17/1); John 7:51, margin "* Deut. 17. 10. and 19. 15."; [Deut. 19:17–20 and the History of Susanna 61–62, whose margins cite each other](cite:sf17/3); [Ecclus. 11:7–8. Theodotion's Susanna at vv. 48–49: H. B. Swete (ed.), *The Old Testament in Greek according to the Septuagint* III, at 582–83 (3d ed., Cambridge 1905).](cite:sf17/4)
[^sf17_text]: [Deut. 17:8–13, 18–20 (1611) (chapter argument "8 Hard controuersies are to bee determined by the Priests and Iudges. 12 The contemner of that Determination must die."; v. 12, "And the man that will doe presumptuously, and will not hearken vnto the Priest (that standeth to minister there before the LORD thy God) or vnto the Iudge, euen that man shall die," margin "† Hebr. not to hearken."; vv. 18–20, the king's copy of the law). The chapter before, Deut. 16:18–19 ("Thou shalt not wrest iudgement, thou shalt not respect persons, neither take a gift: for a gift doth blind the eyes of the wise, and peruert the wordes of the righteous"). John 7:51 ("Doth our Law iudge any man before it heare him, & know what he doth?") carries the margin "* Deut. 17. 10. and 19. 15." Deut. 19:17–20 (both men stand before the judges, who "shall make diligent inquisition"; the false witness suffers what he intended; "those which remaine shall heare, and feare"), margin at v. 19 "* Prou. 19. 5, 9. dan. 13. 62.", and the History of Susanna 61–62, margin "* Deut. 19. 19." (the judges who condemned "without examination" put to death under the false-witness law: the two margins cite each other). Ecclus. 11:7–8 ("Blame not before thou hast examined the trueth: vnderstand first, and then rebuke. Answere not, before thou hast heard the cause"). The false-witness grammar was English practice in the receiving century: Titus Oates, whose sworn evidence had sent men to their deaths, was convicted of perjury before the King's Bench on 8 May 1685, the indictment reciting that he "had sworn, and caused to be put to Death several Persons"](cite:sf17_text/1); [*A True Narrative of the Tryal of Titus Oates for Perjury at the Kings-Bench-Barr at Westminster* 1–4 (Dublin reprint, 1685). The sentence is not in the tract. The 1611's "examination" is the Greek's: Theodotion's text of Susanna reads at v. 48 "οὐκ ἀνακρίναντες οὐδὲ τὸ σαφὲς ἐπιγνόντες κατεκρίνατε θυγατέρα Ἰσραήλ," and at v. 49 "ἀναστρέψατε εἰς τὸ κριτήριον"](cite:sf17_text/2); the older Greek version has "ἀπεκτείνατε," you killed, for "you condemned," and no v. 49. H. B. Swete (ed.), *The Old Testament in Greek according to the Septuagint* III, at 582–83 (3d ed., Cambridge 1905) (the two versions on facing pages).
[^sf18]: [Ps. 82 (1611) (running head "Israels obstinacie. · Psalmes. · Iudges are gods."; argument "The Psalmist hauing exhorted the Iudges, 5 and reproued their negligence, 8 prayeth God to iudge."; v. 2 margin "* Deut. 1. 17."; v. 6 margin "* Iohn 10. 34."). Luke 18:2–6 ("Heare what the vniust iudge saith"). Jer. 36:23–32. The Septuagint, Ps. 81 in its numbering, multiplies the gods, "Ὁ θεὸς ἔστη ἐν συναγωγῇ θεῶν, ἐν μέσῳ δὲ θεοὺς διακρίνει," and reads at v. 6 "ἐγὼ εἶπα Θεοί ἐστε," the words John 10:34 quotes: Swete, *The Old Testament in Greek* II, at 323 (3d ed., Cambridge 1907). Vatablus's note at Ps. 82:1 in the Commelin polyglot (1599), at 156 ("Deos vocat iudices, vt ipse Christus exponit Iohan. 10.35")](cite:sf18/1); [Erasmus, *Annotationes*, In Ioannem cap. X (Basel 1535), at 251, on the Gospel's citation of the verse.](cite:sf18/2)
[^sf18_text]: [Ps. 82 (1611) (running head "Israels obstinacie. · Psalmes. · Iudges are gods."; argument "The Psalmist hauing exhorted the Iudges, 5 and reproued their negligence, 8 prayeth God to iudge."; v. 2 margin "* Deut. 1. 17."; v. 6 margin "* Iohn 10. 34."). Luke 18:2–6 (running head "An vniust iudge."; "Heare what the vniust iudge saith"). Jer. 36:23–32 (running heads "The roule burnt." and "Another written."; the king who "would not heare" burns the roll; the roll is rewritten "and there were added besides vnto them, many like words"). The Septuagint, Ps. 81 in its numbering, multiplies the gods: "Ὁ θεὸς ἔστη ἐν συναγωγῇ θεῶν, ἐν μέσῳ δὲ θεοὺς διακρίνει," God stood in an assembly of gods and in the midst judges gods, where the 1611 has "the Congregation of the mightie," the reading of the Hebraist Latin, Tremellius and Junius "Deus astat in coetu Dei fortis, inter magistratus iudicat" (*Testamenti Veteris Biblia Sacra* (Geneva 1617), at 141) and Pagnini's interlinear "Deus stans in coetu fortis: in interiori diuorum iudicauit" (Arias Montanus ed., Geneva \[1618\], at 31)](cite:sf18_text/1); v. 2 "πρόσωπα ἁμαρτωλῶν λαμβάνετε"; v. 3 "κρίνατε ὀρφανὸν καὶ πτωχόν" (the 1611 "Defend," margin "Heb. iudge"); v. 6 "ἐγὼ εἶπα Θεοί ἐστε," the words John 10:34 quotes and the Vulgate's "Dii estis" (in Codex Vaticanus, Vat. gr. 1209, p. 1366, col. 1, ll. 27–29, the Gospel's citation reads "ΟΤΙ ΕΓΩ ΕΙΠΑ ΘΕΟΙ ΕΣΤΕ," and the scribe, who contracts and overlines ΘΝ, ΙΣ and ΘΥ in the surrounding lines, writes ΘΕΟΙ at v. 34 and ΘΕΟΥΣ at v. 35 in full and unbarred; Vercellone and Cozza, *Bibliorum Sacrorum Graecus Codex Vaticanus*, Novum Testamentum (Rome 1868), at 132; the printed Greek of Erasmus (Basel 1535), at 157, Stephanus (1551), fol. 320r, and Beza (1598), at 406, reads "Ἐγὼ εἶπα, θεοί ἐστε" without the codex's ὅτι, and Beza's type keeps the codex's distinction, "θεοί" and "θεούς" in lower case against "Θεοῦ"); [v. 7 "ὑμεῖς δὲ δὴ ὡς ἄνθρωποι ἀποθνήσκετε, καὶ ὡς εἷς τῶν ἀρχόντων πίπτετε." Swete, *The Old Testament in Greek* II, at 323 (3d ed., Cambridge 1907). The judges' reading of the psalm stood in print beside the Hebrew in 1599: Vatablus's note at Ps. 82:1 in the Commelin polyglot glosses the assembly as "in magnifica & frequenti congregatione iudicum. vel, in congregatione potentiæ. i. potentium, hoc est, in senatu, qui solet valere authoritate. Deos vocat iudices, vt ipse Christus exponit Iohan. 10.35," the columns above it reading, in Pagnini's version, "in medio Deorum iudicat" and, in the Vulgate's, "in medio autem deos diiudicat" (at 156). Erasmus's own annotation on the Gospel's citation of the verse, *In Ioannem cap. X*, in the Basel edition of 1535, at 251, treats the sentence as a question of word order, the margin reading "Ordo perturbatior," and follows Hilary in taking the gods for "sanctis hominibus," the men to whom the word of God came](cite:sf18_text/5); the judges are not named there.
[^sf19]: [Exod. 22:28 (1611) ("Thou shalt not reuile the ‖ Gods, nor curse the ruler of thy people," margin "‖ Or, Iudges." and "* Acts 23. 5.")](cite:sf19/1); Exod. 23:1–2, 7–8 on the same leaf. The margin is inherited: the Bishops' folio of 1602, the translators' base text, prints "‖ Or, iudges." at 22:27; the folios of 1568 (fol. xlviii v, n. o) and 1572 (fol. xlii, n. o) print the note the text quotes, entire in the Notes; [the quarto of 1569 already prints the margin without the note. The Commelin polyglot of 1599, at 170, prints Pagnini's "Iudicibus non maledices" against the margin "H. Diis".](cite:sf19/4)
[^sf19_text]: [Exod. 22:28 (1611) ("Thou shalt not reuile the ‖ Gods, nor curse the ruler of thy people," margin "‖ Or, Iudges." and "* Acts 23. 5.")](cite:sf19_text/1); [Exod. 23:1–2, 7–8 on the same leaf. In the Geneva text of 1560 the word is in the text: "Thou shalt not raile vpō the Iudges, nether speake euil of the ruler of thy people." The margin is inherited. The Bishops' folio of 1602, the translators' base text, prints at 22:27 "Thou shalt not raile vpon the ‖ gods, neither blaspheme the ruler of thy people," with the margin "‖ Or, iudges." and no lettered note](cite:sf19_text/2); [in the copy the translators annotated, Bodleian Arch. A b.18, fol. E4v, the hand strikes "raile vpon" for "reuile" and "neither blaspheme" for "nor curse" and leaves the margin standing. At 23:8 the same folio prints "for gifts blinde the ‡ seeing, and peruert the words of the righteous," margin "‡ Wise.", with the note "d Against bribes."](cite:sf19_text/3); [the hand strikes "seeing," and the 1611 prints "the wise" with "the seeing" in the margin. At 23:3 the 1602 note reads "Trueth of the matter, & not respect of any person, is to be esteemed in iudgement." The two folio Bishops' Bibles before it gloss the verse itself: the 1568 (fol. xlviii v, n. o, read in a copy with its margins uncut) and the 1572 (fol. xlii, n. o) print at "Thou shalt not (o) rayle vpon the ‖ gods" the note "A priuate man may reprehende the magistrate, so it be done with modestie: but without rebukes, railing, and euyl speakyng, both for auoydyng disorder, and considering whose person he representeth" (the 1572's spelling; the 1568 reads "euil speakyng" and "consideryng"), with "‖ Or, iudges." above it](cite:sf19_text/4); [the quarto of 1569 already prints the margin without the note and reads "thy people," as the 1602 and the 1611 do after it. The Commelin polyglot of 1599 prints the two words the other way about at the verse: Pagnini's column "Iudicibus non maledices," lettered to the margin "H. Diis," beside the Vulgate's "Diis non detrahes" and the Greek "Θεοὺς οὐ κακολογήσεις" (at 170).](cite:sf19_text/5)
[^sf20]: [Acts 25:16 (1611)](cite:sf20/1); *id.* 25:18, 26–27, 26:1, 26:32. The 1611's words at the verse are the Bishops' revision's (the New Testament of 1575, "haue the accusers face to face"), its own changes being to drop the Great Bible's favour clause and to read "to die" where the 1568 has "that he shoulde perishe" and the 1575 "that hee should perish", both rendering the printed Greek's "εἰς ἀπώλειαν" (Stephanus 1551, fol. 450v; Beza 1598, at 564; Erasmus's *Annotationes* (Basel 1535), at 328; Erasmus's text of the same year, *Novum Testamentum* (Basel 1535), at 229, prints the itacistic "εἰς ἀπόλειαν"). Erasmus's annotations, *In Acta Apost. cap. XXV*, in the same Basel edition of 1535, at 327–28, gloss the favour the priests ask as "fauorem iudicis" and read v. 15 as the text quotes it, the annotation entire in the Notes. For the canon drawn from the verse, the canon-law paragraph of this Section.
[^sf20_text]: [Acts 25:16 (1611)](cite:sf20_text/1); *id.* 25:18, 26–27, 26:1, 26:32 (the accusers "brought none accusation of such things as I supposed"; "after examination had, I might haue somewhat to write"; "Thou art permitted to speake for thy selfe"; "This man might haue bene set at libertie, if he had not appealed vnto Cesar"). The Great Bible of 1540 reads the answer "It is not the maner of the Romaynes, for fauoure to delyuer any man, that he shulde peryshe, before that he which is accused, haue the accusars before hym, and haue lycence to answere for hym selfe"; [Coverdale's of 1535 has no favour word and cites Deut. 17 in the margin at the verse. The Bishops' folio of 1568 keeps "for fauour" and "haue the accusers before hym," and reads v. 10 "to the Iewes haue I no harme done"](cite:sf20_text/3); the Bishops' New Testament of 1575 has "haue the accusers face to face" and "haue I done no wrong" while still reading "for fauour," so the 1611's words at both verses are the Bishops' revision's, and its own changes are to drop the favour clause and to read "to die" where the 1568 has "that he shoulde perishe" and the 1575 "that hee should perish." Both render the printed Greek's "εἰς ἀπώλειαν," which stands in Stephanus's of 1551, fol. 450v, in Beza's of 1598, at 564, and in Erasmus's *Annotationes* (Basel 1535), at 328 (his text of the same year, at 229, prints the itacistic "εἰς ἀπόλειαν"), and for which the Vulgate has no word: Erasmus's Latin gives "ut pereat," Beza's "quempiam perdendum concedere," and Beza's note 16 glosses the Roman rule as "in gratiam alicuius ad mortem damnare, indicta videlicet causa"; [Beza's Latin as reprinted at Geneva in 1617, at 246, reads "quempiam ad exitium concedere," and Tremellius's Latin from the Syriac beside it "vt dent filium hominis donum ad necem priusquam venerit litigator eius, & arguat eum in facie eius." Erasmus's own annotations on the chapter, *In Acta Apost. cap. XXV*, in the same Basel edition of 1535, at 327–28, gloss the favour the priests ask of Festus as "fauorem iudicis," render δίκην at v. 15 as *sententiam* because "Quis enim postulat damnationem à iudice indicta causa? Sed postulabant, ut ficta specie cognitionis Paulus uideretur iure damnatus," and read the Greek of v. 16 as "donare in perniciem siue in interitum. Est autem χαρίζεσθαι, in gratiam alicuius donare," noting copies that had written "Damnare, pro Donare," the reading the Vulgates of 1592 and 1881 print at the verse (the note at chapter 1.4 § B on the Vulgate loci) where Erasmus's own lemma reads *donare*. The Rheims New Testament of 1582, the English rendering of the Vulgate, reads at the verse "it is not the Romans' custom to yield up any man before that he which is accused have his accusers present, and take place to make his answer for to clear himself of the crimes," at John 7:51 "Doth our law judge a man, unless it first hear him, and know what he doeth?" and at Luke 18:6 "Hear what the judge of iniquity sayeth": *The Vulgate New Testament with the Douay Version of 1582 in Parallel Columns*, at 199, 135, 108 (Samuel Bagster and Sons, London 1872) (the 1582 text in that print's modernised spelling). For the canon drawn from the verse, the canon-law paragraph of this Section.](cite:sf20_text/5)
[^sf21]: [Hobbes, *Leviathan* ch. 22, at 122–23 (London 1651) (Acts 19:40, the accusation to be brought before a magistrate)](cite:sf21/1); for the appointment of judges he cites Exod. 18, 1 Sam. 8, 1 Kings 3:9 and Matt. 23:2–3 as Scripture confirming the sovereign's right of judicature, never as rules binding the judge who holds it.
[^sf22]: [*The Bible and Holy Scriptures Conteyned in the Olde and Newe Testament* (Geneva: Rouland Hall, 1560): Deut. 17:12, fol. 88v, n. h](cite:sf22/1); Deut. 1:17, n. m; [Ps. 82:7, fol. 253, n. e](cite:sf22/3); [Ecclus. 11:8, fol. 428v](cite:sf22/4); [Acts 25:10, nn. d–e. *The holie Bible conteynyng the olde Testament and the newe* (London: Richard Jugge, 1568): Deut. 17:12, fol. cxviij r, n. i](cite:sf22/5); Deut. 1:17, n. g; Ps. 82, argument; Ecclus. 11:7; [Acts 25:11, n. b. The 1611 leaves at every one of these places carry no interpretive note. The reading is a classification of the printed texts](cite:sf22/9); what it does not measure is which Bible the 1607 court read.
[^sf22_text]: [*The Bible and Holy Scriptures Conteyned in the Olde and Newe Testament* (Geneva: Rouland Hall, 1560): Deut. 17:12, fol. 88v, n. h ("So long as he is the true minister of God, and pronoūceth according to his worde")](cite:sf22_text/1); Deut. 1:17, n. m ("And you are his lieutenants"); Deut. 16:20, n. k ("The magistrat must constātly follow the tenor of the Lawe"); [Ps. 82:7, fol. 253, n. e ("No title of honour shal excuse you, but you shalbe subiect to Gods iudgement, and rēder a compt as wel as other men")](cite:sf22_text/4); [Ecclus. 11:8, fol. 428v ("Giue no sentence, before thou hast heard the cause")](cite:sf22_text/5); [Acts 25:10, nn. d–e ("Seing him self betrayed by the ambition of the iudge"; "It is lawful to require the defense of the Magistrate to mainteine our right"). *The holie Bible conteynyng the olde Testament and the newe* (London: Richard Jugge, 1568): Deut. 17:12, fol. cxviij r, n. i ("As long as he is the true minister of god and pronounceth agreeable to his worde")](cite:sf22_text/6); Deut. 1:17, n. g ("And man is but Gods minister"); Ps. 82, argument ("they must dye and make accompt of their doynges aswell as other, howe great in aucthoritie soeuer they be"); Ecclus. 11:7 ("Condemne no man before thou haue tryed out the matter"); [Acts 25:11, n. b (the appeal "the vttermost remedie for such as by wrong iudgement are oppressed"). The 1611 leaves at every one of these places carry no interpretive note. The reading is a classification of the printed texts](cite:sf22_text/10); what it does not measure is which Bible the 1607 court read.
[^sf22a]: [For the 1560 and 1568 notes, the note above in this section. Coverdale's Bible of 1535 and the Great Bible of 1540 print Deut. 1:17 and 17:12 without any note. The translators' own base text, the Bishops' folio of 1602 (Bodleian Arch. A b.18), prints no lettered note at Deut. 1:17 (fol. L4v), still prints note i at 17:12, "As long as he is the true minister of God, and pronounceth agreeably to his word" (fols. M5r–M5v), and keeps note l at 19:17 (fol. M6r)](cite:sf22a/1); the hand that made the 1611 rewrites the verses around them and leaves the notes standing. Of the fifteen Bishops' printings read, 1568 to 1602, the 1:17 gloss is printed in the folios of 1568, 1572, 1574, 1575 and 1578 and in no other, while the notes at 17:12 and 19:17 stand in every one.
[^sf22a_text]: [For the 1560 and 1568 notes, the note at chapter 1.4 § B on the Geneva and Bishops' glosses. *Biblia, the Bible, that is, the holy Scripture of the Olde and New Testament* (Myles Coverdale, 1535), Deut. i ("for the Iudgment is Gods") and Deut. xvij, fo. lxxxij ("the same shal dye"), the margins carrying cross-references only](cite:sf22a_text/1); [*The Byble in Englyshe* (Great Bible, Cranmer ed., 1540), Deut. i ("for the iudgement is Gods") and Deut. xvij, fo. lxxvj v ("that man shall dye"), text only, the pointing hands at the verses marking places where no note was printed. The translators' own base text, the Bishops' folio of 1602 (Bodleian Arch. A b.18), stands between: at Deut. 1:17 it prints no lettered note, only the cross-references "Leui. 19.15 / pro. 24.23," so the 1568's "And man is but Gods minister" had already left the text the translators worked from](cite:sf22a_text/2); at 17:12 it still prints note i, "As long as he is the true minister of God, and pronounceth agreeably to his word," and at 17:9 note g, "Who shall pronounce sentence according as the Priest shall instruct him, agreeably to Gods word," and the hand that made the 1611 strikes and rewrites vv. 9–12 around them ("aske" to "enquire," "to minister" struck, "euen that man," "Or, iudgment") and leaves note i standing on the leaf; Deut. 1:16–17 at fol. L4v, Deut. 17:8–13 at fols. M5r–M5v. At Deut. 19:17, fol. M6r, the 1602 likewise keeps the 1568's note l, "God is present, where his true ministers are," on the leaf where the hand rewrites vv. 15–20 into the 1611's words and leaves the note untouched; [the 1611 prints no note at the verse. At 16:18–20 neither the 1568 nor the 1602 prints a note. The Bishops' folio of 1572 still prints all three glosses: at Deut. 1:17, fol. xciii, n. g ("And man is but gods minister")](cite:sf22a_text/5); at 17:12, fol. Cii v, n. i ("As long as he is the true minister of God and pronounceth agreably to his worde"); at 19:17, fol. Ciii v, n. l ("God is present, where his true ministers are"); so the first of them left the Bishops' text between 1572 and 1602; the folios of 1584 (Deut. 1:17 at fol. 72 r and, in a second setting, fol. 76 r) and 1595 (fol. 74 r) print the verse without it, as do the quartos of 1569 (fol. 87 v) and 1576 (fol. 78 v) and the printing of 1577 (fol. 86 v), while the printings of 1574 (fol. 85 r), 1575 (fol. 84 v) and 1578 (fol. 85 r) still carry it; the gloss is printed in the folios of 1568, 1572, 1574, 1575 and 1578 and in no other Bishops' printing read, being absent from every printing with chapter-and-verse marginal references (1576, 1577, 1584, 1585, 1588, 1591, 1595, 1602) and from the quarto of 1569 with its reduced notes, the 1578 being the last printing read that has it, while the notes at 17:12 and 19:17 stand in every one of the fifteen Bishops' printings read, folio and quarto, from 1568 to 1602.

[^sf23_text]: [*Decretum Gratiani* C. 3 q. 9 cc. 1–6, in Emil Friedberg (ed.), *Corpus Iuris Canonici* I, cols. 529–30 (Leipzig 1879) (Gratian: "De accusatoribus uero uel testibus, quod in absentem uocem accusationis uel testificationis exhibere non ualeant, multorum auctoritatibus liquet."; c. 1 (Telesphorus), "Accusatori omnino non credi decernimus, qui absente aduersario causam suggerit, ante utriusque partis iustam discussionem."; c. 2 (Eleutherius), "Caueant iudices ecclesiae, ne absente eo, cuius causa uentilatur, sententiam proferant, quia irrita erit … Proditoris uero nec calumpnia, nec uox audiatur."; c. 3 (Calixtus), "quia per scripturam nullus accusari potest, sed propria uoce, et presente eo, quem accusare uoluerit, suam quisque agat accusationem."; c. 4 (Cornelius), "Omnia, que aduersus absentes in omni negotio aut loco aguntur aut iudicantur, omnino uacuentur, quoniam absentem nullus addicit nec ulla lex dampnat."; c. 5 (Marcellus), as quoted in text; c. 6 (Damasus), "non fore canonicum quemquam sacerdotum iudicare uel dampnare ante, quam accusatores canonice examinatos presentes habeat locumque defendendi accipiat"). Friedberg's notes attribute cc. 1–6 to Pseudo-Isidore and give the sources: c. 1 from the *interpretatio* to CTh 11.39.9](cite:sf23_text/1); c. 3 from the *interpretatio* to CTh 9.1.15 and the *Statuta ecclesiae antiqua*; c. 4 from Chalcedon, act. 10; c. 5 "petitum ex Act. ap. c. 25. v. 16."

[^sf24]: [C. 3 q. 7 c. 4 (Ambrose, on Ps. 118 *Beati inmaculati*), Friedberg I, col. 527, § 1 (the canon entire in the Notes).](cite:sf24/1)
[^sf24_text]: [C. 3 q. 7 c. 4 (Ambrose, on Ps. 118 *Beati inmaculati*), Friedberg I, col. 527 (rubric "Ille de uita alterius iudicet, qui non habet in se ipso quod puniat."; "Iudicet ille, qui ad pronunciandum nullo odio, nulla offensione, nulla leuitate ducatur. … § 1. Bonus iudex nichil ex arbitrio suo facit et domesticae proposito uoluntatis, sed iuxta leges et iura pronunciat, scitis iuris obtemperat, non indulget propriae uoluntati, nichil paratum et meditatum de domo defert, sed sicut audit, ita iudicat, et sicut se habet natura, decernit; obsequitur legibus, non aduersatur, examinat causae merita, non mutat. Discite iudices seculi, quem in iudicando tenere debeatis affectum, quam sobrietatem, quam sinceritatem. … § 3. In iudicando cordi magis est custodia ueritatis, quam obedientia uoluntatis.").](cite:sf24_text/1)
[^sf25]: [C. 3 q. 8 c. 1 (Zephyrinus, *ad Episcopos Siciliae*), Friedberg I, col. 529, § 1](cite:sf25/1); [the Roman Correctors' gloss on "leges seculi," printed by Friedberg from the Editio Romana, refers it to C. 7.45.14 (Justinian, a.d. 529), Krueger (ed.), *Codex Iustinianus* 684 (the canon, the gloss with its identification, and the Code's own rule at C. 7.48.4, Krueger at 686, are in the Notes).](cite:sf25/2)
[^sf25_text]: [C. 3 q. 8 c. 1 (Zephyrinus, *ad Episcopos Siciliae*), Friedberg I, col. 529 (rubric "Episcopus non nisi a pluribus audiatur et iudicetur."; "§ 1. Nullum namque eorum sententia a suo iudice non dicta constringat, quia et leges seculi idipsum fieri precipiunt. … § 4. Absens uero nemo iudicetur, 'quia et diuinae et humanae leges hoc prohibent.'")](cite:sf25_text/1); [Friedberg's note: "Caput Pseudoisid." The Roman Correctors' gloss on "leges seculi," printed by Friedberg from the Editio Romana, refers it to the Code, "libro septimo, titulo de sententiis et interlocutionibus omnium iudicum, lege penultima," and quotes "Cuius in agendo quis observat arbitrium, eum habere etiam contra se iudicem in eodem negotio non dedignetur": that is C. 7.45.14 (Justinian, a.d. 529), Krueger (ed.), *Codex Iustinianus* 684, the title having sixteen laws of which the fifteenth is Greek, so that the vulgate's "penultimate" is Krueger's fourteenth. The Correctors add that the words carrying the Code reference "non sunt in epistola Zephyrini, neque in vetustis Gratiani codicibus"](cite:sf25_text/2); [Friedberg prints the canon without them. The Code's own rule that "ne quemquam litigatorum sententia non a suo iudice dicta constringat" is C. 7.48.4, Krueger at 686.](cite:sf25_text/3)

The Star Chamber applied the talion by name two days before the Throckmorton hearing, against Robert Pie on 5 May 1602: Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 129–33; [Kesselring, 43 Law & Hist. Rev. at 710 n. 59.](cite:sf26/6)
[^sf26_text]: [C. 2 q. 3, Friedberg I, cols. 451–54 (Gratian: "Qua autem pena feriendi sint qui in accusatione deficiunt, canonum censura diffinit. Penam enim illati criminis cum dispendio existimationis excipient."; c. 1 (Gaius), "Si quis circa huiusmodi personas non probanda detulerit, auctoritate huius sanctionis intelligat se iacturam infamiae sustinere"; c. 2 (Damasus), "Calumpniator, si in accusatione defecerit, talionem recipiat."; c. 3 (Hadrian), "Qui non probauerit quod obiecerit, penam, quam ipse intulerit, patiatur."; Gratian, "Aliud est accusationem non inplere, et aliud conuicium non probare. Illud enim nulli sine abolitione, hoc omnibus licet."; c. 5 (Fabian), rubric "Conuicium non est pro accusatione habendum.", "Si quis iratus crimen cuilibet temere obiecerit conuicium non est pro accusatione habendum, sed permisso tractandi spatio id, quod iratus dixit, per scripturam se probaturum esse fateatur, ut, si forte resipiscens que per iracundiam dixit iterare aut scribere noluerit, non ut reus criminis teneatur.", Friedberg's n. 28: "Cap. Pseudoisid., desumtum ex Leg. Rom. Wisigoth. IX, 1, 3. interpret."; c. 8, from the *Liber Capitulorum*, rubric "De eo, quem penitet criminaliter accusasse."; then Gratian, "Notandum quoque est, quod, sicut in Digest. l. XLVIII. legitur, tit. de abolit. criminum," quoting D. 48.16.1 pr.–1 ("Accusatorum temeritas tribus modis detegitur … Nam si quidem ita pronunciauerit: 'Non probasti,' pepercit ei: sin autem pronunciauerit: 'Calumpniatus es,' condempnauit eum … Nam facti quidem questio in arbitrio est iudicantis: penae uero persecutio non eius uoluntati mandatur, sed legis auctoritati reseruatur."), § 3 as his own sentence ("Temeritas enim facilitatis ueniam continet, et inconsultus calor calumpniae uicio caret, et ob hoc nullam penam huic irrogari oportet."), and the Code on pardon: "Abolicio autem penam remittit, infamiam non tollit. Unde Inppp. Valens, Valentinianus, et Gracianus IX. lib. C. eod. titulo de generali abolicione dixisse leguntur: 'Indulgentia, patres conscripti, quos liberat notat, nec infamiam criminis tollit, sed penae gratiam facit.'"). The Code passage (C. 9.43) is read here as Gratian quotes it. The Star Chamber applied the talion by name two days before the Throckmorton hearing: on 5 May 1602 Coke, as Attorney, informed against Robert Pie, a barrister of the Inner Temple who had procured a fellow barrister's indictment for highway robbery on his own evidence](cite:sf26_text/1); Pie's pleas of 25 Edw. III c. 4, "that he should not be condemned before bill and answer," and of 20 Edw. I were "held … frivolous," and the whole court condemned the offence "By the law of God and the Civil Law, oculus pro oculo, dens pro dente, manus pro manu, etc., and also per legem talionis … he has made Justice a murderer and robber," sentencing him to 1000 marks, the pillory, the loss of both ears and perpetual imprisonment: Hawarde, *Les Reportes del Cases in Camera Stellata*, at 129–33 (Baildon's note citing State Papers, Dom., Eliz., 284, no. 14). Hudson's treatise lists the case among solitary practices proceeded against "as in conspiracy," "so was Pye for malicious seeking to indict Mr Meyrick of the Temple for felony"; [no bill survives, the interrogatories and depositions being TNA, STAC 5/A48/31, STAC 5/A24/17 and STAC 5/A25/31, *Attorney General v. Robert Pye*: Kesselring, 43 Law & Hist. Rev. at 710 n. 59 (quoting Hudson, "Star Chamber," 106).](cite:sf26_text/3)
[^sf27]: [Fourth Lateran Council (1215), c. 38 = X 2.19.11, Friedberg II, cols. 313–14 (rubric "Iudex debet habere notarium vel duos viros idoneos, qui scribant acta iudicii")](cite:sf27/1); [the same canon in J. D. Mansi, *Sacrorum Conciliorum Nova et Amplissima Collectio* XXII, cols. 1023–26 (Venice 1778), Latin and Greek. The English reception is Otho (1237), *De Sigillis Authenticis*, in Lyndwood, *Provinciale* (Oxford 1679), at 67, with Lyndwood at 277–78 and 312](cite:sf27/2); [and Lateran IV c. 8 = X 5.1.24 (*Qualiter et quando*), Friedberg II, cols. 745–46, is the canon law's own statement of the premise the *Floyd* leaf shares and of the remedy it refused.](cite:sf27/3)
[^sf27_text]: [Fourth Lateran Council (1215), c. 38 = X 2.19.11, Friedberg II, cols. 313–14 (rubric "Iudex debet habere notarium vel duos viros idoneos, qui scribant acta iudicii"; "Quoniam contra falsam assertionem iniqui iudicis innocens litigator quandoque non potest veram negationem probare, quum negantis factum per rerum naturam nulla sit directa probatio, ne falsitas veritati praeiudicet, aut iniquitas praevaleat aequitati, statuimus, ut tam in ordinario iudicio quam extraordinario iudex semper adhibeat aut publicam, si potest habere, personam, aut duos viros idoneos, qui fideliter universa iudicii acta conscribant … Et omnia sic conscripta partibus tribuantur ita, quod originalia penes scriptores remaneant, ut, si super processu iudicis fuerit suborta contentio, per hoc possit veritas declarari, quatenus hoc adhibito moderamine sic honestis et discretis deferatur iudicibus, quod per improvidos et iniquos innocentium iustitia non laedatur.")](cite:sf27_text/1); [the same canon in J. D. Mansi, *Sacrorum Conciliorum Nova et Amplissima Collectio* XXII, cols. 1023–26 (Venice 1778), Latin and Greek, headed "De scribendis actis ut probari possint." In England the legatine constitution of Otho (1237), *De Sigillis Authenticis*, opens from the absence of the officer the canon presupposes, "Quoniam Tabellionum usus in Regno Angliæ non habetur," and puts the authentic seal in his place (*Constitutiones Legatinae*, in Lyndwood, *Provinciale* (Oxford 1679), at 67, with John of Athon's gloss; Lyndwood's own gloss at 76 refers to it). Lyndwood at 277–78, on Archbishop Edmund's constitution requiring two or three men in every deanery to denounce the public excesses of prelates and clerks, counts among the denunciations the ecclesiastical forum will hear one "de Judice faciente oppressionem," and holds that the denouncer, unlike the accuser, need not subscribe ("Sed nunquid talis Denunciator debet se subscribere, sicut ille qui accusat? Dic quod non")](cite:sf27_text/2); [at 312 Stephen Langton's constitution forbids archdeacons and their officials to compel purgation on their apparitors' suggestion or to be "Judices & Actores" in their own cause, the gloss adding "Regulare enim est, quod in causa sua propria nemo potest esse Judex." Lateran IV c. 8 = X 5.1.24 (*Qualiter et quando*), Friedberg II, cols. 745–46, the prelate as a target set up for the arrow, "qui quasi signum sunt positi ad sagittam," with an inquiry ex officio in which the superior acts "non tanquam idem sit accusator et iudex," the accused present ("Debet igitur esse praesens is, contra quem facienda est inquisitio"), the articles exposed to him, and the witnesses' very names published ("non solum dicta, sed etiam nomina ipsa testium sunt ei … publicanda"), is the canon law's own statement of the premise the *Floyd* leaf shares and of the remedy it refused.](cite:sf27_text/3)
