[^sf31]: Part 5, chapter 5.4; [the canon's Roman form is CTh 2.2.1 = C. 3.5.1 ("neminem sibi esse iudicem uel ius sibi dicere debere"), Krueger at 243, and D. 5.1.17 ("iniquum est aliquem suae rei iudicem fieri"), Mommsen & Krueger I, at 166.](cite:sf31/2)

### C. Hobbes, 1651: The Same Sources Read to the Opposite Conclusion

Hobbes is the link between the three eras this chapter joins, and he is a link of a particular kind: a witness, not a channel. Four facts make him so.

*Contemporaneity.* *Leviathan* appeared in 1651, forty-four years after the report and ten after the Star Chamber's abolition, while the rule still stood in Coke's *Reports* and before any American court existed to receive it. Its author was the century's theorist of absolute sovereignty. If the age's law contained an officer's exemption from the subject's complaint, he had every reason to state it.

*Same sources, opposite conclusions.* Hobbes reasons from the three sources the *Floyd* rule names and from nothing else. The sovereign's command makes law; Scripture is history of lawful practice; Cicero is maxim; Justinian is the catalogue of a law's sources. He reaches *Floyd*'s result for one person only, the sovereign's own, and refuses it for the judge at every point. The forum stays open: a controversy between the party judged and the judge has a judge, and the judge is a subject (Section B). The sentence is no law to a successor.

> [A]ll Judges, Soveraign and subordinate, if they refuse to heare Proofe, refuse to do Justice: for though the Sentence be Just, yet the Judges that condemn without hearing the Proofes offered, are Unjust Judges, and their Presumption is but Prejudice; which no man ought to bring with him to the Seat of Justice, whatsoever precedent judgements, or examples he shall pretend to follow.[^sf6]

\noindent "No man is supposed to be Punisht, before he be Judicially heard, and declared guilty," and the punishment of the innocent breaks three laws of nature, in the Latin "actiones hostiles."[^sf7] The partial judge "doth what in him lies, to deterre men from the use of Judges," and the judge's fitness is "to devest himselfe of all feare"; fear disqualifies the judge, it does not immunize him.[^sf8] The word for what *Floyd* conferred is "impunity," and it is always a vice of the commonwealth, never a right of an officer; "immunity" in its legal sense does not occur in either text.[^sf9] His late dialogue on the common law, written against Coke's *Institutes* and *Reports*, keeps the same forum. Since judges "may err," it belongs to the King "to give Remedy to them, that by the Ignorance or Corruption of a Judge shall suffer Damage"; and the statute of 4 Henry IV that quiets judgments "until the Judgment be undone by Attaint or by Error" he reads as confirming that a judgment stands only until "the Judgment be undone by Error, or Corruption proved."[^sf45][^sf45_text]

*Vocabulary the receiving order could read.* Hobbes states the ancient rules in the idiom of the law of nature rather than the statute book, and the founding generation had its own idiom for the same propositions: the hearing, the jury, the petition, no King. Whether the founders took these from him is a question of fact, and the shelf answers it. Four of them named him in print, and each named him to reject him. Wilson opened the *Lectures on Law* by refusing the definition of law as the command of a superior, traced it from Blackstone through Pufendorf to Hobbes, and quoted and rejected Hobbes's ground for dominion in irresistible power; Adams placed Hobbes among the Restoration writers for "simple monarchy and absolute power" against "the doctrine, that laws, and not men, should govern"; Hamilton, in 1775, found in Hobbes's state of nature a doctrine that made moral obligation "the mere contrivance of politicians"; Jefferson, in 1816, lamented a French author's adopting "the principle of Hobbes, that justice is founded in contract solely."[^sf10][^sf10_text] Not one of them names Hobbes on judicature. The founders' Hobbes is the theorist of sovereignty they repudiated; the Hobbes of chapters 23, 26 and 28 they did not cite. He therefore enters this chapter as the 1651 witness only, never as a channel of influence, and the founders enter as witnesses that his sovereign was refused at the founding. The refusal sharpens the comparison. The American doctrine that a court cannot be answered by the subject it has judged is not something the founders took from Hobbes, since Hobbes himself kept that forum open and condemned the judge who condemns unheard; and the "energy" the founders did want in an office was, as chapter 2.5 § D shows, a structural property of its design, paired with answerability rather than freed from it.

*The other contemporary voice.* Bacon, the King's Solicitor from 1607 and afterwards Chancellor, first published his essay on judicature in the *Essaies* of 1612 and enlarged it in the *Essayes* of 1625, the text quoted here. The judge's office is "*Ius dicere*, and not *Ius dare*"; "One Foule Sentence, doth more Hurt, then many Foule Examples. For these doe but Corrupt the Streame; The other Corrupteth the Fountaine"; "Patience and Grauitie of Hearing, is an Essentiall Part of Iustice"; and "Let not the Iudge meet the Cause halfe Way; Nor giue Occasion to the Partie to say; His Counsell or Proofes were not heard."[^sf43a] Judges are to be "Lions, but yet Lions vnder the Throne."[^sf43] Four years before the enlarged essay was printed he had been sentenced by the Lords, on his own confession, for taking money as a judge, to a fine of forty thousand pounds, imprisonment at the King's pleasure, and incapacity for office, and he wrote to the Lords that he was glad of one thing: "That hereafter the Greatness of a Judge or Magistrate shall be no Sanctuary or Protection to him against Guiltiness."[^sf44][^sf44_text] The two contemporaries who wrote on the judge, the Chancellor from the bench and the theorist from his study, agree with the roll and against the leaf.

*What Hobbes does not supply.* No "immunity," no record destruction, no *Floyd*, no Star Chamber. His silence is part of the finding. The theorist who had every reason to state an officer's exemption if the age's law contained one stated none, and stated its opposite as a law of nature.

---

## 1.5 Blackstone on the Crown, 1765–69: The King Can Do No Wrong, the Premise Beneath It, and the Rule America Received

Begin with the book through which the rule reached the generation that would receive it. Blackstone's third book opens with a taxonomy that is analytically neutral and that nothing in the founding disturbs: wrongs are either private — "an infringement . . . of the . . . rights belonging to individuals, considered as individuals" — or public, "a breach and violation of public rights and duties, which affect the whole community, considered as a community."[^iv1] The categories are sound; American law uses them everywhere. The prerogative lies not in the taxonomy but in the rule Blackstone states in the seventeenth chapter of the same book, and in the premise he states it on.

"That the king can do no wrong, is a necessary and fundamental principle of the English constitution"; no action lies against the sovereign, "for who shall command the king?"; the law cannot furnish a remedy "without . . . setting up some superior power with authority to call him to account."[^iv3] The rule rests on one premise: the King is the principal, and he is unanswerable because no superior exists to call him to account. Blackstone's own arrangement keeps the case apart: the courts redress wrongs "between subject and subject," he writes, "reserving such injuries or encroachments as may occur between the crown and the subject, to be distinctly considered hereafter."[^iv4] The sovereign shielded from suit is not a twentieth-century construction. It is chapter 17 of one book, and it stands on the King-as-principal.

Blackstone states the rule at its strictest, and the temptation, indulged by the modern doctrine that claims this pedigree, is to read the statement as the practice. It was not. The rule shielded the sovereign alone: whatever is amiss in public affairs, Blackstone says on the same page, "is not chargeable personally on the king; nor is he, but his ministers, accountable for it to the people,"[^iv3] and the sovereign's servants answered for their own wrongs in the ordinary courts, as chapter 1.6 shows in the year the judges' tenure was secured and chapter 4.3 in the century that followed. The chapter supplies the premise and not the practice, and the gap matters to what follows. The premise is what chapter 1.6 and Part 2 watch England and then the Founding repudiate; the practice, the servant answerable, is what the reception left behind.[^ef3]

[^ef3]: The reception-side argument, with the full apparatus, is Part 2, chapters 2.3–2.4.

[^iv1]: [3 William Blackstone, *Commentaries* \*2.](cite:iv1/1)
[^iv3]: [*Id.* \*254–\*255.](cite:iv3/1)
[^iv4]: [*Id.* \*115–\*116 ("between subject and subject . . . reserving such injuries or encroachments as may occur between the crown and the subject").](cite:iv4/1)

## 1.6 England Repudiates the Court and Its Premise, 1641–1701

Within a generation of the resolution, and fifteen years before the report that carried it was printed, the court that decided the case no longer lawfully existed: England had abolished it. The Court of Star Chamber was dissolved by statute in 1641, *An Act . . . for taking away the Court commonly called the Star Chamber*, 16 Car. 1, c. 10, its jurisdiction "cleerely and absolutely dissolved taken away and determined" as of 1 August 1641, on the ground — recited from Magna Carta and the medieval due-process statutes — that its "Censures and Decrees . . . have by experience beene found to be an intollerable burthen to the Subjects and the meanes to introduce an Arbitrary Power and Government," its judges having "undertaken to punish where no Law doth warrant."[^vr11][^vr11_text] The charter's chapter had been pleaded in the court itself in May 1603, by a recusant prosecuted *ore tenus* who, Hudson records, delivered into court the *nullus liber homo* in his defence; the Lord Keeper answered with the disjunctive, "per legale judicium parium suorum, which was by indictment, or some writ original, vel per legem terræ," the court's own process being the law of the land, and Hawarde's note of the sitting has him hold that the court "hathe aunciente warraunte & authoritie bothe by Common & statute lawe to proceede in this kinde."[^vr11a][^vr11a_text] What Parliament recited in 1641 as the ground of abolition, the court had heard from the bar and overruled thirty-eight years before. The tribunal that authored the immunity rule was thus condemned and dissolved by its own Parliament, for want of due process, thirty-four years after *Floyd* and a century and a third before the Declaration of Independence — the same Act sweeping away the very Council in the Marches of Wales that had assembled the case against the executed man.

The same Parliament reached the judges' record itself. On 26 February 1640/1 the Lords ordered the Exchequer record of the judgment in *Hampden's Case*, and "the several Rolls in each several Court of Kings Bench, Common-Pleas, Exchequer, Star-Chamber, and Chancery, wherein the Judges extrajudicial Opinions in the Cases made touching Ship-money be entered," brought into the House, "and that a Vacat shall be made in the Upper House of Parliament of the said several Records."[^vr23a] The clerk's memorandum records it done:

> *Vacatur istud Recordum & Judicium . . . per considerationem & judicium Dominorum Spiritual. & Temporal. in Curia Parliam. & irrotulamentum ejusdem cancelatur* [This Record and Judgment are vacated . . . by the consideration and judgment of the Lords Spiritual and Temporal in the Court of Parliament, and the enrolment of the same is cancelled] . . . And that all the Rolls be rased cross with a Pen, and subscribed with the Clerk of the Parliaments hand. All which was accordingly done in open Court.[^vr23]

\noindent Carrying up the Commons' impeachment of the judges that summer, Edward Hyde asked the Lords how the guilty could have been punished "when the Judges themselvs have been Delinquents," and said of the six who had upheld the levy that "upon vulgar fears" they had "delivered up the precious Forts they were trusted with."[^vr24] The record that "import[ed] verity in [itself]" was cancelled by the hand of the sovereign the rule had reserved to protect; and the fault charged against the judges was fear.

That abolition was a beginning, not an end. The same statute that dissolved the Star Chamber opened a constitutional reformation which the next century and a half carried to completion, and at each stage the reform repudiated a feature of the court just abolished. The inquisitorial *ex officio* oath — the compelled self-accusation the Star Chamber had forced on John Lilburn in 1637, and which *Floyd*'s own decree reproduced when it imprisoned the complainant until he should "confess[] his abettors" — fell with the court, and its repudiation "[was] implanted . . . into the Bill of Rights" as the privilege against self-incrimination.[^vr12] The Glorious Revolution of 1688 and the Bill of Rights of 1689 condemned the suspending and dispensing prerogative and the revived prerogative tribunals of the later Stuarts, and declared that "cruell and unusuall Punishments" ought not be "inflicted" — a clause occasioned by a sentence for the perjury that had procured wrongful executions, and carried almost verbatim into the Eighth Amendment.[^vr13][^vr13_text] The Act of Settlement of 1701 secured what the Star Chamber had most conspicuously denied — judges holding office "during good behaviour" rather than at the sovereign's pleasure, the model the Framers wrote into Article III, and whose absence the Declaration of Independence would indict in the King's having "made Judges dependent on his Will alone."[^vr14]

The same year fixed where the cost of an officer's wrong then fell. In *Lane v. Cotton*, decided in the Easter term of 1701 before Holt, Turton, Powys and Gould, a subject whose Exchequer bills had been taken from a letter inside the London post office sued the two Postmasters-General; three judges held the head of the office not answerable for his servants' default and the servant answerable, Holt dissenting because "the care of the whole is committed to him, and the rest are but his deputies" (the headnote is quoted at chapter 4.3).[^vr29][^vr29_text] The case is not a judge's, and that is its use here: in the year the judges' tenure was secured, the Crown's immunity for its servants' wrongs was paid for by the servant's own exposure, and the subject who could not reach the Postmaster could reach the receiver. What the reception would carry across the water as one rule the English law of 1701 held as two, the sovereign shielded and the servant answerable; Part 4, chapter 4.3 returns to what became of the second half.

[^vr13]: [Bill of Rights 1689, 1 W. & M. sess. 2, c. 2 ("\[t\]hat excessive Baile ought not to be required nor excessive Fines imposed nor cruell and unusuall Punishments inflicted"), *Statutes of the Realm* VI, at 142–43](cite:vr13/1); [the petitioning prelates it recites are the Seven Bishops of chapter 1.3 § E and chapter 1.4 § B. The cruel-and-unusual clause passed almost verbatim into U.S. Const. amend. VIII](cite:vr13/2); [it was occasioned by the sentence in *Titus Oates's Case* (1685). *The Memoires of Titus Oates, Written for Publick Satisfaction* 14 (Thomas Graves, London 1685) ("His Sentence, though it seem Severe, is much less than he deserves"). *See* The Federalist No. 84 (Hamilton) (naming the lineage; the citation documents the founding generation's knowledge of the descent, not an endorsement of bills of rights, which No. 84 opposes).](cite:vr13/3)
[^vr13_text]: [Bill of Rights 1689, 1 W. & M. sess. 2, c. 2 (declaring "the pretended Power of Suspending of Laws or the Execution of Laws by Regall Authority without Consent of Parlyament" to be "illegall," and "\[t\]hat excessive Baile ought not to be required nor excessive Fines imposed nor cruell and unusuall Punishments inflicted"). The same Act, *Statutes of the Realm* VI, at 142–43, recites among the late King's offences "Committing and Prosecuting diverse Worthy Prelates for humbly Petitioning to be excused from Concurring to the said Assumed Power," and declares "That it is the Right of the Subjects to petition the King and all Commitments and Prosecutions for such Petitioning are Illegall" and "That the Freedome of Speech and Debates or Proceedings in Parlyament ought not to be impeached or questioned in any Court or Place out of Parlyament"](cite:vr13_text/1); [the petitioning prelates are the Seven Bishops of chapter 1.3 § E and chapter 1.4 § B. The cruel-and-unusual clause passed almost verbatim into U.S. Const. amend. VIII](cite:vr13_text/2); it was occasioned by the sentence in *Titus Oates's Case* (1685) — a punishment for the perjury that had procured the wrongful executions of those Oates accused. A pamphlet of the same year, hostile to Oates, called the sentence severe while holding it too light: *The Memoires of Titus Oates, Written for Publick Satisfaction* 14 (Thomas Graves, London 1685) ("His Sentence, though it seem Severe, is much less than he deserves; our English Law-makers, as never imagining the possibility of so unexampled an Offender, having provided no Punishment equal to the Demerits of such unprecedented Villany"). *See* The Federalist No. 84 (Hamilton) (naming the lineage — "Such was MAGNA CHARTA . . . Such was the PETITION OF RIGHT . . . Such, also, was the Declaration of Right presented by the Lords and Commons to the Prince of Orange in 1688, and afterwards thrown into the form of an act of parliament called the Bill of Rights" — though in service of the argument that such "stipulations between kings and their subjects" have "no application to constitutions professedly founded upon the power of the people"; the citation documents the founding generation's knowledge of the descent, not an endorsement of bills of rights, which No. 84 opposes).
[^vr14]: Act of Settlement 1701, 12 & 13 Will. 3, c. 2 (judges' commissions to run *quamdiu se bene gesserint* — "during good behaviour" — removable only upon the address of both Houses), ending the Stuart practice of tenure at the royal pleasure. The provision is the acknowledged model of the Article III tenure guarantee. *See* The Federalist No. 78 (Hamilton) ("The experience of Great Britain affords an illustrious comment on the excellence of the institution." — the essay's closing sentence); [U.S. Const. art. III, § 1](cite:vr14/2); Declaration of Independence (1776) ("He has made Judges dependent on his Will alone, for the tenure of their offices, and the amount and payment of their salaries.").

---

# Part 2 — American Reception and Genealogy, 1761–2024

*Part 2 follows the rule across the Atlantic and traces its American genealogy from the bottom up, from what the opinions themselves cite. Chapter 2.1 shows the Founding cutting all three bars and enumerating official impunity secured by a sham proceeding among the marks of a tyrant. Chapter 2.2 asks what crossed in 1791 and what was received in 1869, and finds that the rule crossed by citation, without the record and without the King to whom its operative clause sent the subject's plea; it states the book's first ground, the transfer of the plea to a King the Republic does not have, and asks whose decisions, preceding American jurisprudence, count as supporting American stare decisis, finding that English decisions are evidence of the common law received and not precedents of the American hierarchy. Chapter 2.3 traces the chain of the modern doctrine and finds a constitutional source at no node. Chapter 2.4 reads the entry by citation in 1869 to 1896 and the selected pedigree that set the liability cases aside, a pedigree that descends, through Coke's own* Marshalsea*, from* Bonham's Case*, the decision that forbids it. It reads the bar's record of 1951 to 2000, in which no brief for an official names* Floyd v. Barker *and the question of the source was put once, by the Hunzikers, two farmers without counsel, and the two occasions on which the source was put in issue before the Court. What it shows is an entry made by citation rather than adjudication, the question about the source asked from the bar and never answered as such. Chapter 2.5 reaches the apex in the presidential decisions of 1982 and 2024, finds it resting on a same-day pair that cannot supply each other's authority, audits the apex opinion's authorities and finds seven inverted, reads the fearlessness rationale against the judicial oath Parliament fixed in the summer of 1868 and against the Federalist's own definition of energy, and finds at every node one hypothetical, the official deterred by exposure, credited for the official and never tested against the citizen.*



---

## 2.1 The Revolution and the Founding, 1761–1791

The reformation England began in 1641 crossed the Atlantic and became a revolution. The general writs of assistance that James Otis attacked in 1761 as "the worst instrument of arbitrary power . . . that ever was found in an English law book" produced the Fourth Amendment, and the Court would later read the Fourth and Fifth Amendments together against that "recent history of the controversies on the subject, both in this country and in England."[^vr15] And the Declaration of Independence, submitting to a candid world the facts that "define a Tyrant," enumerated the Star Chamber's own marks among them: the deprivation of "the benefits of Trial by Jury," the "transporting us beyond Seas to be tried," the making of "Judges dependent on his Will alone," and — most exactly — the "protecting [of officials], by a mock Trial, from punishment for any Murders which they should commit on the Inhabitants of these States."[^vr20] Official impunity secured by a sham proceeding is the *Floyd* pattern precisely: a judge shielded for a wrongful execution by a court that would not examine the record. The founding charter named that pattern an act of tyranny fit to justify revolution. And the statute behind that grievance had justified itself in the doctrine's own voice. The Administration of Justice Act of 1774 — the "Murder Act," in the colonists' name for it — removed the trials of officials charged with capital offenses out of the province on the stated ground that officials must not "be discouraged from the proper Discharge of their Duty, by an Apprehension, that in case of their being questioned for any Acts done therein, they may be liable to be brought to Trial . . . ."[^vr17] The fear rationale thus appears twice on the pre-constitutional record as power's own justification for official impunity — in 1607 as the Star Chamber's reason, and in 1774 as Parliament's, a parallel confession and not a link in the chain, whose citations run through *Floyd v. Barker* and never through the statute — and the founding answered it both times: the first court was abolished, the second statute was enumerated as tyranny. What later courts would receive as a neutral structural prediction is, on the record the founding left, the tyrant's stated ground.

The bearing of that record is structural, not rhetorical. The American Bill of Rights and the immunity rule descend from the same root and run in opposite directions. The Fourth, Fifth, Sixth, Seventh, and Eighth Amendments, the Article III tenure the Act of Settlement modeled, and the habeas guarantee the Suspension Clause preserves are, severally and together, the codified repudiation of what the Star Chamber was and did — jury-less, oath-compelled, prerogative-dependent, punishing "where no Law doth warrant." *Floyd v. Barker* is the thing they repudiate. To receive that court's rule as the foundation of official immunity is therefore not merely to build on a court that England dissolved; it is to draw the rule that exempts the official from account out of the very tribunal whose abolition is the origin of the American due-process tradition, and to do so in the face of a founding charter that enumerated impunity-by-sham-process among the marks of a tyrant. The reception reached past the Constitution's own origin to the antithesis it was written to end.

The American constitutional order dismantled the three bars one by one. **The second bar fell** with the Crown: there is no King; questioning an official is not lèse-majesté; sovereignty resides in the people, and officials answer *downward* to them through Article III.[^v5] **The first bar fell** when verity by status gave way to verity by process: official acts became examinable — by grand jury and petit jury, under the rules of evidence, on direct appeal, by habeas corpus. No act is true because of who performed it; every act is testable by process.[^v6] **The third bar fell** with the right-remedy principle: "where there is a legal right, there is also a legal remedy," and the officer-suit framework exists precisely so that the sovereign's immunity never leaves the injured party without a forum.[^v7] The whole accountability structure of the Founding, Article III, the grand jury, the rules of evidence, the Suspension Clause and the individual damages remedy, is the negation of the three bars, built to ensure the subject is never routed to a forum that cannot grant relief. And it was a deliberate reception: the common law was taken up only so far as applicable to the new order — "only that portion which was applicable to their situation" — and a monarchical immunity premised on answerability to the Crown was not applicable to a republic.[^v8]

[^vr15]: [James Otis's 1761 argument against the writs of assistance is recounted — with his characterization of the writ as "the worst instrument of arbitrary power, the most destructive of English liberty, and the fundamental principles of law, that ever was found in an English law book" — in *Boyd v. United States*, 116 U.S. 616, 625 (1886), which construed the Fourth and Fifth Amendments against "the contemporary or then recent history of the controversies on the subject, both in this country and in England."](cite:vr15/1)
[^vr17]: [Administration of Justice Act 1774, 14 Geo. 3, c. 39, pmbl. & § 1, in 12 *Statutes at Large* 75–76 (1776) (orthography as printed; removing to another colony or to Great Britain the trials of persons charged with "Murther, or other capital Offence" committed "in the Execution of his Duty as a Magistrate, for the Suppression of Riots, or in the Support of the Laws of Revenue," where the governor certifies that "an indifferent Trial cannot be had within the said Province"). One of the Coercive ("Intolerable") Acts](cite:vr17/1); the colonial epithet was the "Murder Act," and the Declaration's "mock Trial" grievance is its standard referent.
[^vr20]: [The internal standard: *Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1307 (questioning a judge for supposed corruption "extends to the annihilating of a record, or of any judicial proceedings before them"; the defacement order on the same page — the note at chapter 1.2 § A). The Declaration grievances this book quotes follow the National Archives' transcription of the engrossed text: "the benefits of Trial by Jury"](cite:vr20/1); "transporting us beyond Seas to be tried" ("for pretended offences"); "He has made Judges dependent on his Will alone, for the tenure of their offices, and the amount and payment of their salaries"; the mock-Trial clause, whose engrossed antecedent is "them" (the quartered troops) — this book's bracket "[of officials]" disclosing the substitution; and "every act which may define a Tyrant." How the receiving legal order measures the destruction of the accusation record — as felony and forfeiture of office, as ground to presume the destroyed proof true, as a due-process violation and a denial of access to courts, and against the era's own proof-taxonomy besides — is set out, source by source, in Part 3, chapter 3.3 and its apparatus, and the era's own proof taxonomy at chapter 1.3 § B.
[^v5]: [*Prohibitions del Roy*, 12 Co. Rep. 63, 65, 77 Eng. Rep. 1342, 1343 (1607) (*Rex non debet esse sub homine, sed sub Deo et lege*)](cite:v5/1); [U.S. Const. art. III.](cite:v5/2)
[^v6]: [U.S. Const. amend. V (grand jury; Due Process)](cite:v6/1); [*id.* amends. VI, VII](cite:v6/2); art. I, § 9, cl. 2 (Suspension Clause); Fed. R. Evid.
[^v7]: [*Marbury v. Madison*, 5 U.S. (1 Cranch) 137, 163 (1803)](cite:v7/1); [*Ex parte Young*, 209 U.S. 123 (1908)](cite:v7/2); [*Larson v. Domestic & Foreign Commerce Corp.*, 337 U.S. 682, 689–90 (1949).](cite:v7/3)
[^v8]: [*Van Ness v. Pacard*, 27 U.S. (2 Pet.) 137, 144 (1829).](cite:v8/1)

## 2.2 What Crossed in 1791, and What Was Received in 1869

### A. The Transfer Thesis: A Rule That Needs a King Crosses to a Republic Without One

Part 1 has read the inheritance entire: the three bars fixed in the 1607 texts (chapter 1.3 § A); the cause in which they were fixed by destroying the complaint rather than answering it, so that the doctrine carries the name of the man the decree fined and imprisoned (chapter 1.2);[^ih7] the premise on which Blackstone rested the rule (chapter 1.5); the two limits the rule's own text stated, and the forum it reserved and never opened to a subject (chapter 1.3 §§ B, D–E); and England's repudiation of the tribunal, its methods and its premise between 1641 and 1701 (chapter 1.6). Two consequences belong here rather than there, because they are American.

The American line revived the rule anyway, and not as a defense external to the courts but as a determinant of their power: by converting the jury's fact questions into a judge's threshold ruling (chapter 3.4 § C), the immunity built on that abolished court decides whether the Seventh Amendment jury the Constitution commands within the Article III judicial power ever sits. A prerogative court dissolved for arbitrary process now supplies the rule by which an Article III court withholds the jury the Constitution preserves.

The common law crossed the Atlantic on the condition chapter 2.1 quoted, that the receiving generation adopted only the portion applicable to their situation, and this rule fails the condition on its face. Its operative clause does not bar the judge's answer; it transfers it, from the ordinary forum to "the King himself," who by the report's own account is to deliver justice to all his subjects and delegates that power to his judges.[^ih14a] The clause names a receiver who adjudicates, and the American order has none; nor had the receiver ever answered where the rule was made, so that the rule was overruled, in the American idiom, by silence. That argument, the first of the book's two grounds, is made once, in the two paragraphs that follow, on the record of chapter 1.3 § E. What in fact happened at the threshold is where this book's genealogy begins: in 1869, *Randall v. Brigham* received the rule by citation, without the record, under the victim's name — and the chain built on that reception kept the bar and dropped the receiver.[^ih14]

**The first ground: the transfer, and the plea unanswered.** The rule's own words do the work. A judge "shall not be drawn in question before any other Judge, for any surmise of corruption, except before the King himself," being "only to make an account to God and the King, and not to answer to any suggestion in the Star-Chamber"; and the King "is *de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects" and delegates that power to his judges (chapter 1.3 §§ A–B).[^vst4a] The clause does not extinguish the plea. It removes it from the court that could hear it and lodges it with the one person who, on the rule's own theory of delegation, could hear it: the source of the judicial power himself. For the transfer to be law rather than a bar, the receiver must receive, and the rule admits no substitute forum and no substitute remedy, so that its whole operation consists of one act, the King's answer. The record of that forum is in chapter 1.3 §§ D and E. No subject's plea against a judge is recorded answered before the King himself. Where subjects approached the forum from below, the petition itself was prosecuted, with fine, prison, the pillory and submission in every court; and the one recorded moment at which the King spoke to the question, on receiving a Chancery suitor's book against the Lord Chancellor in 1618, was to say that his judges "are subject to his account only, and to none else upon earth" and to hand the book to his Attorney General, the court that sentenced the petitioner naming the re-hearing he had sought "a Commission of Grace, not of Right."[^vst4b] The forum was convened once from above, in 1616, against the rule's own author, on a grievance of the King's and no subject's; it was refused from below in 1618; and the court that made the rule was abolished in 1641 (chapter 1.6). A rule whose only operation is the King's answer, performed for no subject, has been refused by the rule's own apex. No opinion reversing it was needed and none exists; the refusal is in the pattern, and the pattern was complete a generation before the court ceased to exist. That is a reading of the record, stated as such: no instrument says the King would not hear a subject; the record says he did not, and the rule's logic says what that means.

American jurisprudence then received the rule without the one thing that made it a rule rather than a bar. The transfer clause names a receiver who adjudicates. Article III names none: the source of the judicial power here is the Constitution itself, which cannot be sat before, take an account or examine a judge, and which delegates the power to courts so that the answer is given in court (chapter 3.1 § A). The common law crossed on the receiving order's own condition, that the founding generation adopted "only that portion which was applicable to their situation" (chapter 2.1)[^vst4c], and this rule fails the condition twice, on its face for want of a receiver and in fact as refused where it was made (§ B below). The receiving courts did not perform the transfer badly; they had nowhere to transfer to. What they did instead, in 1869 and after, was keep the bar and drop the receiver (below), and the modern doctrine's offer of impeachment and the political process as the remedy is not the reserved forum performed badly but a substitute invented because that forum cannot exist here (chapter 3.1 § A).

What stood ready for reception in 1791, then, was not a rule awaiting adoption but a rule whose court had been abolished, whose record had been destroyed and whose receiver did not exist. The condition on which the common law crossed had content: a monarchical immunity premised on answerability to the Crown presupposed a Crown to answer to, a live forum above the immunized official, and a subject whose wrongs belonged to someone else. The Founding supplied none of these; its constitutional structure — the jury guarantees, the privilege against self-incrimination, the tenure of judges, the prohibition on cruel and unusual punishments — is, as Part 1, chapter 1.6 showed, the codification of England's own repudiation of the court that made the rule. On the English record this book has read, the rule arrived at the threshold with its tribunal abolished, its report's root sentence inverted in print, its cause file mutilated by its own decree, and its rationale twice answered as the tyrant's stated ground.

It crossed anyway, by the 1869 citation chapter 2.4 § A examines, and the record it crossed without was not unreachable; it was unasked for.[^ec2] When the affirmance of the first American case to adopt the rule was argued in 1811, counsel objected that the Star Chamber had been abolished and that the report's "annihilating of a record" clause had been left out of the quotation, and one senator dissented that judges answerable to the King "as their principal and constituent" could not be so answerable in a republic — objections lodged on the record and answered by none of the fourteen who affirmed.[^ec4] No American decision cites the affirmance; the English report *Bradley* quotes does, and lists it pinned to the pages of the dissent. A century and a half later the deepest reach any Justice has made toward the source stopped at the same place: dissenting in *Pierson v. Ray*, Justice Douglas called the doctrine "a more sophisticated manner of saying 'The King can do no wrong,'" cited *Floyd and Barker* by name to the Star Chamber and quoted its report — and asked nothing of what the court had done in the cause.[^ec5] When the rivalry itself was at last argued to the Court as a party's position — in *Pulliam v. Allen* (1984), that Coke had expanded the immunity "for the purpose of giving one type of rival court an advantage over another" — the Court adopted it as history, wrote that "Coke and his colleagues of the Star Chamber" had declared the King's Bench judges immune "in competing courts," and stopped there: its Star Chamber is Coke's seat, not a tribunal whose process is examined.[^ec6] The receiving tradition has read the print with care, on both sides. It has not read the file. The baseline defence written a century and a half later rests where *Randall* rested: it grounds absolute judicial immunity at common law around 1871 on *Bradley*, on *Randall*'s "as old as the law" and on Bishop's "however erroneous, mistaken, or even corrupt," cites no English judicial-immunity case, and so restates the reception as its defence.[^ec2a] The same canvass finds that for officers other than judges the malice limb survived into the American common law of 1871, an immunity defeated by proof of an improper motive; it was the judges' branch of that corpus that removed it.[^ec2b]

### B. Whose Decisions Count: English Cases as Evidence of the Common Law, Not as American Precedent

Two chapters of Part 3 leave one question standing. Chapter 3.5 § C shows that the doctrine weighs a Star Chamber resolution of 1607 against two centuries of American decision without stating the standard it applies, and that the one standard the receiving order stated, applicability to the situation of the receiving generation, is a standard the doctrine has never applied to *Floyd* and the rule cannot meet. Chapter 3.3 measures the founding act by the receiving system's own law. What both chapters presuppose and neither decides is the prior question: whose decisions, preceding American jurisprudence, count as supporting American stare decisis, and whether an English decision that fails the reception condition was ever law here at all. This section answers it; chapter 1.4 has read the leaf and the sources it names.

Before the limits chapter 3.5 § C draws, a prior question: by what standard does the doctrine weigh an English decision against an American one on the same question? It has never stated one. It has applied one at every node, and the two can be laid side by side. English decision is weighed by age and recital, without its record: *Floyd* received as "as old as the law"; *Kemp*, *Scott*, and *Groenvelt* cited for the rule they announced while the cases on the same pages that confined it were ruled past; the record of the founding cause never read by any court in the chain. American decision is weighed the other way: Congress wrote "every person," and the Court presumed it did not mean judges; a Justice read the legislative record and showed the presumption false; *Pulliam* let a citizen through on the doctrine's own history, and Congress narrowed it within twelve years; the Hunzikers, two farmers without counsel, put the source in issue, and a footnote declined. The measure the chain applies is therefore this: an English recital outweighs an American dissent on the record, an English word outweighs an American enactment, and English antiquity outweighs the American reception condition chapter 2.1 quoted, which is the one standard the receiving order actually stated. The vocabulary conceals the choice. Two centuries of the Court's own decisions on official liability are a body of law made by judicial decision; the doctrine calls that body "precedent" and reserves the name "common law" for the English inheritance, so that its own dissents, narrowings, and enactments read as departures from the common law rather than as the common law's development on American soil. A doctrine that cannot state the standard it applies has not measured anything. It has chosen — and the standard it displaced is the only one the receiving order ever stated, and the one, as Part 1, chapter 1.3 showed, that the rule fails on its face and had already failed at its source.[^vf7]

The defence that the doctrine is settled mistakes what kind of thing is said to be settled. Common law is the body of judicial decisions that interpret de jure law according to the principle of stare decisis. A node is one such decision. Stare decisis is a court's rule of adherence to its own corpus of decision: that it should generally stand by what has been decided. The first is a corpus; the second is adherence to the corpus; neither supplies the other's authority. When the immunity is defended as "recognized," "well settled," or "supported by our history," the sentence has the form of a stare decisis claim — stand by what was decided — but the thing pointed at is not an American decision on the question. It is the English inheritance, received only so far as applicable to the new order, and at the far end of the chain a decree the receiving court never read. Stare decisis cannot reach that. It binds a court to its own prior holdings and those of its superiors; it does not convert an unreceived foreign decree into a holding by citing it. A citation is not a decision, and stare decisis attaches to decisions. The two defences thus answer different objections and neither answers the one put: the common-law defence answers whether there is a pedigree, and produces a citation; the stare decisis defence answers whether this Court has decided the question, and produces a recital. Between them lies the question this book asks — whether the rule was ever received as law here — and neither reaches it. Stare decisis stands by what was decided. The immunity stands on what was cited.[^vi10]

*The definitions are ancient.* The paragraph above stated the definitions this section applies: common law is the corpus a jurisdiction's courts make by decision, and stare decisis is a court's adherence to its own corpus; chapter 4.1 keeps the same terms for the comparison. What this section adds is that the definitions are not modern. Justinian's *Institutes*, the elementary book of the Roman law as Coke's generation could read it, says of the Emperor's judicial decisions that "whatever the Emperor settles by rescript, or decides in his judicial capacity, or ordains by edicts, is clearly a statute," and in the same title that a penalty inflicted on an individual does not go beyond the person and is not to be followed as a precedent.[^sf1] The Code says it as a command to judges. In 529 the Emperor forbade any judge or arbiter to follow a decision he thought wrongly given, or the sentences of the highest prefects and magnates, "non enim, si quid non bene dirimatur, hoc et in aliorum iudicum uitium extendi oportet, cum non exemplis, sed legibus iudicandum est" (for if something is not well decided, that ought not to be extended into a fault of other judges as well, since judgment is to be given by laws, not by examples), and required all his judges to follow "ueritatem et legum et iustitiae … uestigia," the footsteps of truth, of the laws, and of justice.[^sf1a] Hobbes, in 1651, says the same of every judge, sovereign and subordinate: "though the Sentence of the Judge, be a Law to the party pleading, yet it is no Law to any Judge, that shall succeed him in that Office"; "No mans error becomes his own Law; nor obliges him to persist in it"; and "all the Sentences of precedent Judges that have ever been, cannot all together make a Law contrary to naturall Equity."[^sf2] The sixth-century compilation and the seventeenth-century theorist say what the definitions say: a sentence binds its parties; it becomes law only by the lawgiver's will; an unjust one never does.

[^sf1]: [Justinian, *Institutes* 1.2.6, in J. B. Moyle (trans.), *The Institutes of Justinian* 5 (2d ed., Oxford 1889) (what the Emperor determines "has the force of a statute, the people having conferred on him all their authority and power by the *lex regia*"; but "\[s\]ome of these of course are personal, and not to be followed as precedents," such as "a penalty inflicted for individual wrongdoing," which "do not go beyond the particular person").](cite:sf1/1)
[^sf1a]: [C. 7.45.13 (Justinian to Demosthenes, praetorian prefect, a.d. 529), in Paul Krueger (ed.), *Codex Iustinianus* 684 (editio maior, Berlin 1877): "Nemo iudex uel arbiter existimet neque consultationes, quas non rite iudicatas esse putauerit, sequendum, et multo magis sententias eminentissimorum praefectorum uel aliorum procerum (non enim, si quid non bene dirimatur, hoc et in aliorum iudicum uitium extendi oportet, cum non exemplis, sed legibus iudicandum est), nec si cognitionales sint amplissimae praefecturae uel alicuius maximi magistratus prolatae sententiae: sed omnes iudices nostros ueritatem et legum et iustitiae sequi uestigia sancimus." Krueger's margin records the Byzantine receptions, *Basilica* 9.1.79 and *Peira* 51.33, on which see Part 5, chapter 5.4.](cite:sf1a/1)
[^sf2]: [Thomas Hobbes, *Leviathan* ch. 26, at 144–45 (London 1651) (W. G. Pogson Smith ed., Oxford 1909, at 213–15). The sentences run in the order p. 144 ("No mans error …"; "all the Sentences of precedent Judges …") and p. 145 ("though the Sentence of the Judge, be a Law to the party pleading …"). Latin: *Leviathan* cap. 26, at 133 (Amsterdam 1668): "In Iudiciis ergo Praejudicia valere non debent, neque sententiam iniquam excusare possunt."](cite:sf2/1)

*Coke's ground, refused by his contemporary.* The second paragraph of this section showed that the vocabulary which calls the American corpus "precedent" and reserves "common law" for the English inheritance conceals a choice. The ground of that choice is older than the vocabulary, and a contemporary of the rule rejected it by name. Coke's own account of what makes the common law law, as Hobbes quoted it from the commentary on Littleton, was that it is "an *Artificiall perfection of Reason, gotten by long study, observation, and experience*."[^sf3a] Hobbes quoted the phrase, cited the leaf, and answered it:

> For it is possible long study may encrease, and confirm erroneous Sentences: and where men build on false grounds, the more they build, the greater is the ruine: and of those that study, and observe with equall time, and diligence, the reasons and resolutions are, and must remain discordant.[^sf3]

\noindent The refusal matters to this section's question because it refuses the only ground on which a 1607 resolution could bind a court that never sat in its hierarchy: not the will of any lawgiver the receiving order recognizes, but the accumulated study of the profession that produced it. Hobbes's answer restates the definitions above. Study confirms error as readily as truth, and a sentence is law to its parties only. The chain of American decisions that cites the 1607 rule and reads no record (above, and chapter 2.4) is the structure Hobbes describes: building on a ground no one has examined, and growing more confident with each course laid.

[^sf3]: [Hobbes, *Leviathan* ch. 26, at 139–40 (London 1651) (Pogson Smith ed. at 207), the margin reading "Sir Edw. Coke, upon Littleton, Lib. 2. Ch. 6. fol. 97. b." Coke is named three times across the 1651 English and the 1668 Latin texts](cite:sf3/1); *Floyd*, the Star Chamber, and the *Reports* are never named. On Coke's definition and its uses, see Allen D. Boyer, *Understanding Authority and Will: Sir Edward Coke and the Elizabethan Origins of Judicial Review*, 39 B.C. L. Rev. 1 (1998); Michael Lobban, *The Common Law Mind in the Age of Sir Edward Coke*, Amicus Curiae, no. 33 (2001); Ian King, *The Legal Writing of Sir Edward Coke, the Anglo-Saxons, and Lex Terrae*, 39 The Seventeenth Century 329 (2024); and, on the prefaces to the *Reports*, Antonia Smith, *The Prefaces to Sir Edward Coke's Reports and Modern Historical Jurisprudence*, 54 Victoria U. Wellington L. Rev. 613 (2023).
[^sf3a]: [Hobbes, *Leviathan* ch. 26, at 139–40 (London 1651) (Pogson Smith ed. at 207), the margin naming "Sir Edw. Coke, upon Littleton, Lib. 2. Ch. 6. fol. 97. b."](cite:sf3a/1)

*The three tests, applied.* The core of this section is four tests, each read across four columns: the 1607 court, Hobbes in 1651, the modern doctrine, and Rome. Read down, each column is consistent with itself. Read across, the modern doctrine agrees with the 1607 court on every test and disagrees with both Hobbes and Rome on every test. That is a finding about sources, not about motive. The argument on each test is made elsewhere in this book, and one sentence each is enough here.

*Whose decision binds.* The chain received the rule on its antiquity alone and hardened it by re-citing the same English line (above, and chapter 2.4 § A); against the *Institutes* and Hobbes's chapter 26 it treats a sentence as a law to every successor, and a foreign court's sentence, refused by its own apex, as a law to a hierarchy that did not exist when it was given (above).[^sf48]

*Which way fear runs.* The one rationale of the modern doctrine at every node is that officials must act "fearlessly," a rationale that entered from a 1949 tort opinion and was recited until it sounded like law (chapter 2.5 § C). Rome aimed the law's fear at the reckless litigant: the *Institutes* close on the pains taken by those who had charge of the law "to deter men from reckless litigation," by "money fines, the employment of the oath, and the fear of infamy";[^sf32] Hobbes made the partial judge the cause of the subject's flight from the courts and required of the good judge that he divest himself of all fear (chapter 1.4 § C); and the judicial oath Parliament fixed by statute puts the English judge on the same side, swearing to do right "without Fear or Favour" (chapter 2.5 § C). Coke's own account of the Star Chamber put the fear where Rome did, on the offender, and named the judges' fear of great men as the mischief the court existed to meet: its end was that "*Ut poena ad paucos, metus ad omnes perveniat*, without respect of persons, be they publique or private, great or small," and its matter "oppression, and other exorbitant offences of great men, (whom inferiour judges and jurors (though they should not) would in respect of their greatnesse be affraid to offend)."[^sf46] The 1607 court alone, and the doctrine that cites it, put the fear in the judge and the deterrent on the complainant.

*Where the plea goes.* The modern rule keeps the bar and has no receiver: under Article III there is no King before whom the plea against a judge may be brought, and the doctrine has never named a substitute (chapter 3.1 § A; Conclusion to Parts 2 and 3). Rome made the judge pay the party and rewarded the subject who accused him (Part 5, chapter 5.3); Hobbes gave the party a judge (chapter 1.4 § C); English law before the rule gave him a petition against the justices' delays, an action for unjust delay, and a King's Bench to correct the errors of every judge (chapter 1.4 § B); and the receiving order's own answer is the jury and the court (chapter 3.4 § C), which the doctrine's threshold ruling removes.

*The word for the thing.* The 1607 report has no word "immunity"; its phrase is the account to God and the King (chapter 1.4 § A). Hobbes has "impunity," a vice. Rome has *impunitas* for the wrongdoer who escapes, and for the sovereign alone the position that he cannot be made a defendant, which is Hobbes's Latin too, "ne Reus quidem fieri," said of the sovereign and never of the judge.[^sf33] "Immunity" as a right of the officer is the doctrine's own word, not its sources'.

[^sf32]: [Justinian, *Institutes* 4.16 pr., in Moyle (trans.), *The Institutes of Justinian* 204 (2d ed., Oxford 1889) ("great pains have been taken by those who in times past had charge of the law to deter men from reckless litigation"; the means "money fines, the employment of the oath, and the fear of infamy"). The Roman remedies against the judge himself, the *iudex qui litem suam fecit* at both grades and the unjust judgment as an injury to the litigant (D. 5.1.15.1; Inst. 4.5 pr., 4.4.1), are set out in Part 5, chapter 5.3.](cite:sf32/1)
[^sf33]: [Hobbes, *Leviathan* (1668), cap. 18, at 88 ("non potest qui Summam habet Potestatem à Civibus occidi, aut quomodocunque Iure puniri. Nam qui Injuriam facere non potest, ne Reus quidem fieri, multo minus condemnari potest"), the Latin of ch. 18, at 90 (1651), quoted in chapter 1.4 § B. For *impunitas* as the wrongdoer's escape, D. 48.19.5 pr. ("satius enim esse impunitum relinqui facinus nocentis quam innocentem damnari"), quoted in the Roman-law paragraph of chapter 1.4 § B.](cite:sf33/1)
[^sf46]: [Coke, 4 Inst. 63 (cap. 5), quoted in the King paragraph of chapter 1.4 § B](cite:sf46/1); [and *id.* at 66 (the 1644 pagination, bracketed in the 1797 margin; the 1797 print's own running head reads †65), "Quod arbitrio judicis relinquitur, non facile trahit ad effusionem sanguinis," the court's rule against its own discretion in punishment.](cite:sf46/2)
[^sf48]: [The rule's American reputation at the end of the nineteenth century is stated by Ardemus Stewart, *The Irresponsibility of the Judiciary*, 43 Am. L. Reg. & Rev. 383, 383 (1895) ("one of the oldest and most firmly settled rules of the common law"; "If he had jurisdiction, he is secure"). The reason is drawn from Hawkins and from a Scottish appeal, the report's page is misprinted (Stewart's "12 Co. Rep. 223"), and the leaf's own sources are not mentioned.](cite:sf48/1)

*A rule received by citation to sources already answered.* English decisions preceding American jurisprudence are evidence of the common law received, admissible under the reception condition; they are not precedents of the American hierarchy, and stare decisis does not run to them. *Floyd* fails the condition twice: it had no receiver, and it was refused at its source.

The sources add three things, each a finding about sources. First, *Floyd* is not an English common-law decision at its own leaf: for the rule it cites no English case, and the one English instance it names, Thorpe's judgment, records a Chief Justice tried and condemned for corruption. Its sources are the King's person, a Roman poet's line through a Roman orator, and Scripture as a prerogative court glossed it. Second, on each of those sources the receiving order spoke in terms, and chapter 3.3 § B states how. Third, a contemporary of the rule, reasoning from the same three sources and holding the most absolute theory of sovereignty the century produced, reached the opposite result on every point but the sovereign's own person, and the founders who read him rejected even that. A rule whose three sources the receiving order each answered, and whose contemporary theorist of sovereignty refused it, fails the reception condition not twice but at every point where the condition can be applied. What the American chain calls adherence to precedent, in *Floyd*'s case, is adherence to a foreign court's gloss on sources the American order had already answered.

The conclusion can be put in one sentence, with its reach stated in the same breath. The modern American doctrine of judicial immunity stands alone: against the ancient sources its founding case cites, each of which spoke against it in terms; against the English record before and around that case, which at the roll condemned the Chief Justice who broke his oath and at the leaf cites no English decision for the rule; and, on the classification made in Part 4 of this work, against the de jure law of the orders there compared. The first two are measured at the record in this section and in chapter 1.4. The third is a classification of de jure law, the text as written; what it does not measure, for any order but the American, is that order's common law, the congruency between the two, and its adherence to stare decisis, and for England this work measures those in part. The doctrine is not the common law of England received. It is a prerogative court's resolution of 1607 that cited no English case, received into the American order through Coke's report without examination of its sources, and called precedent by the chain that followed. Chapter 3.6 states what a court faithful to its own precedents may do with that finding; Part 4 states the four terms by which the comparison is made and the measurement not made; Part 5 gives the older record's answer.

[^ih14]: [*Floyd v. Barker*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1306–08 (the judicial-acts limit; the reserved forum operating — "some were banished, and some were fined and imprisoned")](cite:ih14/1); [*Van Ness v.
