## 2.3 The Chain in One Column: The American Doctrine Node by Node, and No Constitutional Source at Any Node

This chapter traces the immunity chain from the bottom up, building the genealogy only from what the opinions themselves cite, and lays it out in a single column. Chapters 2.4 and 2.5 then examine the two places where the column is decided: the entry, where a 1607 rule was received by citation rather than adjudication and has since been put in issue from the bar without answer; and the apex, where the doctrine reached the presidency on a base the genealogy shows cannot bear it.

A doctrine grounded in constitutional text can be tested from a foundation; this one cannot, because it names none. The immunity doctrine does not begin in Article II, or in any clause, or even in an American court reasoning from constitutional first principles. It enters United States law in **1869**, and it enters not by adjudication but by an act of citation: *Randall v. Brigham* took the rule and its rationale from Coke's report of the 1607 Star Chamber decision in *Floyd v. Barker*, that "the superior judges are the delegates of the king . . . to him alone are they accountable," and examined no record and produced no constitutional provision.[^ii1] That citation is the doctrine's American source, the unexamined point of entry to which the bottom-up trace returns in chapter 2.4 § A, where the entry is read in full.

The fear rationale, however, had entered American law earlier still — sixty years before *Randall*, and in a form that shows that a transplant freed of every textual limit was a choice, not a necessity. In *Coffin v. Coffin* (1808), construing the speech-or-debate privilege of a written state constitution, Chief Justice Parsons grounded legislative immunity expressly in fear: the privilege exists "to support the rights of the people, by enabling their representatives to execute the functions of their office *without fear of prosecutions, civil or criminal*," preserving "the free and unreserved exercise of the duties of a representative, *unawed by the fear of legal prosecutions*."[^iicf1] That is the fearlessness rationale (chapter 2.5 § C) in its first American voice, and it already counted the citizen's lost remedy as the admitted price — "a private benefit must submit to the public good." But *Coffin* did two things the later doctrine discarded. It grounded the privilege in *constitutional text* — the enacted speech-or-debate clause — rather than in a borrowed maxim of antiquity. And it set the privilege's *ceiling* in that same text, holding that in a republic of written constitutions privilege may be claimed only as the people conferred it, "for a struggle for privileges, in this state, would be a contest against the people, to wrest from them what they have not chosen to grant."[^iicf2] The fear rationale was thus first received under discipline: bounded by the text that granted it and disclaimed wherever the text did not reach. What entered the *judicial* line in 1869 was the same rationale stripped of both limits — fear without a textual ground and without a textual limit. *Coffin* is therefore not a node in the chain the table below traces but the alternative beside it, the proof that the rationale could have been held to text, and was not. The American reception then proceeds branch by branch — legislature (1808), judiciary (1869), executive (1896), Presidency (1982–2024) — each step further from the textual discipline the first observed, as chapter 2.5 § C traces in detail.

What begins in **1951** is narrower and later: the reading of that received immunity *into* a federal statute whose text makes "[e]very person" who deprives another of constitutional rights "liable to the party injured."[^ii1b] In *Tenney v. Brandhove*, the Court declined to find that 42 U.S.C. § 1983 had abrogated the common-law immunity of legislators, reasoning that "[w]e cannot believe that Congress — itself a staunch advocate of legislative freedom — would impinge on a tradition so well grounded in history and reason by covert inclusion in the general language."[^ii2] The ground is a tradition and a presumption about intent; the opinion concedes that congressional power even to reach the legislators at issue "would be a big assumption."[^ii3] Justice Douglas, in dissent, named the cost: "If a committee departs so far from its domain to deprive a citizen of a right protected by the Constitution, I can think of no reason why it should be immune."[^ii4] He lost, and the structure of that node set the pattern for the statutory line above it: a received common-law immunity read into a remedial statute on a presumption, over a dissent that named the citizen's constitutional injury.

The presumption was not merely unsupported; it was affirmatively false, and a dissenting Justice proved it from the legislative record sixteen years later. In *Pierson v. Ray*, the Court read judicial immunity and a police good-faith defense into the same statute, reasoning that "[t]he legislative record gives no clear indication that Congress meant to abolish wholesale all common-law immunities."[^ii5] Justice Douglas answered from the 1871 debates: "every member of Congress who spoke to the issue assumed that the words of the statute meant what they said and that judges would be liable," with the Act's opponents objecting *because* it stripped immunity.[^ii6] His textual point was unanswerable — "'[e]very person' would mean every person, not every person except judges"[^ii7] — and again he lost. Nor was the text the Court construed the whole text Congress had passed: § 1 of the Act of 1871 made the wrongdoer liable "any such law, statute, ordinance, regulation, custom, or usage of the State to the contrary notwithstanding," words dropped when the Revised Statutes of 1874 recompiled the section; two recent studies read the clause as displacing the common-law defences the Court would later read in, and the omission as changing nothing.[^ii7nc] The doctrine's foundational premise, in short, is contradicted by the very record it claims to rest on.

From there the chain ascends by policy. *Scheuer v. Rhodes* generated the balance of competing values that later opinions would quote, resting executive immunity on the "danger that the threat of . . . liability would deter [an official's] willingness to execute his office" and on the maxim that "it is not a tort for government to govern."[^ii8] *Wood v. Strickland* extended the qualified immunity to school officials on "common-law tradition . . . and strong public-policy reasons."[^ii9] None of these decisions produced constitutional text; each rested on the prior link and a prediction about official behavior.

Three months before *Butz*, the judicial line had moved the other way, and the two decisions of the same Term are the doctrine's two sides. In *Stump v. Sparkman* (1978) an Indiana circuit judge had approved a mother's petition to have her fifteen-year-old daughter sterilized on the day it was presented, in an ex parte proceeding with no hearing, no notice to the daughter, and no guardian ad litem; the court of appeals had held the approval "an illegitimate exercise of his common law power because of his failure to comply with elementary principles of procedural due process."[^iicb1] The Court reversed. It took from *Bradley* the rule that immunity fails only in the "clear absence of all jurisdiction," and it added the instruction that has governed every judicial-immunity case since: "the scope of the judge's jurisdiction must be construed broadly where the issue is the immunity of the judge," so that a judge "will not be deprived of immunity because the action he took was in error, was done maliciously, or was in excess of his authority," and "is absolutely immune from liability for his judicial acts even if his exercise of authority is flawed by the commission of grave procedural errors."[^iicb2] The citizen's constitutional interest — the due-process holding below — survives in the reports only in dissent: Justice Stewart's "[a] judge is not free, like a loose cannon, to inflict indiscriminate damage whenever he announces that he is acting in his judicial capacity," and Justice Powell's naming of "the central feature of this case — Judge Stump's preclusion of any possibility for the vindication of respondents' rights elsewhere in the judicial system."[^iicb3] The history the Court recited had been contested before it: amici had told the Court, eleven weeks earlier, that the common-law immunity was "somewhat uneven," confined in England to superior courts only by an Act of 1848, and adopted by thirteen states by 1871 while six held judges liable for malice; the opinion does not mention the brief (chapter 2.4).[^iicb23] *Stump* is the node at which the *Bradley* exception was fixed: the jurisdiction on which liability had turned since *Marshalsea* (chapter 2.4) was henceforth to be read "broadly" precisely where its breadth decides whether the judge answers.

*Butz v. Economou* (1978) is the turning point, and it deserves emphasis because it is the one node where the citizen's side is named in constitutional terms — and prevails. Rejecting absolute immunity for most federal executives, the Court reasoned that to grant it would mean "a suit under the Constitution could provide no redress to the injured citizen," would "seriously erode the protection provided by basic constitutional guarantees," and would leave officials free to infringe "such important personal interests as liberty, property, and free speech."[^ii10] The citizen's constitutional interest is not merely acknowledged; it is the operative reason the immunity is *limited*. *Butz* is the furthest point the citizen's constitutional interest reached in the chain; after it, that interest recedes and survives only in dissent.

The citizen's interest recedes as the doctrine rises to the apex office. In *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* (1982), the Court announced absolute presidential immunity from civil damages for official acts, grounding it in "Article II interests" and in *Spalding v. Vilas* (1896), and producing no Article II text.[^ii11] The injured citizen, a constitutional protagonist in *Butz* four years earlier, became "a regrettable cost," set against "broad public interests" and "the effective functioning of government"; the "sufficient" substitute for a remedy was said to be impeachment, the press, Congress, and the vigilance of the electorate — checks on the *office*, nothing for the *person*.[^ii12] Justice White, joined by three others, called the result what it was: "a reversion to the old notion that the King can do no wrong."[^ii13] The citizen-protective view that commanded a majority in *Butz* was now a four-vote dissent. The same day, *Harlow v. Fitzgerald* recast qualified immunity in "objective" terms, and in the sentence that did so conceded that the recasting was only essentially one: "By defining the limits of qualified immunity essentially in objective terms, we provide no license to lawless conduct."[^ii14a] Objectivity admits no degrees. A standard essentially objective is objective as a statement is mostly true, and the adverb is the kind of qualifier the Method's § C teaches the reader to watch, one the later restatements shed (chapter 2.5 § C). The same decision removed from the jury a question the Court conceded was one "of fact that some courts have regarded as inherently requiring resolution by a jury" (chapter 3.4 § C).[^ii14] And the standard it defined, a reasonable official who did not know that a right was clearly established, cannot fit the office the companion decision governs: the President is charged to take care that the laws be faithfully executed and holds the Department of Justice to that end, so that *Harlow*'s reasonable official who did not know the law has no counterpart in the office (chapter 2.5 § A; chapter 3.5 § D), which is why the apex opinion of 2024, invoking *Harlow* for the immunity's existence, never applied its standard to the President. *Clinton v. Jones* (1997) is the exception that proves the rule: a sitting President enjoys no immunity from civil suit for unofficial conduct — but even there the citizen's interest is framed as a procedural "interest in bringing the case to trial," and the holding turns on the official's burden being speculative on the record, not on the citizen's right.[^ii15] And in *Trump v. United States* (2024), the culmination, the doctrine reached absolute immunity for "core" acts and at least presumptive immunity for "official" acts at the "outer perimeter," while forbidding the use of immune acts even as evidence; accountability appears all but only in dissent: the root "accountab-" occurs forty-five times in Justice Jackson's dissent, on all but one of its twenty-one pages, five times in Justice Sotomayor's, and once in the opinion of the Court, there describing the broader immunity the Court rejects.[^ii16]

Between the 1982 pair and *Clinton*, the judicial line produced the one modern decision that let a citizen through, and then the one act of Congress the chain contains. In *Pulliam v. Allen* (1984) a Virginia magistrate had jailed two men, unable to post the bail she set, on charges that carried no jail term; the Court held, five to four, that judicial immunity bars neither prospective injunctive relief nor the attorney's fees that follow it, because Congress meant § 1983 "to protect the people from unconstitutional action under color of state law, 'whether that action be executive, legislative, or judicial.'"[^iicb4] *Pulliam* is also the one modern decision that went to the root. Its history opens at *Floyd and Barker* (1607), quotes the report's "annihilating of a record" passage as "the theory upon which the concept of judicial immunity was built," and examines no record — the reception described in chapter 2.2, performed again in 1984.[^iicb5a] The history was the respondents' own, argued from two 1980 law-review studies, and the Court took it exactly as far as narrowing the doctrine's reach (chapter 2.4).[^iicb5]

Congress answered in 1996. Section 309 of the Federal Courts Improvement Act wrote into § 1983 that "in any action brought against a judicial officer for an act or omission taken in such officer's judicial capacity, injunctive relief shall not be granted unless a declaratory decree was violated or declaratory relief was unavailable," and into § 1988(b) that such an officer "shall not be held liable for any costs, including attorney's fees, unless such action was clearly in excess of such officer's jurisdiction."[^iicb6] It is the only node in the chain that was enacted rather than received, and the hearing that produced it opened with an 1868 English Exchequer judge quoted without his name and with the charge that *Pulliam* "disregards four centuries of unbroken precedent" (chapter 2.4).[^iicb7] Two features of the enactment bear on the chain. The damages immunity *Pierson* presumed remains where *Pierson* left it, in no statute; what Congress enacted was a limit on the one remedy *Pulliam* had allowed. And the limit carries the *Bradley* exception — fees survive where the act was "clearly in excess of such officer's jurisdiction" — so that the only immunity Congress has ever written turns, as the tradition did, on jurisdiction rather than status.[^iicb6a]

The chain, laid out, reads in a single column:

| Year / Case | Immunity created or moved | Antecedent claimed → produced | Constitutional source | The citizen's constitutional interest |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1607 *Floyd v. Barker* | English judicial immunity by sworn status; the three bars | crown prerogative ("delegates of the king") | **None** (English) | — |
| **1869 *Randall v. Brigham*** | **the American entry** — immunity received "as old as the law" | Coke's report of *Floyd* (1607); citation, not adjudication | **None** | — |
| 1896 *Spalding v. Vilas* | transplanted to executive officers | English *judicial* immunity (*Scott v. Stansfield*) | **None** | — |
| 1951 *Tenney* | Legislative immunity read into § 1983 | "tradition" + "we cannot believe Congress" → presumption | **None** | named in dissent (Douglas), lost |
| 1967 *Pierson* | Judicial + police immunity into § 1983 | no "clear indication that Congress meant to abolish" → presumption refuted by the 1871 record | **None** | concededly unconstitutional arrest; officers win |
| 1974 *Scheuer* | Executive qualified immunity; the balance | common law → policy ("not a tort for government to govern") | **None** | reduced to assumed cost |
| 1975 *Wood* | QI extended to school officials | tradition + "public-policy reasons" | **None** | operates only as the cutoff |
| 1978 *Stump* | Judicial immunity for an ex parte sterilization order — no notice, no hearing; "grave procedural errors" immunised | *Bradley* 351–52 → jurisdiction "construed broadly where the issue is the immunity of the judge" | **None** | due-process holding below reversed; named only in dissent (Stewart + 2; Powell) |
| 1978 *Butz* | QI for federal executives; rejects absolute | the *Scheuer* balance | **None** | **the furthest point for the citizen: names and limits immunity** |
| 1982 *Nixon v. F.* | Absolute presidential immunity (civil) | "Article II interests" + *Spalding* | **None** | demoted to "regrettable cost"; White + 3 dissent |
| 1982 *Harlow* | "Objective" QI; subjective prong removed | common law + policy (efficiency) | **None** | jury fact-question removed |
| 1984 *Pulliam* | (Exception) no immunity from prospective injunctive relief or § 1988 fees | *Floyd* (1607) read from the print; the 1871 Congress's purpose | — | wins, 5–4: § 1983 reaches state action "whether . . . executive, legislative, or judicial" |
| 1996 Pub. L. 104-317 (amending § 1983) | Congress limits *Pulliam*: no injunction against a judicial officer absent a violated or unavailable declaratory decree; no fees unless "clearly in excess of . . . jurisdiction" | the 1989 hearing: Kelly C.B. (1868) quoted unnamed; "four centuries of unbroken precedent" | **None** | the *Pulliam* plaintiffs, jailed on non-jailable charges, cited by the opposition; overridden |
| 1997 *Clinton v. Jones* | (Exception) no immunity, unofficial acts | — | — | wins as a *procedural* interest |
| 2024 *Trump v. U.S.* | Absolute/presumptive presidential immunity | *Nixon v. F.* + "Article II" + *Youngstown* + *McDonnell* | **None** | appears **only in dissent** |

Every cell in the fourth column reads the same. That column is the bottom-up argument.

What stands in that column's place, node after node, is a recital of age. The recitals can be laid out in the same way, each with what it names as the ground of the age it claims:

| Year / Case | The recital | What it names for the age |
|---|---|---|
| 1869 *Randall* | "This doctrine is as old as the law"; "maintained by a uniform course of decisions in England for centuries, and in this country ever since its settlement" | *Floyd and Barker*, "said as long ago as 1608," from Coke's report; no decision named |
| 1872 *Bradley* | "a general principle of the highest importance"; "the settled doctrine of the English courts for many centuries, and has never been denied, that we are aware of, in the courts of this country"; Kent's "a deep root in the common law" | *Floyd and Barker*, "reported by Coke, in 1608"; *Yates v. Lansing* (N.Y. 1810) |
| 1896 *Spalding* | "the same general considerations of public policy and convenience which demand for judges of courts of superior jurisdiction immunity" | the judges' immunity, carried to the heads of departments |
| 1951 *Tenney* | "taproots in the Parliamentary struggles of the Sixteenth and Seventeenth Centuries"; "a tradition so well grounded in history and reason" | the Bill of Rights of 1689 and the state constitutions, for legislators |
| 1967 *Pierson* | "Few doctrines were more solidly established at common law than the immunity of judges …, as this Court recognized when it adopted the doctrine, in *Bradley*"; "this settled principle of law" | *Bradley* |
| 1974 *Scheuer* | "The modern concept owes much to the elaboration and restatement of Coke and other judges of the sixteenth and early seventeenth centuries" | Holdsworth; *Floyd v. Barker*, placed in the King's Bench |
| 1976 *Imbler* | "roots extending to the earliest days of the common law"; "The common-law rule of immunity is thus well settled" | *Floyd v. Barker* (1608); *Yates v. Lansing*; *Bradley*; for prosecutors, five lower-court cases of 1933 to 1949, three of them federal (n. 21) |
| 1978 *Butz* | *Bradley*'s "settled doctrine of the English courts for many centuries" | *Bradley* |
| 1978 *Stump* | "The governing principle of law is well established and is not questioned by the parties"; "this long-established principle" | *Bradley*; *Randall*; *Pierson* |
| 1980 *Owen* | "a tradition of immunity … so firmly rooted in the common law"; "so well grounded in history and reason" | *Pierson*; *Tenney* |
| 1982 *Harlow* | the immunity of legislators and of judges "now is well settled" | *Eastland*; *Stump* |
| 1982 *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* | "rooted in the constitutional tradition of the separation of powers and supported by our history"; "taken for granted for nearly two centuries" (Burger, C.J., concurring) | Story's *Commentaries* of 1833; "our history" |
| 1991 *Mireles* | *Bradley*'s "general principle of the highest importance" | *Bradley* |
| 1993 *Buckley* | the prosecutor's immunity "well settled" | *Imbler* |
| 2012 *Rehberg* | "long recognized"; "long-established absolute immunity"; "well grounded in history and reason"; "an entrenched feature of our § 1983 jurisprudence" | *Tenney*; *Burns*; *Pierson* |
| 2024 *Trump* | "rooted in the constitutional tradition of the separation of powers and supported by our history" | *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* |
| the sovereign's side: 1858 *Beers*, 1890 *Hans*, 1907 *Kawananakoa*, 1979 *Nevada v. Hall*, 1999 *Alden*, 2019 *Hyatt* | "an established principle of jurisprudence in all civilized nations"; the answer "public property since before the days of Hobbes"; "enjoyed as a matter of absolute right for centuries"; "The generation that designed and adopted our federal system considered immunity from private suits central to sovereign dignity"; "well established and widely accepted at the founding" | no authority (*Beers*); *Beers* (*Hans*); Hobbes, Bodin, Eliot and Baldus (*Kawananakoa*); Pollock and Maitland on feudal origins (*Nevada v. Hall*, overruled in 2019 with its premise kept); Blackstone and Iredell's dissent in *Chisholm* (*Alden*); *Alden*, Madison and Hamilton (*Hyatt*) |

The recitals share three features. Each names an earlier node of the chain, and where a node names anything older than the chain it names Coke's report of *Floyd*, dated a year late and once placed in the wrong court, Kent's opinion of 1810, or a treatise. The one enacted text of any age in the column is the Bill of Rights of 1689, and it protects legislators. And none names a decision that had read the record of the case the recital descends from; the settledness is the recital's own. The doctrine's weight thus rests on a claim of age, and the claim concerns a real question, among the oldest any legal order has answered: whether a subject may proceed against the judge who wronged him. Part 5 reads the answers, from Babylon to the England of the dooms, and every one of them is a remedy (chapter 5.7). If age settles the question, it settles it against the rule.[^ii_age1]

## 2.4 Entry by Citation, Not Adjudication

### A. The Entry by Citation: *Randall*, *Bradley*, *Spalding*, 1869–1896

Chapter 2.3 placed the doctrine's American entry in 1869; the entry repays a closer look, because it set the terms for everything built on it. The rule did not enter United States law by examination but by citation: in *Randall v. Brigham*, the Court received it on its antiquity alone, examining no record and producing no constitutional provision — the citation to Coke's report of *Floyd v. Barker* *was* the reception.[^ii17] *Bradley v. Fisher* (1872) then hardened the rule, and *Spalding v. Vilas* (1896) carried it to the executive branch — each by re-citing the same English line, never re-examining the foundation.[^ii18] And the foundation those courts declined to examine was, on its now-recoverable record, itself a case of the very evidentiary closure the immunity rule performs — a conviction obtained on an un-authenticated, recanted record and sealed by the destruction of the complaining bill (Part 1, chapter 1.2). The chain's root node is not neutral ancient authority; it is the wrong the doctrine exists to foreclose, decided by foreclosing it. The receiving bar's own inventory of what it took shows the same entry from the other side. In the decade of *Yates v. Lansing*, the Pennsylvania judges reported to their legislature which British statutes were in force, and the digest printed from their report carried the statute of Edward I defining conspirators with Coke's *Institutes* as its gloss, stated the writ of conspiracy as the acquitted man's remedy for "the infamy, imprisonment, and vexation done to him" on Coke's authority, and marked the section of 3 Hen. VII c. 1 that constituted the Star Chamber as not in force; it carried no trace of *Floyd*.[^iirob1a] What was received as law in force was the complainant's remedy; what arrived half a century later, by citation, was the court's rule against him.[^iirob1]

*Spalding* is the deepest available antecedent for American *executive* immunity, and it announces its own provenance without disguise. It extends immunity to a cabinet officer by analogy to judges: "the same general considerations of public policy and convenience which demand for judges of courts of superior jurisdiction immunity . . . apply to a large extent to . . . heads of Executive Departments."[^ii19] And it builds that analogy on English and American *judicial*-immunity precedent — quoting *Scott v. Stansfield*, an 1868 decision of the English Court of Exchequer, for the proposition that a judge must be free to act "without fear of consequences."[^ii20] The root of American executive immunity is therefore neither American, nor constitutional, nor executive: it is a foreign judicial decision, transplanted by analogy and carried forward by a recurring keyword. The doctrine did not lose its constitutional source somewhere in the twentieth century. It never had one, because the act that brought it into American law was a citation, not an adjudication. The Court has said as much of its own method: in deciding whether officials performing a function are entitled to absolute immunity it has "generally looked for a historical or common-law basis for the immunity in question," and of the cognate sovereign immunity it has said that "the principle has never been discussed or the reasons for it given" and that "it has never been understood how the doctrine of sovereign immunity came to be adopted in the American democracy."[^iiadm1]

The antiquity *Randall* invoked will not survive the examination *Randall* omitted. The "uniform course of decisions in England for centuries" the Court received on faith was not uniform. The same reporters and courts the immunity line cites held, in the same volumes, the opposite rule for the case that mattered: a judge or officer who acts without jurisdiction is personally answerable in trespass, the proceeding *coram non judice* and "an action will lie against" him in false imprisonment — and the liability turns on his *knowledge*. Coke himself laid that rule down in *The Case of the Marshalsea* (1612); a century later *Smith v. Bouchier* (1734) entered judgment against the very judge who had ordered an arrest on an insufficient oath, the proceeding "*coram non judice*, and a mere nullity," because "the Judge and the plaintiff knew" the oath would not support it.[^iicur1] A second counter-current ran beside the first: that an officer could not be stripped of his place without notice, cause, and an opportunity to be heard, the deprivation otherwise void and a writ of restitution awarded — the rule of *Bagg's Case* (1615) and *Willis v. Gipps* (1846), and the very rule *Randall* enforced on the other half of its own judgment, in holding that an attorney may be deprived of his office only where "notice should be given to the attorney of the charges made and opportunity afforded him for explanation and defence."[^iicur2] The tradition the Court called uniform in fact ran in three directions, and the doctrine kept only the line that shielded the official.

### B. A Pedigree by Selection: The English Cases That Held Officers Liable Were Left Out

That the selection was made with the contrary authority in view is visible on the face of the immunity cases themselves. *Kemp v. Neville* (1861) — the decision that strung the English chain together to extend immunity to a university officer — marshalled the jurisdiction-liability cases by name in the same breath, and escaped them only by holding that its officer "had jurisdiction in respect of the matter, and the person, and the place"; *Scott v. Stansfield* (1868), the decision *Spalding* would carry into American executive law, had *Houlden v. Smith* — a judge "is answerable" when he acts without jurisdiction — and the malice question *Thomas v. Churton* had pointedly reserved pressed upon it at the bar, and ruled past both.[^iicur3] The immunity tradition was not inherited intact; it was selected, by courts that had the limiting cases before them, that tied their own protection to the jurisdiction those cases made decisive, and that wrote a shield turning on status anyway. A "history and tradition" assembled by setting aside the parts of the history that cut the other way is not the deeply rooted practice the Court demands of a citizen who invokes a tradition (chapter 3.5 § E); it is a selection — and the selection, not the tradition, is the holding. That claim is no longer an assertion resting on examples: the full course of decisions the doctrine invokes has been reconstructed case by case — one hundred forty-nine decisions, from the Liber Assisarum of 1354 through the 2025 Term, each read in the authoritative report and charted for what the doctrine's own sources say about fear, jurisdiction, notice, and the citizen left before the court — and the reconstruction is maintained as a verified documentary record companion to this book.[^iigen1][^iigen1_text]

The examination yields a deeper finding still: the immunity line descends, through its own citations, from the one decision that forbids it. *The Case of the Marshalsea* — the fountainhead of the jurisdiction-liability rule, and Coke's own — rests in turn on *Dr. Bonham's Case* (1610), which its authoritative report invokes by name (8 Co. Rep. 121).[^iibon1] *Bonham* is the authority the genealogy cannot afford to read, because Coke there held three things, each fatal to an office-only immunity: that "the common law will controul Acts of Parliament . . . and adjudge them to be utterly void" when they run "against common right and reason" — the principle American law made the foundation of judicial review in *Marbury*; that no man may be judge in his own cause, *iniquum est aliquem suæ rei esse judicem* — the root of due-process disqualification; and that officials who hold "an authority only," and are "not made judges, nor a Court given them," answer for their commitments "in an action of false imprisonment."[^iibon2] The crown read the passage the same way, and objected to it at the time. In October 1616, in the proceeding that ended with Coke's removal from the King's Bench (Part 1, chapter 1.3 § D), the King caused cases from the Reports to be collated and required Coke's answer to five, "being all such as were expatiations of his own, and no judgments"; the fourth was *Bonham*, put to him in the Lord Chancellor's own words — "what he means by this passage, that in many cases the common law shall control acts of Parliament, and sometimes judge them to be merely void" — and the fifth was *Bagge's Case*, on the King's Bench's authority to correct "any manner of misgovernment."[^iibon4] The two propositions the receiving tradition would take from Coke — that power exercised against common right is void, and that the King's Bench stands above the rival jurisdictions — were the two the King put in issue against their author. When Coke's successor was sworn the next month, the Lord Chancellor named the fault from the bench, holding up the new Chief Justice's grandfather as the good judge:

> He challenged not power . . . to judge Statutes and Acts of Parliament to be void, if they conceived them to be against common right and reason; but left the King and the Parliament to judge what was common right and reason.

\noindent The immunity chain inherited the second as history and never noticed that the first had been challenged in the same breath.

A word on the man. This book criticizes Coke's report at length; it makes no finding on his state of mind in 1607, which no record before it can settle. Two facts about his disposition are on the record. In June 1616, when the twelve judges were called before the King over the command to stay proceedings, all kneeled and confessed error; Coke alone answered that when the case came he would do what was fit for a judge to do (chapter 1.3 § D). And as Attorney General he took up a Star Chamber prosecution for the rapes at Snargate in 1598, which the local justice had refused to bring, when rape was seldom prosecuted; one of the three complainants was a maidservant of fourteen, assaulted by three men and left for dead, and he pressed the cause to a verdict.[^iibon5] Neither fact decides what he meant in 1607. Both stand beside the report.

The same lineage appears again inside the chain: *Groenvelt v. Burwell* (1700), a node the immunity cases recite, arose from the identical College-of-Physicians commitment power *Bonham* had confined; and *Randall* supplies the proof from its own authority. Its due-process branch relies on *Dimes v. Grand Junction Canal* (1852), which voided the Lord Chancellor's decree on the principle "that no man is to be a judge in his own cause" — and which roots that principle, in turn, on Coke's Reports, the same reporter whose eighth volume carries *Bonham*. The disqualification rule *Randall* invokes thus descends, on the face of *Randall*'s own citation, to the books that hold no official may judge his own cause and power exercised against common right void.[^iibon3] The contradiction lies inside the very corpus the Court received on faith: the doctrine that grants the official an office-only shield traces, by its own authorities, to Coke's holding that no official may judge his own cause, that power exercised against common right is void, and that the officer who exceeds his authority answers in damages — the holding the Founding made the foundation of judicial review and due process, and the precise antithesis of the immunity drawn from the same books.

### C. The Bar's Record, 1951–2000: How the Doctrine Was Argued

The reconstruction can be tested from below as well as from above, and the test has been run. Beside the reported decisions, the record assembled for this book now holds the doctrine as it was *argued*: a run of immunity briefs filed in a single federal circuit between 1951 and 2000, on both sides, together with the printed record of the 1989 Senate hearing that produced the 1996 codification (chapter 2.3) — every quoted passage read in the digitized print.[^iicb8][^iicb8_text] The briefs show the working method of the doctrine, and it is the method of 1869 at every level. Of the eleven documents filed for an official, nine cite *Bradley*, and none reaches behind it except as *Bradley*'s own quotation of *Taaffe v. Downes* travels inside a passage quoted whole. That is the defendants' side of the set. The six briefs filed for citizens are read below; one of them, the Hunzikers' of 1989, names *Floyd* and *Marshalsea* and rejects them. The Department of Justice, defending two federal judges in 1976, opened with *Randall*'s "uniform course of decisions in England for centuries" and named no English decision; the judge's counsel in *Stump*, at the circuit stage, wrote "This was the settled law of England and is similarly accepted in this country" and cited *Bradley*; by 1979 a municipal defendant's entire argument was a single paragraph of *Stump*.[^iicb9] Not one brief filed for an official names *Floyd v. Barker*. The only English decision a defender cites by name is *Marshalsea*'s rival line carried in *Bradley*; the only brief that pleads *Marshalsea* itself as authority is the *plaintiff*'s, in 1951, and it pleads the liability rule: "Such has been the law from the days of the *Marshalsea*, 10 Coke 68. It was recognized as such in *Bradley v. Fisher*."[^iicb10] The citizen reached further into the tradition than any defender in the set, and reached for the line the doctrine had left out.

The briefs also show the selection happening. Judge Stump's own counsel stated the exception with the knowledge element *Bradley* gave it — "[w]here the act is done in the clear absence of all jurisdiction and this is known to the judge, there is no immunity" — and then asked for a rule under which the knowledge could not matter: "Fundamental concepts underlying 'jurisdiction' are deistic. Judges, however, are mere humans and prone to err . . . to err is human; thus, such protection is both necessary and just."[^iicb11] In *McCloud v. Rector* — a litigant jailed at eight in the evening on opposing counsel's telephone call, with no petition filed and no process served — the judge's brief conceded at most a "slight 'excess of jurisdiction'" and argued that jurisdiction over the person must not count toward the exception "because it is the very act of obtaining 'jurisdiction of the party' . . . which often gives rise to suits against judges. . . . If a contrary meaning is intended then the cases are simply wrong."[^iicb12] The plaintiff's reply set *Bradley* against *Bradley*: the power of a court "should never be exercised without notice to the offending party . . . . This is a rule of natural justice," at page 354, against the shield at page 351.[^iicb13] That is the notice-and-hearing counter-current of *Bagg's Case*, pleaded from *Bradley*'s own text; and the argument that answered it — that the limit must be read out because it is where the suits arise — became the law in *Stump*, whose "construed broadly" instruction a state attorney general was quoting as settled canon by 1980.[^iicb14]

Two further things the briefs record deserve stating, because the reports do not. The first is what the shield's own defenders conceded it left standing. Defending a state judge in 1974, the Illinois Attorney General told the court that the plaintiff retained the Judicial Inquiry Board, habeas corpus, appeal, and criminal prosecution — that "the judicially fashioned doctrine of official immunity does not reach 'so far as to immunize criminal conduct proscribed by an Act of Congress,'" and that "18 USC § 242 provides a method of fully protecting citizens from judges who commit corrupt acts in office."[^iicb15] The accountability the doctrine's beneficiaries pointed to in 1974 is the accountability the apex opinion would later hold the office could not bear (chapter 2.5 § B).

The second is how rarely the pedigree itself was put in issue, and by whom. Across four decades of briefing in the record, the plaintiffs who answered argued the doctrine's own exceptions — jurisdiction, notice, function — competently and from inside it. The question this book asks was put to the court of appeals once, in January 1989, by two litigants without counsel. Donald and Joan Hunziker were hog farmers of Stephenson County, Illinois. On 10 April 1985 a state judge issued the bank that had financed their farm a writ of replevin ex parte, without notice to them; the next day the sheriff and three deputies came unannounced to their farmstead and took much of their livestock, grain and feed, and farm equipment; no hearing on the merits, their brief says, was ever held.[^iicb15a] Appealing the dismissal of a civil-rights complaint against, among others, the judge who issued the writ, they argued under their own headings that the Supreme Court's decisions

> rely upon *Floyd v. Barker* (1607) and *the Case of the Marshalsea* (1613), which is misplaced reliance, for those cases arose in the period of time . . . when the King and his courts were the law, and the people acting through their Parliament had not yet established their right to rule the realm.

\noindent They argued that those cases "clearly assumed judicial liability under several circumstances" and "*Bradley* glossed over such liability";[^iicb16a] that

> [t]he unquestioning importation to the colonies of such precedents lifted from the early English law without benefit of such exceptions has caused a substantial distortion in the development of the American law, which effectively adopted the rule but without its exceptions;

and that the transplant "totally ignores the varying SOURCE of power of the judiciary under the two forms of government."[^iicb16] They asked the court: "Is it not ironic that the Courts of the United States, with sovereignty plainly reposed in the PEOPLE should rely upon concepts of judicial immunity derived from a system assuming the power to govern by divine right?"[^iicb17]

The argument was built from a 1980 law-review study the Hunzikers quoted for the proposition that the statements in *Stump* and *Bradley*

> are unworthy of the overwhelming weight they are given because they are poor history at two levels: the courts have not understood correctly the primary sources, the prior cases, and the courts have evaluated inadequately the circumstances out of which the earlier cases arose.[^iicb18]

\noindent That study had already been answered, in 1985, in a footnote of the article by a state supreme court justice that the Senate hearing of October 1989 reprinted — the only document in the set that reaches 1607 from the doctrine's side. It recites Coke's "vehement and violent presumption of law, that a justice sworn to do justice, will do injustice" as the doctrine's "ringing affirmation," from the print, and tells its readers that Justice Douglas's derivation of the rule from "the King can do no wrong" is "highly questionable" — eight pages after reporting that Coke held judges "accountable only to God and the King."[^iicb19]

The court of appeals affirmed in an unpublished order, decided on the briefs without oral argument, and disposed of the argument in a footnote:

> A majority of the Hunzikers' appellate brief is dedicated to decrying the concept of judicial immunity and explaining the Supreme Court's erroneous reliance on English common law and American legislative history to justify applying the doctrine to Sec. 1983 cases. We decline to address the Hunzikers' contentions concerning the concept of judicial immunity.[^iicb21]

The same order disposed of the sheriff by the same relocation. Illinois law made the replevin bond the sheriff's own precondition: the plaintiff "shall give to the sheriff or other officer a bond with sufficient security in double the value of the property" before service, the officer may serve only "[u]pon the bond being given," and an officer who "returns an insufficient bond" is "liable to the party injured for all damages such party may sustain by reason of such neglect" where "the bond was insufficient when taken" — and the state court had itself, two months after the seizure, required the bank to secure the bond it had given.[^iicb22a] The court of appeals asked instead whether the *order* was facially valid, held that "[t]he writ itself was proper," and extended the judge's immunity to the officers who executed it; the statute that made the officer answer for his own default is not mentioned.[^iicb22][^iicb22_text] The judge's immunity was sustained on *Stump*. Set out plainly: a writ served on an unsecured bond was held proper against a statute that required a secured bond before service; the officers who served it were covered by the judge's immunity; the argument from 1607 was declined in a footnote; and the order that did all this was unpublished and, by the circuit's own rule, not to be cited as precedent. The order of 1607 took the subject's bill off the file, "cancelled, and utterly defaced"; the order of 1990 kept the subject's argument, and its own answer to it, out of the record of the law. What Congress did in 1996 is chapter 2.3's last row.

### D. The Source Questioned Twice and Never Answered: *Stump* and *Pulliam*

The neighbouring immunity in the same record does not behave this way: the sovereign-immunity prints go to *Chisholm* and to Blackstone by name, and four Justices contest the source directly.[^iicb20] In the judicial-immunity line the source has been put in issue from the bar twice at the Supreme Court, and neither time did the Court answer it as a question about the source. Eleven weeks before *Stump* was decided, a coalition of disability-rights organizations told the Court that "[w]hile some form of judicial immunity has a long history, *see Floyd v. Barker's Case*, 77 Eng. Rep. 1305 (Star Chamber 1608), that history is somewhat uneven," traced the English rule through Pollock and Maitland and the Justices Protection Act of 1848, counted the American states in 1871, and concluded that Congress "could not have been laboring under the impression that the common law of either England or the United States unalterably, uniformly, or universally held that judges were absolutely immune"; the opinion answers none of it.[^iicb23] Six years later the respondents in *Pulliam* argued, as parties, that the immunity "was subsequently expanded by Lord Coke for the purpose of giving one type of rival court an advantage over another," that "[i]n this context Lord Coke first enunciated the now familiar justification of independence of the judiciary, from the Star Chamber and, in fact, from all but 'God and the King,'" and that no modern justification "can . . . be derived from a need for independence from the Star Chamber." The Court adopted the history — *Floyd* as a move in the King's Bench's contest with its "17th- and 18th-century rivals," citing the respondents' own authority for it — and used it to hold that the immunity does not bar prospective relief. It did not ask what the rival court had done in the cause; the history it took was a history of purpose, argued from two law-review studies, and the record was no more before it than before any court in the line.[^iicb24] *Pierson*, by contrast, was decided with no English decision before the Court from either side: none of the six filings cites one, and the majority's *Scott v. Stansfield* and the dissent's *Floyd* were supplied from the bench. The litigants who put the question in the court of appeals had no bar. Nor had the litigant at the English root: *Fray v. Blackburn*, the case *Bradley* quotes for the harassment rationale, was decided on demurrer against a plaintiff appearing in person, and the report twice records that the judge's counsel "was not called upon."[^iicb25] At the forum the rule itself reserved, the pattern was already old: Wraynham, who had asked the King for a hearing against the Lord Chancellor, answered the Attorney General in person at the Star Chamber bar and was fined.[^iicb26] Chapter 3.6 § D returns to what that pattern explains.

[^iigen1_text]: The record comprises two instruments prepared for this book and available from the author: *Appendix: The Case-by-Case Genealogy*, one entry per decision, 149 entries, 1354–2025, each closing with a note stating the edition and page read; and *The Immunity/Standing Genealogy Matrix*, the same 149 decisions charted across fifteen analytic fields — among them the fear/fearlessness language each case uses, whether its hypothetical harms were tested against any record, which contrary authorities it cited and ruled past, whether its warrant is text or recited antiquity, and whether protection turns on jurisdiction or status. Every English decision but one was read in the English Reports reprint (*Prohibitions del Roy* rests on a text transcription of the reprint), with argument-of-counsel, headnote-versus-body, and editorial-apparatus attributions noted entry by entry; [American quotations were read in the official reports, with the same syllabus-versus-opinion discipline applied (*e.g.*, the *Owen* "compelled both" formulation, corrected from the Syllabus to the opinion of the Court, 445 U.S. 650–51). Where a scan is partial or a phrasing survives only in a secondary report, the entry says so.](cite:iigen1_text/3)
[^iibon1]: [*The Case of the Marshalsea*, 77 Eng. Rep. at 1039–40, invokes *Dr. Bonham's Case*, 8 Co. Rep. 121, for the officer-liability rule.](cite:iibon1/1)
[^iibon2]: [*Dr. Bonham's Case*, 8 Co. Rep. 113b, 77 Eng. Rep. 638 (C.P. 1610) (Coke, C.J.), the operative holdings at 8 Co. Rep. 118a (the common law "will controul Acts of Parliament . . . and adjudge them to be utterly void" when "against common right and reason"; "*iniquum est aliquem suæ rei esse judicem*" — no man may be a judge in his own cause) and 121a (the censors "are not made judges, nor a Court given them, but have an authority only," so the cause of their commitment "is traversable in an action of false imprisonment"). On *Bonham* as the root American law made of judicial review (*Marbury*), see above in this Section](cite:iibon2/1); the *Marbury* lineage is the standard account, framed here as received-in-America rather than as settled English law.
[^iibon3]: [*Groenvelt v. Burwell*, 1 Ld. Raym. 454, 91 Eng. Rep. 1202 (K.B. 1700) (Holt, C.J.) (the same College-of-Physicians commitment power adjudicated in *Bonham*)](cite:iibon3/1); [*Dimes v. Proprietors of the Grand Junction Canal*, 3 H.L.C. 759, 793, 10 Eng. Rep. 301, 315 (H.L. 1852) (Lord Campbell) (the maxim "that no man is to be a judge in his own cause should be held sacred," and "not . . . confined to a cause in which he is a party," voiding the Lord Chancellor's decree for his undisclosed interest in the canal company), which roots that maxim on *Sir John Egerton v. Lord Derby*, 12 Co. Rep. 114 — a report of Coke's, the same reporter whose eighth volume carries *Bonham*. Because *Randall* cites *Dimes* as authority for its due-process holding, the disqualification principle descends, on the face of *Randall*'s own citation, to the Coke/*Bonham* root.](cite:iibon3/2)

I (London 1845), at 317–18, 321–22 (Ellesmere and Bacon deliver the five questions on 17 October 1616; question 4, "In Dr. Bonham's case . . . Lib. 8"; question 5, "In Bagge's case . . . Lib. 11")](cite:iibon4/1); the King's paper of October 1616, with the words the text quotes, and the removal of November 1616 are cited in the notes at Part 1, chapter 1.3 § D. On the King's Bench-supremacy history the Court adopted in *Pulliam*, see *supra* chapter 2.3 and the note at chapter 2.4 § D.
[^iibon5]: [Louis A. Knafla, *Sir Edward Coke and the Star Chamber: The Prosecution of Rapes at Snargate, 1598–1602*, in K.J. Kesselring & Natalie Mears eds., *Star Chamber Matters: An Early Modern Court and Its Records* 61 (Univ. of London Press 2021), at 61 (the case "unusual in that rape cases were seldom prosecuted in the early modern era" and "unusual in that Sir Edward Coke decided to take it on and then, after a lengthy intermission, put all his weight behind the prosecution in Star Chamber"; "Local authorities had refused to prosecute the men who were accused of the atrocities, but eventually Coke stepped in"), 62 (Coke "relentless in his search for information as the men at first fled into France and elsewhere"; the verdict, the three chief perpetrators fined £200 each and three lesser ones £40 each), 64 (the complainants: Agnes Horn, "aged thirty-five"; Joan Grigsby, "aged thirty"; "Mary Gamby, aged fourteen"), 65 ("Mary Gamby was left in dire straits, assaulted by three men who left her for dead"; the parish justice, William Lamb, told the complainant the assault was "but a trick of youth"), 70 (rape "a crime denounced in the abstract but not much prosecuted in practice"), 73 (the Home Counties assize record of the two reigns: "about 40% of the cases are of children aged two to twelve"). The chapter is Knafla's, in the volume Kesselring edited with Mears. The kneeling of June 1616 is documented at chapter 1.3 § D.](cite:iibon5/1)
