## 2.5 The Apex: Presidential Immunity, 1982–2024

The chain reaches the presidency in 1982 and completes the ascent in 2024. This chapter examines the apex on its own terms: the two same-day decisions on which the presidential doctrine rests (Section A); the apex opinion's use of its own authorities, inverted at seven points (Section B); the fearlessness rationale and the pedigree it conceals (Section C); the Federalist's "energy," read against its source (Section D); and the asymmetry with which the doctrine credits its founding hypothetical — and what alone can displace it (Section E).

### A. Two Decisions of One Day as Each Other's Authority

The modern presidential doctrine rests on a base that cannot bear it. The two decisions *Trump v. United States* treats as antecedent authority — *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* and *Harlow v. Fitzgerald* — were argued the same day and decided the same day, June 24, 1982, as companion cases in a single volume of the United States Reports.[^ii21] They reference each other, but only as locational markers within the volume: *Harlow* states that "[a]s we reiterated today in *Nixon v. Fitzgerald*, *ante*, p. 731, our decisions consistently have held that government officials are entitled to some form of immunity."[^ii22] "As we reiterated today" is not a citation of established antecedent; it is an acknowledgment that both opinions are productions of the same moment. Neither can be the other's source, because neither preceded the other.

The contrast with another famous same-day pair is dispositive. *Brown v. Board of Education* and *Bolling v. Sharpe* were also decided the same day, and also cross-reference each other.[^ii23] But each is independently grounded in constitutional text — *Brown* in the Fourteenth Amendment's Equal Protection Clause, *Bolling* in the Fifth Amendment's Due Process Clause — so the cross-reference coordinates two textually grounded holdings rather than substituting for a missing ground. *Brown* performed a *corrective return* to a text that an earlier decision had misread; the antecedent was the Amendment, always present. The two 1982 immunity decisions perform the inverse: each invents what the constitutional text does not supply, and each leans on the other to obscure that neither produces it. Bidirectional cross-citation stands in for antecedent authority. Chief Justice Burger's *Harlow* dissent makes the move visible in a phrase, asserting that the Court had decided "what has been taken for granted for 190 years"[^ii24] — a claim of antecedent existence offered without the 1792–1981 case law that would establish a 190-year tradition, because no such case law exists. Traced backward, the chain terminates in a same-day pair, resting on an 1896 decision of narrow context, resting on an English maxim the Founding overthrew. At no layer is a constitutional provision produced.

Two further features of the pair confirm that it cannot bear the weight placed on it. First, the two decisions are not merely non-sequential; they are *substantively incompatible*, so that treating them as one coherent antecedent compounds the defect rather than curing it. *Harlow* makes liability turn on an objective and *conditional* test — the official is protected *unless* he violated "clearly established" law — a standard whose entire premise is the official's possible good-faith ignorance of the law's content. *Nixon v. Fitzgerald*, decided the same hours, makes the President's immunity *absolute* and *unconditional*: it attaches even where the law is settled and the breach is knowing. The two standards cannot be combined into a single rule, and the conditional one cannot even apply to the office the unconditional one governs. *Harlow*'s measure is a reasonable official who did not know that a right was clearly established, its limits defined, in the Court's own adverb, "essentially in objective terms," and objectivity admits no degrees. Article II charges the President to take care that the laws be faithfully executed and places the Department of Justice at his command to do it (chapter 3.5 § D); an officer who bears the constitutional duty to know the law and execute it, with the whole apparatus of federal legal advice at his disposal, cannot at the same time claim the benefit of a standard built to excuse the official who did not know. There is no reasonable President who did not know the law against whom his conduct could be measured; the measuring rod does not exist for the office. That is why the apex doctrine had to take the *absolute* branch, and why the apex opinion, invoking *Harlow* for the existence of presidential immunity, never applied *Harlow*'s standard to the President: it could not. Taking the absolute branch, it left the "objective" standard it simultaneously claims to inherit with no work to do. A foundation assembled from two holdings that contradict each other is not strengthened by having two; it is the weaker for it.

Second, the apex opinion concedes the very absence the genealogy alleges. Met with the dissent's premise "that . . . no constitutional text supports Presidential immunity," the Court does not produce the text. It answers that "a specific textual basis has not been considered a prerequisite to the *recognition* of immunity."[^iinc1] That sentence is the verb test (the Method, § C) confessed in a single clause: to *recognize* is to acknowledge something already there, and the Court, asked for the antecedent its verb presupposes, replies that no antecedent is required.

Nor is the concession the apex opinion's alone. *Fitzgerald* itself had already made it: *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* granted that, "[b]ecause the Presidency did not exist through most of the development of common law," the immunity could "draw its evidence primarily from our constitutional heritage and structure" — conceding that the office has no common-law antecedent, and turning, in the same breath, to a *tradition* and a *structure* rather than a *text*.[^iinc2] And the principal dissent at the apex names the maneuver in terms: "[a]ware of its lack of textual support," the majority falls back on immunities "recognized" as "rooted in the constitutional tradition," just as *Fitzgerald* had leaned on "historical evidence to overcome the lack of any textual basis for its immunity."[^iinc3] What the bottom-up trace infers from the outside, the chain confesses from within. An immunity "recognized" without a textual basis, resting on a same-day pair that cannot supply its own authority, has not been recognized. It has been declared, outside the Article V ratification the Constitution requires of such a declaration.

### B. The Source-Text Audit: Seven Authorities Cited for the Opposite of What They Say

If the chain produces no constitutional source, one might expect its apex opinion at least to transmit its case-law authorities faithfully. It does not. Applying the source-text audit (the Method, § C) to the apex decision yields seven instances in which a supporting citation, read against its own source, does not bear the weight placed on it, and in which the deviation runs, each time, in the direction of more immunity. The instances are set out below; each is verifiable by anyone holding the reporter volumes.

*First, the inserted word.* The opinion's sentence and the sentence it cites, each as printed:

> To the extent that may be the case, objective analysis of "content, form, and context" will necessarily inform the inquiry. *Snyder* v. *Phelps*, 562 U. S. 443, 453 (2011) (internal quotation marks omitted).[^ii25]

> Deciding whether speech is of public or private concern requires us to examine the "'content, form, and context'" of that speech, "'as revealed by the whole record.'" *Dun & Bradstreet*, *supra*, at 761 (quoting *Connick*, *supra*, at 147–148). . . . In considering content, form, and context, no factor is dispositive, and it is necessary to evaluate all the circumstances of the speech, including what was said, where it was said, and how it was said.[^ii26]

\noindent *Snyder* uses no such word; it describes the inquiry in expressly subjective terms. The single inserted adjective, "objective," converts a contextual balancing test into a categorical one, and the "(internal quotation marks omitted)" parenthetical conceals the substitution as a stylistic edit.

*Second, the inverted holding.* The opinion's sentences and the concurrence they cite, each as printed:

> In the latter case, the President's authority is sometimes "conclusive and preclusive." *Id.*, at 638 (Jackson, J., concurring). When the President exercises such authority, he may act even when the measures he takes are "incompatible with the expressed or implied will of Congress." *Id.*, at 637. The exclusive constitutional authority of the President "disabl[es] the Congress from acting upon the subject." *Id.*, at 637–638.[^ii27]

> When the President takes measures incompatible with the expressed or implied will of Congress, his power is at its lowest ebb, for then he can rely only upon his own constitutional powers minus any constitutional powers of Congress over the matter. Courts can sustain exclusive presidential control in such a case only by disabling the Congress from acting upon the subject. Presidential claim to a power at once so conclusive and preclusive must be scrutinized with caution, for what is at stake is the equilibrium established by our constitutional system.[^ii27a]

\noindent But *Youngstown* held that the President had *exceeded* his constitutional authority; it is the foundational modern decision *scrutinizing and rejecting* a claim of conclusive and preclusive power, as the opinion itself concedes elsewhere.[^ii28] The cited concurrence's framework is a method for *determining which zone* a presidential act occupies, not a grant of categorical immunity. The opinion imports the vocabulary while discarding the scrutiny.

*Third, the reversed direction.* The opinion's sentence and the passage it cites, each as printed:

> The hesitation to execute the duties of his office fearlessly and fairly that might result when a President is making decisions under "a pall of potential prosecution," *McDonnell* v. *United States*, 579 U. S. 550, 575 (2016), raises "unique risks to the effective functioning of government," *Fitzgerald*, 457 U. S., at 751.[^ii29]

> But conscientious public officials arrange meetings for constituents, contact other officials on their behalf, and include them in events all the time. The basic compact underlying representative government *assumes* that public officials will hear from their constituents and act appropriately on their concerns—whether it is the union official worried about a plant closing or the homeowners who wonder why it took five days to restore power to their neighborhood after a storm. The Government's position could cast a pall of potential prosecution over these relationships if the union had given a campaign contribution in the past or the homeowners invited the official to join them on their annual outing to the ball game.[^ii30a]

\noindent *McDonnell* used the phrase to *narrow* a federal bribery statute, so as to protect ordinary officials performing routine constituent service from over-prosecution, and *vacated* a conviction because the jury instructions failed to define "official act," holding that the determination required resolution by a properly instructed *jury*.[^ii30] A decision that limited official liability and committed the operative question to the jury is deployed to expand immunity and remove the question from the jury. Its actual holding is omitted.

*Fourth, the relocated phrase.* The opinion's sentence and the sentence it cites, each as printed:

> The Framers accordingly vested the President with "supervisory and policy responsibilities of utmost discretion and sensitivity." *Fitzgerald*, 457 U. S., at 750.[^ii31]

> This grant of authority establishes the President as the chief constitutional officer of the Executive Branch, entrusted with supervisory and policy responsibilities of utmost discretion and sensitivity.[^ii32]

\noindent The quoted phrase is not the Framers' language; it is Justice Powell's 1982 gloss in *Nixon v. Fitzgerald*, relocated to "the Framers" by the verb "vested." The opinion then reads the Federalist's "energy" as a warrant for freedom from accountability,[^ii31a] and "energy," as chapter 2.5 § D shows, is a structural term the Federalist defines by enumeration, not a psychological one. A judicial phrase from 1982 is presented as founding design.

*Fifth, the omitted limit.* The opinion's statement of the 1982 holding, the Story clause that holding rested on, and the 1982 Court's own limit, each as printed:

> In *Nixon* v. *Fitzgerald*, for instance, we recognized that as "a functionally mandated incident of [his] unique office," a former President "is entitled to absolute immunity from damages liability predicated on his official acts." *Id.*, at 749.[^ii33a]

> The president cannot, therefore, be liable to arrest, imprisonment, or detention, while he is in the discharge of the duties of his office; and for this purpose his person must be deemed, in civil cases at least, to possess an official inviolability.[^ii33b]

> Consequently, our holding today need only be that the President is absolutely immune from civil damages liability for his official acts in the absence of explicit affirmative action by Congress. We decide only this constitutional issue, which is necessary to disposition of the case before us.[^ii33]

\noindent The opinion builds on *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* throughout while omitting that decision's own limiting clause. The apex opinion extends the doctrine to *criminal* prosecution, the case Story carved out, and applies it against the explicit affirmative action of the criminal code, the condition under which *Fitzgerald* said immunity does *not* attach. The 2024 holding is the inverse of the 1982 rule it claims to rest on.

*Sixth, the same-Term contradiction.* The opinion's sentence, and the sentence and note of the case it builds on, each as printed:

> Even if the President were ultimately not found liable for certain official actions, the possibility of an extended proceeding alone may render him "unduly cautious in the discharge of his official duties." *Fitzgerald*, 457 U. S., at 752, n. 32.[^ii34]

> However, we cannot conclude that advisers will be moved to temper the candor of their remarks by the infrequent occasions of disclosure because of the possibility that such conversations will be called for in the context of a criminal prosecution. . . . "A juror of integrity and reasonable firmness will not fear to speak his mind if the confidences of debate are barred to the ears of mere impertinence or malice. . . . The chance that now and then there may be found some timid soul who will take counsel of his fears and give way to their repressive power is too remote and shadowy to shape the course of justice."[^ii35]

\noindent *United States v. Nixon*, decided the same Term as *Scheuer*, confronted that very rationale and rejected it, borrowing Cardozo's answer for the juror: the chance of a timid soul is "too remote and shadowy to shape the course of justice," and the measure is the juror "of integrity and reasonable firmness." The opinion builds on a case whose reasoning refutes the premise it borrows.

*Seventh, the foreign root.* The deepest antecedent's own words, and the later Court's account of them, each as printed:

> We are of opinion that the same general considerations of public policy and convenience which demand for judges of courts of superior jurisdiction immunity from civil suits for damages arising from acts done by them in the course of the performance of their judicial functions, apply to a large extent to official communications made by heads of Executive Departments when engaged in the discharge of duties imposed upon them by law. . . . [H]e cannot be held liable to a civil suit for damages on account of official communications made by him pursuant to an act of Congress, and in respect of matters within his authority, by reason of any personal motive that might be alleged to have prompted his action; for, personal motives cannot be imputed to duly authorized official conduct.[^ii36b]

> But *Spalding* did not involve conduct manifestly or otherwise beyond the authority of the official, nor did it involve a mistake of either law or fact in construing or applying the statute. It did not purport to immunize officials who ignore limitations on their authority imposed by law. . . . It is also evident that *Spalding* presented no claim that the officer was liable in damages because he had acted in violation of a limitation placed upon his conduct by the United States Constitution.[^ii36]

\noindent As chapter 2.4 established, the judicial immunity *Spalding* carried across is the English immunity of *Scott v. Stansfield*, common-law and confined to matters within the officer's authority, and *Butz* itself distinguished it as common-law, not constitutional. The chain's foundation is foreign judicial common law, inflated into unlimited constitutional presidential immunity.

The inversions do not begin with the apex opinion. The earliest sits in the foundational paragraph of the doctrine's own root. Having declared that the most sincere judges "would not be free from continual calumniations," the report closes the sentence with a classical warrant: "for which reason the orator said well, *invigilandum est semper, multæ insidiæ sunt bonis*."[^ii37c] The Latin says: one must always be watchful; many are the snares laid for the good. The orator is Cicero, and the line is in *Pro Plancio*; but Cicero is quoting a poet, and says so. The words are the counsel that Atreus gives his sons in the tragedy of Accius, and Cicero, commending them to his own son, says the poet wrote them "to kindle the spirit of industry and ambition" in "us and in our children."[^ii37d] The good are hemmed by snares, and therefore they must be wakeful and labour. The report turns the maxim the other way: the good are beset, therefore they must not be questioned. A counsel of diligence under exposure becomes, in four lines, a ground for exposure's removal, and "the orator said well" is true of where the line was found and false of who wrote it.[^ii37a] The strategic paraphrase the apex opinion practises on its American authorities was practised, at the doctrine's source, on a Roman one. And the maxim did not stay Roman. Nor was the word new in 1872: the report's second resolution had called the questioning of jurors "a cause of infinite vexation" and a means to deter them from the King's service, *ad deterrendos et detrahendos juratores a servitio Regis*.[^ii37f] The same conjecture entered American law in 1872 in quotation marks: the rule exists to "prevent [judges] being harassed by vexatious actions," an English judge's words of nine years before. Five pages later it stood as the Court's own proposition, without citation: judges "would be subjected to the same vexatious litigation upon such allegations, whether the motives had or had not any real existence."[^ii37e] From there the word travels through the chain under its American names: "frivolous," "insubstantial," "harassment," "disgruntled litigants." It reaches the apex, in the dissent's own words and as a fact, as "vexatious private litigation." At no node is the vexation counted.[^ii37b] The word runs through ten opinions of the chain: for the fear in *Bradley*, *Spalding*, *Mitchell v. Forsyth* and *Rehberg v. Paulk* and in separate opinions of Justices White, Rehnquist, Powell and Stevens; against it in *Clinton v. Jones* and in Justice White's *Fitzgerald* dissent, quoting Marshall at Burr's trial; and in the apex dissent as a fact. The sources given for it are an English judge of 1863, Hawkins's *Pleas of the Crown*, Marshall in 1807, the Court's own *Imbler* and, in Justice Powell's dissent, "the English common-law cases" unnamed. None cites the sentence of 1607 in which the word first did this work.[^ii37g]

The pattern also has a mechanism, and it is the strategic paraphrase described in the Method, § C. A characterization that strips a source's qualifiers does not merely misquote it; it hardens the doctrine, because the stripped version is what later opinions cite. The qualified-immunity standard was hedged at its birth: the official who "knew or reasonably should have known," set in the disjunctive against one who acted "with the malicious intention" to injure, the whole defined only "essentially" in objective terms, an adverb that concedes what the adjective claims.[^ii36a] Each restatement shed a qualifier, until the apex opinion could treat a frankly contextual balancing test as flatly "objective" (the first inversion above). The hedges the source insisted on leave the doctrine not by overruling but by paraphrase, one dropped word at a time; and once dropped, the standard is cited as though they had never been there.

These are not the imprecisions endemic to long opinions. They form a pattern, and the pattern is directional: in every instance the deviation enlarges the immunity. Read together with chapters 2.3 and 2.4 and Section A, the audit completes the bottom-up finding. The doctrine identifies no constitutional source at any node; it entered American law by citation rather than adjudication; its modern form rests on a same-day pair that cannot supply its own authority; and the authorities its apex opinion invokes, read in their own words, repeatedly hold the opposite of what they are cited for. The shield rests on a chain of citations that, examined link by link, produces no constitutional source.

### C. Fearlessness: The One Rationale at Every Node, and the Oath It Inverts

A single policy rationale runs through the whole chain and holds it together: officials, and the Executive above all, must be free to act "fearlessly," undeterred by the "pall of potential prosecution."[^ii37] It is the one justification common to every node, which makes it worth isolating: if it falls, each immunity decision is left resting on the constitutional text it never produced. The rationale does not originate in the Constitution. It originates in a 1949 tort opinion, where Judge Learned Hand wrote that subjecting officials to suit would "dampen the ardor of all but the most resolute, or the most irresponsible, in the unflinching discharge of their duties."[^ii38] From that observation about tort exposure the rationale was imported into constitutional immunity and recited until it sounded like law.[^ii39]

The recitation conceals a descending pedigree. As the keyword "fearless" travels from office to office, the authority each case actually invokes grows thinner. For legislators, the privilege rests on *constitutional text* — the Speech or Debate Clause and the state analogues construed in cases like *Coffin v. Coffin*. For judges, it rests on *English common law*, imported through *Bradley v. Fisher*'s reliance on *Scott v. Stansfield*. For executive officials, it rests on a *transplant* of that judicial common law (*Spalding*), and then, for school boards and others, on *bare analogy* to the transplant.[^ii40] Constitutional text gives way to common law gives way to analogy, while the word holds constant — and beneath the judicial and executive nodes lies a foreign root deeper still, running through *Taaffe v. Downes* (1813) to *Floyd v. Barker* (1607). The root's own words, the resolution on jurors and then the resolution on the judge, as the report prints them:

> And it will be a cause of infinite vexation and occasion of perjury and smothering of great offences, if such averments and supposals shall be admitted after ordinary and judicial proceeding: and it will be a means *ad deterrendos et detrahendos juratores a servitio Regis*.[^ii40a]

> [I]t was agreed, that insomuch as the Judges of the realm have the administration of justice, under the King, to all his subjects, they ought not to be drawn into question for any supposed corruption, which extends to the annihilating of a record, or of any judicial proceedings before them, or tending to the slander of the justice of the King, which will trench to the scandal of the King himself, except it be before the King himself; for they are only to make an account to God and the King, and not to answer to any suggestion in the Star-Chamber; for this would tend to the scandal and subversion of all justice. And those who are the most sincere, would not be free from continual calumniations, for which reason the orator said well, *invigilandum est semper, multæ insidiæ sunt bonis*.[^ii40b]

\noindent The word is what carries the rationale from office to office; the constitutional ground exists only at the legislative root and is abandoned at every step after it.

The rationale was, moreover, originally public-regarding, and the modern doctrine inverts it. In its textually grounded form the privilege ran *to the people*: *Coffin* secured it "not with the intention of protecting the members against prosecutions for their own benefit, but to support the rights of the people"; *Pierson* described judicial immunity as existing "not for the protection or benefit of a malicious or corrupt judge, but for the benefit of the public"; *Tenney* tied it to "the public good," "not for [the official's] private indulgence."[^ii41] The doctrine has converted that public-regarding purpose into a personal exemption for the office-holder.

The rationale also misstates what the immunity at its roots ever withheld. No foundational immunity was a release from *all* consequence; each was a release from a single form of suit — the private action for civil damages — with other accountability left standing. The legislator remained answerable to his House and to the criminal law; the judge, immune from the damages action, remained "responsible to the people" and subject to being "called to an account by impeachment"; the President was to remain answerable to ordinary criminal prosecution, the inviolability the early commentary allowed him reaching civil matters alone.[^ii41a] The fear the doctrine purports to relieve was therefore never the fear of every consequence; the roots kept the official answerable — which is fearlessness under consequence, not impunity. The apex decision breaks the pattern at both ends: it extends immunity to *criminal* prosecution, the case the founding-era commentary expressly reserved, and withdraws, for official acts, the accountability every root had preserved (Part 1, chapter 1.3). What the lineage treated as a shield against one form of suit becomes, at the apex, a shield against the law itself.

And the rationale is self-refuting on its own chosen word. To act "fearlessly" is to act rightly despite the possibility of consequence; courage is meaningful only where consequence is possible. Remove every consequence and the official is not rendered fearless but merely safe. There is nothing left to be fearless about. What the doctrine produces is therefore not a fearless officer but an unaccountable one. The structural point is deeper: accountability, exposure to consequence, is the error-correction that holds power to a reality outside the ruler's own will. Strip it away and the will becomes the only reality the officer answers to. A maxim that an office-holder can do no wrong does not restrain the unaccountable ruler; it manufactures him. The Constitution forecloses the maxim in its own text: the Impeachment Clause and the Twenty-Fifth Amendment both presuppose that the highest officer *can* do wrong, and prescribe removal, not immunity, as the answer.[^ii42]

And the category error is written on the tradition's own instruments. The keyword the chain carries as construction appears as enacted law at one point in this lineage's record — the judicial oath — and there it runs the other way. In the form Parliament fixed by statute, the English judge swears that he "will do Right to all Manner of People after the Laws and Usages of this Realm, without Fear or Favour, Affection or Illwill."[^iioath1] The oath does not promise the judge freedom from fear; it assumes fear will attend the office and forbids it to govern the judging — fearlessness as a discipline the officer brings to the bench, not a benefit the bench confers — and the statute enforces the demand with the office itself: the officer who declines the duly tendered oath vacates, or never enters, the seat. The paragraph through which the American line received its fear rationale speaks in that formula and reverses its direction: judges, wrote Kelly, C.B., in *Scott v. Stansfield*, must be free to administer the law "independently and freely, without favour and without fear," and "at liberty to exercise their functions with independence and without fear of consequences" — the judge's promise repurposed as the public's debt, the sworn discipline reissued as the conferred shield.[^iioath2][^iioath2_text] The carrier's own noun concedes the category: what it defends is "[t]his provision of the law," and a provision is precisely what the oath's virtue cannot be — courage can be sworn, demonstrated, and demanded; it cannot be issued. The dated record completes the exhibit: the Exchequer delivered that paragraph on 3 June 1868; Parliament fixed the oath's formula in statutory words fifty-eight days later, on 31 July 1868; and *Randall v. Brigham*, decided in the December Term of the same year, received the immunity into American law on 15 April 1869. In one summer the tradition published the promise's inversion and enacted the promise; within nine months the inversion had crossed the Atlantic. The office demands what cannot be issued; the doctrine issues exemption from the demand.

Finally, the rationale is refuted within the genealogy's own Term. The same Court that made the chilling-fear premise dispositive in *Scheuer* (1974) confronted the identical premise months later, the same Term, in *United States v. Nixon* — and rejected it, holding the speculative fear "too remote and shadowy to shape the course of justice" and demanding officials "of integrity and reasonable firmness" who "will not fear."[^ii43] That standard is the present argument in binding form: the competent officer's fearlessness is brought to the office, not conferred by immunity, and the timid functionary the doctrine worries over is "too remote and shadowy" to ground a constitutional rule. The apex opinion completes the irony. It dismisses the dissents' warnings as "fear mongering on the basis of extreme hypotheticals," then rests its own holding on a hypothetical of precisely the same kind — an Executive "unable to boldly and fearlessly carry out his duties" amid a cycle of factional prosecutions.[^ii44] Speculative future harm is "extreme" when urged against official power and "the more likely prospect" when urged for official immunity. The fear at the doctrine's root is a hypothesis the Court will credit from no one but itself, in its own favor.

### D. "Energy" Is Structural, Not Psychological

The fearlessness rationale's one founding-era ground is the Federalist's account of executive "energy," and the ground does not hold. The apex opinion reads the Framers' design of an "energetic, vigorous" Executive as a warrant against the burdens of accountability.[^ii45] But Hamilton defines energy by exhaustive enumeration: "The ingredients which constitute energy in the executive, are, unity—duration—an adequate provision for its support—competent powers."[^ii46] Every ingredient is *structural* — a feature of the office's design, chiefly *unity*, a single executive rather than a council. None is psychological; none concerns the office-holder's freedom from legal consequence.

The reading cannot be rescued as loose paraphrase, because the pattern is uniform across the source — and it holds equally for the companion term the apex opinion pairs with energy. The opinion's own keyword set is "vigor[]" and "energy," lifted from *The Federalist* No. 70; so run the census on both. Of the thirty appearances of "energy" and "energetic" in The Federalist, twenty-nine denote the structural power of an institution or the design of an office and one the intensity of a popular sentiment; of the twenty-three appearances of the "vigor" family, each denotes the force or efficiency of governmental structure or action — the "vigour of government," a "vigorous executive" — and none the office-holder's subjective freedom from legal consequence.[^ii47] Across the fifty-three founding-era uses of the very two words the Court invokes, not one carries the psychological sense the immunity reading needs; the inserted meaning is the Court's, not the source's. And the same survey's conclusion is dispositive against the immunity reading: in No. 77 Hamilton pairs energy with answerability, describing an office that "combines . . . all the requisites to energy" while its occupant remains "at all times liable to . . . prosecution in the common course of law."[^ii48] Energy and accountability are paired by design, not opposed. To read the structural term in a psychological sense — to make "energy" mean the office-holder's need to be undeterred by suit — is an equivocation the source forecloses by enumeration in its own sentence.

### E. One Hypothetical, Credited for the Official and Never Tested Against the Citizen

The doctrine's one policy rationale is a prediction — that without the shield the official will be deterred, made "unduly cautious," unable to act "fearlessly" (chapter 2.5 § C). A claim about conduct that has not occurred is a hypothetical, and a doctrine resting on one is legitimate only so far as it credits hypotheticals evenhandedly. This lineage does not. It credits the conjecture that favors official power and dismisses the identical species of conjecture when it favors the citizen — and at the apex it does both in a single breath, resting immunity on the prospect of an Executive "unable to boldly and fearlessly carry out his duties" while dismissing the dissent's warning of the opposite harm as "fear mongering on the basis of extreme hypotheticals."[^vias1] Speculative future harm is "the more likely prospect" when the official is its subject and an "extreme hypothetical" when the citizen is. The evidentiary weight of a prediction is made to turn on whom the prediction protects — which is not an evidentiary standard at all.

The asymmetry is not peculiar to the apex; it runs through the whole of this book. The mechanism has been one hypothetical since 1607, asserted always for the official and never measured against the citizen's stake. In *Floyd v. Barker* the conjecture was that to question a sworn judge would "trench to the scandal of the King himself" — the imagined collapse of sovereign authority, credited without proof to bar the suit (Part 1, chapter 1.2).[^vias8a] The fear had been pleaded before, by the judges themselves and against the subject. In 1399 the Commons prayed the King "that . . . the Justices, be not received hereafter for their excuse, to say, that they do not dare to say the law for doubt of death"; the answer was that the King held his justices "good, sufficient and loyal" and would "cause to reform and amend" where any complained.[^vias8] The court Coke served as Attorney General stated whose fear the rule secured when it sentenced a petitioner against the Lord Chancellor in 1604: let all men take heed how they complain in words against any magistrate, "for they are gods," and the complainant must have very good matter, "for feare he ouerthrowe not himselfe"; in 1638 the price of accusing a judge in open court was five thousand pounds to the King and ten thousand to the judge.[^vias9] The excuse the Commons asked to have refused is the rationale the doctrine later made its foundation. In *Coffin v. Coffin* (1808) it was the legislator's need to act "unawed by the fear of legal prosecutions," the defamed citizen's remedy expressly subordinated to it (chapter 2.3).[^vias8b] In the modern chain it is the "pall of potential prosecution" and the chilled, "unduly cautious" officer. At every node the same prediction — *the official, exposed to suit, will fail to act* — is accepted as sufficient ground, while its converse — *the official, immune from suit, will act lawlessly* — is dismissed as alarmism. A rule that admits a prediction as decisive for one party and excludes the same prediction as speculation against the other has abandoned the discipline of the Method: it no longer applies law to facts; it chooses which fear to indulge. The doctrine does not rest on what officials have done. It rests on what the Court is willing to imagine they would do without it — and the Court imagines it only in the direction that enlarges the prerogative. That is the sense in which a hypothetical, not a constitutional provision, became the law of immunity.

The asymmetry has never been cured from the citizen's side, and the reason marks the point at which this book's method departs from every argument in the record. The citizen's advocates have answered the official's conjecture with a conjecture of their own. Justice Douglas, dissenting in *Pierson*, named the doctrine "a more sophisticated manner of saying 'The King can do no wrong,'" reached *Floyd v. Barker* by name, and then met the majority's fear with Chief Justice Cockburn's — "I cannot believe that judges . . . would fail to discharge their duty faithfully and fearlessly" — and with his own: "What about the judge who knowingly turns a trial into a 'kangaroo' court?"[^vias4a] His conclusion was a weighing: Congress "concluded that the evils of allowing intentional, knowing deprivations of civil rights to go unredressed far outweighed the speculative inhibiting effects which might attend an inquiry into a judicial deprivation of civil rights."[^vias4] Justice Sotomayor, dissenting at the apex, answered the "pall of potential prosecution" with the "twisted irony" of a Take Care Clause read as a license, and with a catalogue of nightmares — "Immune, immune, immune" — offered, in her own words, "[e]ven if these nightmare scenarios never play out."[^vias5]

The Hunzikers, without counsel, answered *Bradley* with the irony that a sovereign people's courts rest on a rule born of divine right (chapter 2.4 § C).[^vias6] Each is right about the incoherence it names, and each is a hypothetical set against a hypothetical: the majority's fear that the official will be deterred, the dissent's fear that he will be lawless, and a court free to credit whichever it prefers. Douglas's "speculative" is the right word, and it concedes the method: the fear remains a consideration to be weighed, which one Congress outweighed and the next Court re-weighs. Irony is a comment on the weighing; it does not end it. What ends it is the one thing no participant in the record has put before a court: the proceeding in which the shared conjecture was born. Part 1, chapter 1.2 has stated what that record shows — a bill destroyed without answer, an alibi set aside, examinations written in English "contrary to their sayings in welsh" — and those are not predictions about what officials might do but facts about what a tribunal did.[^vias7] A record is not weighed against a fear. It displaces the need for one.

# Part 3 — The Constitutional Case Against the Doctrine

*Part 3 makes the constitutional case against the doctrine and states the terms on which a court bound by every decided case may decline it. Chapter 3.1 shows the modern apparatus rebuilding the three bars the Founding cut, bar for bar, and states the output. Chapter 3.2 states the two Articles the doctrine names and neither of which supplies what it needs: no receiver under Article III for the plea the rule transfers, and no court on the Article V route by which alone the Constitution gains a word. Chapter 3.3 measures the founding act by the receiving order's own law of records, evidence and access, and states what that order made of each source the 1607 court invoked. Chapter 3.4 states, clause by clause, the guarantees the rebuilt bar on proof displaces: the Fourth Amendment's reasonableness and the second reasonableness the doctrine placed before it, the Fifth Amendment's due-process boundary between authenticating evidence and crediting it and the Sixth Amendment's jury and confrontation, the Seventh Amendment jury, the rule of construction the Ninth and Tenth Amendments supply, and the Fourteenth Amendment's enforcement and equal-protection commands. Chapter 3.5 tests the doctrine by its own textualist canons and by the law of the custom it claims, and finds that it fails them. Chapter 3.6 states what a court may do without overruling anything. A Conclusion to Parts 2 and 3 closes the American argument.*



---

## 3.1 The Three Bars Rebuilt: Modern Immunity Reinstalls the Mechanism of 1607

Part 2, chapters 2.3 through 2.5 established what the immunity chain rests on: a premise, a mechanism, and a fear, none of them constitutional. This chapter shows what they do when they operate together. The combined apparatus is not a novel construction; it is the reconstruction of a mechanism the common law perfected and the Founding dismantled, under which a wrong by an official could be neither proven, nor heard anywhere but before the sovereign, nor remedied by the sovereign. Part 1, chapter 1.3 set out the three bars as the 1607 reports fixed them, and the documented miscarriage they were fixed to seal; this chapter takes the mechanism forward. Part 2, chapter 2.1 showed the Founding cutting all three bars. Section A shows the modern doctrine rebuilding them, and Section B states the output. The bar that matters most for the citizen's liberty is the first, the bar on proof; it is examined clause by clause at chapter 3.4, §§ B and C: the foundational due-process boundary its return destroys, and its procedural form, the Seventh Amendment jury it displaces.

### A. One for One: The Bar on Proof, the Political Forum, the Remedy Withheld

The modern immunity doctrine reinstalls the three bars, one for one. **The first bar returns**: the apex decision forbids using an official's protected acts even as evidence — "[w]hat the prosecutor may not do . . . is admit testimony or private records of the President or his advisers probing the official act itself."[^v9] That is the irrebuttable presumption in modern form: no proof may be received against the official act. **The second bar returns** as the routing of review to the political branches — impeachment and the political process offered as the remedy for official wrongdoing, which is to say accountability "before the King himself," the sovereign's own body auditing itself. **The third bar returns** as the foreclosed individual remedy: immunity forecloses the damages suit, so that, once more, "the party cannot have remedy against the King."[^v9a] The reserved forum was convened once against a judge of England, in 1616, on the King's grievance and no subject's, and removed by a writ "for certain causes now moving us" a judge the King himself had called "no ways corrupt, but a good justice" (chapter 1.3 § D).[^v13] And the forum cannot be convened here at all. The rule does not extinguish the judge's answer; it removes it — "not to answer to any suggestion in the Star-Chamber," but "only to make an account to God and the King" — to a receiver who "is *de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects" and examined his judges "before himself in the Parliament."[^v14a] Article III names no such receiver; chapter 2.2 § A states the consequence, and chapter 3.2 § A reads it from the text.[^v14] No proof, only the political branches, no party remedy: the three bars of 1607, rebuilt. The other-channels reassurance — that immunity does not mean unaccountability because impeachment and the political process remain — is not an answer to the three bars. It is the three bars in modern form: the channels it names are the sovereign's own forum, owing the injured party no remedy and admitting no proof against the official act.

### B. The Output: Official Wrong Made Unreviewable

The three bars compose a closed system, and the system has an output. No proof may be heard against the official act or determination (the first bar); the grievance is routed to the political branches (the second bar); and those branches owe the injured party no remedy and cannot adjudicate his claim (the third bar). Run together, the three bars produce the structural unreviewability of official violation at the instance of the injured public — not as an accident of doctrine, but as the designed output of the prerogative they were built to serve. The "alternative remedies" each bar recites — the political process, impeachment, the enforcement discretion of the very officials who defaulted — deliver the grievance to the defaulting agents themselves. *Before the King himself.* A remedy that lies only before a forum which cannot grant it is no remedy at all. This is the exact violation of the maxim chapter 3.6 § C states as a rule of decision, that enforcement capacity must precede compliance capability: the three bars demand the citizen's compliance with a legal order whose every avenue of enforcement against the order's own agents they have closed, compliance without the enforcement capacity that, by the maxim, must precede it.

## 3.2 Article III and Article V: No Receiver, and No Court to Make One

The doctrine names two Articles when it explains itself, and neither supplies what it needs. To the plea it routes away it offers a forum, and Article III names none. To the charge that it invented an immunity it answers that it recognized one, and the only route by which the Constitution gains a word is Article V, on which no court stands. Section A states the Article III ground: no receiver for the transferred plea, and no exercise of the judicial power in a standardless sort or in an immunity that ends the case before a fact is found. Section B states the Article V ground: the doctrine's own rationale, taken at the only strength on which it does any work, asks for a change in the constitutional design, and the Constitution assigns that change to the people and to Congress. Section C reads both grounds in one volume of the United States Reports, where the Court stated the discipline and, eight pages on, breached it.

### A. Article III: No Court Stands in the King's Place, and a Standardless Sorting Is Not the Judicial Power

The rule of 1607 did not extinguish the subject's plea against a judge; it moved the plea to a King, who was "*de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects" and examined his judges "before himself in the Parliament."[^av1] Article III vests "[t]he judicial Power of the United States" in courts and names no receiver above them.[^av2] Chapter 2.2 § A drew the consequence from the transfer clause; this section reads it from the Article: a rule whose operative clause requires a receiver has, under Article III, nowhere to run.

Article III also says what the power it vests is. "It is emphatically the province and duty of the judicial department to say what the law is."[^av3] "Jurisdiction is power to declare the law."[^av4] A determination that states no rule declares no law. The apex classification supplies three labels with no criterion for sorting conduct among them and, by the Court's own account, no capacity to perform the sort on a full record (chapter 3.5 § A). A sort that cannot be stated as a rule cannot be applied by a second judge to the same facts, cannot be reviewed against anything, and cannot bind the next case. What remains is the disposition of a citizen's claim by a judgment that answers to no law, and that is not the judicial power exercised but the judicial power given up to will. The Court has described the loss from the reviewing side: where a legal principle "can be given meaning only through its application to the particular circumstances of a case," deference to the trier of fact would "strip a federal appellate court of its primary function as an expositor of law."[^av5] A standard that has never been stated cannot be expounded at all.

The form the modern immunity takes produces the same result from the other side. Qualified immunity is "an immunity from suit rather than a mere defense to liability," and "like an absolute immunity, it is effectively lost if a case is erroneously permitted to go to trial."[^av6] So framed, the doctrine ends the Article III case before any fact in it is found: the case or controversy the Constitution commits to the courts is contracted at the threshold by a rule that decides no question the Constitution puts. The apex doctrine adds the bar on proof against the official act (chapter 3.1 § A) and specifies no stage, standard or record for its own application (chapter 3.4 § C). Between the immunity from suit and the bar on proof the judicial power is exercised on nothing. The claim is disposed of, and the law is not declared.

### B. Article V: The Cure the Rationale Asks For Belongs to the People and to Congress

The doctrine's stated reason for itself points at the amendment power. The reason is energy: the official, and the President above all, must be free to act without the deterrent of suit or prosecution, or the office cannot function (chapter 2.5 § C). Chapter 2.5 § D read the word at its source and found it structural: the Federalist defines energy by enumerating the features of the office, and pairs the office that "combines . . . all the requisites to energy" with a holder who remains "liable to . . . prosecution in the common course of law."[^av7] The rationale therefore carries one of two meanings, and each is fatal. If energy is the structural property the Constitution already supplies, the immunity is no part of it and the rationale has no text to rest on. If the office cannot function without an immunity the text does not contain, the rationale asserts a deficiency in the constitutional design, and the Constitution says who cures a deficiency in its design: the people and the States by amendment under Article V, and Congress by law under the Necessary and Proper Clause.[^av8] Neither is a court. A court that supplies a structural feature the text does not contain does not interpret the instrument; it amends it, through the one body from which the amendment power was withheld. There is no third reading. The Constitution sets for its offices only objective qualifications, age, citizenship and residence, binds every officer by oath to the Constitution itself, and prescribes no state of mind for any officer of any branch that a court could be protecting.[^av9] The office-holder's frame of mind is not a constitutional element, and a doctrine built to protect it protects nothing the Constitution names.

The Court's own sentences say which of the two it stands on. *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* called the immunity "a judicial recognition of immunity," traced what "[d]ecisions subsequent to *Spalding*" had "extended," reasoned from the doctrine "[a]s construed by subsequent cases," held by "[a]pplying the principles of our cases," rested on what "our decisions have recognized," and conceded "our primary reliance on constitutional structure and judicial precedent" in the face of "the fragmentary character of the most important materials reflecting the Framers' intent."[^av10] Five years later the Court described qualified immunity's founding act without euphemism: *Harlow* "completely reformulated qualified immunity along principles not at all embodied in the common law."[^av11] Reformulation along principles embodied nowhere is construction, and the Court has said so of itself; chapter 2.5 § A records the same concession at the apex. Under the Constitution a text gains a word by one route, proposal and ratification under Article V, and no court stands anywhere on that route (the Method, § C). The enumeration confirms the allocation: the Constitution grants one personal immunity, to legislators, for their speech and debate, bounded by its subject; sets beside it a privilege from arrest that stops by name at treason, felony and breach of the peace; and, by the Eleventh Amendment, withdraws from the judicial power one class of suits against the States themselves, a shelter for no officer (chapter 3.5 § A).[^av19] A further immunity, if the order is to have one, is theirs to add by the route the instrument prescribes, or Congress's to enact under the powers Article I vests, and never a court's to construe. The Ninth Amendment marks the same line from the other side: a right retained by the people pre-exists government and may be declared, but an immunity for government cannot pre-exist government and must be made, and the making of law is the legislative power (chapter 3.4 § D).[^av12] What the people retain includes the construction of their own instrument. The judiciary holds that power in trust, and a trustee who turns the instrument against its authors to enlarge the trustee's own protection has not construed it.[^av13]

The foreign line supplies the exemplar of the honest route and the measure of the other. When *Chisholm v. Georgia* held a State suable by a citizen of another State, the class of suits left the judicial power by amendment: the Eleventh Amendment withdrew suits against a State "by Citizens of another State, or by Citizens or Subjects of any Foreign State."[^si9] No amendment was proposed for the mirror class, suits by citizens against foreign states and their servants. The courts removed it by comity in 1812; Congress confirmed the removal by statute in 1976, reaching "the courts of the United States and of the States"; and for the servants an Executive letter and a presumption of incorporation do the work with no text at all (chapter 4.6).[^si10] The ground given for the removal was a state of mind: the courts "will not embarrass" the political branch. The Constitution confers no right and withholds none on anyone's feelings, and this section has already met the ground in the judicial line as the official's freedom from fear (chapter 2.5 § C); the Executive's embarrassment is the same kind of ground, and no more a clause than the judge's fear. Congress may withdraw an immunity. It cannot, by statute, take from a citizen the court Article III opens or the jury the Seventh Amendment preserves; the power to ordain and withhold the jurisdiction of inferior federal courts does not reach a State's courts, and a rule of who may not be sued is not an allocation of jurisdiction. Whichever instrument closes the courthouse to a citizen's plea, a case or a statute, the question is the one this section puts: name the clause, or concede construction.

### C. One Volume, the December Term 1868: *McCardle* Looks for Express Words, *Randall* Receives the Rule Without Any

The two grounds meet in a single volume of the United States Reports. In the seventy-fourth volume the Court, dismissing an appeal whose statutory basis Congress had withdrawn, stated the discipline that governs any claim of power: "We are not at liberty to inquire into the motives of the legislature. We can only examine into its power under the Constitution; and the power to make exceptions to the appellate jurisdiction of this court is given by express words."[^av14] "Jurisdiction is power to declare the law, and when it ceases to exist, the only function remaining to the court is that of announcing the fact and dismissing the cause."[^av15] And "judicial duty is not less fitly performed by declining ungranted jurisdiction than in exercising firmly that which the Constitution and the laws confer."[^av16] The Court policed the source of power when policing it cost the petitioner his review. Eight pages on in the same volume, in a case of the same Term, the same Court imported the judge's exemption from civil suit on the authority of the King's delegation, "a uniform course of decisions in England for centuries," and the report of 1607, and cited no clause, identified no express words, and examined no power under the Constitution.[^av17] It then named the substitute: judges "are responsible to the people alone" and "may be called to account by impeachment," but "responsible they are not to private parties in civil actions for their judicial acts."[^av18] The examination this book asks of the doctrine is not a new method. It is pages 514 and 515 applied to page 536: produce the express words, or the power is ungranted, and declining it is duty.

What a court bound by every decided case may do with both grounds is stated at chapter 3.6. The point here is narrower. The doctrine's two accounts of itself, a forum for the plea and the recognition of an immunity that already existed, each name a provision that does not do what the doctrine needs. Article III supplies no receiver and vests a power that a standardless immunity from suit does not exercise. Article V reserves to the people the act the Court performed. A doctrine that needs an amendment to stand and has not got one is not a tradition the Constitution received. It is a change to the Constitution made by hands the Constitution did not authorize.

### D. Article I, Section 8: The Record Congress Needs and the Branch That Holds It

Article III does not leave the citizen's case against a foreign state to construction. The judicial power extends "to Controversies … between a State, or the Citizens thereof, and foreign States, Citizens or Subjects," and no amendment has touched those words. When the founding generation wished to remove a class of controversies from the same sentence, a citizen's suit against a State, it used Article V and had the Eleventh Amendment ratified within two years of *Chisholm* (§ B above). Nobody has done the same for "foreign States." What removed the clause was Marshall's construction of 1812, that a nation's consent to a foreign sovereign's exemption is implied; then the Department of State administering that construction by letter; then the Court in 1943 and 1945 calling the court's surrender to the letter its duty, and in 1983 conceding that the letters had on occasion followed "political considerations" rather than any rule.[^cs1] On this chapter's test that is authority declared, not ratified: a word taken from Article III outside the only route by which its text changes. The 1976 Act took the decision from the Department and gave it to the courts under a rule of Congress's making, which is the reform Article I permits; the suggestion of immunity for the servant, described at chapter 4.6, returns the decision to the Department for the one class of defendant the Act left out.

The record the court would have made is also the record the First Article runs on. Congress is to "provide for the common Defence," to "define and punish … Offences against the Law of Nations," to "declare War, grant Letters of Marque and Reprisal, and make Rules concerning Captures," to "raise and support Armies" on appropriations of no longer than two years, to "provide and maintain a Navy," and to "make Rules for the Government and Regulation of the land and naval Forces"; and no money leaves the Treasury but by its appropriation, with "a regular Statement and Account" to be published. Every one of those powers, exercised toward a state that has attacked a citizen, is exercised on a fact: what the state did. The Court has said where Congress gets its facts. The power of inquiry "is an essential and appropriate auxiliary to the legislative function," employed "in American legislatures before the Constitution was framed and ratified"; the power to investigate is "inherent in the legislative process," and Congress "has assiduously performed an 'informing function.'"[^cs2] Against that function the Executive holds three instruments the courts have recognized: the classification of records; the privilege "against revealing military secrets," recognized in 1953 for an Air Force accident report withheld from the plaintiffs who sued over the crash; and the settlement of the citizen's claim in its own name, which the Court in 1981 called a change in the substantive law.[^cs3] The citizen's own version of the claim, that he is entitled to a Congress that knows, the Court has refused. A taxpayer who sued in 1974 to compel the "Statement and Account" the Ninth Section requires was held to have no standing, in a sentence that concedes the whole difficulty: "the absence of any particular individual or class to litigate these claims gives support to the argument that the subject matter is committed to the surveillance of Congress, and ultimately to the political process."[^cs4] The citizen is sent to Congress for the record, and the record is what Congress is denied. The circle has no receiver in it, which is § A's finding in another form: the surveillance the Court commits the matter to is the surveillance the withholding defeats.

The one crime the Constitution defines runs on the same record. Treason "shall consist only in levying War against them, or in adhering to their Enemies, giving them Aid and Comfort," and the Court reads it as "two elements: adherence to the enemy; and rendering him aid and comfort." Who is an enemy is not a court's finding. Whether hostilities exist "is a question to be decided by him," the President, "and this Court must be governed by the decisions and acts of the political department of the Government to which this power was entrusted."[^cs5] An ally, by contrast, is made in the open. Israel is one by a treaty of 1951 that the Senate consented to, by the statute of 1996 that deems it designated a major non-NATO ally, and by the Act of 2014 in which Congress declared it "a major strategic partner of the United States."[^cs8] So the branch that keeps an ally's attack out of every court is the branch whose acts fix, conclusively on the courts, whether that ally is an enemy; aid to a state that has killed citizens is not treason while the Executive declines to name it one, and the Executive giving the aid makes the naming. Madison saw the inverse danger and built the clause against it: "new fangled and artificial treasons, have been the great engines, by which violent factions … have usually wreaked their alternate malignity on each other," so the Convention "opposed a barrier to this peculiar danger, by inserting a constitutional definition of the crime, fixing the proof necessary for conviction of it." The fear was treason enlarged by the political branches; the record of 1967 shows it contracted by the same hands, and the barrier of definition and proof protects in both directions only if the enemy fact is made in the open.[^cs6] The founding design for a citizen injured by a foreign state had three routes, and none of them ran through the Executive alone: the court, under Article III's clause; reprisal, which the law of nations gave for a denial of justice to a subject and the Constitution gave to Congress; and war, which it gave to Congress too. The Executive's settlement of the citizen's claim in its own name is in none of them. The Court that upheld it in 1981 said where it came from, "long-continued practice, known to and acquiesced in by Congress," and in the same paragraph that "[p]ast practice does not, by itself, create power."[^cs7] That is the doctrine this book has followed since 1607: a subject's remedy removed by the sovereign's own act and later declared law because the removal went unchallenged. What the *Liberty* shows, and chapter 4.6 records, is that the removal disables at once the citizen's remedy under Article III, Congress's predicate under Section 8, the Statement and Account under Section 9, the enemy element of treason, and the facts on which the House would act under Article II, Section 4. Each of those the Constitution placed somewhere other than the branch that now holds them all, and the Court's own cases mark where each one went.

## 3.3 The Act That Made the Rule, Measured by American Law

The book's case against the doctrine rests on two grounds. The first is the transfer thesis, the finding Parts 1 and 2 establish and chapter 3.1 applies, stated once at chapter 2.2: the rule of 1607 did not extinguish the subject's plea against a judge but transferred it to a King, the King never answered it, and the American order has no King to answer it. The second is the receiving order's own measure of the act the rule was made to protect, a sworn bill taken off the file and utterly defaced, which the law that received the rule treats as a felony, a spoliation, a denial of due process and of access to the courts, and the exercise of a power its first freedom withdrew. Either ground bars the rule at the threshold. Together they describe what was received in 1869: a bar without the forum that gave it meaning, resting on an act the receiving law punishes. This chapter states the second ground, and then what the receiving order made of each source the 1607 leaf named.

### A. Destroying a Sworn Complaint: Felony, Spoliation, Denial of Due Process and of Access

**The second ground: the founding act, measured by the receiving order's law.** The rule was made in the act of destroying a subject's sworn complaint. The order that ended the cause of 1607 directed that the bill be "taken off the file and cancelled, and utterly defaced," and the report defends the judge's exemption by extending it to "the annihilating of a record" (chapter 1.2). The legal system that received the doctrine measures that act in every part of its law. Its Constitution textualizes the obligation of "Records"; its founding charter enumerated among the acts of a tyrant the summoning of legislatures away from "the depository of their public Records."[^ih15d] Its criminal code makes the custodian of court-filed records who "conceals, removes, mutilates, obliterates, falsifies, or destroys" one a felon who "shall forfeit his office and be disqualified from holding any office under the United States" — element for element the decree's "taken off the file and cancelled, and utterly defaced," answered with the precise remedy the immunity forecloses.[^ih15b] Its law of evidence presumes destroyed proof adverse to the destroyer: in the founding generation of the American reports, Story's trigger for condemning the spoliator of papers was "a vehement presumption of bad faith" — the same word-pair the received print deploys for the official, pointed the other way. Its due-process doctrine makes the bad-faith destruction of potentially useful evidence a constitutional violation, bad faith meaning conduct that "indicate[s] that the evidence could form a basis for exonerating" the accused — which the decree does not indicate but adjudicates. And its right of access to courts names official concealment that extinguishes a meritorious case as itself the constitutional injury: the cover-up that "rendered hollow [the plaintiff's] right to seek redress."[^ih15c] And its first freedom withdrew from the state the power the order's second verb exercised. In the statute book of the order's own generation *deface* is the word for forbidden objects to be marred past use — images and books to be "openlye brent or otherwayes defaced and destroyed," and, in an ecclesiastical commission of 1620, "massing Stuff Reliques or other like superstitious things" to be defaced so that "they may not serve for any supersticious Use" — and a court that "utterly" defaces a subject's complaint has treated it as the state treated the objects of a forbidden worship: a thing whose existence is the offence, to be destroyed rather than answered.[^ih18a] That is a power the Religion Clauses deny to every American tribunal.[^ih18] Measured by the receiving system's own law, the founding act is a disqualifying felony, a due-process violation, a denial of access, the exercise of a power the First Amendment withdrew, and grounds to presume the destroyed accusation true. The comparison is offered as measure, not as retroactive application: a doctrine that claims the sanction of history must take its founding act as the receiving system's law characterizes it, and nothing here revives a knowledge claim against the receiving courts. The objection that the defacement was merely a manner of its age thus fails at both ends of the transmission: the age's own court had just declared records sacred — and the age that received the doctrine criminalized its founding act, attaching to it the exact accountability the doctrine exists to prevent.[^ih15][^ih15_text]

### B. What American Law Made of Each Source the Report Named

Chapter 1.4 § B read the King, the Roman law, Scripture and the canon law at their own pages, and stated what the 1607 court and Hobbes made of each. What the receiving order made of each stands here, source by source; the conclusions drawn from the sources together are at chapter 2.2 § B.

| Source | The receiving order |
|---|---|
| The King | No receiver survived reception. The court that named the King as the judge's only judge was dissolved for want of due process in 1641 (chapter 1.6); the Crown's last use of its rule against a petitioner, the Attorney General's citation of *Wrenham* at the Seven Bishops' trial in 1688 for the proposition that "the Justice of the Decree is not to be questioned," ended in acquittal (chapter 1.3 § E); the Bill of Rights of 1689 declared it "the Right of the Subjects to petition the King" (chapter 1.6); and the First Amendment made "the right of the people … to petition the government for a redress of grievances" a right against the government itself (chapter 4.6). Under Article III there is no King, the officer answers downward to the people (chapter 2.1), and the founders who read Hobbes refused even the sovereign's own exemption (chapter 1.4 § C). |
| Roman law | The rules the leaf inverted are the receiving order's constitutional text. No one condemned absent or unheard became the jury and due-process guarantees; the signed accuser Rome rewarded became the grand jury's presentment and indictment; the record every act must survive became verity by process, every official act examinable by grand jury, petit jury, rules of evidence, appeal and habeas corpus (chapter 2.1); and Rome's action by the party against the judge who made the suit his own is the officer suit the Founding kept so that no injured party is left without a forum (chapter 2.1; Part 5, chapter 5.3). |
| Scripture | The receiving order answered as Hobbes had: Scripture as history and not as law. Its founding text forbids any "law respecting an establishment of religion" (First Amendment), so no verse can be authority for a rule about judges; and the verses the 1607 court read as exemption were, at their own pages and in the reporter's own charge of the same year, commands to the judge and the sentence of his death (chapter 1.4 §§ A–B). |
| Canon law | What the receiving order answered was the forum's method, not the church's law. The *ex officio* oath the Star Chamber shared with the High Commission fell with the court in 1641 and was implanted in the Bill of Rights as the privilege against self-incrimination (chapters 1.6, 2.2); the canon that no one is condemned absent or unheard the Founding kept as due process and jury trial; and the record Lateran IV required against the unjust judge is the record the American order requires of every official act (chapter 2.1). The one instrument the 1607 court destroyed, a signed bill filed in court, is the instrument the receiving order protects. |

The two grounds are independent, and the chapters around them keep them so. Chapter 1.4 reads the sources the 1607 leaf cited at their pages, and chapter 2.2 § B asks whose decisions count; chapter 3.6 states what a court may do with both grounds without overruling anything; Part 4 puts the first ground's question to other sovereigns, and Part 5 to the older record.

Crime and forfeiture: 18 U.S.C. § 2071(a)–(b) (destruction of any record "filed or deposited with any clerk or officer of any court of the United States" a felony; a *custodian* who destroys "shall forfeit his office and be disqualified from holding any office under the United States") (deriving from Act of Mar. 4, 1909, ch. 321, §§ 128–129, 35 Stat. 1088, 1111–12; before it Rev. Tallmadge*, 160 U.S. 379, 383 (1896) (adopting Lord Mansfield's rule that evidence is "weighed according to the proof which it was in the power of one side to have produced and in the power of the other side to have contradicted," with the presumption against the party withholding) (quoting Lord Mansfield in *Blatch v. The taxonomy behind the grade, argued at chapter 1.3 § B: Co. Litt. 6b ("three sorts, *viz.* violent, probable, and light or temerary. *Violenta praesumptio* is manie times *plena probatio*"), carried into Blackstone's chapter on trial at 3 Bl. Due process: *Arizona v. Youngblood*, 488 U.S. 51, 58 (1988) (absent "bad faith on the part of the police, failure to preserve potentially useful evidence does not constitute a denial of due process of law"; the limitation confined to cases in which the police "by their conduct indicate that the evidence could form a basis for exonerating the defendant")](cite:ih15_text/6); [*California v. Warner Communications, Inc.*, 435 U.S. 589, 597 (1978) ("the courts of this country recognize a general right to inspect and copy public records and documents, including judicial records and documents")](cite:ih15_text/8); [*Nixon v. Administrator of General Services*, 433 U.S. 425 (1977) (sustaining the statutory preservation of a former President's records over his objection).](cite:ih15_text/9)
