## Conclusion to Parts 2 and 3

The federal courthouse is closed to the citizen an official has wronged, and the closure has one ancestor. The doctrine that turns him away is not a tradition the Republic inherited. It is a 1607 decree of the Star Chamber, received into American law in 1869 by a citation that read the report and not the record, read into a statute whose text makes "every person" liable, and carried to the presidency on a same-day pair of decisions that could not supply each other's authority. Traced from the bottom up, the chain identifies a constitutional source at no node. Its justification at every node is a hypothetical — the official who, exposed to suit, will fail to act — credited for the official and never tested against the citizen; and the case in which that hypothetical was born is one whose surviving file shows the complaint destroyed rather than answered, the exculpatory examinations withheld from the jury, and the witnesses' words taken down in a language they did not speak. The doctrine rebuilds the three bars the Founding cut: no proof against the official's act, no forum but the sovereign's, no remedy from the sovereign. At its root the rule did not extinguish the subject's plea against a judge; it transferred the plea to a King who never answered one, and the Republic has no officer to whom such a transfer can run. The promise of accountability leads, as it always did, nowhere.

What makes the doctrine vulnerable is the same thing that has let it endure. It has held because it has never been made to account for itself — because a threshold doctrine disposes of cases before the merits, because the source has been put in issue from the bar and never answered as a question about the source, and because the structural questions are never reached. None of that is a holding. It is an absence of holding, and an absence of holding is not precedent. A court faithful to every decided case can still require the shield to show the warrant it has presupposed and never produced; can decide, as questions of first impression, the questions the lineage never measured its rule against under the Fourth, Fifth, Sixth, Seventh, Ninth, Tenth and Fourteenth Amendments; and can accept the critiques that hold even if the doctrine is assumed sound. The Founding's answer to the fear the doctrine invokes was not to exempt the official but to bind him, and to keep a forum open for the citizen he wrongs. Restoring that answer does not demand the overruling of anything. It demands only that the order of operations the Constitution prescribes be observed: that enforcement capacity precede the compliance it is invoked to compel. A fear answered by a fear settles nothing. A record ends the weighing. The record exists. It has only to be read.

---

# Part 4 — The Same Question Put to Other Sovereigns

*Part 4 puts the question the rule reserved, what a subject may do against a judge, to other sovereigns, and puts it to their texts as written. Chapter 4.1 fixes three questions and four terms, de jure law, common law, stare decisis and congruency, and states for each order the degree to which it is examined. Chapter 4.2 reads the English rule as it stands today, in which no published judgment found since 1975 has named* Floyd v. Barker*, so that the American chain alone carries the Star Chamber's sentence forward under the victim's name; chapter 4.3 the eighteenth-century rule that shielded the sovereign and left the servant answerable, and the one occasion, in 1972, on which that rule was put to the Supreme Court as a rule of due process and dismissed without opinion. Chapter 4.4 reads the laws of the kingdoms that answer the subject, and chapter 4.5 the codes that answer the sovereign against the judge. Chapter 4.6 finds the American text channeling the plea and the American corpus nullifying it. Chapter 4.7 sets the orders on two axes, who pays for a wrong decision and whether the judge is exposed for it, and states a finding in six parts, with England grouped on the first axis with the authoritarian codes and on the second the most immune of all. Chapter 4.8 states what the comparison adds to the transfer thesis.*

## 4.1 Three Questions Put to Every Order, and Four Terms Defined

A rule that sends the subject's plea against a judge to a forum can be judged by whether the forum answers. The kingdoms of Europe built fora that answer the subject. The authoritarian codes built fora that answer the sovereign against the judge. England abolished the King's forum and built neither. What remains in England is the reserved forum of 1607 with the King removed and nothing put in his place.

Three questions, each answerable from a text without any claim about a mind, organize the comparison. First, *channel or nullify*: when a subject says a judge has wronged him, is there a forum that must receive the plea and can answer it against the judge, or does the law say the plea does not exist? Second, *cost*: when the wrong is a wrong decision, who pays for it, the judge, the sovereign, or the subject who suffered it? Third, *transmission*: does the modern scope match the founding record, or was something removed or added in transmission?

*Floyd v. Barker* answered the first question by channeling: judges are not to be questioned for corruption "except it be before the King himself."[^cp1] The reserved forum was the King. Part 1 showed that the King never used it to answer a subject; he used it once, in 1616, to remove a judge on his own displeasure (chapter 1.3). This Part asks what other sovereigns did with the same question.

Four terms are kept apart throughout. *De jure law* is the enacted or promulgated text as written. *Common law* and *stare decisis* carry the meanings chapter 2.2 § B gave them, the corpus and the adherence to it. *Congruency* is the measured distance between a state's de jure law and its common law. This Part classifies de jure law for every jurisdiction. For America it also states the common law and the adherence to stare decisis, both examined in Parts 2 and 3; for England the common law is examined in part, in Part 1, and is to be brought to the same degree; for the other states the common law is capable of examination and is not examined here. Every comparative sentence below is a sentence about de jure law unless it says otherwise.

## 4.2 The English Rule as It Stands Today

In England the rule outlived its root and then lost sight of it. The immunity of judges stands today on statute and on a 1975 restatement: no action lies against a justice within his jurisdiction, and outside it only on proof of bad faith; no proceeding lies against the Crown for any act "of a judicial nature"; a judicial act may be questioned "only" by appeal or judicial review, with damages against the Crown alone and only for wrongful detention.[^ec7][^ec7_text] The restatement is *Sirros v. Moore*, where Lord Denning dated the rule from "the year 1613, if not before," the year of *The Marshalsea*, and Ormrod L.J. named *Floyd v. Barker* among "a few reported cases of attempts to bring such actions against judges of a superior court," a judge of assize "sued for conspiracy," in which it was held that no action lay "for that which he did openly in court as judge."[^ec8][^ec8_text] No published judgment of an English court since has been found naming *Floyd*, and the National Archives' index of every published judgment from 2001 returns none: the three most recent statements of the rule, in 2019, 2025 and 2026, cite *Sirros* and its nineteenth-century sources and stop there.[^ec9] The English chain no longer names its first link; the American chain still does, in *Randall* and in Justice Douglas's dissent, and it is the American chain alone that carries the Star Chamber's sentence forward under the victim's name.

Lord Denning's statement of the rule is the one every recent English judgment repeats, and it deserves to be read entire:

> Ever since the year 1613, if not before, it has been accepted in our law that no action is maintainable against a judge for anything said or done by him in the exercise of a jurisdiction which belongs to him. … No matter that the judge was under some gross error or ignorance, or was actuated by envy, hatred and malice, and all uncharitableness, he is not liable to an action. The remedy of the party aggrieved is to appeal to a Court of Appeal or to apply for habeas corpus, or a writ of error or certiorari, or take some such step to reverse his ruling. Of course, if the judge has accepted bribes or been in the least degree corrupt, or has perverted the course of justice, he can be punished in the criminal courts. That apart, however, a judge is not liable to an action for damages.[^cp2]

The year Lord Denning dates the rule from is 1613, *The Marshalsea*.[^cp3] *Re McC* kept the superior/inferior distinction Lord Denning had tried to abolish. The three judgments of 2019, 2025 and 2026 state the rule from *Sirros*, *Scott v. Stansfield*, *Kemp v. Neville* and *Re McC*, and the National Archives' full-text index of every published England and Wales judgment from 2001 to 2026 returns no result for *Floyd v. Barker*, while *Sirros v. Moore* returns more than a dozen.[^cp4]

Read on the texts alone, three things follow. The English rule is nullification of the subject's remedy against the judge for any decision within jurisdiction, and nullification of the remedy against the Crown for the same act. The subject's only channel is appeal, which corrects the decision and compensates nothing. The judge's exposure is criminal for bribery and disciplinary for personal misbehaviour; no forum receives a subject's plea that a decision was unjust.[^cp5]

## 4.3 The Eighteenth-Century Rule: The Sovereign Shielded, the Servant Answerable

Two English cases fix the configuration from which the modern rule departed, and both are read here at the page of the *English Reports*. In *Lane v. Cotton* (1701) the headnote as printed states the rule: the head of a public office under Government, with power to appoint and remove the servants of the office, "is not responsible to an individual for a loss occasioned by the default of such servants. The servant who is guilty of the default, is." Turton, Powys and Gould, JJ., held for the postmasters-general; Holt, C.J., for the plaintiff.[^cp16] In *Macbeath v. Haldimand* (1786) the headnote reads: "An officer appointed by Government, treating as an agent for the public, is not liable to be sued upon contracts made by him in that capacity."[^cp17a] Ashhurst, J., gave the reason: "Great inconveniencies would result from considering a governor or commander as personally responsible in such cases as the present. For no man would accept of any office of trust under Government upon such conditions."[^cp17b] Buller, J., stated the rule: "in any case where a man acts as agent for the public, and treats in that capacity, there is no pretence to say that he is personally liable."[^cp17c] Lord Mansfield, on the petition of right, observed that "though a petition of right would lie, yet it would probably produce no effect."[^cp17]

The Law Library of Congress states the configuration in one line: the King could do no wrong, so the Crown was not liable for its servants' torts, so "the servants of the crown became personally liable for any torts committed by them, even if they were acting in an official capacity."[^cp18] In tort the cost fell on the servant; in contract it fell on a petition the court doubted would produce anything. Neither case concerns a judge, and that is the point: the eighteenth-century sovereign's immunity was paid for by the servant's exposure, and the modern judicial rule keeps the sovereign's immunity while removing the servant's exposure. The subject, who in 1701 could sue the receiver Breeze, in 2026 can sue no one. The place of these cases in the English narrative is Part 1, chapter 1.6.

The configuration was put to the Supreme Court of the United States once, in 1972, as a rule of due process, and the record of that argument is on the shelf. Allison Krause was killed by the Ohio National Guard at Kent State University on 4 May 1970. Her father's action against the State was dismissed under Ohio's sovereign immunity; the Ohio Court of Appeals had stated, as the brief quotes it, that the State's agents were personally immune for acts on the State's behalf "well intentioned or not," and an Ohio statute of 1967 provided that a Guardsman on duty at a disorder "is not answerable in a civil suit for any act performed within the scope of his military duties … unless the act is one of willful or wanton misconduct."[^cp35a] Supporting the appeal, David E. Engdahl stated the eighteenth-century configuration in American terms. The rule of personal official liability had been "applied with remarkable strictness and harshness,"[^cp35b] so that a constitutional violation by an agent of the state admitted

> no defense to an action for damages against the agent that he had acted in good faith, on his best skill and judgment, in literal compliance with the terms of a statute, or even under the direct orders of a superior officer.

\noindent It was "only because there was considered to be no other effective means for such redress" that the Court had ever been able to find sovereign immunity to direct suit consistent with the Due Process Clause.[^cp35c] It followed, the brief argued, that a state which curtailed the officer's liability and retained its own immunity "would clearly violate the due process clause," a question "this Court has never considered."[^cp35d] The Court dismissed the appeal for want of a substantial federal question, and the Ohio judgment stood. The record therefore holds the American statement of the rule in *Lane v. Cotton*, put to the Court as a constitutional question and dismissed without opinion. The subject who in 1701 could sue the receiver Breeze could, in Ohio in 1972, sue neither the Guardsman nor the State.[^cp35][^cp35_text]

## 4.4 The Kingdoms That Answer the Subject

Other sovereigns answered the question *Floyd* reserved to the King, and the record of their answers is a record of texts. The kingdoms of Europe built the forum the rule promised and England never opened, and one kingdom outside Europe, Malaysia, opened a court against its own Rulers within living memory; each is read below at its own text.[^ec10][^ec10_text] These are classifications of de jure law, of the texts as written; they say nothing of any kingdom's common law or of the congruency between the two, and the point they make needs no more: the forum *Floyd* named and closed is a forum other sovereigns opened.

Each kingdom's own text says what a subject may do against a judge and what the forum may do in answer. Denmark's Special Court of Complaints, established in 1939 and composed of a Supreme Court judge, a high-court judge, a district judge, a legal scholar and an advocate, receives the plea of anyone "who considers himself wronged by improper or unseemly conduct on the part of a judge in the exercise of his office"; it may dismiss, express disapproval, fine the judge, or remove him where the conduct is grave or repeated, by judgment in open court, appealable to the Højesteret.[^cp6a] Decisions are not reviewable there; conduct is.[^cp6] Norway's Supervisory Committee for Judges, two judges, an advocate and two lay members appointed by the King, receives complaints from parties, counsel, experts, witnesses "and other persons" directly affected who consider that a judge has breached the duties of office or acted contrary to good judicial conduct; it may criticise or warn, and damages, criminal and removal proceedings go to the ordinary courts.[^cp7] Sweden's Penal Code makes it the crime of *tjänstefel* for a person exercising public authority intentionally or negligently to disregard what applies to the task; the Riksdag's Ombudsman may prosecute, judges have been convicted, and the State Disciplinary Board warns, docks pay or dismisses on referral.[^cp8] In the Netherlands whoever complains of how a judge behaved toward him in the exercise of his function may, unless the complaint concerns a judicial decision, ask the Procurator General to bring the judge before the Hoge Raad, which declares whether the judge behaved properly or not: no sanction, no damages, a declaration by the highest court.[^cp9]

Belgium keeps the living personal action of a subject against a judge. Judges "may be taken to task" (*pris à partie*) where they are guilty of fraud in the course of the proceedings or the judgment, where the law expressly so provides, where the law declares judges liable in damages, or where there is a denial of justice; the action is brought by petition at the registry of the Cour de cassation and served on the magistrate, and it descends from the Ordonnance of 1667.[^cp10] Spain and France moved the money to the State. Until 1 October 2015 the Spanish subject could sue the judge directly; the Organic Law of the Judiciary now provides that damages caused by judges in the exercise of their functions give rise to the liability of the State for judicial error or abnormal functioning of the administration of justice, "without, in any case, the injured parties being able to proceed directly against" the judges, the State recovering from the judge where the damage came from intent or gross fault; the 2015 amendment's preamble gives the reason, to align judges with other public employees and follow the Council of Europe.[^cp11] In France the *prise à partie* of the Ordonnance of 1667 ran through the 1806 Code until 1972; the Code of Judicial Organization now provides that the State must repair the damage caused by the defective functioning of the public service of justice, its liability engaged only by gross fault or a denial of justice, the State recovering from the magistrate before a civil chamber of the Cour de cassation, and the victim unable to act directly against the magistrate.[^cp12]

Malaysia is a kingdom of nine Rulers, and it changed course within living memory. From 1957 the King and the Rulers were immune from all civil and criminal proceedings. On 30 November 1992 a school hockey coach, Douglas Gomez, was summoned to the Sultan of Johor's palace and reported an assault by the Sultan himself. The Constitution (Amendment) Act 1993, passed on 9 March 1993 by 167 votes of 180, rewrote the immunity clause so that "[n]o proceedings whatsoever shall be brought in any court against the Ruler of a State in his personal capacity except in the Special Court established under Part XV," a court of the Lord President, the two Chief Justices and two others, which hears proceedings against the King or a Ruler "in his personal capacity"; no such action lies without the consent of the Attorney General; a charged Ruler ceases to act, a Regent sits, and a conviction carrying more than a day's imprisonment ends the reign unless pardoned.[^cp13a] Gillen's reading is that the form gives the executive additional leverage over the Rulers, three of the five judges being appointed at the Prime Minister's behest and the Attorney General's consent required before the court can sit.[^cp13] This is a kingdom whose founding record of absolute immunity was amended downward, on a subject's complaint, within four months.

Two further texts belong beside the kingdoms. Japan's Constitution requires the Diet to "set up an impeachment court from among the members of both Houses for the purpose of trying those judges against whom removal proceedings have been instituted," and forbids any disciplinary action against judges by an executive organ; any person may file a charge with the Judge Indictment Committee.[^cp14] Canada's Judges Act, as replaced in 2023, lets any person complain to the Canadian Judicial Council, seats lay persons on review and hearing panels, provides mandatory sanctions short of removal, and reserves removal to a recommendation to Parliament.[^cp15]

In every European kingdom, then, the subject has a forum that must receive a complaint about the judge's conduct and can answer it against the judge, up to removal in Denmark, personal damages in Belgium, or criminal conviction in Sweden. In France and Spain the money for a wrong decision comes from the State, which recoups from the judge on gross fault. The kingdoms channel. The one modern kingdom that changed course channeled the plea against the sovereign's own person to a court, with the executive's consent required to open it.

## 4.5 The Codes That Answer the Sovereign Against the Judge

The codes that hold judges most closely to account operate the other half of Coke's sentence: Russia, China and North Korea expose the judge to the sovereign for an unjust judgment or for the fault in reaching it, and leave the subject no remedy against him for the decision; each is read below at its own text, with Saudi Arabia's beside them.[^ec11][^ec11_text]

Russia's Constitution declares that "judges shall possess immunity" and that everyone has the right to state compensation for damage caused by the unlawful actions of state bodies and their officials.[^cp19] The Law on the Status of Judges provides that a judge, including after the termination of his powers, cannot be held to any liability for the opinion expressed by him in the administration of justice and the decision taken by the court, unless a final criminal verdict finds him guilty of criminal abuse or of rendering a knowingly unjust judgment; a criminal case against a judge is opened by the Chairman of the Investigative Committee with the consent of the Higher Qualification Collegium; and disciplinary sanctions, from remark to early termination of powers, lie for culpable conduct that lowered the authority of the judiciary, "including as a result of a gross violation of the rights of participants in the proceedings."[^cp20] The Civil Code's last sentence on the point is that harm caused in the administration of justice is compensated only if the judge's guilt is established by a criminal verdict that has entered into force; the Constitutional Court in 2001 held that condition constitutional for judicial acts resolving a dispute on the merits and opened non-merits wrongs, delay and unlawful procedural acts, to compensation on any judicial finding of fault.[^cp21]

China's Constitution gives citizens "the right to make to relevant State organs complaints or charges against, or exposures of, any State organ or functionary for violation of law or dereliction of duty," and a right to compensation for losses caused by infringement of their civil rights by any State organ or functionary.[^cp22] The State Compensation Law pays for wrongful detention and conviction and for a court's unlawful compulsory measures or wrongful execution in civil and administrative cases, and requires the paying organ to charge its functionaries who acted intentionally or with gross negligence with part or the whole of the cost; no article compensates an erroneous judgment on the merits in a civil or administrative case.[^cp23] The Judges Law sanctions the judge who has "intentionally violated laws and regulations in handling cases" or "wrongly handled a case and caused heavy losses due to gross negligence," and judge disciplinary boards find intentional breach of duties, gross negligence, ordinary negligence or no breach; the Supervision Law places the civil servants of the people's courts within the supervisory organs' remit.[^cp24]

North Korea's Constitution entitles citizens to submit complaints and petitions, which "the State shall investigate and deal with … impartially as stipulated by law"; judges and people's assessors are elected and recalled by the Presidium of the Supreme People's Assembly and the local assemblies, and the Central Court "is accountable to the Supreme People's Assembly."[^cp25] The Criminal Law makes it an offence for a court worker to reach an unjust decision or judgment, punishable by up to one year of labour training and, in grave cases, up to five years of reform through labour; the Law on Compensation for Damage makes the person culpable for the damage pay, and no clause makes the State liable for a judicial act.[^cp26]

Saudi Arabia's Law of the Judiciary is the closest formal counterpart of the English rule. Judges "may not be sued for acts carried out in the course of performance of their duties except in accordance with the conditions and rules pertaining to the disciplining of judges"; a three-judge Disciplinary Panel of the Supreme Judicial Council hears the matter in confidential session, its judgment "shall not be subject to appeal," and the penalties are reprimand and termination of service; complaints against judges are investigated by the Judicial Inspection Department only after the Supreme Judicial Council refers them.[^cp27]

In each of these codes the judge is exposed to the sovereign: criminally for an unjust judgment in Russia and North Korea, by discipline and recourse for intentional violation or gross negligence in China, by political recall in North Korea, by a confidential unappealable panel in Saudi Arabia. In none of them does the subject have a money remedy against the judge for the decision, and in Russia and China the State's liability for a merits decision is conditioned on a criminal verdict or excluded. The subject's plea is received, by Russia's collegia, by China's constitutional complaint, by North Korea's petition, but it is answered by the sovereign's apparatus, on the sovereign's terms, and the finding runs against the judge only when the sovereign so decides. This is Coke's reserved forum in operation: the King questioning the judge. It is the forum Part 1 shows England's King never used for a subject.

Read against the two questions this book has put to every text, the codes sort with a clarity the doctrine's defenders would not expect. On who pays for a wrong decision, the English and American de jure law stand with these codes: the subject pays, and the kingdoms alone moved the cost to the sovereign. On the judge's own exposure the order inverts: the codes punish and recall, the kingdoms censure and remove, and the Anglo-American judge answers to no one for a decision, the exposure Part 1, chapter 1.3 documented in the Star Chamber's own docket surviving abroad as an instrument of the state and extinguished at home. Anglo-American immunity thus shares with the codes the nullification of the subject's remedy and differs from them by nullifying the sovereign's as well. What England abolished in 1641 was the King's half of the forum; what it never built was the subject's.[^ec12]

## 4.6 America: The Statute Opens the Courthouse, the Case Law Closes It

America is not a kingdom, and it earns its place in this Part on the vocabulary rule: America is the one jurisdiction in this record where the de jure law and the common law answer the subject's question in opposite directions, so the two must be stated separately and the congruency between them measured rather than assumed.

The de jure texts channel. The Constitution provides that judges "shall hold their Offices during good Behaviour"; that "all civil Officers of the United States, shall be removed from Office on Impeachment for, and Conviction of, Treason, Bribery, or other high Crimes and Misdemeanors"; that "Judgment in Cases of Impeachment shall not extend further than to removal from Office, and disqualification …: but the Party convicted shall nevertheless be liable and subject to Indictment, Trial, Judgment and Punishment, according to Law"; and that the people have "the right … to petition the Government for a redress of grievances."[^cp28b] No clause of the Constitution confers immunity on a judge.[^cp28] Section 1983 provides:

> Every person who, under color of any statute, ordinance, regulation, custom, or usage, of any State …, subjects … any citizen … to the deprivation of any rights, privileges, or immunities secured by the Constitution and laws, shall be liable to the party injured in an action at law, suit in equity, or other proper proceeding for redress.

\noindent It has one exception: "in any action brought against a judicial officer for an act or omission taken in such officer's judicial capacity, injunctive relief shall not be granted unless a declaratory decree was violated or declaratory relief was unavailable."[^cp29a] The exception was added in 1996; before 1996 the de jure text contained no word about judges.[^cp29] The judicial-conduct statute lets "any person alleging that a judge has engaged in conduct prejudicial to the effective and expeditious administration of the business of the courts" file a written complaint; it lets the chief judge dismiss a complaint "directly related to the merits of a decision or procedural ruling"; and it lets the judicial council suspend assignments, censure or reprimand privately or publicly, certify disability or request retirement, but not remove.[^cp30] A person "unjustly convicted" whose conviction is set aside on the ground of innocence may recover from the United States, up to $100,000 for each year of incarceration for a plaintiff unjustly sentenced to death and $50,000 for any other; the Federal Tort Claims Act excepts discretionary functions and claims "arising out of … false imprisonment, false arrest, malicious prosecution, abuse of process," and names no judge; and whoever under color of law willfully subjects any person to the deprivation of federal rights commits a crime.[^cp31] Read as written, the American de jure law channels. The subject may petition; "every person" acting under color of state law "shall be liable"; any person may complain of a judge; the innocent convict is paid; the willful deprivation is a crime. The only de jure nullification is the 1996 proviso, and it reaches injunctions alone.

The common law nullifies, and it begins by citing the English cases as evidence. *Randall v. Brigham* cites *The Marshalsea*, *Floyd v. Barker* and *Hamond v. Howell* in its footnotes. *Bradley v. Fisher* holds that judges of courts of superior or general jurisdiction "are not liable to civil actions for their judicial acts, even when such acts are in excess of their jurisdiction, and are alleged to have been done maliciously or corruptly," excepting only acts "in the clear absence of all jurisdiction over the subject-matter."[^cp28a] *Pierson v. Ray* holds that § 1983 did not abolish the immunity:

> Few doctrines were more solidly established at common law than the immunity of judges from liability for damages for acts committed within their judicial jurisdiction, as this Court recognized when it adopted the doctrine, in *Bradley v. Fisher* … This immunity applies even when the judge is accused of acting maliciously and corruptly.

\noindent Justice Douglas, dissenting, traced the rule to its root:

> Since the King could do no wrong, the judges, his delegates for dispensing justice, "ought not to be drawn into question for any supposed corruption [for this tends] to the slander of the justice of the King." *Floyd & Barker*, 12 Co. Rep. 23, 25 … Because the judges were the personal delegates of the King they should be answerable to him alone.

\noindent *Stump v. Sparkman* holds that

> a judge will not be deprived of immunity because the action he took was in error, was done maliciously, or was in excess of his authority; rather, he will be subject to liability only when he has acted in the "clear absence of all jurisdiction."

\noindent *Pulliam v. Allen* held that "judicial immunity is not a bar to prospective injunctive relief against a judicial officer," and twelve years later Congress wrote the contrary rule into § 1983.[^cp32a] *Mireles v. Waco* holds that judicial immunity "is an immunity from suit, not just from ultimate assessment of damages," is "not overcome by allegations of bad faith or malice," and "is overcome in only two sets of circumstances," nonjudicial acts and acts "in the complete absence of all jurisdiction."[^cp32]

The executive's own manual for asserting immunity states the corpus in the same terms. The Department of Justice's Torts Branch monograph of 1985 sorts the immunities as this Part does: "Some applications, such as Speech or Debate and Presidential immunities, are grounded in the Constitution itself…. Others, while rooted in our common law traditions, are essentially judge-made doctrines." Of judges:

> Judges are protected by an absolute immunity that was recognized at English common law. The Supreme Court adopted the immunity early in *Bradley v. Fisher*…. This immunity is so firmly established that a clear indication in the legislative record will be required before the Court will presume that Congress intended to abolish the doctrine in enacting general remedial provisions.

\noindent Its reading of the rule's reach, that *Bradley* and *Stump* "completely bar any inquiry into a judge's hidden reasons for deciding a matter before the court," is the first of the three bars restated in 1985.[^cp33] The government's litigating position thus states the congruency mechanism in one sentence: the corpus is presumed to survive any de jure text that does not name it.

Congruency, measured. On the subject's damages remedy the de jure text says "every person … shall be liable" and the common law says the judge is not; the distance is total, and *Pierson* closes it by reading the corpus into the statute.[^cp34a] The corpus it read was not one rule: on the count of every state then existing, thirteen had adopted the absolute rule by 1871 and six held judges liable if they acted maliciously, so the statute's "every person" met a common law split thirteen to six, and *Pierson* took the majority's rule as "solidly established."[^cp34] On injunctive relief the common law said the remedy survived, in *Pulliam*, and the de jure text was then amended to the corpus, in 1996. The one measured act of congruency in the American record runs from the corpus to the text, not from the text to the corpus. As to stare decisis, *Pierson*'s sentence states the adherence claim exactly: the Court "recognized … when it adopted the doctrine, in *Bradley*," and stands by *Bradley*; *Bradley*'s own authority is *Randall*'s footnote of English cases, which are evidence of a common law received, not decisions of the American hierarchy.[^cp34b] The adherence is to the Court's own corpus; the corpus's ground is the King's forum that Justice Douglas names and the majority does not. The genealogy is Part 2, chapters 2.3 to 2.5; its constitutional consequences are Part 3.

On the two axes, then: the de jure text gives the subject a damages action against every person under color of law and pays the innocent convict; the common law removes the judge from "every person"; the Tort Claims Act offers no route; the subject pays for a merits error, as in England. The payment to the innocent convict is the counterpart of China's criminal compensation and of nothing in the kingdoms' regimes for judicial error. On the judge's exposure there is none civil at common law; criminal exposure under the deprivation-of-rights and bribery statutes; impeachment for "high Crimes and Misdemeanors" with removal the only judgment; and a complaint procedure open to "any person" that by statute excludes the merits and cannot remove. America stands with England on both axes, with this difference: England's de jure law says the same thing its common law says, and America's does not.

The mechanism appears again in American law outside the judicial line, in the one setting where the Constitution's text is said to commit the question to the political branches. When a foreign official is sued in an American court for acts done in office, the foreign state may ask the Department of State to recognize his immunity; if the Department agrees, the Department of Justice files a suggestion of immunity, and the court, on the Executive's own account of the law, "surrendered its jurisdiction."[^si1] In 2016 the United States filed such a suggestion for Israel's former Minister of Defense, sued by the parents of an American citizen killed by Israeli forces at sea. It told the court that "the Department of State has determined that Barak is immune from suit" and that "[t]he Court must defer to that determination"; that the United States "expresses no view on the merits of Plaintiffs' claims"; and that "it is for the Executive Branch, not the courts, to determine whether the conduct alleged was taken in a foreign official's official capacity," a fact the foreign state's diplomatic note supplied.[^si2] The court dismissed, and the court of appeals affirmed because to judge the minister "would be to enforce a rule of law against the sovereign state of Israel," declining to say whether the Executive's letter bound it or only weighed with it.[^si3]

The three bars of 1607 are in that record, relabelled. The plea is routed to the sovereign alone: the foreign state asks, the Executive determines, the court surrenders. The forum that receives it does not adjudicate: the branch whose word closes the courthouse disclaims the merits. And no proof is heard against the record: the one fact on which the immunity turns is taken from the court and the jury and answered by the accused sovereign's own note, with the gravest wrongs known to the law of nations expressly no exception.[^si4] The origin is the one this book has traced. Marshall's first case of exemption in 1812 was "the exemption of the person of the sovereign," because a sovereign "is not understood as intending to subject himself to a jurisdiction incompatible with his dignity," and the receiver he named was "the sovereign power of the nation," which "is alone competent to avenge wrongs committed by a sovereign," in questions "for diplomatic, rather than legal discussion."[^si5] The servant took the sovereign's status in 1895, when "the acts of the official representatives of the state" became "those of the state itself," the reverse of the eighteenth-century configuration chapter 4.3 records, in which the sovereign's immunity was paid for by the servant's exposure.[^si6] What reached the killing of a citizen in 2016 came by citation. The sentence that the courts "will not embarrass" the political branch "by assuming an antagonistic jurisdiction" was written in 1882 of claims settled by "negotiation" or "war," was applied in 1943 and 1945 to the seizure of foreign ships, and was applied to a former minister accused of an extrajudicial killing without any court adjudicating the step.[^si7] Whether an Executive letter binds a court on a question of law the Supreme Court has never decided; one circuit holds that it does not, the Executive calls that holding legal error, and the Solicitor General has twice asked the Court not to resolve it.[^si8] This is the only line of the doctrine in which a receiver is named at all, and the receiver is diplomacy. The application differs from the judicial line in two respects. The first is the relation. Every immunity this book has traced sits inside one bond, sovereign and subject: the subject owes allegiance, the sovereign owes protection, and the immunity is the sovereign refusing its own subject's plea in a cause where the sovereign is the accused. The parents owed Israel nothing and Israel owed them nothing; there was no bond for a plea of immunity to breach. The bond that existed ran between the citizen and the United States, and it was the United States that closed its court, not because it was the accused but because it preferred its relations with the accused, on the ground that its courts "will not embarrass" the political branch. A foreign sovereign has no immunity of its own in an American court. Marshall grounded every exemption on "the consent of the nation itself," a consent the forum implies and may withdraw, so the immunity the court of appeals gave was not Israel's act but the forum's, a standing waiver made on the citizen's behalf without asking him, and the reason it is given is reciprocity: each sovereign keeps its officials out of the others' courts, and each pays its share with its subjects' remedies. The clause the court rested on, that to judge the minister "would be to enforce a rule of law against the sovereign state of Israel," is a real-party test taken from the American Law Institute's Restatement, and it attributes to the state exactly what the domestic line refuses to attribute. *Ex parte Young* holds that an officer acting against the supreme law is "stripped of his official or representative character," because "[t]he State has no power to impart to him any immunity from responsibility"; the foreign line holds that the unlawful act is the state's own, and that is why no one answers for it.[^si11] The second is the repair. Congress holds it, and used it for foreign states in 1976. It did not use it for their servants, and it could not use it, by statute, to take from a citizen the court and the jury the Constitution gives him (chapter 3.2 § B).

What the second respect leaves open, the first closes, and what it costs is the record. A judgment is a record, and the record is what the third bar denies. The dismissal means that no court has found that a citizen was killed, by whom, or under what authority; in law the killing has not happened, and the one fact the immunity turned on rests on the accused state's note.[^si4] The declaration is the justice a payment cannot supply, and the law knows it: the Court holds that nominal damages redress "a completed violation of a legal right," because "every violation [of a right] imports damage," so a dollar and a finding is a judgment where a letter and a dismissal is not.[^si12] The record is also how this law has grown. In 1980 the Second Circuit held a Paraguayan police official answerable in an American court for torture, with the United States as friend of the court on the plaintiffs' side, and Congress answered the judgment in 1992 with the statute the parents sued under, which the House report described as establishing "an unambiguous and modern basis for" claims of torture and extrajudicial killing.[^si13] In 1964 the Court held that "the Judicial Branch will not examine the validity of a taking of property within its own territory by a foreign sovereign government," and Congress answered within the year that "no court in the United States shall decline on the ground of the federal act of state doctrine to make a determination on the merits," beside the suspension of aid it had already enacted for the citizens whose property was taken.[^si14] Holders of terrorism judgments against Iran could not reach its assets until Congress made the assets reachable, twice, and the Court upheld the second act; a statute reaching back to the attacks of September 11, 2001 passed over the President's objections.[^si15] In each the court's record came first and the political branches acted on it. Hamilton gave the federal courts the causes of foreigners for the stated reason that "the denial or perversion of justice by the sentences of courts" is "with reason classed among the just causes of war": adjudication was the alternative to vengeance, and a judgment in hand is what lets the Executive negotiate from a finding rather than avenge an allegation.[^si16] The Suggestion of Immunity removes the record, and with it the receiver the Constitution names, the justice the parents came for, and the thing on which repair depends. What it removes is not only the parents' case. The record of what a foreign state did to a citizen is also the fact on which Congress acts, when it declares war or grants reprisal or defines an offence against the law of nations or appropriates for the common defence, and the fact on which the one crime the Constitution defines depends, since treason is adherence to an enemy and an enemy is what the political branches say it is. Chapter 3.2 § D names the clauses. The branch that withholds the record from the court holds every one of them.

The suggestion was not the first form of the mechanism to reach an American killed by Israeli forces. On 8 June 1967 Israeli aircraft and torpedo boats attacked the USS *Liberty* in international waters off the Sinai; thirty-four of her crew were killed, and the Department of State afterwards presented 164 claims for the wounded. No suit was brought and none could have been. The statute that opened the courthouse to a citizen's case against a foreign state was nine years away, and before it the immunity was the Department's to give by letter, on the practice of 1943 and 1945 this chapter has already named; the Court itself later admitted that "political considerations led to suggestions of immunity in cases where immunity would not have been available." The claim went where the doctrine sends it. The Department told Israel the attack "must be condemned as an act of military irresponsibility reflecting reckless disregard for human life," presented the claim in its own name, and Israel paid the United States Government $3,323,500 in 1968 "on behalf of the families of the 34 men killed," $3,566,457 in 1969 for the wounded and the Government's own expenses, and $6 million in 1980 for the ship.[^si17] The Court's name for that transaction, given in 1981 for the hostages' claims against Iran, is that the President "has exercised the power, acquiesced in by Congress, to settle claims and, as such, has simply effected a change in the substantive law governing the lawsuit," a power it found in "long-continued practice, known to and acquiesced in by Congress," of which the *Liberty* payments are part.[^si18] The one forum the crew had was the Navy's Court of Inquiry, and its senior counsel swore in 2004 that the court was given a week, was kept from the Israeli witnesses, did not receive "the more than sixty witness declarations from men who had been hospitalized," and was told by its president that he had been "ordered … to conclude that the attack was a case of 'mistaken identity' despite overwhelming evidence to the contrary." That is an officer's sworn account of what a dead admiral told him, and this book states it as no more; but it is the only account of the inquiry by anyone who sat in it, and it is now in the Congressional Record, placed there on 8 June 2026 by a Member who had "at least a dozen" of the survivors in the gallery and asked the House, fifty-nine years after the fact, for an investigation and a resolution: "Support them while they are still alive."[^si19] That the crew were servicemen changes nothing in the account. Congress has since said so in terms: the statute that strips a designated state's immunity for an extrajudicial killing names "a member of the armed forces" beside "a national of the United States" as a victim whose claim it opens and gives him the action by name; the bar on a serviceman's claim for injury "incident to their service" runs against his own government and no other; and the statute's own exclusion of an "act of war," defined as an act "in the course of … armed conflict between military forces of any origin," marks the line between an enemy's attack, which is the service, and an ally's, which is not, though where the *Liberty* falls on that line the statute leaves to argument.[^si20] What this book holds is that the line runs with the bond: the sovereign that sends a citizen into the company of its allies owes him its court against those allies no less than against a stranger, and no clause says otherwise. In 1967 there was no statute and no court, and the record was made once, by the sovereign, and fixed. In 2016 there was a statute and a court, and the record was removed by letter. Between the two lies the reform of 1976, which took the immunity from the Department and gave it to the courts under a rule, and which the suggestion for the servant reverses. The receiver in both is the same, and so is the plaintiff's sovereign, and so is the accused.

The suggestion rests on a premise stated in 1812 and not examined since. Marshall found the foreign sovereign's exemption in the forum's own consent and said what kind of consent it was: express or implied, and in the latter case "less determinate, exposed more to the uncertainties of construction," drawn from a world "of distinct sovereignties, possessing equal rights and equal independence" and from what such sovereigns would agree to.[^si21] That is a presumption about facts, and the Court's other doctrines put facts of that kind to proof. *Hilton v. Guyot* defined comity with the citizen inside it, "having due regard both to international duty and convenience, and to the rights of its own citizens or of other persons who are under the protection of its laws," and refused conclusive effect to a French judgment "upon the broad ground that international law is founded upon mutuality and reciprocity," France giving none to ours.[^si22] *Sabbatino* made the act of state doctrine yield to "a treaty or other unambiguous agreement regarding controlling legal principles," and the Act of 1976 itself withholds the immunity where the foreign state "has waived its immunity either explicitly or by implication."[^si23] Two questions follow that the suggestion in *Doğan* never asked: whether the two states are bound to each other by instrument, and whether the foreign state's own law gives the relief the letter withholds. For the United States and Israel both are answered on the record. The Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and Navigation of 1951, in force since 1954, accords the nationals of each party national treatment "with respect to access to the courts of justice … in all degrees of jurisdiction, both in pursuit and in defense of their rights."[^si24] Israel's statute on the State's liability provides that "for the purpose of liability in torts, the state is like any incorporated body," with a proviso for "combatant activity of the Israel Defence Forces" that is raised as a plea and decided; its Supreme Court records that thousands of claims by inhabitants of the territories injured by its security forces "were tried in the courts in Israel in accordance with the Israeli law of torts"; and its own statute on the immunity of foreign states is drawn on the lines of ours, with the same exception for a tort within the territory.[^si25] Where the foreign state gives the relief by its own law and is bound by treaty to open its courts to our nationals as we open ours to its, there is no incompatibility of sovereignty or of jurisdiction for a court to presume. What is left is the alliance itself, a fact made by treaty and statute in the open, which the letter neither proves nor cites.

## 4.7 Two Axes: Who Pays for the Wrong, Who May Punish the Judge, and Where England Sits

| Jurisdiction | A. The subject's money remedy for a wrong decision | B. The judge's personal exposure for the decision |
|---|---|---|
| **England** | none against the judge; none against the Crown; Human Rights Act damages only for wrongful detention. **The subject pays.** | none civil; criminal only for bribery or corruption; discipline for personal conduct only; removal by Address of both Houses |
| **America (de jure / common law)** | de jure: "every person … shall be liable"; the innocent convict is paid; the Tort Claims Act excepts discretion and false imprisonment. Common law: the judge is not within "every person." **The subject pays for a merits error.** | de jure: impeachment for "high Crimes and Misdemeanors," removal the only judgment; the deprivation-of-rights crime; complaint by "any person," merits excluded, no removal. Common law: none civil, "even when … maliciously or corruptly"; the injunction survived until the text was amended in 1996 |
| **Continental kingdoms** | **The State pays** (France on gross fault or denial of justice; Spain for judicial error; Denmark, Norway, the Netherlands and Belgium by state-liability regimes) | none civil save Belgium's *prise à partie*; discipline by courts and boards the subject may reach; Sweden's criminal *tjänstefel*; Denmark's removal by the Klageret |
| **Russia** | the treasury pays only on a criminal verdict against the judge for a merits decision; non-merits wrongs since 2001. **The subject pays for a merits error.** | **criminal offence** of a knowingly unjust judgment; disciplinary removal by the collegia |
| **China** | the State pays for wrongful detention or conviction and unlawful enforcement; **no compensation for a merits error.** | discipline and recourse for intentional violation or gross negligence; Party-state supervision |
| **North Korea** | the culpable person pays; no state clause. **The subject pays.** | **criminal offence** of an unjust judgment; **recall** by the assembly at any time |
| **Saudi Arabia** | no suit save through discipline. **The subject pays.** | discipline only, reprimand or termination, confidential, no appeal |

The finding, stated as the texts allow it, has six parts. First, on Axis A, England groups with Russia, China, North Korea and Saudi Arabia: in all five the injured subject carries the cost of a wrong decision, and the continental kingdoms stand apart, having moved that cost onto the State. Second, on Axis B the association inverts: England's judge is the most immune of all, with no civil exposure, no criminal exposure short of bribery, and no removal for a decision, while the Russian, Chinese and North Korean codes make the judge personally punishable for an unjust judgment and removable by a political or supervisory organ; the Coke-era exposure of the judge, the censures and fines Part 1 records, survives in those codes as an instrument of control and was extinguished in England. Third, the Saudi code is the closest formal counterpart of the English rule: a nullification clause, discipline as the only route, and that route controlled by the judiciary's own council. Fourth, therefore, Anglo-American immunity shares with the authoritarian codes the nullification of the subject's remedy, and differs from them by also nullifying the sovereign's remedy against the judge; the kingdoms differ from both by paying. Fifth, America stands with England on both axes and adds the fact the vocabulary exists to record: its de jure law channels and its common law nullifies, so that the American position on Axis A is a statement about the corpus, not the text, and the congruency between the two was closed once, in 1996, by amending the text to the corpus. Sixth, the four objects are not equally examined across the table, and the table must say so: for America the common law and the adherence to stare decisis have been examined in Parts 2 and 3, and the congruency question is those Parts' subject; for England the common law has been examined in part, in Part 1 and in chapter 4.2, and is to be examined to the same degree; for the kingdoms and the authoritarian states this book classifies de jure law only, their common law, congruency and adherence to stare decisis being capable of examination on the texts now held and not examined here.

What the table claims, in the book's terms, is therefore narrow. It classifies each state's de jure law, the enacted or promulgated text read as written. It does not classify any state's common law. It does not measure the degree of congruency between a state's de jure law and its common law: whether China's boards and Russia's collegia decide as the Judges Law and the Status of Judges law say, or as the Party-state directs; whether North Korea's recall operates as the Constitution reads or as the regime's instrument; whether Malaysia's Special Court hears what the Constitution says it hears or what the Attorney General's consent lets through. And it does not measure any state's adherence to stare decisis. A de jure code that exposes the judge to the sovereign is not thereby a common law that protects the subject. The claim is the one that can be read off the pages: on the one question the subject asks of the de jure law, who pays for the wrong done to him, England's text answers as Moscow's, Beijing's, Pyongyang's and Riyadh's texts answer, and not as Copenhagen's, Oslo's, Stockholm's, The Hague's, Brussels's, Madrid's or Paris's.

The three-way distinction is general to governance and is the one American jurisprudence has misplaced. American usage runs "common law," "case law" and "precedent" together, so that a stare decisis claim ("well settled") is made about a body that is not the court's own corpus, and a de jure text is read through what courts have since done with it rather than as written. This book keeps the three apart by rule: *de jure* for the text, *common law* for the corpus of decision and custom, *stare decisis* for adherence to that corpus, and *congruency* for the measured distance between the first and the second.

## 4.8 What the Comparison Adds to the Transfer Thesis

The transfer thesis holds that *Floyd* moved the subject's plea against a judge to the King, and the King never answered it. The comparative record adds three things.

First, the reserved forum is not a dead letter everywhere. The authoritarian codes operate it: the sovereign's apparatus questions the judge, finds against him, removes or punishes him. What they operate is the King's half of Coke's sentence, "the King himself shall take account of it, and no other."[^cp36] The subject's half, the de jure duty "to deliver justice to all his subjects," is the half that has no forum in those codes either. England and the authoritarian states share the removal of the subject's half; they differ in that the authoritarian states kept the King's half and England discarded it.

Second, the kingdoms show the alternative that was available. Denmark's Klageret, Norway's Tilsynsutvalg, the Dutch Procurator General's motion, the Belgian *prise à partie*, the French and Spanish state-liability regimes are each a forum that receives the subject's plea and can answer it, without exposing the judge to the sovereign's displeasure. They are the channel Coke's rule promised and England never built. Malaysia built one within four months in 1993 when a subject's plea against the sovereign himself could no longer be refused.

Third, the eighteenth-century English record shows England once had the channel for every servant but the judge: the subject could sue the receiver, the constable, the sheriff. The modern rule extended the judge's exemption to the Crown and confined the subject to appeal. The transmission did not merely remove the King's forum; it removed the servant's exposure that had made the King's immunity tolerable in 1701.

The thesis therefore stands on a wider record: the rule's reserved forum was never used for the subject in England; it is used against judges, and only against judges, in the states whose codes are set beside it here; and the kingdoms that kept a subject's forum are the ones whose immunity is narrowest.

The full record, with every text and its tier, is the author's *The Kingdoms That Answer: The Subject's Plea Against the Judge in the Comparative Record* (2026) (unpublished, on file with author), §§ 3–7; American de jure law is set out there as well (§ 6A) and in this book at chapter 4.6, save one text not read there: 28 U.S.C. § 351(a), which lets "[a]ny person" complain of a judge.
