# Part 5 — The Older Record: Remedies Against the Judge before 1607

*Part 5 reaches back before 1607 and reads six legal orders for what each did with the judge who did wrong, in the vocabulary Part 4 has fixed and at the page of the edition named in each note. Chapter 5.1 puts the question. Chapter 5.2 reads Babylon and Athens: twelvefold restitution and expulsion from the seat, appeal and the citizen's charge at the audit. Chapter 5.3 reads Rome: the subject's action against the judge who made the cause his own, the Code's penalty on the judge who judged badly, and the sovereign's prosecution. Chapter 5.4 reads Byzantium, which kept both remedies, salaried the bench and bound the emperor to the laws. Chapter 5.5 reads Francia, the duty stated in the capitularies and death for the bribed judge in the law. Chapter 5.6 reads England before the rule, the fine to the king and the loss of every judicial dignity from 959, with the judge's oath that he knew no better a defence to the penalty that bars no complaint and closes no forum. Chapter 5.7 states the finding and its degree of examination: on no page read did any of the six exempt a judge, for what he did as judge, from the remedy its own text provided.*

## 5.1 The Question Put to the Older Record

Parts 1 to 4 read a rule from 1607 forward: what a subject may do against a judge, and what the sovereign may do, once the judge has decided within his jurisdiction. Chapter 1.4 has already put three Roman texts to the reader, for what the 1607 court borrowed from them and reversed; this Part reads Rome's own law of the judge, and the law of the orders before and after it, for what each did with the judge who did wrong. It puts the same two questions to the legal orders that stood before England's, and it puts them in the same way, to the enacted or compiled text read as written, in the edition named in each note, at the page. It asks of each order whether the subject held a remedy against the judge, whether the sovereign held one, and whether either was displaced by a shield. It does not ask how far any order's courts obeyed its texts; where a case-digest survives, as it does for eleventh-century Byzantium, that is said, and where nothing but the code survives, that is said too.[^ao1]

The answer the older record gives is uniform in one respect and various in every other. Six legal orders, from the eighteenth century before Christ to the twelfth century after, each answer the judge with a remedy. None answers him with an immunity. The forms differ: restitution and expulsion, denunciation and audit, a personal action and a public prosecution, a salaried bench, a fine to the king and the loss of rank, the forfeiture of a court. The absence is the same in all of them. The rule Part 1 found made in 1607, that a judge answers to no subject, and to no court but the King's own, for what he did as judge, has no antecedent on any page read here.

## 5.2 Babylon and Athens: Restitution, Expulsion, the Citizen's Charge

The oldest text is the plainest. The fifth section of Hammurabi's code provides that if a judge has pronounced a judgment, delivered a sealed decision, and afterwards altered it,

> they shall call that judge to account for the alteration of the judgment which he had pronounced, and he shall pay twelve-fold the penalty which was in said judgment; and, in the assembly, they shall expel him from his seat of judgment, and he shall not return, and with the judges in a case he shall not take his seat.[^ao2]

\noindent The section before it makes the bribed witness bear the penalty of the case he swore in. The remedy is the assembly's; the measure is twelvefold; the judge is removed for life. The text is read in Harper's translation of 1904, and this Part claims no more of it than the translation carries.

Athens in the fourth century before Christ put the judge's superiors under the courts and the courts within reach of any citizen. The *Constitution of Athens* records that the people took from the Council of Five Hundred "the power to put to death and to imprison and to fine," and enacted that when the Council condemned or fined anyone, the thesmothetae were to bring the condemnation before a court, "and whatever the jurors voted, that was to be final."[^ao3] The Council still tried most magistrates, "and especially those who handle money"; but "the judgment is not final, but subject to appeal to the court," and "private persons also may lay an information against any magistrate they please for not using the laws," with the same appeal.[^ao4] After a magistrate's term, and after the public auditors had passed his accounts, a further officer sat in the market by the statue of his tribe's hero, and "if anyone wishes to lodge a charge, private or public, against any of those who have rendered their accounts before the court, within three days of the day on which he rendered them," he wrote on a whitened tablet his own name and the defendant's, the wrong he alleged and the damages he claimed, and gave it to that officer, who examined it and sent private charges to the deme-judges and public ones to the thesmothetae for the court, "and whatever the jurors decide is final."[^ao5] Sandys's comment on the passage states its sense exactly: even after the audit had been passed, officials remained "liable to be prosecuted by private persons in respect to the manner in which they had discharged their duties."[^ao6] Athens therefore held both remedies open, the citizen's denunciation and the citizen's post-audit charge, and set the governing body itself under the court's final vote.

## 5.3 Rome: The Subject's Action and the Sovereign's Prosecution

Rome kept two remedies against the judge open at once and left them in the *Digest* for the Byzantine compilers to carry forward. The first was the subject's. Ulpian's title on trials provides that a judge "is understood to make the suit his own when he has given judgment with evil intent in fraud of the law," which is shown "if he is clearly convicted of favour, or enmity, or even bribery," and he is then compelled "to pay the true value of the suit."[^ao7] The liability was personal: Julian's view that the action lay against the judge's heir "is not true, and has been noted by many."[^ao8] The second remedy was the sovereign's. Under the title on the Julian law against extortion, any person "who, holding some power, has taken money for judging or not judging, or for deciding" was liable; the action ran against his heirs for one year from his death; and by Macer's day the penalty had become extraordinary, exile or worse, and capital where a judge condemned an innocent man for money.[^ao9] The two remedies differed on the one point the English pleas of Part 1 would also raise, survival against the heir, and the difference is itself the proof that two distinct actions were in view.

The elementary book taught the first remedy to every student, and the *Codex* carried both remedies into the sixth century. The *Institutes* class the judge's liability among the obligations that arise as if from delict: the judge "who delivers an unjust or partial decision" cannot "but be held to have done a wrong, even though it may be due to ignorance," and "a pecuniary penalty will be imposed on him."[^ao36] Ortolan, commenting on the passage, draws out two things the text leaves implicit. The action against the judge did not wait on the failure of other remedies: appeal generally lay, and a sentence given against the law could be treated as null and the cause begun afresh, yet the litigant "appears to have had, in every case, the action against the judge who had made the suit his own," since he might be unwilling or unable to begin again against an adversary who had, for instance, become insolvent. And the judge's wrong is classed as arising as if from delict only because no body was injured, as it would be under the *lex Aquilia*, so that "the greater or lesser culpability of the physician or the judge counts for nothing in the question."[^ao40][^ao40_text] Under the title on injuries, "a litigant is said to have received an 'injury' when the praetor or judge delivers an unjust judgment against him."[^ao37] The *Codex* gives the penalty of the judge who judged badly a title of its own. Constantine's law of 319 provides that against the judge "who, corrupted by price or by favour, has judged wrongly," the one he injured is to have his redress, "not only by the loss of his reputation but also by the hazard of the suit."[^ao38] And the extortion title of the same code, the constitutions the *Basilika* would carry into Greek, tells judges that the penalty for their own acts is to be recovered from them or from their heirs, and by an edict of 386 invites anyone of any rank whom a judge has oppressed, or who knows that a sentence was sold, to come forward while the judge holds office or after he has laid it down, "to bring the charge, to prove what he has brought, and, when he has proved it, to carry off both the victory and the glory."[^ao39]

The governor's duty stood beside them. The provincial governor "must prohibit unlawful exactions and acts done by violence, and sales and undertakings extorted by fear or made without payment of the price," and the governors of whole provinces "have the power of the sword."[^ao10] Justinian added, in 544, the rule that judges were on no account to write in their sentences that anyone had been ordered to be produced or to appear "by imperial word issued without writing," and that whoever acted against the chapter should "be compelled to make good from his own substance the damage brought upon the injured person, through the competent judge, and moreover shall suffer the loss of belt and rank."[^ao11] Fifteen years earlier the same emperor had fixed the rule of decision that chapter 2.2 § B quotes, that judgment is given by laws and not by examples, and the Byzantine courts were still applying it five centuries on.[^ao12] Rome's answer to the judge was therefore two actions and a stated duty, with the sovereign's own unwritten command excluded as a shield. Nothing on these pages exempts the judge from either action for what he did as judge; the one exemption is the heir's, and it belongs to the subject's action alone.

## 5.4 Byzantium: Both Remedies Kept, the Bench Salaried

The question this Part puts to Byzantium is whether the Roman remedies survived the eight centuries between Justinian and the Norman Conquest, and the tenth-century *Basilika* answers it in Greek. Its seventh book carries the title on trials under a new rubric, "on judges," and within it Ulpian's rule stands entire: the judge "makes an unlawful judgment who gives sentence deceitfully in circumvention of the law, from evident enmity or favour or gain, and he pays the true value of the suit"; his heir is not liable; and if a litigant makes the judge his heir, another judge is appointed, "for a man judges unjustly in his own cause."[^ao13] The sixtieth book carries the extortion law, and here the compilers did what the Latin text had not: they renamed it. The rubric reads "on the Julian law brought against magistrates or judges or assessors who have stolen in a suit."[^ao14] Under it the Roman fragments stand in order, the officer "who takes money for judging, or for giving or withholding a decision," the action against heirs within the year, the capital penalty; and to them the compilers joined the constitutions of the *Codex*, one of which "wills that magistrates be accused by anyone of theft or any other unlawfulness, both while they are magistrates and after they have ceased to be."[^ao15] The scholiast applied the clause to the judge who sold his sentence, and added that he "is accused of this even while he is still governor of the province."[^ao16] The same volume's prolegomena record the renaming as the compilers' own act.[^ao17]

Two other Byzantine texts state what the sovereign did about the judge before the subject ever sued. The *Ecloga* of Leo III, promulgated in 740, sets out in its preface that "those appointed to administer the laws" must keep free of every human passion and "neither despise the poor nor leave a powerful wrongdoer unexamined," because judges "corrupted by money, or granting favours from friendship, or avenging enmity, or cowed by power, cannot direct judgment aright."[^ao18] It then states what the emperors had done:

> being zealous to put a complete stop to such shameful gain, we have ordained that from our pious treasury salaries be furnished to the most glorious quaestor, to the antigrapheis, and to all who serve in judicial business, so that they take nothing whatever from any person judged before them, lest the prophet's word be fulfilled in us also: they sold justice for silver.[^ao19]

\noindent The bench was salaried from the treasury, and the reason given is that judges had been selling judgment. The *Eisagoge* of 886 defines the emperor himself as "a lawful authority, a common good to all his subjects, neither punishing from antipathy nor doing good from partiality," and provides that he "is bound to vindicate and maintain, first, all that is written in divine Scripture; then what has been laid down by the seven holy councils; and further the approved Roman laws."[^ao20] It carries no title on judges, and none was needed; the *Basilika* held theirs.

The *Peira*, the eleventh-century digest of the judgments of Eustathios Rhomaios, shows the rules applied. Its title on judges records that the magistros used the *Basilika*'s rule on jurisdiction by consent "as a note, condemning a judge, the praetor, who had dragged the wife of Anemas into his court" over a matter already decided, and that the compiler asked how it was that the law made "the judge who drags into his court a man who does not consent subject to the law on violence."[^ao21] The rule is then stated: if the judge, "not heeding the plea to his jurisdiction, coerces him, neither the judge's jurisdiction, that is his sentence or punishment, holds against the defendant, but rather the judge is subject to the law on violence."[^ao22] Appeal is described as the ordinary corrective, by which "the malice and the unskilfulness and the ignorance" of judges are set right.[^ao23] A judge's acts died with him: when the son of a late prefect produced his father's unsigned notes of a case, the court rejected them, "for a man is shown to be heir of his father's estate, not of his judgments."[^ao24] And the court stated the rule of decision it worked under, quoting Justinian's constitution that

> no judge or arbiter is to think that consultations he has not rightly understood must be followed, much less the judgments of the most eminent prefects or other exarchs; for what is badly judged must not extend to the detriment of other judges, since one must judge not by example but by law.[^ao25]

\noindent Byzantium, on these pages, kept both of Rome's remedies against the judge, salaried the bench so that no litigant paid, bound its emperor to the laws, and in its one surviving case-digest condemned a sitting judge for coercing a party outside his jurisdiction. It also forbade, as a matter of enacted law, the practice of deciding by prior decision; the point bears on the definitions chapter 2.2 § B sets out and is stated here as the law states it.

## 5.5 Francia: The Duty Stated, the Bribed Judge Killed

The Frankish record divides the judge's duty from the judge's penalty and puts them in two kinds of text. The capitularies state the duty. Charlemagne's general admonition of 789 commands "those to whom the power of judging is given" to "judge justly, as it is written, not for gifts, because gifts blind the hearts of the prudent and subvert the words of the just, not in flattery, nor in regard of persons," and adds that the judge must first learn the written law "lest through ignorance he stray from the way of truth," and then take care not to decline from right judgment "through the flattery of some, or the love of any friend, or the fear of any powerful man, or for reward."[^ao26] The general capitulary for the missi of 802 puts it in one sentence: "That judges judge justly according to the written law, not according to their own discretion."[^ao27] The capitulary on the doing of justice of 811 to 813 reserves the causes of bishops, abbots, counts and the powerful to the emperor's own presence, and confines the count of the palace to "the doing of justice for the poor and less powerful."[^ao28] Louis the Pious in the 820s addressed the counts directly: "let no cause whatever, neither the taking of gifts nor anyone's friendship nor hatred nor fear nor favour, compel you to deviate from the state of rectitude, but between neighbour and neighbour always judge justly."[^ao29]

The penalty is in the *Lex Ribuaria*. Its title "that no one take gifts in judgment"[^ao30a] orders,

> by consent and counsel and paternal tradition and the custom of the law above all things, that no magnate, mayor of the palace, domesticus, count, grafio, chancellor, or man raised to any rank, sitting in judgment in the Ribuarian province, receive gifts to pervert judgment. And if anyone is caught in this, let him compound with his life.[^ao30]

\noindent The following title forbids any fiscal judge to exact the king's fine before the wrong has been composed; the judge who does so from greed "compounds according to the laws," and the fine is paid not to the judge who committed the fault but to the receiver of the settlement, a third part to the fisc.[^ao31] Francia therefore stated the judge's duty in the emperor's voice and put death on the bribed judge in the folk-law's; the two texts are read here in Boretius's and Sohm's editions, and the *Lex Ribuaria* is read in its Carolingian recension, which is the form in which the capitularies would have met it.

## 5.6 England before the Rule

For a century and a half before Glanvill wrote, and six centuries before *Floyd v. Barker*, the English kings' own dooms answered the judge who judged wrongly with a fine and the loss of rank, and let him escape only on his oath. Edgar's code of Andover, of 959 to about 962, provides, in the Latin of the *Quadripartitus*, that "the judge who shall judge an unjust judgment to anyone shall give the king a hundred and twenty shillings, unless he dare swear that he knew no better," and the bishop of the shire was to collect the fine to the king's hand; the Old English adds that he "shall lose his thegnship for ever, unless he buy it back from the king as the king will allow him."[^ao32] Cnut's code of 1027 to 1034 restated the rule with the motives named: whoever "sets up an unjust law or judges an unjust judgment, for hatred or the taking of money," is liable to the king in a hundred and twenty shillings under English law, unless he dare swear that he knew no better, and loses his rank unless he redeem it; under Danish law, the fine for breach of law.[^ao33] The compilers of Henry I's reign, between 1114 and 1118, gathered the rule into a title "on unjust judgment" and generalised it: whoever

> from anger or hatred or fear or love or greed or any regard whatever judges unjustly or sets up an unjust law, let the penalty be a hundred and twenty shillings, and let him be deprived of his thegnship and of every judicial dignity, unless he redeem himself with the king, as shall be in the king's mercy.

\noindent The oath that he knew no better remains the exception,

> whether it happen in the county court or in any plea of the king, in his own cause or another's, among equals in courts or in their divisions or in their own places, or between lord and man: everywhere let it be forbidden.

\noindent The compilers distinguished the wilful wrong from the ignorant one, and added that a judge who both judges unjustly and inflicts the judgment pays the wergild.[^ao34]

The oath deserves a sentence of its own, because it is the earliest English form of a good-faith exception and it runs the other way from the modern one. It is a defence to the king's penalty, sworn by the judge himself on the relics, and it presupposes that the judge stands answerable in the first place; it bars no complaint and closes no forum. What the modern rule made of good faith is Part 2's subject.

Glanvill closes the sequence at the end of the twelfth century with the remedy against a court. A seignorial court was not bound to defend its record by battle, "but it is bound to maintain its judgment by battle," where a party alleged that the court "had made him a false judgment," and it defended itself by the man who had rendered it.[^ao35a] "And if the court is convicted of it, the lord of the court remains in the king's mercy and loses his court for ever; and moreover the whole court remains in the mercy of the lord king."[^ao35b] The king's writ commanded the sheriff to have the record made in the county and brought "before me or my justices" by four lawful knights.[^ao35] The subject's plea of false judgment thus lay against the court itself, was decided by the king's justices on the record, and cost the lord his jurisdiction.

The subject's own suit did not end with Glanvill. The appeal of felony, the private action of the party or the kin against the wrongdoer, survived the rise of the indictment and outlived the Star Chamber by nearly two centuries. In the King's Bench rolls of 1485 to 1495 there are 398 appeals; the suit of the party "always took precedence over that of the king; as soon as an appeal was notified to the court process on indictment had to cease," and when Parliament in 1487 let the King's suit proceed within the year it did "not attempt to give precedence to the king's suit but merely excused it from having to wait upon that of the party."[^wh1] The action was brought against constables and sheriffs too, though the appellant's oath forbade it and such appeals were usually stopped before a declaration, and its object was not the felon's death but the party's compensation, "the wergild to which society still held a widow entitled."[^wh2] It was abolished in 1819, after the last appellee threw down his glove in the King's Bench.[^wh3] In the order that made the rule of 1607, the subject's plea against every other wrongdoer still outranked the King's; the plea against a judge alone was sent to the King and nowhere else.

## 5.7 What the Older Record Shows

Read as this Part has read them, the six orders return one finding and two data. The finding is that every legal order examined answered the judge with a remedy and stated his duty, and that none answered him with a shield: twelvefold restitution and expulsion in Babylon; appeal from the Council and the citizen's post-audit charge in Athens; the subject's action, the Code's penalty on the judge who judged badly, and the sovereign's prosecution in Rome, with the accuser rewarded and the emperor's unwritten order excluded as a defence; both kept in Byzantium, with a salaried bench, an emperor bound to the laws, and a judge condemned in the case-digest; duty in the Frankish capitularies and death for the bribed judge in the Frankish law; a fine to the king and the loss of every judicial dignity in England from 959, the forfeiture of the court under Glanvill, and the subject's own appeal of felony, outranking the King's suit, to 1819. The first datum is that Byzantium forbade, as enacted law, judging by prior decision, so that no doctrine of adherence to precedent can be attributed to it, and the point is stated in the vocabulary Part 4 defines. The second is that the Anglo-Saxon good-faith oath was a defence to a penalty, not a bar to a complaint.

The degree of examination is stated so that the finding is not read for more than it is. Each text was read at the page of the edition cited, and the Greek and Old English were read a second time by another reader. The Akkadian of Hammurabi's code was not read; the translation was. The *Ecloga*'s penal title, the *Lex Ribuaria*'s other titles, Beames's English Glanvill, and every Islamic, Chinese, Egyptian and Indian source stand outside this Part and are named here as not examined. Within what was examined, the negative is complete: on no page read did any of the six orders exempt a judge, for what he did as judge, from the remedy its own text provided.

The finding meets the doctrine on the ground it chose. The American chain defends the rule by age (chapter 2.3): "as old as the law," "the settled doctrine of the English courts for many centuries," "roots extending to the earliest days of the common law," "jurisprudence in all civilized nations."[^ao41] The question is indeed ancient, and this Part has read the ancient answers. Every one is a remedy. The orders that judged a subject's plea before English adjudication did not settle the question the rule's way, and the English order before the rule, in the dooms and in Glanvill, settled it as they did; the divergence is the decree of 1607. The rule is settled, then, only if the record is read from 1607 forward and no further back. Read the other way, the rule is the innovation and the remedy is the tradition, and if antiquity is to decide the question it decides it against the law that made the rule and the law that received it.

[^ao40_text]: [Ortolan, *Explication historique*, III, 450–451. § 1783: "Ce n'est pas que les erreurs, les injustices ou les violations du droit dont il s'agit, fussent irréparables par d'autres moyens. On pouvait avoir généralement la voie de l'appel. Et même, dans certains cas, cette voie n'était pas nécessaire: tel était celui où la sentence contenait une violation formelle de la loi (si specialiter contra leges, vel S. C., vel constitutionem fuerit prolata. — Cum contra sacras constitutiones judicatur)," and, of such sentences, "on pouvait recommencer de nouveau la cause (causa denuo induci potest. — Potest causa ab initio agitari)." § 1784: "Cela posé, il est naturel de se demander si le recours contre le juge, comme ayant fait le procès sien, pouvait être exercé même dans ces cas où le plaideur avait le moyen de faire réformer la sentence inique, ou de la faire considérer comme nulle; ou bien si ce recours se bornait seulement aux cas où la sentence était irrévocable. Le plaideur paraît avoir eu, dans tous les cas, l'action contre le juge qui avait fait le procès sien; car il pouvait, par plusieurs raisons, ne pas vouloir ou ne pas pouvoir recommencer un nouveau procès contre son adversaire: par exemple si celui-ci était insolvable et ne lui offrait plus aucune garantie, ou par tout autre motif qui lui faisait préférer d'attaquer le juge." ("That settled, it is natural to ask whether the recourse against the judge, as having made the suit his own, could be exercised even in those cases where the litigant had the means of having the unjust sentence reformed or treated as null, or whether the recourse was confined to the cases where the sentence was irrevocable. The litigant appears to have had, in every case, the action against the judge who had made the suit his own, for he might, for several reasons, be unwilling or unable to begin a new suit against his adversary, for instance if the adversary had become insolvent and no longer offered him any security, or for any other reason that made him prefer to proceed against the judge.") § 1785 reads Gaius's measure, "in quantum de ea re aequum religioni judicantis videbitur," and Ulpian's "veram aestimationem litis" (D. 5.1.15.1, quoted in the first paragraph of this chapter) as the same rule, the judge of the action making the estimate. § 1786: "On se demande, à ce propos, pourquoi l'incurie, l'imprudence du médecin étaient rangées, chez les Romains, parmi les délits, et passibles de l'action de la loi Aquilia; tandis que celles du juge ne constituaient qu'un quasi-délit. La véritable raison est que, dans le premier cas, le médecin a lésé un corps, causé un préjudice à un corps (corpori), ce qui rentre dans les prévisions de la loi Aquilia, et par conséquent dans une action pour délit; tandis qu'il n'en est pas de même dans le second cas. Mais on voit que le plus ou moins de culpabilité entre le médecin ou le juge n'entre pour rien dans la question." ("One asks, in this connection, why the carelessness or imprudence of the physician was ranked among the Romans with the delicts and made subject to the action of the lex Aquilia, while that of the judge constituted only a quasi-delict. The true reason is that in the first case the physician injured a body, caused harm to a body, which falls within the provisions of the lex Aquilia and therefore within an action for delict, while it is not so in the second case. But one sees that the greater or lesser culpability of the physician or the judge counts for nothing in the question.") Translations the author's.](cite:ao40_text/1)

---

# Conclusion

This book set out to do two things with one decree, to restore the record it was made by destroying and to put the question it reserved to every order that has answered it. The five Parts are now in, and what they return can be stated in the order they were read.

Part 1 restored the record. The case that founded official immunity was a documented miscarriage of justice, a death by drowning recast as murder, a conviction on examinations taken down in a language the witnesses did not speak, and a court that answered the surviving kinsman's sworn complaint against the trial judge by defacing it. The report that carried the rule out of that court stated its root sentence backwards; the forum the rule reserved was tested once from above, in 1616, against the rule's own author, and once from below, in 1618, against a subject who was punished for asking; and the legal order that made the rule dismantled the court, its methods and its premise, feature by feature, between 1641 and 1701. What stood ready for reception in 1791 was the repudiation. Parts 2 and 3 followed what was received instead, and their Conclusion, placed where the American argument ends, states it: a 1607 decree received in 1869 by a citation that read the report and not the record, read into a statute that makes "every person" liable, carried to the presidency on a same-day pair of decisions that could not supply each other's authority, resting at every node on a hypothetical credited for the official and never tested against the citizen, keeping of a tradition that ran in three directions the one line that shielded the official, a line that descends through Coke's own *Marshalsea* from the decision that forbids it, and rebuilding, bar for bar, the mechanism the Founding cut. The rule's operative clause transferred the subject's plea to a King who never answered one, and the Republic has no officer to whom such a transfer can run. When the Hunzikers, two farmers without counsel, put the source in issue in 1989, the answer came in a footnote of an order the court kept out of the record of the law. Measured by the receiving order's own law, the act that made the rule is the wrong the rule exists to foreclose. Those are the two grounds, and either bars the rule at the threshold.

Part 4 put the same question to other sovereigns and returned a finding in six parts, stated as the texts allow it. On the question the subject asks, who pays for the wrong done to him, England's de jure law answers as the codes of Moscow, Beijing, Pyongyang and Riyadh answer, and not as the kingdoms of the continent answer, which moved that cost onto the State. On the question the sovereign asks, whether the judge is exposed for his decision, the association inverts: England's judge is the most immune of all, while the authoritarian codes keep the judge personally punishable and removable by a political or supervisory organ. The Coke-era exposure of the judge that Part 1 records survives in those codes as an instrument of control and was extinguished in England. The Saudi code is the closest formal counterpart of the English rule: a nullification clause, discipline as the only route, and that route controlled by the judiciary's own council. Anglo-American immunity therefore shares with the authoritarian codes the nullification of the subject's remedy and differs from them by also nullifying the sovereign's; the kingdoms differ from both by paying. What the authoritarian codes operate is the King's half of Coke's sentence, "the King himself shall take account of it, and no other"; the subject's half, the duty "to deliver justice to all his subjects," has a forum in neither England nor those codes.[^concl1] The kingdoms show the alternative that was always available, a forum that receives the subject's plea and answers it without exposing the judge to the sovereign's displeasure, the channel the rule promised and England never built; and England's own eighteenth-century record shows the channel once open to every servant but the judge, so that the transmission removed, with the King's forum, the servant's exposure that had made the King's immunity tolerable in 1701. England's chain no longer names its first link; the American chain alone carries the Star Chamber's sentence forward under the victim's name. America stands with England on both axes and adds the fact the book's vocabulary exists to record: its de jure law channels the plea and its common law nullifies it, so that the American position is a statement about the corpus and not the text, and the distance between the two is the finding.

Part 5 reached back before 1607 and read six orders for what each did with the judge's answer. Every one answered the judge with a remedy and stated his duty, and none answered him with a shield: twelvefold restitution and expulsion in Babylon; appeal and the citizen's post-audit charge in Athens; the subject's action, the Code's penalty on the judge who judged badly and the sovereign's prosecution in Rome; both remedies kept in Byzantium, with a salaried bench and an emperor bound to the laws; duty in the Frankish capitularies and death for the bribed judge in the Frankish law; a fine to the king and the loss of every judicial dignity in England from 959, the subject's own appeal of felony outranking the King's suit to 1819, the judge's oath that he knew no better a defence to the penalty that bars no complaint and closes no forum. On no page read did any of the six exempt a judge, for what he did as judge, from the remedy its own text provided. The rule of 1607 is not the common inheritance of legal orders; among the orders this book has read, it is the exception.

The degree of examination is stated so that the whole is not read for more than it is. For the American order the book has measured the de jure law, the common law, the congruency between the two and the adherence to stare decisis, at the record and from the bottom up. For England it has measured the de jure law and, in Parts 1 and 4, the common law and its congruency in part. For every other order it has classified the de jure law, the text as written, and has not measured that order's common law, its congruency or its adherence to precedent; where a text was read in translation or a title left unread, the Part says so. The comparative finding is therefore a classification of texts, and the book claims nothing more for it. The archival finding is a reading of a record, checked by a professional hand, and the book claims exactly that for it.

One question remains, and it is put to both orders that carried the rule forward, not to the American alone. The report gave the rule its reason in a single sentence: the King "is *de jure* to deliver justice to all his subjects," and because he "cannot do it to all persons" he delegates his power to his judges, "who have the custody and guard of the King's oath." The same page records what the court did with the one subject who asked for that justice against a judge. It held the matters "not examinable in the Star-Chamber" and ordered his bill destroyed "without any answer to it." Read together, the sentence and the order define the delivery of justice from the sovereign's side. Justice is what the sworn judge does; the subject appears only as the person it is delivered to; his plea that it was not done is, in the report's words, a "slander of the justice of the King"; and no proof is admitted against the judge. The question is whether justice can be delivered to a subject whose plea is refused unheard. Every order this book read before 1607 answered it with a remedy against the judge. England today answers by appeal, which corrects the decision and compensates nothing, and receives no plea against the judge. America answers with the rule of 1607 itself, under the complainant's name. On no page of the record this book has read is a plea of Anglo-Saxon law refused unheard called justice done. The duty the report states is owed to all the King's subjects, and it is kept or broken at the subject who asks.

What remains is the observation on which the American Conclusion closed, now with the wider record behind it. The doctrine has held because it has never been made to account for itself, and an absence of holding is not precedent. A court faithful to every decided case can still require the shield to show the warrant it has presupposed and never produced, can decide the structural questions the lineage never reached, and can accept the critiques that hold even if the doctrine is assumed sound. The Founding's answer to the fear the doctrine invokes was to bind the official and keep a forum open for the citizen he wrongs; the kingdoms that kept such a forum are the ones whose immunity is narrowest; and the older record knew no other answer. Restoring that answer demands the overruling of nothing. It demands that enforcement capacity precede the compliance it is invoked to compel. A fear answered by a fear settles nothing. A record ends the weighing. The record exists, and this book has read it. The questions that would undo the doctrine have gone unanswered because they have gone unput, and the remedy is the simplest one the law affords: ask.

**Founding-Era and Historical Sources**

- 1 William Blackstone, *Commentaries on the Laws of England* (1765).
- 3 Joseph Story, *Commentaries on the Constitution of the United States* (1833).
- 3 William Blackstone, *Commentaries on the Laws of England* (1768).
- 8 Hen. 6, c. 12 (1429) (Eng.) (rasure of a record whereby a judgment is avoided made felony; cited as digested at Jenk. 162, *see* the note at chapter 1.2 § B).
- *A Collection of the Proceedings in the House of Commons against the Lord Verulam, Viscount St. Albans, Lord High Chancellor of England, for Corruption and Bribery* (2d ed., London 1721).
- *A Complete Collection of State-Trials*, vol. I (Francis Hargrave ed., 4th ed., London 1776) (item XIX, the proceedings of 27 January 1605/6 against the eight Gunpowder defendants, with the Attorney General's prosecution; every quoted passage collated against the 1776 print; *see* the notes at chapter 1.3 § C).
- *A True Account of the Gunpowder Plot* (extracted from Lingard's *History of England* and Dodd's *Church History* with Tierney's notes and documents; "Vindicator" ed., London, C. Dolman 1851) (the huc-usque record of Coke's hand on the Fawkes examination, stated at pp. 8 & 44; quotations from the Internet Archive copy; the manuscript description rests on the 1851 editors' inspection; *see* the note at chapter 1.3 § C).
- *A True Narrative of the Tryal of Titus Oates for Perjury at the Kings-Bench-Barr at Westminster* (Dublin reprint, 1685).
- Act of Settlement 1701, 12 & 13 Will. 3, c. 2 (Eng.) (judicial tenure during good behaviour; model for U.S. Const. art. III, § 1).
- *Acts of the Privy Council of England*, n.s. vols. 21 (A.D. 1591) & 22 (A.D. 1591–92) (J.R. Dasent ed., HMSO 1900–01) (the Anglesey enforcement record fifteen years before *Floyd* — the Wood–Bulkeley censure arc and the Foulk Thomas matter; *see* the note at chapter 1.1; corpus copies complete, fixity manifests beside them).
- Administration of Justice Act 1774, 14 Geo. 3, c. 39 (Eng.) (the "Murder Act"; removal of officials' capital trials out of Massachusetts, justified in the preamble by the apprehension-of-being-questioned rationale; the Declaration's "mock Trial" grievance's referent).
- Alfred Boretius (ed.), *Capitularia regum Francorum*, vol. I (MGH, Legum sectio II; Hahn, Hanover 1883).
- *An Act for the Regulating of the Privy Council, and for taking away the Court commonly called the Star Chamber*, 16 Car. 1, c. 10 (1641) (Eng.) (dissolving the Court of Star Chamber and the Council in the Marches of Wales).
- *Biblia Sacra Vulgatae Editionis* (Typographia Apostolica Vaticana, Rome 1592) (the Sixto-Clementine editio princeps).
- *Biblia Sacra Vulgatae Editionis Sixti V et Clementis VIII auctoritate edita* (Desclée, Lefebvre, Tournai 1881).
- *Biblia, the Bible, that is, the holy Scripture of the Olde and New Testament* (Myles Coverdale, 1535).
- Bill of Rights 1689, 1 W. & M. sess. 2, c. 2 (Eng.) (the cruel-and-unusual-punishments clause carried into U.S. Const. amend. VIII).
- C. E. Zachariä von Lingenthal (ed.), *Collectio librorum juris Graeco-Romani ineditorum: Ecloga Leonis et Constantini, Epanagoge Basilii Leonis et Alexandri* (Barth, Leipzig 1852).
- C. E. Zachariä von Lingenthal (ed.), *Jus Graeco-Romanum*, pars I, *Practica ex actis Eustathii Romani* (Weigel, Leipzig 1856).
- C. W. E. Heimbach (ed.), *Basilicorum libri LX*, vols. I (1833), V (1850) and VI, *Prolegomena et Manuale Basilicorum* (1870) (Barth, Leipzig).
- *Calendar of the Close Rolls, Edward III*, vol. IX (1349–1354) (Public Record Office, London).
- *Calendar of the Patent Rolls, Edward III*, vol. IX (1350–1354) (Public Record Office, London).
- *Calendar of Wynn (of Gwydir) Papers, 1515–1690* (National Library of Wales 1926) (the number space keying the published catalogue descriptions cited in the Wynn Papers notes at chapter 1.2 § D).
- Cicero, *Pro Plancio*, in *Cicero: The Speeches* (N.H. Watts trans., Loeb Classical Library 158, 1923).
- *Cobbett's Complete Collection of State Trials*, vol. II (T.B. Howell ed., London 1809) (the proceedings against Wraynham, 1618, and against Lord Chancellor Bacon, 1621).
- *Cobbett's Complete Collection of State Trials*, vol. III (T.B. Howell ed., London 1809) (Oliver St John's speech to the Lords on the ship-money judgment, 1640).
- Edward Coke, *La Size Part des Reports* (London 1607) (the English preface "To the Reader" — the reply-refusal to Persons and the reported-the-Text self-description; quoted from the first-edition leaves, from the BL microfilm; *see* the note at chapter 1.3 § C).
- Edward Coke, *The Fourth Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England* (London 1797 ed.) (ch. 7, The Court of Kings Bench; the record-permanence passage at p. 73, in the 1797 print; *see* the note at chapter 1.2 § B).

**Manuscript and Archival Sources**

- Harvard Law School Library, MS 149 (Star Chamber Reports, 1607–1623), ff. 81r–83r (the second, independent contemporaneous account of *Floyd v. Barker*).
- National Library of Wales, Wynn of Gwydir Papers, GB 0210 GWYDIR (letters series NLW MSS 9051E–9069E; documents series NLW MSS 463B–470E) (cited at catalogue-description tier only, by the Library's own published item descriptions; *see* the Wynn Papers notes at chapter 1.2 § D).
- The National Archives (UK), E 178/5052 (Exchequer, King's Remembrancer, special commissions of inquiry: Anglesey, 3 Jas. I) (the 1605 escheat commission and its November 1605 inquisition; author's working transcription, structural-layer readings only; *see* the note at chapter 1.1).
- The National Archives (UK), STAC 8/203/38 (*Lloyde v. Lewys*, Anglesey, June 1607) (the cause's surviving file — examinations, sixteen-article interrogatory instrument, and a defendant's answer; the bill not extant, catalogued "Bill wanting"; quoted from the author's transcription of record copy RC8368179 (2026), conformed to the professional verification transcription, whose final texts of all three surviving documents returned in August 2026; *see* the transcription note at chapter 1.2 § C).
- The National Archives (UK), STAC 8/204/31 (Star Chamber; examinations of 30 April 1608) (the follow-on file recording the second prosecution upon the same transaction; outside the commissioned transcription's scope — author-layer readings, disclosed as such; *see* the note at chapter 1.2 § F).
